## T: T and G–tenting

### T and G
 
 <i>abbr.</i> tongue and groove(d)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> ... cleanliness of person are consonant with lean, mat-covered, earthen floors, but that T. and G. flooring kept clean is nicer and healthier; ... <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,214</i>)

### T house
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  There are one and two storey weather-boarded houses, square and bell-shaped tents, T houses and V huts. <i>(Oct 21, 1934,Nelson Examiner,4</i>)

### T-shaped shed
 
 <i>n.</i> an early design of woolshed, where a shearing board is located on the perpendicular and wool handling is done at one end.

>  <b>CAIRD</b> The most generally adopted, and probably, the most useful form of shed, is what was commonly called the T-shaped shed. <i>(1874,Notes on Sheepfarming in NZ,23</i>)

### t-tub
 
 <i>n.</i> a member of the tangata whenua

>  Despite the Maori history of this area, the mock-medieval ceremony contains not a single nod to the t-tubs - as one of our leading politicians once cheerfully designated the tangata whenua - but we'll let that pass. <i>(Nov 20, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)

### T.A.B.
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Sa-eeda Ahmed, Akers, arrak,/ Bints, Bomonti. backsheech, barrak;/ Centipedes and Cleopatra,/ Dust and desert, djinns and Fatima;/ `Eggs-cook, ana miskeen,'/ Fever, flies, and felaheen;/ Gehenam, Galabeyah, gods,/ Harems, hooshters, heat and hods;/ `Igri wallah, imshi Y'allah.'/ Jackos, jackals, `gala gala';/ Khamseen, kan kan, tala hena,/ Lice and locusts, love and Lena;/ Mirage, Mahomet, mosquistoe,/ Niggers, T.A.B. and C2;/ Olives oranges, askarie./ Peanuts, pyramids, and gharry;/ `Queens' and `Quacks' and `k'weis kateer,'/ Ruins, rum, and Gyppo beer;/ Scorpion, sand, sun, sin, and Scarab,/ Tibbin, taube, and tricky Arab;/ Whizz-bangs, wadis, `yin-al-deenak,'/ Yids, and yesterday, yeh meenak;/ Kestrels, kingfishers, and kites,/ Torrid days and sleepless nights;/ Mosques and minarets--maleesh;/ `O'ariglak,' `feluce--mafeesh'--/ Bookra, badein, ack-ack-emma,/ The soldier's farewell--ma-es salemma; <i>(Jul 10, 1919,Quick March,33</i>)

### T.C.
 
 <i>abbr.</i> (Teachers) Training College

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> The most widely known game with insects is a game named variously by T.C. students as Butcher Boys (Gisborne, New Plymouth, Kaponga, [405] Te Pohu, Newall, Sth. Wellington), Butcher Bats (Ohau, Takaka), Penny Doctor Beetles (Tauranga, Pipiriki, Rotorua), or Fishing (Morunui). It is a game in which straws which have been suitably moistened with spittle are placed in an insects [sic] hole and are flicked out when the insect bites the end of the straw. Most of the reports of this practice come from the North Island, though it was known in the South as early as 1900 (Waikouaiti). <i>(1953,Unorganized Games,1/404</i>)

### T.E.P.
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  T.E.P. <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

### T.H. Lowry
 
 <i>n.</i> Maori

>  There are many examples of rhyming slang in use at Paparua:.. <i>T.H. Lowry</i> for Maori. <i>(Oct 1999,NZWords,2(2) : 2</i>)

### T2
 
 <i>n.</i> a traffic lane restricted to vehicles carrying at least two people at peak times.

>  The letter began by acknowledging the letter sent by the association, but stated that the appropriate time to address any changes to the operating conditions of the T2 lanes is when the formal review takes place. <i>(Apr 12, 2006,Northern Courier,5</i>)

>  When the T2 transit lanes are not in operation motorists are encouraged to use the right-hand lane. <i>(Apr 26, 2006,Capital Times,11</i>)

>  The T2 lanes are restricted to vehicles carrying two or more people at peak times and are designed to improve traffic flows. <i>(Sep 11, 2006,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

>  The kerbside lanes are T2 lanes, which means only vehicles carrying two or more people during these operating hours can use them. <i>(Feb 15, 2006,Porirua City News,1</i>)

>  Transit is listening to the concerns Paremata residents have about T2 lanes ... Like Wellington's bus lanes, the start of and parts of the T2 lanes will be painted green. <i>(May 2, 2006,Kapi-Mana news,1</i>)

>  Mrs Osvald says motorists who break the rule and use the T2 lanes outside the transit hours are proving to be dangerous ... <i>(Feb 15, 2006,Porirua City News,1</i>)

>  .. Transit is going to install additional T2 markings on the road inside the kerbside lanes. <i>(Jan 15, 2006,Porirua City News,1</i>)

>  While Transport Safety Minister Harry Duynhoven was observing traffic, two trucks went down the closed T2. <i>(Jul 13, 2007,Dominion Post,A6</i>)

### ta moko
 
 <i>n.</i> [-] (See [moko](mob-mys.md#moko))

>  At this stage it is envisaged that the Awanuiarangi's visual arts faculty will move to the site - carving, weaving, ta moko and contemporary art - with room for the expansion there of other faculties. <i>(Nov 2, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,4</i>)

>  A three-year degree programme covering five different aspects of Maori art including: Weaving (raranga), Carving (whakairo), Painting (te tuhi whakaniko), Architecture (whaihanga), and Body Art (ta moko). <i>(Dec 2, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,10</i>)

>  Sometimes it was simply the landscape; at others a more assertive symbolism replaced the birds, as in the ta moko of "Vanishing Sign II" (1975). <i>(Oct 18, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),54</i>)

>  The exhibition of the cream of Maori art, including weaving, carving, ta moko and kapa haka performances, runs for 10 days. <i>(Aug 4, 2005,NZ Herald,A10</i>)

### TAAC
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Total Allowable Commercial Catch [of fish]

>  Orange Roughy Management Company chief executive George Clement said while it won't change the Total Allowable Catch (TACC) it will mean the industry will be able to take more fish from the fields. <i>(Jun 26, 2001,Evening Post,9</i>)

### taapini
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Parapercis colias </i>.. [other name(s)] N.Z. cod, taapini fillets, coalfish <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

### tab
 
 <i>n.</i>



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i>tab, to keep (Phr.)</i> to stand guard while some illegal activity is taking place. [also to keep tabs on someone=check on what he/she is doing or where he/she is???] <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),19</i>)

### TAB
 
 <i>n.</i> the Totalisator Agency Board set up in 1952; any one of its local betting agencies [ORIGIN: acronym, also attrib. below]

>  At one stage TABs had to be down alleys, now they're in shop-fronts. <i>(Feb 28, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

>  TAB offices would become dens of vice. <i>(Aug 3, 1972,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  Another suggested a foaming jug and a TAB queue "would be better examples of our national thought and pride." <i>(Jan 21, 2001,Sunday Star Times,D2</i>)

>  'Betting called TAB' is an institution which complete embraces the whole nation. <i>(Oct 27, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12</i>) [NOTES: Quoted from Listener, August 5, 1960.]

>  Sited well away from churches, with just one discrete sign in the windows, the Totalisator Agency Board's (TAB) first two branches were forbidden by law to advertise their opening... "The New Zealand TAB was the first of its type in the world and I have to say it was pretty successful." <i>(Apr 2, 2001,Evening Post,13</i>)

>  It's a day off - with the only difference that Anzac Day is not yet Mondayised - it's off to the football or into the TAB or into the garden. <i>(Apr 25, 1993,Sunday Times (NZ),8</i>)

>  Few Bank of New Zealand employees have worked as ticket-sellers for the T.A.B. <i>(Jan 21, 1953,NZ Truth,12</i>)

>  Think instead, says Kevin Gutschlag, one of four young "risk managers" employed in the TAB's sports betting operations, "of a bank or a dealing room, of people trading forex". <i>(Sep 29, 1998,Evening Post,28</i>)

>  A chance meeting at the TAB brings them back together 30 years on. <i>(Apr 7, 2004,Capital Times,12</i>)

>  It has a neat T.A.B. on the window... The collators and sub-collators were on duty at the Eva Street headquarters of the T.A.B. <i>(Apr 18, 1952,Evening Post,14</i>)

>  There is ample evidence that the T.A.B. has captured the interest of our teen-agers. <i>(Jan 16, 1953,Evening Post</i>) [NOTES: [p?: Letters to Editor]]

>  I was talking to a solemn man the other day who took a very serious view of the TAB, mainly because a cheerful and feckless neighbour of ours ... had backed a double .. <i>(Jun 1953,Here & Now,16</i>)

>  <b>JOURNET</b> Citizens have a busy day investing at the T.A.B [heading] <i>(1954,Take My Tip,71</i>)

>  <b>A.R.D. FAIRBURN</b> You will find Social Security going at full blast - long queues for the T.A.B. (off-course totalisator), ... <i>(Nov 30, 1955,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Nov 30 A.R.D. Fairburn in Letters (1981) 236]

>  <b>GOLDBLATT</b> T.A.B., the Government betting office with the grand title of Totalizator Agency Board ... draws its support from ... every adult age group. <i>(1957,Democracy at Ease,8</i>)

>  <b>FAIRBURN</b> Trams were run backwards, and the T.A.B./ Took all the business away from the boozers/ <i>(1958,Poetry Harbinger,17</i>) [NOTES: (`A Dream of John Ball')]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> ... on to Worcester Street with the church cowed by the State Fire shouldering it, the old Civic and the new T.A.B., ... <i>Ibid.</i> 62 There is not a large crowd either, because of the weather or the T.A.B. ... <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,26</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> ... Poetry was not yet as popular as the T.A.B. and there was no legitimate reason why it should be ... <i>(1963,Reservoir, The,168</i>) [NOTES: (The Triumph of Poetry)]

>  <b>BOOTH</b> `Stop off at the TAB., will you, Jim,' ...the converted shop which now carried the big sign `Totalistor Agency Board', the open sesame for off-course betting. <i>(1964,Footsteps in the Sea,64</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> ... , she was carrying a parcel of fish and chips wrapped in newspaper and still hot from the cooking vats in Mr. Vuzich's shop next to the T.A.B. where she must have bought it, ... <i>(1965,Outcasts,18</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> The introduction of off-course betting in 1949 brought the doubles totalizator and the TAB (`there shall be a board [175] to be known as the Totalizator Agency Board' - Gaming Amendment Act, 1949). <i>Ibid.</i> 175 The currency of the abbreviation TAB [for Totalizator Agency Board] is ensured ... <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,174</i>)

>  <i>T.A.B. n</i>. Totalizer [sic] Agency Board, organisation which legally conducts betting on horse races in N.S.W. and Victoria. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),19</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> Here, you can put this on at the T.A.B. One quid double. Off the course. <i>(1982,Collected Plays,28,1968 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Wide Open Cage)]

>  <b>ARMFELT</b> The T.A.B.'s the betting shop for the races - the Totalisator Agency Board. <i>(1971,Catching Up,31</i>)

>  <b>MITCHELL</b> <i>T.A.B</i>.: System of investment similar to purchase of shares in Mineral Securities or Rolls-Royce. <i>(1972,Half-Gallon Quarter-Acre Pavlova Paradise, The,183</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> T.A.B.: Totalisator Agency Board; betting shop. <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden,141</i>)

>  <i>TAB abbrev. for: ... 2. Austral. & N.Z</i>. Totalizator Agency Board. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,1188</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 780]]

>  <i>T.A.B. VIEW ON TWO DOUBLES </i> <i>(Oct 29, 1957,Evening Post,14</i>)

>  <b>JOURNET</b> The T.A.B. shop is the easiest thing in the world to use. Its straightforwardness is startling. There is no attempt to mesmerise you with jingling bells or fabulous dividends and no nonsense about weights or jockeys. ... Many patrons cannot accept this simplicity and try to complicate things for themselves by examining the charts on the T.A.B. shop walls. These are cyclostyled sheets of paper giving the name of every horse running that day together with an appropriate number by the name. <i>(1954,Take My Tip,71</i>)

>  A happy punter friend of mine, a regular patron of the TAB.. tells me that last Saturday he picked the first leg in every double in the country run that day. <i>(Jun 10, 1970,Evening Post,44</i>)

>  Dolapol.. looks the logical winner of the second leg of the Woodville TAB double. <i>(Feb 13, 1970,Evening Post,RaceGuide News 1</i>)

>  [Heading] TAB Agents want Fee Disclosure. <i>(Jun 3, 1970,Evening Post,16</i>)

>  It's a world away from the Pacific Island and Maori hub of East Tamaki, where just streets away old state houses sit alongside TABs and taro shops and the local primary school has barbed wire around its perimeter. <i>(Nov 30, 2003,Sunday Star Times,C6</i>)

### tabernacle
 
 <i>n.</i> the TAB (Totalisator Agency Board)

>  Put your money on the jockey, my grandfather was wont to say when we were over the road at the tabernacle (TAB), and it was my duty to "follow the Chinaman up to the tote and see what he's betting on." <i>(Jul 9, 2002,Dominion Post TV Week,B9</i>)

### table
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The constant outflow of crushed quartz and water from the stamp boxes is received on a table, having on it grooves or ripples, containing quicksilver and a large extent of amalgamated copper plates smoothly nailed on. This may be called the silver table; ... The flow then passes over blanket tables, which arrest mechanically all the heavier particles. <i>Ibid.</i> 179 The blanket tailings or blanketings, as they are otherwise called, consist mostly of iron pyrites and other sulphides, cobines with quartz, and contain a fair proportion of gold ...that had escaped over the silver tables. These tailings are treated in berdans with extra quicksilver and ground up. The berdans now in use at the Thames ... are generally five feet in diameter ... At one time a couple of rotating balls were considered sufficient for the amount of crushing required; now, the general practice is to have a loose ball as well as a stationary one attached to a chain, and it is called a drag ball; this drag does more work than a loose ball ... for the drag grinds the bowl as much as the tailings. <i>(1876,TrNZI,8/177</i>) [NOTES: (Goodall Thames Gold-fields)]

>  The battery has three stampers in each box, double cams, quickslver placed in battery boxes, grating perpendicular, with round holes. The copper-plate tables are about 12ft. long by 5ft. wide, and raised about 14in. above the blanket boards; ... [The blankets] appear to be ordinary grey blankets of a very inferior description. <i>(1868,Thames Miner's Guide, The,93</i>)

>  <b>MOFFATT</b> Yet still they dig and delve, be the weather foul or fair, / And talk of plush and tables, and a thousand pounds a share. <i>(1896,Adventures by Sea and Land,70,1970 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [a1882] 1896 Moffatt Adventures (1979) 70 [Walter D. Moffatt, d. 3.1.1882: poem]]

>  <b>HEINZ</b> Under the chute board, which carries the water and sand to the head of the table, is the long inclined board called the `table', and on this plush is fitted for its entire length. <i>(1952,Prospecting for Gold,35</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> to out-drink, to make drunk (occas. be under the table, to be drunk, `under the weather')

>  <b>NEWTON</b> ... the gang set about `putting me under the table'. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,35</i>) [NOTES: nf OED, P [but check under]]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> ... the gang set about `putting me under the table'. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,35</i>) [NOTES: nf OED, P [but check under]]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> ... the gang set about `putting me under the table'. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,35</i>) [NOTES: nf OED, P [but check under]]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BLYTH</b> <i>Gold Saving Table</i>: A sloping surface covered with goldsaving cloth over which are placed slats to break up the wash and catch the heavier particles. <i>(1935,Gold Mining Year Book,11</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> a wool table

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> Erina had gathered up Mutu's last fleece and thrown it expertly on the table before the classer. A few random wrist movements removed the necks and edging pieces. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,3</i>)

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> Erina had gathered up Mutu's last fleece and thrown it expertly on the table before the classer. A few random wrist movements removed the necks and edging pieces. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,3</i>)

### table-drain
 
 <i>n.</i> a shallow drain

### table-hand
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The table hands sat in their aprons, ready to start. <i>(Dec 2, 1897,Otago Witness,58</i>)

>  <i>(Oct 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,311</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> A common fault is for a wool-table to be too high, which makes harder work for the table hands and the `fleeco'. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,92</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) an employee who works on a wool table or as assistant wool classer.

>  It is necessary for him to see that all the table-hands have all the needful packs.. <i>(Oct 1902,NZ Farmer,567</i>)

>  <b>STAPLES</b> The table-hand, a retired farmer, picked off the dirty bits and rolled the fleece and it was picked up by the presser. <i>(1964,Cooks and Shepherds Come Away,117</i>)

>  <b>STAPLES</b> I'm very grateful to the table-hand who taught me all I know about wool-classing. <i>(1964,Cooks and Shepherds Come Away,118</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> The rousties flew up and down the board while the table-hands tore at the necks and skirtings the moment the fleece was thrown. [table hand] <i>(1969,Open Country Calling,258</i>)

### tableman
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) an employee who works on a wool table, skirting and rolling fleeces. (See [table-hand; wool roller](../T#table-hand;-wool-roller))

>  Tablemen and Pressers wanted; also, a couple of Cooks. <i>(Nov 24, 1873,Tuapeka Times,2</i>)

>  Evidence was given by Walter Lindsay, laborer, and James Price, tableman, as to accused coming to town and disposing of the horse, saddle, and bridle. <i>(Oct 22, 1898,Poverty Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  I think yearly wages will have to come down, before any reduction can justly be made in wages of tablemen and knockabouts at shearing. <i>(Oct 26, 1885,Ida Valley Station Letterbook</i>)

### tablet
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> Inside the station they had the big red metal boxes that work the old-fashioned tablet signalling system. If you ever wonder what drivers are picking up or unloading on the end of a curved piece of wood, it is the tablet or biscuit. A train cannot get through a section of rail without the biscuit matching the one in the red metal box, which is connected to the manually operated signals. Automatic signals are phasing this system in to rural museums. <i>(1985,G'day Country,32</i>)

### tablet porter
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> An old A class engine was sighing gently, ... while the driver and fireman chatted with the guard and tablet porter. <i>(1978,Elver, The,43</i>)

### tablet station
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The tablet station and post office at Mangamahoe, Wairarapa, was completely destroyed by fire.. on Saturday morning. <i>(Aug 8, 1916,Rangitikei Advocate,4</i>)

### taboo
 
 <i>v.</i>



 
 <i>adj.</i> [also tabu]

>  The hand which has passed that [of] Rauparaha is tabu to every English gentleman or lady. <i>(Jan 11, 1845,New Zealand Spectator & Cook's Strait Guardian,1</i>)

>  The Government was patriarchal, and was divided into three classes, all of which were frequently represented by one person, viz.: The prophet, the charmer, and the hereditary head of the tribe, who was "taboo" or sacred; in fact, the institution of the "taboo" was interwoven with all their institutions, and the penalty for breaking it was death. <i>(Feb 1, 1862,Otago Daily Times,2</i>)

>  He who swam in the sacred waters became "taboo". <i>(Dec 14, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,14</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  That near Otaki a chief, who was drowned, had been washed ashore, and in consequence the natives had tabooed, or made sacred, the ground, which precluded persons from proceeding to Wanganui by land. <i>(Jun 12, 1841,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,2</i>)

>  They also taboo the nearest relatives' hands, that is to say, make them sacred, so that they cannot eat or touch anything with them for a certain perod [sic]. <i>(Sep 5, 1840,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)

>  He was for some time.. tabooed by all on board. <i>(Feb 19, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,20</i>)

>  The process of "tabooing" the lands had caused the natives to meet and talk together. <i>(Aug 16, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,14</i>)

>  Certain warehousemen who were wont to develop the muscles of their legs by climbing Grey-street on their way home, after a hard day's toil, have tabooed the street, and now proceed by round-about roads on their homeward journey. <i>(May 23, 1891,Observer (Auckland),12</i>)

>  The forest in order to preserve it from the fate of similar of its predecessors, has been "tabu'd." <i>(Jan 5, 1878,Grey River Argus,4</i>)

>  Not content with preventing the use of free passes and privilege tickets by through express trains from 16th December to 3rd January, the department has also tabooed all through passenger, mail, or express trains from 23rd December to 3rd January. <i>(Dec 23, 1910,Evening Post,8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [cf tapu] (See [tapu](t-ten.md#tapu))

>  The heathen taboo, as firmly binds the missionary native as it does the unconverted. <i>(Sep 28, 1852,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  In the meantime either a system of taboo must be invented, or the party in possession of the claim will most likely have worked out the ground, and departed for fields and pastures new. <i>(Mar 30, 1864,Otago Daily Times,5</i>)

>  <b>BIDWILL</b> I said that a Pakiha could do no harm in going up, as no place was taboo to a Pakiha; that the taboo only applied to Mowries; and finally, that if the mountain was an atua, I must be a greater atua, or I could not have got to the top of it, . <i>(1841,Rambles in NZ,85,1952 ed.</i>)

### tabooed
 
 <i>adj.</i> [see also tapued]

>  <b>ELDER</b> Whenever we come near a piece of tabooed ground and ask the reason why it is tabooed, if a person has been buried in it we always receive for an answer <i>`atua</i> lies there.' <i>(1934,Marsden's Lieutenants,78</i>) [NOTES: 1815 Mar Kendall in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 78]

>  <b>McCRAE</b> ... it was called Whari Taboo which means `a sacred house' and in which it was usual to deposit whatever was tabooed. <i>(1820,Journal,15,1928 ed.</i>)

>  <b>CRUISE</b> [Tetoro] tying a thread pulled out of his Kaka-how or upper garment, round the guard of the trigger, said it was <i>tabooed</i> .... and must be his when he got to New Zealand. <i>Ibid.</i> 84 So cautious were the natives lest we should approach those tabbooed grounds, that they had persons ready to warn us off ... <i>(Feb 16, 1820,Journal,23,1957 ed.</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> The cock was a much more heinous delinquent, as no place would satisfy him for his roost but the top of a small building which was <i>tabooed</i>, and of course made sacred against the profane touch of all animals, winged or not winged. <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/211</i>)

>  <b>MARSDEN</b> They were fishing for none but swordfish, with short lines, and all the fish they caught of this kind were tabooed, and could not be disposed of as they were to be preserved for their winter food <i>(1819,Letters & Journals of Samuel Marsden,145,1932 ed.</i>)

>  <b>EARLE</b> The white taboo'd day [sc. Sunday], when the packeahs (or white men) put on clean clothes and leave off work. <i>(1832,Narrative of a Nine Months Residence in NZ,146</i>)

>  <b>JOHN KING</b> When they understood the stone was taboo'd they let it abide. <i>(May 3, 1823,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1823 May 3 John King in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 255]

>  When the first canoe arrives, no one but the men who belong to her, are allowed to approach near; the natives say she is taboo'd till her first [76] load of fish are landed. <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,75,1986 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HODGSKIN</b> When land is purchased of the chiefs, it is tabooed to the buyer, the taboo being a religious ceremony performed by the chiefs; ... <i>(1841,Narrative of Eight Months Sojourn in NZ, A,11</i>)

>  <b>McKILLOP</b> The piece of ground on which they were encamped having been tabued, or made sacred, ... <i>(1849,Reminiscences,184</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> On this fence were hung almost the whole of the deceased's personal effects - his blankets, the highly-valued <i>kaitaka</i> mat (which has now almost ceased to be made), his musket and double-barrelled gun, cartouch-box, tomahawk, and many other things - all destined to hang there forever, tabooed to the memory of the departed. <i>(1881,Poenamo,Bk3/5/171</i>)

>  <b>MANING</b> They were, in fact, as irreverent pakehas used to say, `tabooed an inch thick', and as for the head chief, he was perfectly unapproachable. <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,(viii) 122</i>)

>  CORRESPONDENCE RESPECTING THE "TABOOED" GROUND. <i>(Jul 10, 1841,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>) [NOTES: Headline.]

### tabu
 
 <i>n.</i> typical Army ball(s)-up [(cf NABU, SABU, P: TCCFU: typical coastal command fuck up; ca1941-44 TABU: Army: mostly officers': N. Africa: 1940-45]

>  <b>FULLARTON</b> There are three words in the Middle East soldiers' vocabulary describing the normal maladministration of an army. A tabu, an acrostic word, is `typical Army ball-up', a Sabu is self-adjusting ball-up, while a Nabu is a non-adjustable ball-up. <i>Ibid.</i> 207 <i>Tabus</i> had dwindled to a minimum. <i>(1944,Troop Target,38</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> (See [tapu](t-ten.md#tapu))

### taerarua
 
 <i>n.</i> a bisexual [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  There is no traditional word for "straight" or "bixxxxxx" since the first one you have to translate literately [sic] (totika) and the second you have to form using Maori equivalents for the English compound (taerarua = taera xxxxxx desire) rua (two)) <i>(Dec 9, 2007,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: www.maori.org.nz/papa_panui/forum_topic.asp?]

>  Yes, there are deep seeded [sic] issues about Takatapui/tootika/taerarua in every society/community/religon [sic] and all of these issues need to be addressed in a forum such as this ... <i>(Dec 11, 2007,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: www.maori.org.nz/papa_panui/forum_topic.asp?]

### taewa
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also tiver](ten-tot.md#tiver))

>  The indigenous Maori potato Taewa could be a hot new gourmet food on menus, thanks to trials now under way at seven sites around New Zealand... At their best, Taewa are a flavoursome root vegetable in colours ranging from purples to reds and yellows, which are traditionally boiled or steamed... [The research] builds on a number of other research projects already carried out into Taewa, which have helped identify four cultivars... These are called Tutaekuri, Kowiniwiri aka Karuparera, Moemoe and Huakaroro. <i>(Aug 28, 2003,Dominion Post,C7</i>)

>  [Hdg] Maori spud seen to be money spinner. Taewa - the Maori potato - which has an important place in Maori culture, could be a money-spinner... The urenika variety, with its bright purple flesh and skin, and the kowiniwini, with its purple and cream skin, would look a treat on any restaurant table.... It was probable the taewa arrived by ship in the mid to late 18th century and had become established by the time of the major settlement about 1840... Northland's Ngati Hine people have identified the Maori varieties karoro, peruperu, uwhiwhero, kowiniwini and urenika as possible pre-European varities. <i>(May 18, 1999,Dominion,12</i>)

>  The accompanying sauteed watercress and pikopiko were real enough, as was the bed of kumara mash studded with pieces of tawea - the correct name for the elongated cultivar of Maori potato known crudely as tuae kuri (and if you don't know the meaning of that, I'm not telling, except to say that kuri means dog). <i>(Sep 18, 2003,Dominion Post,D5</i>)

>  A research project to re-establish taewa - Maori potatoes - as a sustainable commercial horticultural crop has received $100,000 from a new Technology New Zealand fund for Maori collectives. Taewa have been grown in New Zealand for centuries, forming the basis of the market garden industry established in the 1800s to supply early European settlers. <i>(Aug 8, 2003,Dominion Post,C4</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Taewa - the ordinary potato introduced by early voyagers. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,65</i>) [NOTES: c1880 South Island Maori Lists `Wahi Mahika Kai' in 1945 Beattie Maori Place-names of Canterbury 65]

>  Their hospitality was carried out in a very respectful manner, and the poor woman wished to do me still more honour. She took some preserved meat out of a `taha' (Native gourd). <i>(1928,Grace Pioneer Missionary,237</i>) [NOTES: 1871 Oct 28 T.S.Grace Journal in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 237]

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> Of old, long ago, I had life -/ I have lived!/ And a <i>taha</i> fill'd, is it not full?/ A web woven, is there no rest? [fn1] <i>Taha</i>: A calabash. <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,195</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> [The chiefs] had their share brought to them in calabashes called <i>taha, ... </i> <i>(1948,Taina,112</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,93</i>)

>  A taha Maori element was included in today's opening of the Post-Primary Teachers' Association conference in Wellington. The `Maori dimension' came in the form of a special Maori welcome for the Prime Minister, Mr Lange, .. <i>(Aug 21, 1984,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  A taha Maori element was included in today's opening of the Post-Primary Teachers' Association conference in Wellington. The `Maori dimension' came in the form of a special Maori welcome for the Prime Minister, Mr Lange, .. <i>(Aug 21, 1984,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  Academics working on the study believe that taewas, as well as heving an important place in Maori culture, are a potential money-spinner. <i>(Nov 9, 2000,Dominion,11</i>)

>  You say potato, I say taewa. The commercialisation of the Maori potato, or taewa, has been a long-term project for researcher Nick Roskruge. In July a hui was held for marae groups who now grow taewa semi-commercially, feeding their families and selling the surplus... There are more than a dozen varieties of taewa, some purple-skinned, others with mellow yellow flesh. <i>(Nov 2003,Massey,No. 15/8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) an early variety of Maori potato. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  The historical journey of the humble Maori potato, taewa, will soon be traced using DNA technology .. Taewa expert Nick Roskruge, who specialises in indigenous horticulture, will travel to Lima in June to compare taewa DNA with other potatoes deposited there. <i>(Apr 22, 2009,Dominion Post,B3</i>)

>  Maori are working with Massey University students planting about 25 varieties of Maori potatoes (taewa) as part of continuing research into seed and growth characteristics. <i>(Nov 9, 2000,Dominion Post,11</i>)

>  The humble potato chip was given a new look with the introduction of taewa fries at the West Coast Wild Foods festival. Different varieties of taewa are grown at the Palmerston North campus as part of research managed by Nick Roskruge. <i>(May 2, 2002,Internet - see Notes,News</i>) [NOTES: www.agriculture.massey.ac.nz]

>  Mr Roskruge said the Taewa potatoes were also an important source of genetic variety. <i>(May 9, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,19</i>)

### TAF
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [acronym for] trading among farmers, a scheme where farmer shareholders of Fonterra shares can trade shares. [ORIGIN: acronym]

>  Fonterra stands a great chance of cementing its name as one of the biggest players in global dairying as the TAF journey begins. But every shareholder must be on the journey. Leaving behind the 33.5% who voted against TAF is not an option. <i>(Jul 3, 2012,Rural News,22</i>)

>  Hawera shareholder Tony Buist - the sole dissenter to the TAF proposal at the satellite meeting in Hawera two years ago - said Friday's meeting in the town was orderly, and farmers asked sensible questions. While those asking questions did not seem to favour TAF, animosity towards Fonterra was not apparent. As in 2010, he would vote against TAF which, if approved, would be compromised "pretty quickly," he said. <i>(Jun 14, 2012,Daily News,A15</i>)

>  Fonterra's 10,500 shareholders voted overwhelmingly more than a year ago for TAF, but in the gap between then and getting legislation to allow it, new concerns have surfaced. A petition to directors has requested another vote on TAF before the scheme became a "fait accompli". <i>(Oct 18, 2011,Waikato Times,A13</i>)

### tag
 
 <i>n.</i> a graffiti painter's signature

>  Graffitti Speak... Tag: An artist's signature, usually very stylised. One of the most common types of graffiti seen in Wellington region. More often done with big felt tip markers. <i>(Nov 30, 2000,Evening Post,19</i>)

>  Mr Chin said it was impossible to blame one person for a spate of tags because those involved had taken to signing each other's tag. <i>(Dec 28, 1991,Evening Post,3</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to daub graffitti on

>  I would never tag someone's fence. I would never tag someone's letter box. <i>(Nov 30, 2000,Evening Post,19</i>)

>  Terry O'Shea said many people in the area [sc. Island Bay] had had their fences and garage doors tagged. <i>(Dec 28, 1991,Evening Post,3</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a heaviest grade of prime lamb carcass.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> tag: the heaviest prime lamb grade of all and very rare now in view of the universal demand for light carcasses. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,82</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a tree lucerne or tagastase (Chamaecytisus palmensis).

>  We started off with tagaste for fodder (stock, not us) .. He tried a new fodder legume, Garcia, which was outstripping tag until the annual grass grub cloud appeared on the horizon. Then the Garcia were themselves stripped while the tag looked on. <i>(Aug 1987,Growing Today,32</i>)

>  Tagastase will not survive heavy frosts, wet soils and hard grazing. <i>(1995,Grasslands Range of Forage and Conservation Plants,58</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) an identity tag fitted to the ear of an animal. (See [primary tag and secondary tag](../P#primary-tag-and-secondary-tag))

>  Specially marked sets of six or more tags are available for the temporary marking of mastitis cows, cull cows etc. [tagg] <i>(Dec 1970,NZ Dairy Exporter,33</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) rough pasture weeds and scrub.

>  The days of burning in the autumn or spring have gone, and the rough tag is all controlled with cattle. <i>(Oct 9, 1952,NZ Farmer,15</i>)

>  Considerable progress can be made in controlling tagg when stock are mobstocked all year round. <i>(Mar 1986,Proceedings 1985 Hill & High Country Seminar,113</i>) [NOTES: Proceedings 1985 Hill & High Country Seminar]

>  Rank pasture: tag. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### tagasaste
 
 <i>n.</i> tree lucerne

### tagged potato
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a potato grown by a member of a growing association [ the bag of which is identified with a tag naming the group.]

Complaints regarding inferior grade of "tagged" potatoes are investigated by a committee of the association, the association having authority to deprive offenders of benefits and even membership in the case of deliberate faulty packing .. to establish confidence on the part of purchasers in respect to the quality of "tagged" potatoes, and incidentally to command for their produce a premium over other potatoes. <i>(Aug 15, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,137</i>)

### tagger
 
 <i>n.</i> graffiti painter

 <i>n.</i> graffiti-writing

>  Groups used the term tagging to try to legitimise graffiti writing, which was simply intentional damage. <i>(Feb 3, 1992,Dominion,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a used-car dealer's tactic

>  Tagging - an `unusual and deceptive' method for car dealers to woo customers - has come to New Zealand. ... Tagging involves a dealer leaving a note on a parked car, offering the owner a chance to sell the vehicle to a keen buyer. The idea is for the owner to go to the dealer's yard to discuss a valuation and sale - then buys a replacement vehicle. <i>(Aug 2, 1986,Sunday Star Times,A3</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the marking of animals with identity tags, usually in the ear.

### taha
 
 <i>n.</i> calabash

>  They also tell us that W. Thompson reiterated the old demands - that the road should not cross the Mangatawhiri, <i>i.e.</i> into Maori land, and that the steamer should not come into his calabash (<i>taha</i>, meaning the river Waikato), but keep to the sea. <i>(Jan 24, 1863,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> Waiatara readily handed over to them <i>taha</i> - calabashes - filled with birds preserved in their own fat. <i>(1926,Tutira,93</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> Then he sets snares around their edges [sc. shallow-water troughs], pours about an inch of water from his tahaa (gourd) into them. <i>(1928,Where the White Man Treads,19</i>)

### taha Maori
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori perspective or way of doing things. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  They liked the new dunnies in A Block, the Beasley twins next door and Taha Maori. <i>(Aug 25, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),96</i>)

>  "My language.. is what I am. Without it a person cannot be educated in taha Maori. <i>(Sep 28, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

>  In the debate about the place of taha Maori in the education system I am behind any attempt at giving education and school life a Maori perspective as well as a Pakeha one. <i>(Jun 28, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

>  A taha Maori element was included in today's opening of the Post-Primary Teachers' Association conference in Wellington. The `Maori dimension' came in the form of a special Maori welcome for the Prime Minister, Mr Lange, .. <i>(Aug 21, 1984,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  The taha Maori discovered by Joe and the expiatory suffering experienced by Simon both became transferred to her by proxy, .. <i>(1985,Landfall,156/417</i>)

>  <b>IHIMAERA</b> Now, from te taha Maori' the view is different. <i>(1986,Matriarch, The,73</i>)

>  <b>REILLY</b> Anything short of revolving one's entire curriculum around `taha Maori' will be seen as not enough. <i>(1986,Deputy Head,74</i>)

>  Programmes such as Taha Maori in schools are making our schoolchildren more complete New Zealanders, former race relations conciliator Hiwi Tauroa said today. Mr Tauroa said Taha Maori was improving children's education, giving a bigger dimension of what this country was all about. <i>(May 13, 1986,Evening Post,36</i>)

>  Education Minister Russell Marshall has strongly rejected suggestion [sic] that the taha Maori programme in schools is preventing children from gaining basic educational skills. ... Mr Lelliott says he has been contacted by hundreds of parents over the past year concerned that the increased emphasis on taha Maori (Maori dimension) in schools was taking away time from basic education. <i>(May 14, 1986,Dominion,2</i>)

>  Sir, - I object to recent remarks about taha Maori reportedly made by Mr John Lelliott, general manager of the Wellington Education Board. <i>(May 23, 1986,Dominion,14</i>)

>  There is no taha Greek, taha Indian, taha Vietnamese, taha what-have-you being taught to our children, but taha Maori, yes! <i>(Jul 31, 1986,Dominion,10</i>)

>  Taha Part-Maori [headline] When an examination fails four `whites' for each `Maori', is it rational to denounce it as `pro-white' because statisticians tell us it `ought to have' failed five? <i>(Aug 16, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),10</i>)

>  Open plan schools ... are able to put into practice many of the family values of taha Maori in what is sometimes termed a whanau grouping. <i>(Sep 1986,Metro,68</i>)

>  Last year when I asked my intermediate age son if he was doing taha Maori at school he replied he wasn't and he wasn't interested because he isn't a Maori. <i>(May 1987,Metro,69</i>) [NOTES: Roger `Auckland Life']

### taha Pakeha
 
 <i>n.</i> Pakeha ways (contrastive with taha Maori)

>  What was left of Danish influence was absorbed by New Zealand practicality and indifference, the suffocating nature of which is well illustrated by our present search for cultural roots, incongruously dubbed taha pakeha. <i>(Mar 14, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),27</i>)

>  <b>KING</b> Her dealings with the Education Department ... had given her the reputation of being somebody who understood taha Pakeha, the Pakeha system. <i>Ibid.</i> 32 None of these responsibilities, which largely dealt with taha Pakeha or the European aspects of life, eroded Heremia's identity as a Maori. <i>(1983,Whina,14</i>)

>  <b>HOWE</b> It would be just as valid to talk of a `taha Pakeha'. If we combine the definitions of Pakeha and taha above, for example, we might well refer to the way Pakehas view the world, and how they act upon the world. <i>(1985,Towards Taha Maori,4</i>)

### tahae
 
 <i>v.</i> to steal

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> So on the way home we stopped the truck and tahaed some of his [a mean farmer's] turn <i>(1978,Glory and the Dream, The,113</i>)

### tahae-ing
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>CAMBELL</b> ... Cook ... interfered and asked Te Hira why he was <i>tahai</i>-ing the <i>goahore</i>. He meant <i>`taking</i> away the pot', but in his imperfect knowledge of the language he used the word <i>stealing.</i> <i>(1881,Poenamo,Bk2/7/121</i>)

 <i>n.</i> frostfish

 <i>n.</i> (See [takeke](t-ten.md#takeke))

### tahepa
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [taiepa](t-ten.md#taiepa))

### tahora
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [Ma.] a New Zealand-bred cultivar of white clover (Trifolium repens).

>  Some examples [of adaptive cultivars] are matua prairie grass..pitau and tahora white clover, oranga lucerne.. <i>(Sep 1985,NZ Dairy Exporter,66</i>)

>  Banks Peninsular [sic] farms divide into two main areas, the sunny faces and the shady faces whose needs are totally different. Shady faces: Pawera clover 2 kg, Tahora white clover 3 kg ... <i>(Sep 1985,NZ Journal of Agriculture,16</i>)

>  .. the smallest-leaf cultivar (tahora) outyielded the larger-leafed pitau and kopu under set-stocking. <i>(Nov 1987,NZ Dairy Exporter,55</i>)

>  Tahora - small-leaved, densely stoloned and low-growing, bred from collections made on moist New Zealand hill country pastures. <i>(1995,Grasslands Range of Forage and Conservation Plants,4</i>)

### tahu
 
 <i>n.</i> a second-stage eel

>  `Matamoe' is the term used by Wellington Maoris to refer to the adult <i>Anguilla australis</i>; but the three stages of growth are also known. The first stage, called `hao,' or `tuna heke,' is a small silver-bellied eel ... It is common in swamps and is sometimes called `swamp-eel' by settlers. The second stage is called `tahu' ... <i>(1925,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,8/30</i>) [NOTES: (Phillipps)]

### tahuhu
 
 <i>n.</i> ridgepole (Ryan) [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Hapu Hui Agenda item 13. Mural for tahuhu of wharekai to be completed by Christmas. <i>(Nov 18, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,45</i>)

>  <b>Grace</b> The reason for the piece of tahuhu, or ancestral backbone, being kept from the old house to be put into the new one is because the wharenui is the repository of talk, and rafters are its storage place... <i>(2001,Dogside Story,141</i>)

### tahurangi
 
 <i>n.</i> a red blanket used as trade between early European traders and Maori. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  They [the Wairarapa Maori] probably remembered the answer given by a local chief to Te Rangihaeta in 1846, when the latter wanted Wairarapa to join him in a raid on Wellington _ "<i> Kei a wai he tahurangi maku?"</i> (With whom is a <i> tahurangi </i> for me?) <i> Tahurangi </i> was the Maori name of the old-fashioned red blankets. The wise chief knew that to slay the pakeha would be to cut off the supply of European products, hence the red blanket saved Wellington. <i>(1921,Proceedings RSNZ,25</i>)

>  <b>Best</b> <i> Tahurangi </i> also means a red aspect of the heavens; and the red blankets brought hither by early traders and other were named <i> tahurangi </i> by the natives. <i>(1922,Astronomical Knowledge,62</i>)

>  <b>Barrer</b> The tahurangi is the old-fashioned red blanket, and the wise chief knew that to kill the pakeha meant the cutting off of supplies of these and other useful pakeha products. <i>(1939,Brave Days: Pioneer Women of New Zealand,79</i>)

### tai pare
 
 <i>n.</i> flood tide

>  <b>POLACK</b> The timber-dealer in conducting his raft down the river at flood tide (<i>tai parre</i>[e-acute],) drops an anchor, (a heavy piece of wood or bag of stones) at the end of the flood (<i>tai pakoa</i>[a1-acute]) in deep water, that he may be enabled to pursue his route at the finish of the ebb (<i>tai timu</i>.) <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/170</i>)

### tai pari
 
 <i>n.</i> ebb tide (See [tai timu](t-ten.md#tai-timu))

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> We returned the seven miles but found the Tide, ti Puddie <i>[tai pari]</i> or Ebb Tide, had made so that the Boat would not float, so we staid that night. So we Cooked our Pork and Potatoes, drank my Grog and took a Stroll. [fn] The Maori phrase literally means flowing tide. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),43,1963 ed.</i>)

### tai timu
 
 <i>n.</i> with TAI PARI perhaps indicative of the change in time and place of Pakeha vernacular knowledge of Maori Williams 361 <i>Tai (i) ... Sea.: timu `ebb(ing)'</i> (he tai timu<i> `low water'); pari</i> `flowing (of the tide)'. Both were intelligible to many English speakers up to at least the 1930s, and possibly the 1950s in the North Island, as witness the following joke first heard by me as a child in Havelock (South Island) possibly from an original King Country version; and first mentioned as early as 1845 by Walter Brodie in <i>The State of New Zealand </i>where it is attributed to Mr [?initial] Williams. Tim and Paddy were walking along the beach when they came across two Maoris arguing, as it semed to them, ferociously. One was crying out `Tie Tim!' and the other, `Tie Paddy!'. Paddy took to his heels shouting: `You can bloody well tie Tim if you like, but I'm damned if you'll tie Paddy!' Enjoyment of the joke depends not only on knowing the meaning of the Maori forms, but also on a non-Maori perception of Maori /r/ as [d], and failure to hear a devocalised /u/ final. In 1986 it is usually necessary to `prepare' the joke with tedious explanation.

>  <b>POLACK</b> The timber-dealer in conducting his raft down the river at flood tide (<i>tai parre</i>[e-acute],) drops an anchor, (a heavy piece of wood or bag of stones) at the end of the flood (<i>tai pakoa</i>[a1-acute]) in deep water, that he may be enabled to pursue his route at the finish of the ebb (<i>tai timu</i>.) <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/170</i>)

### taia
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [taiaha](t-ten.md#taiaha))

### taiaha
 
 <i>n.</i> art of taiaha use

>  He formed a freindship with former Maori Battalion colonel and scholar Pita Awatere, in prison for stabbing a man to death in a domestic row, and learned from him more about Maori lore, especially taiaha. <i>(Dec 2004,North & South,68</i>)

>  His son Paora works here administering taiaha courses nationally and taking language courses, and is a star performer in the champion kapa haka group Te Roopa Manutake, which his dad started in 1968 and still leads. <i>(Dec 2004,North & South,63</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a long wooden weapon, pointed at one end, used for striking and jabbing; a Maori quarterstaff [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  He carries a tuiaha [sic]. <i>(Jan 23, 1896,Otago Witness,42</i>)

>  <b>IHIMAERA & LONG (ed.)</b> Spring forward challenger with your wero / the flashing taiaha, the pukana / - prance and leap, spear the tongue in and out / like the lizard. <i>(1982,Into the World of Light,105</i>)

>  Taiaha <i>n</i>. an ornamented wooden club. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,11</i>)

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> Hemera gave me a huia's tail as well as a valuable lance (<i>taiaha</i>) decorated with the hair of the Maori dog. <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,189,1933 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> The <i>taiaha</i>, the most shapely and effective wooden weapon of the Maori, is a kind of two-handed sword, with the added advantage that both ends can be used, the tongue-shaped point as well as the broad blade. <i>(1935,Hero Stories of NZ,58</i>)

>  <i>taiaha</i>, weapon of hard brown wood, up to six feet long or more, with tongue-like point and slightly-broadening balde for thrust and stroke, the tongue proceeding as if from a carved mouth, with [150] paua eyes inserted for realism: used somewhat in the manner of a quarter-staff: often adorned below the head with a plaited flax band with red kaka-feathers were ominous of victory or defeat; victory if they glowed when the protecting cover was removed, defeat if they looked dead and dull: a weapon prized as highly by the owner or collector as the mere, and much used by chiefs in oratory as well as in war. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/149</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)

>  The Maori word for a Chief's walking-staff, sometimes used in fighting like the old-time quarter-staff. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),403</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>taiaha</i>: spear-like weapon of hard wood about 5 ft. long. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,194</i>)

>  <b>BARROW</b> <i>(1963,Life & Work of Maori Carver,36</i>)

>  <i>Taiaha n.</i> Maori chief's walking-staff [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),626</i>)

>  <b>BRATHWAITE</b> TAIAHA A weapon, superficially like a broad-hafted spear, but actually a broadsword. It is made of hardwood, with the `point' of the blade rounded, the edges sharp, and a carved, pointed tongue where the hilt would be on European broadsword. It is wielded in a manner that has something of the two-handed sword, the quarterstaff and the modern rifle-and-bayonet combination. The carved tongue is decorated where it joins the blade, with a collar of dog-hair. <i>(1967,Evil Day,381</i>)

>  <i>(1968,Landfall,221/359</i>)

>  <i>taiaha</.i> ... n. Maori spear-like weapon used for striking rather than thrusting; lighter version used as chief's staff [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),19</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> I am the taiaha left among people/ who dance and twirl poi/ in gaudy halls/ of plastic maoridom. <i>(1979,Eyes of the Ruru,13</i>) [NOTES: (Taiaha Haka Poem)]

>  <i>taiaha n. N.Z.</i> a carved weapon in the form of a staff, now used in Maori ceremonial oratory. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,1190</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 782; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 490]]

>  In his hands it can be cat's paw or taiaha. <i>(Mar 1999,NZ Books,9(1) : 4</i>)

>  the leader carries a toy shovel instead of a "taieha" or gun <i>(Aug 28, 1880,NZ Mail,19b</i>)

>  Some boys (and one woman) have been warming up for more than an hour outside, going through taiaha exercises. <i>(Dec 1996,Mana,No. 11 : 21</i>)

>  An English tourist has been admitted to hospital after he was accidentally hit in the face by a taiaha flourished during a traditional Maori welcome. <i>(Sep 27, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

>  ...Carlos Spencer led the haka by smacking Jonny Wilkinson over the head with a taiaha. <i>(Nov 29, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

>  With a taiaha bearer out in front, a procession under the flags of the nation of Tibet and Maori sovreignty made its way to the banks of the Whanganui River. <i>(Dec 14, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),41</i>)

>  Tangaroa, God of the Sea, stood on a rock at the entrance to the harbour. A warrior, cast in bronze, he raised his taiaha, challenging the unwary, the strangers, and new arrivals to his world. <i>(2003,Huia 5: Contemp. Maori Fiction,65</i>)

>  [Hdg] Have taiaha, will travel... "I am also going to take my taiaha with me in case I decide to bring back some heads." <i>(Sep 30, 2000,Dominion,17</i>)

>  Mukuro, a principal chief of the place who with a Taia in his hand and a magnificent Meri in his belt occupied a prominent position. <i>(Apr 15, 1842,Journal of Ensign Best,349,1966 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> (Morris) <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,1/139</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> ... the <i>taiaha</i>, a kind of two-handed sword made of the hardest wood of the country; and the <i>tewhatewha</i>, a wooden battle-axe, having a sharp-pointed handle to enable it also to answer the purpose of a spear. <i>(1856,Traditions & Superstitions,244</i>)

>  <b>6</b> ... two friendly chiefs ... rushed out from the friendly mob with only a shawl round the loins, armed with a `taiaha' and reed spears; ... <i>(Apr 6, 1861,Otago Witness</i>)

>  <b>MOSER</b> The first old man who spoke ... commenced walking up and down between the rows for some time, brandishing his `tai-aha', or spear, and saying nothing. <i>(1863,Mahoe Leaves,38</i>)

>  The first of them [sc Maoris] was an old man with a handsome taiaha, he came upon their tracks & shouting `He pakeha - he pakeha' was immediately shot. <i>(1863,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/61,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  <b>MEADE</b> With Mair's advice he selected a handsome <i>taiaha</i>(a sort of spear, or sword-shaped club, of dark red wood, carved and polished, with the plume of white dog's hair and small scarlet feathers, and more as a mark of the bearer's rank as chief than as a weapon). <i>(1870,Ride Through Disturbed Districts</i>) [NOTES: 1870 Meade Ride Through Disturbed Districts in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 499]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Taiaha,</i> n. a flat weapon of hard wood, about 5ft long, having one end carved in the shape of a tongue. <i>(1871,Dictionary of the NZ Language,144</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> They used a variety of manual weapons, such as the mere, the hani, the hoeroa, the patu, the taiaha, the toki, etc. ... <i>(1878,Forty Years in NZ,249</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> (Griffiths Collection) <i>(1879,Waikato War,87</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> In his right hand he brandished a <i>taiaha</i>, a six-foot Maori broadsword of hard wood, with its pendulous plume of feathers hanging from the hilt.. [Bk3/5/177] Wonderfully strange is the effect that this at last produces when the speaker has worked himself up to the highest stage of intense action, when he rushes forward, his mat flying wildly around him, brandishing with a peculiar quivering motion a <i>taiaha</i>- wooden broadsword - or a tomahawk, rushing forward with high-toned voice and hastily-spoken words, ... <i>(1881,Poenamo,Bk2/4/80</i>)

>  (Morris) <i>(1889,TrNZI,22/505</i>) [NOTES: (Wilson and Tregear)]

>  <b>COLENSO</b> ... whilst here and there a <i>hani</i> (or <i>taiaha</i>, a chief's staff of rank, &c.) was seen erected, adorned with the long flowing white hair of the tails of the New Zealand dog and crimson cloth and red feathers. <i>(1890,Treaty of Waitangi,10</i>) [NOTES: (Brombie)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Maori word for a chief's walking-staff, a sign of office, sometimes used in fighting, like a quarterstaff. <i>(1898,Austral English,453</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The quarter-staff or sword (<i>taiaha</i> or <i>hani</i>) was made of heavy hard wood. It also served the purpose of a spear ... <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,312</i>)

>  The <i>Taiaha</i>, as a few New Zealanders may know, is an old-time fighting weapon of the Maori. <i>(1917,New Zealand At the Front,1</i>)</i>

### taiaha-user
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>COWAN</b> The expert <i>taiaha</i>-user has been known to defeat a swordsman. <i>(1935,Hero Stories of NZ,58</i>)

### taiaha-wielder
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>COWAN</b> A tall man with a good long reach was a most formidable <i>taiaha</i>-wielder. <i>(1935,Hero Stories of NZ,58</i>)

### taiapa
 
 <i>n.</i> rail fencing

>  <b>POLACK</b> Those who were unable to procure this much-valued weapon, sported bayonets fixed to long spears, paddles, and even rail fencing (<i>taiapa</i>[a2-acute]). <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/87</i>)

### taiapure
 
 <i>n.</i> (Marine) a local or protected fishery [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  The Fisheries Minister has agreed in principle to a proposed taiapure (local fishery) in the waters surrounding Te Wakatehaua Island (the Bluff), Ninety-Mile Beach. Taiapure (local fisheries) can be established in areas of special significance to an iwi or hapu as a source of food or for spiritual or cultural reasons. <i>(Oct 2008,Rural Bulletin,4</i>)

>  In the 90s, Tawhiao set up a taiapure (protected fishery) around Matakana. <i>(Aug 14, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

>  Six years after Parliament passed a law to provide for the setting up of taiapure - protected fishing grounds - just one has been officially established. <i>Ibid.</i> 89 A taiapure is a coastal fishery which is of particular significance to iwi as a source of food or for cultural or spiritual reasons. <i>(Dec 1996,Mana,No. 11 : 87</i>)

>  The Huakina Development Trust application for guardianship (taiapure) was made in 1990 when the Fisheries Ministry decided to create system for greater Maori input into fisheries issues to meet its obligations under the Treaty of Waitangi... The taiapure offered Maori an opportunity "to participate directly in fisheries management. <i>(Apr 13, 1997,Sunday Star Times,A5</i>)

>  These [maps] can be used to monitor the effectiveness of marine reserves, mataitai, and taiapure through 'before-and-after' .. comparisons... <i>(Sep 2006,Climate Update, The (NIWA),Coasts and Oceans</i>)

>  Increasingly, iwi and managers of taiapure and mataitai have regulatory responsibilities for customary management of the coastal environment and kaimoana resources. <i>(May 2, 2007,Niwa Update,1</i>) [NOTES: Brochure on 'Knowledge of customary coastal and kaimoana management'.]

### taiaroa
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [Ma.] a New Zealand-bred cultivar of wheat.

>  The principal varieties for spring sowing are Cross 7, Tuscan and Tainui. Taiaroa and Jumbuck are also occasionally sown. Taiaroa, which is similar to tainui, is occasionally grown in Southland and parts of South Otago. <i>(Jul 16, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,75</i>)

### TAIC
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Transport Accident Investigation Commission

>  The article about the.. Soundsair Cessna accident includes criticism of the Transport Accident Investigation Commission (Taic)... A Taic accident investigator's job is to identify, to a reasonable level of proof, the causes and circumstances of an accident or incident, in order to prevent similar occurrences. <i>(Mar 15, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12</i>)

>  The Transport Accident Investigation Commission (TAIC), an independent body formed in 1990, is required by law to investigate any aircraft crashes that have significance to transport safety... <i>(Jan 18, 2004,Sunday Star Times,A11</i>)

>  The report's authors - the Transport Accident Investigation Commission (TAIC) - are furious... TAIC will give the newspaper a chance to explain itself... <i>(Apr 3, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

### taiepa
 
 <i>n.</i> in early use often spelt tie-up an old word for a fence, built usually of at-hand, bush materials. [Maori taiepa, taiapa]

>  The entrance to the village marae, the assembly ground, in front of the carved meeting-house, "Tama-te-Kapu," is through a high taiepa or fence of manuka stakes, with carved posts, in imitation of the olden stockades, and a similar fence is erected in front of the carved Mission-hall "Te Ao-Marama" ("The World-of-Light"), where the Church of England Maori choir gives concerts and Maori dances. <i>(Jun 4, 1910,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  Let the Queene and the pakehas occupy the seacoast and be a fence (taiepa) around us. <i>(Jun 5, 1857,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  In a strategical point of view, you cannot fail to see that the road from here to Pungarehu is most important, and should not be allowed to remain closed any longer by a Maori taiepa. <i>(Jan 29, 1881,Hawera & Normanby Star,3</i>)

>  The old man said to his son, "E haere ana ahau i te tai-ata: here koe i te tai-po." This the court interpreter translated, "I am sitting on the fence (taiepa): you may go to the devil." <i>(Apr 16, 1902,Otago Witness,69</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> ? relation with TIE-UP: an illustrtion at page 308 a photograph taken c1869 of Lieut. Gasgoyne's home a raupo and thatched hut, shows ,a post and rail fence at the front, made of posts with four rails of trimmed saplings interlaced with vines ?to keep stock out. A gate similarly made swings open in the fence. The rails are literally `tied up' with vines. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/308,1983 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> ? relation with TIE-UP: an illustrtion at page 308 a photograph taken c1869 of Lieut. Gasgoyne's home a raupo and thatched hut, shows ,a post and rail fence at the front, made of posts with four rails of trimmed saplings interlaced with vines ?to keep stock out. A gate similarly made swings open in the fence. The rails are literally `tied up' with vines. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/308,1983 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> K|aeo tah|epa; Rail for a fence. <i>Ibid.</i> 205 Taihep|a; A fence. (or, Tahepa.) <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,154</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Several natives on the beach from Waikari who had brought <i>Tacopa</i> for fencing. [MS apparently<i> tacopa</i>: ed. fn suggests <i>taiepa, </i>or perhaps the sailing cutter <i>Taeopa</i>] <i>(Jun 21, 1828,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,135,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> A creeper [torotoro] is used for binding up fences, as we would use Rope Yarns. It is soaked first, or used quite fresh, for what is called (a tie up) [taiapa] a fence to keep Goats out of a Garden or to answer as a railing. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),34,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> During this melee [e1,e2-acute] they flourished their paddles and hanis, some making use of taiapa [a2,a3-acute], or rail-fence, in lieu of spears : they each returned to their places.<i>Ibid.</i> 1/223 A taiapa [a2-acute], or fence, surrounded each plot of ground to prevent the dogs and pigs from following the natural bent of their inclinations. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/161</i>)

>  <b>WEEKES</b> A spot was selected in front of the house and with native assistance a `Tieupha' [i-acute][Taiepa] fence was soon completed. This is made of stout sticks six feet high stuck in the ground perpendicularly and tied round in two or three places with rods and a tough creeper (clematis) more durable than any cord. <i>(1846,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1846 Weekes's Journal in 1940 Rutherford and Skinner New Plymouth Settlement 120]

>  <b>MANING</b> A long line of similar articles, my property, which had graced the <i>taiepa</i> fence the night before, had disappeared. <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,(iv) 69</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> I am getting on very well with the clearing; and vary the monotony by putting up a <i>taiepa</i> (fence) around my pig yard [near Gisborne]. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,161,1935 ed.</i>)

>  <b>VAILE</b> <i>taiapa</i> a Maori fence <i>(1939,Pioneering the Pumice,299</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> `Yes; he fell down by the taiepa of the pa, and then I ran home.' <i>(1948,Taina,177</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i> TAIEPA FENCE, TIE-UP FENCE (TIE-UP WOOD =TAIEPA for fencing, with a possibility that this may refer to, e.g., bundled wood such as split palings for fencing)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> Buying tie-up wood for fencing, and setting boundaries in order to its being put up. <i>(1820,Journals and Correspondence,72</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>MORGAN</b> Thursday and yesterday making a tie up fence. <i>(Jul 14, 1861,Journal of William Morgan,30,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> ... a poor fellow ... was hit out in the open in front of us, and I had to pass over the `tie-up' [the fence around the Maori village of Pangarahu], and give a hand to carry him in. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,139</i>)

>  <b>NESFIELD</b> ... twenty or thirty whares with their usual `tie-up' fences around them formed the outside Pah. <i>(1881,Chequered Career,43</i>)

>  <b>KNIGHT</b> [reference Rangitikei 1850s] The bush as I said, made one fence, the stream another and the Maoris made a `tie-up' fence on the other sides, Father insisting strongly on the quality of the `aka' vine used for tying. <i>(1975,Poyntzfield,42</i>) [NOTES: c1920s Eliza McKenzie in <i>Outlook</i> in 1975 Knight Poyntzfield 42]

### taigook
 
 <i>n.</i> possibly <i>ta[a]huka</i> Williams 361 <i>Ta[a]huka ... </i>A cloak resembling a <i>korowai</i>, but without <i>hukahuka</i>.: [fringes or thrums on a cape]

>  <b>FORSTER</b> [The chief] was covered with a black <i>Taigook</i> [a-macron, o1-grave], a Mat for cloathing, it was close woven, very strong & of a fine jett black & here and there patched with white Dogskin. [ed. fn] This word is omitted by George: possible [sic] it was <i>tieke</i>[e-macron], a black flax cloak or <i>topuni</i>[o-macron] (<i>topaki</i>[o-macron], a black dogskin cloak. George describes it as `a perfect black cloak of the close-wrought kind, patched in compartments with dog-skin', <i>Voyage</i>, I, 223. <i>(Jun 4, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/291</i>)

### taihapa
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [taeipa](../T#taeipa))

### Taihapian
 
 <i>n.</i> Person from Taihape

>  There were red one, yellow ones, blue ones and even ones with pictures painted on them, but the colour favoured by the more discerning Taihapian should be, of course, none other than black. <i>(Apr 15, 1998,Dominion,3</i>) [NOTES: re gumboots / gummies]

### taiho
 
 <i>n.</i> a common pron. and spg of taihoa as an interjection

### taihoa
 
 <i>v.</i> to delay, proceed slowly; stop, halt [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  .. we feel like we are watching one of those anti-booze binging ads where the sober doppelganger cautions the increasingly intoxicated other half to taihoa. <i>(Jun 10, 2006,Dominion Post,B7</i>)

>  Perhaps we should taiho [sic] for a bit on this "hug an Aussie" campaign. <i>(Oct 11, 2011,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  You would have thought that this major indiscretion and lack of good judgment would have sent signals to the brain to tai ho and get back on the straight and narrow. <i>(Nov 3, 2001,Dominion Weekend Magazine,6</i>)

>  It is time to taihoa. Time to pause and ponder on the state of the debate over race. <i>(Mar 20, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),5</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> ... we must be up and doing and not taihoa-ing and letting the fern grow under our feet; .. <i>(1881,Poenamo,4/5/284</i>)

>  He had been there a good many years "Taihoaing" all the time and he had been told "sit down!" <i>(Sep 30, 1904,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)

>  <b>WILY & MAUNSELL</b> ... he succeeded in persuading the natives to keep quiet for the moment - in fact to <i>taihoa. </i> <i>(1938,Robert Maunsell,129</i>)

>  Pete had a dekko up a chimney. But there was a lay-back ... So they tai-hoa'd while they tucked into some brunch. <i>(Apr 19, 1971,Grand Limerick Tour, A,96</i>)

>  You try one thing on a piano and it sounds fantastic. But you try it on another and it sounds completely different - so we'll tai ho on that for a moment. <i>(Apr 9, 2003,Capital Times,7</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  He has as yet given no indication of a disposition to drop into the old "taihoa" ways. <i>(Dec 31, 1904,NZ Observer,4</i>)

>  Because of Mr Parr's energy and intolerance of the "Taihoa" Department [sc. Health Department], we are told the unpleasant truth that the Orakei native community is infected. <i>(Sep 6, 1913,NZ Observer,2</i>)

>  This was in reply to a complaint.. that Sir William [Fraser] had earned the right to be called "the taihoa Minister" in consequence of his dilatory tactics. <i>(Aug 6, 1918,Lake Wakatip Mail,4</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> I am persuaded that if all the swearing of my after-life was put against it in an opposite column the <i>taihoa</i> one would carry the day. <i>(1881,Poenamo,Bk4/4/269</i>)

>  `The Waikare Land Board Collects Its Rents A.D. 1915' in 1980 Grant Unauthorised Version 105 [In background a pa with a rge palatial building flying a flag inscribed `TAIHOA PALACE'.] <i>(1910,NZ Herald,LLoyd cartoon</i>)

>  <b>LAMBERT</b> Animated by an enthusiasm that completely controverts the Pakeha's conception of a Maori as a <i>taihoa</i> worker, eight Maori carvers ... have a notable work in hand. <i>(1936,Pioneering Reminiscences of Old Wairoa,137</i>)



 
 <i>exclam.</i> wait-a-bit! hold on! [ORIGIN: Maori, also taiho, tao hoor, tie-ho, taisho, et al ca1890-1910 P.S. Ardern Used as an exclamation `wait a bit!' in Auckland colloquial; Anglicised pron. usually [tai|hou] (occas. esp. in Northland, [|tai ou]) or [tai|hou ]: R. Mason notes [taihou] heard in Christchurch in the 1950s; [taihou] heard in Northland only]

>  "Taihoa" does not mean careful consideration, prudent and wise advancement, careful deliberation, putting on the brake, chewing things over, although it may be used for these reasons. English-ised, this word corresponds to our own adverb 'by-and-by'- postponing indefinitely for any reason or for no reason at all what may be said or done at once. It has always meant this and can never mean anything else, whether applied to the occasional or habitual procrastinations of a pakeha,Maori chief, or honourable Parliamentarian grievously pestered by urgent petitions of insatiate constitutents. <i>(Jan 5, 1912,Marlborough Express,4</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> "Taisho, Mac." [Note : the bush-boy corruption of the Maori "Taihoa, " "Wait a bit." <i>(1905,Bush Boys of NZ,170</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> `You couldn't have a Maori for a boss, though, they don't know what time means. `Taihoa,' that's what they say, and they all go back to the <i>pa</i> in the long run.' <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,189</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> Usually when in `funds' he [the Maori] distributes largess with a lordly air, <i>Taihoa</i> (let tomorrow look after itself) being his traditional policy. <i>(1941,Numismatic History of NZ,4</i>)

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> When a forward young [Maori] woman approached very near,/ And she dropped a remark that she meant me to hear,/ It was `<i>Tisho</i>!' its meaning is, `Steady! Go slow!'/ But don't orthography happen to know; I replied she was <i>`kapai' </i>(excessively good), <i>(1950,Farm & Station Verse,16</i>) [NOTES: 1889 Williams `New Chums Letter Home' also in 1950 Woodhouse Farm Verse 16]

>  But Taiho as the Maori says <i>(May 28, 1891,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/583,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (E. Mitchelson).]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Taihoa, </i>Maori phrase, meaning `Wait a bit.' Much used in some circles in New Zealand. The `Standard' gives it wrongly as `Anglo-Tasmanian,' probably because Mr. Wade's book was published in Hobart. [cites Wade 1842 and Campbell Poenamo 1881] <i>(1898,Austral English,453</i>)

>  <b>REEVES</b> Very few words have been adopted from the vigorous and expressive Maori. ... [exceptions include] `taihoa' equivalent to the Mexican <i>man~ana ... </i> <i>(1898,Long White Cloud, The,403</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> `Taisho, Mac. I'll be there in a minute.' [fn] <i>Taisho</i>. The bush-boy corruption of the Maori <i>`Taihoa' </i>`wait a bit'. <i>(1905,Bush Boys of NZ,170</i>)

>  The honorable the Native Minister is easily the most popular man in the house. Unfortunately he is troubled with the malady known as `Taihoa' which has its equivalent in the sleeping sickness of Africa. <i>(Jul 13, 1907,NZ Truth,108/1/7</i>)

>  Trebor: The curse of the New Zealand trade union movement is the time-server. The man who takes a paid position in a Union and sleeps on it, lives on it, loafs on it. Taihoa. <i>(Jun 2, 1911,Maoriland Worker,13/1/3</i>)

>  One asks oneself why the Cabinet Minister in whose constituency Morere is placed hasn't induced the Government to make the place more attractive and accessible. And then one remembers: `Taihoa!' <i>(May 10, 1912,Triad, The,13</i>)

>  (jocular?) Warriors, Wahines, Kuri, Poaka, Tamariki, Kumeras, Karos, Interpreters (1st, 2nd and 3rd grade), Poets Laureate, Poi Dancers, Guides, &c. on application to the Registrar. ...WAIPIRO - - An acursed drink ... TAIHOA - - The Police have the matter in hand POAKA - -a pig PUKEKO A bokakker out of season KORERO A long talk UTU - - The spike HAPU - - A Tribe KUMERA - - A sweet Spud <i>(1913,Auckland University Carnival Book,27</i>)

>  <b>CHURCH</b> What the deuce do you mean? Taihoa! Taihoa! This gent has come along with me. The less shine he makes the better. <i>(1916,Tonks,250</i>)

>  TAIHOA. - Later on. <i>(1917,New Zealand At the Front,xiv</i>)

>  `Ah, well, Dad - you never know - taihoa, old man!' <i>(Dec 10, 1921,Quick March,11</i>)

>  taihoa! interj. later on; presently. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,11</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Still, the young settlers said, `<i>Taihoa</i>'! We'll wait and see,' and went on with their usual duties. <i>(1935,Hero Stories of NZ,143</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> Taihoa (`hold your horses') ... <i>(1937,Wednesday's Children,123</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> Old Hone and Hori made rotten stock-masters. They sat in the sun, smoking and dreaming, saying `Taihoa' over their pipes, and letting their thin Jersey cows bellow ... unmilked. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,122</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Taihoa</i>. Wait a bit!: New Zealand coll.: from ca. 1840. Direct ex Maori. Morris. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,860</i>)

>  Slang words of Maori origin were once common, but most of them are dead and dying [including] `go-ashore,' a cauldron (kohua), taihoa, ... <i>(Apr 2, 1938,Press, The,18</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]

>  <b>BAKER</b> ...taihoa, hold on! wait a bit!... <i>(1941,NZ Slang,56</i>)

>  <i>taihoa,</i> by-and-by; lotus-land procrastination. `How one's temper is tried by that irritatingly provoking word "taihoa".' . <i>J. Logan Campbell,</i> Poenamu (1881), p. 87. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/157</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>BENNETT</b> <i>Taihoa</i>! `wait a bit', and <i>kia ora</i> `good luck' ... are well-established adoptions [from Maori]. <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,93</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> New Zealand colloquialisms which are of Maori origin include; ... <i>taihoa</i>: wait a moment <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),117</i>)

>  <i>taihoa expression used for postponing action, decisions, etc. - wait a bit. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)

>  A Maori phrase meaning `wait a bit'. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),403</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> Stay around here, with me, you might see some you like better sooner or later. Taihoa. Take your time. <i>(1965,Among the Cinders,205</i>)

>  <i>phr.</i> Maori phrase meaning `wait a bit'. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),626</i>)

>  <i>taihoa ... (Phr.) </i>Maori phrase meaning <i>wait a bit. </i> <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),19</i>)

>  <i>tai hoor</i>: easy does it <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <b>WILSON</b> `Hey, taihoa there, mate. Black power, eh? Never going to get fonged any more. Never ever ever.' <i>(1984,South Pacific Street,47</i>)

>  A lot of eager gentlemen burst into my room and said: `We want the logs ... now.' I said: `Taihoa, gentlemen, we haven't finished growing them yet.' <i>(Sep 19, 1986,National Business Review,34</i>)

>  <i>taihoa int. NZ</i> wait! [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,782</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 490]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>taihoa</i> wait! Hang on a minute, mate; Williams translates as `by and by'. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,111</i>)

>  <b>GRACE</b> There is too much taihoa about you Maoris. <i>(1910,Hone Tiki Dialogues,4</i>)

>  <b>BENTLEY & FRASER</b> Ecology freaks at Tryphena/ Say `Taihoa' on mussels and kina;/ <i>(1985,Grand Limerick Tour, A,[No.] 41</i>)

>  We had words you didn't find in the dictionary but which we all used: "porangi", "whare", "taihoa". <i>(Jan 1992,North & South,110</i>)

>  The Lands Court is making slow progress with the goldfield block, which is distressing to our local storekeepers, no money being as yet in circulation, and the natives are living on credit - <i>taiho, the Court finish, my pay</i> is the order of the day. <i>(Jul 16, 1880,Thames Star,3</i>)

>  They would say "Tie-ho" when they meant "hold on a bit". <i>(Aug 11, 2004,Capital Times,2</i>)

>  When Mr. Gorst attempted to expedite their movements, the only answer he could get was taihoa, taihoa, "wait a while, wait a while." <i>(Jul 7, 1862,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  It appears that Mr. Broughton tried to get some discussion in regard to the Confirmed Leases Bill, but was prevented by cries of "Taihoa" (wait a while), and many interruptions occurred. <i>(Jun 15, 1892,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

>  NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS. To "Punch," and to "A Lover of New Zealand," <i>taihoa</i>. It is better to say as little as possible for a while. <i>(Jun 2, 1857,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  But some years ago the City Board used harsh means to get the rates in by putting a defaulter in prison; but the press condemned them for it. So who will please? Taihoa: we will see. <i>(Sep 18, 1872,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  Mr Barton's sixth point was that the relief sought by the Maori Trustee should not be granted because of its delay in making his application... On both side the policy of tai hoa seems to have been the outstanding characteristic of this dispute. <i>(1965,NZ Law Reports,1108</i>)

>  'So it's spring-tide. Eight o'clock tide in the harbour. Say half-past seven out here. It's not too late. Let's give the crays a burl.' 'You're a tiger for punishment,' said Hira, 'Let's go home.' 'Taihoa, boy, taihoa. This is the place for crays and this is the time.' <i>(Dec 1958,Te Ao Hou,25: 50</i>)

>  "Well, <i>taihoa</i> all the Kings and Queens of Europe will be <i>kuapau</i>, and the Queen of England will be the only one left; and she will be the <i>rangitira nui</i>. <i>(Aug 9, 1848,New Zealand Spectator & Cook's Strait Guardian,2</i>)

>  Hami tendered the half crown: Mr. Yates said, "Taihoa, wait till you pay for all the guns." <i>(Jul 24, 1860,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  soon - taishoa (or agunnee) [taihoa (akuanei)] <i>(1826,Boultbee Journal,112,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1826-1827 list of Maori words in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 112]

>  <b>WADE</b> <i> `Taihoa'</i>. This word has been translated, By and by; but in truth it has all the latitude of directly, - presently, - by and by, - a long time hence, - and nobody knows when ... the deliberate reply is, `Taihoa' ... this patience-trying word. <i>(1842,Journey in NZ,66</i>) [NOTES: [repub. in 1895 NZ Reader 124]]

>  Glad we were to pay off our Maori lads & and be done with their provoking `taiho!' `waiho![=rest]' (<i>presently wait</i>), ... <i>(Apr 15, 1851,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/90,1960 ed.</i>)

>  The plaintiff [in a Maori Court] is put off by the everlasting answer `taihoa'. <i>(1862,AJHR,E-9/11</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> <i>`Taihoa'</i>, (wait), said he [Rauparaha], `You and I will go together'. <i>(1866,Chatham Islands,21</i>)

>  The only Maori words I knew were kapai and taihoa. <i>(1869,Punch or the Auckland Charivari,119</i>)

>  I proposed to have the collection now for the two Native Teachers, which was agreed to some time ago, but had no success, the question being shelved by the remark, so common amongst them when they wish to avoid a matter, `Taihoa' (by and by). <i>(1928,Grace Pioneer Missionary,302</i>) [NOTES: 1878 Mar Mrs Grace Journal in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 302]
