>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> That irritatingly provoking word, `taihoa' ... [88] The drawled out t-a-i-h-o-a fell upon the ear. ... [271] Great is the power of taihoa. ... [276] The imperturbable taihoa, given to us with the ordinary placid good humour. <i>Ibid.</i> Bk2/5/87 We had intended to make an early start in the morning ... but whoever has had to do with Tongata Maori well knows how one's temper is tried by that irritatingly provoking word `taihoa'. [fn] By-and-by. <i>Ibid.</i> Bk4/4/268-269 <i>`Taihoa, taihoa, taihoa',</i> was ever the answer, /drawled out in the most inimitable and imperturbably good-humoured voice, each <i>taihoa</i> longer than the one before. <i>Ibid.</i> Bk4/4/269 In vain my protestations. <i>`Taihoa, taihoa,</i> all in good time - what's the hurry, O Pakeha!' time was made for slaves - hurry no man's cattle, as we in the classics say. `What's the odds, a day or two sooner or later? There's Motu-Korea; it won't run away - <i>taihoa nei?' </i>with a chuck back of the head as they ended with the interrogative. <i>(1881,Poenamo,87</i>)

>  Found Captain Romeril[????] telling the natives my extension leases were no good. Stirring up the natives to injure us. Tai hoa Captain. Most of the men and all the women at fude[????]. Waitangi is the home of evil. <i>(Jan 6, 1892,Chudleigh Diary,378</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> By taking the native lad into [sic] and treating him as a home apprentice he will imbide the charms which embellish ... the orderly pakeha's home life; ... assuredly it is worthier ... than ... the insolent cry of : `Come, bestir yourselves, there has been too much taihoa.' <i>Ibid.</i> 272 Month after month 52 children are contemptuously told `taihoa; these things take time.' <i>Ibid.</i> 283 To attain [a better life] unaided is a dream of the expert at experiments, the foolish crier of `Bestir yourselves, there has been too much taihoa.' <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,261</i>)

>  <b>SATCHELL</b> `Taihoa' (wait), said the [Maori] guides, when they deigned to take notice at all. <i>(1905,Toll of the Bush,405</i>)

>  <b>GORST</b> the plaintiff was put off with the everlasting Maori answer- `taihoa' (by and by). <i>(1908,New Zealand Revisited,214</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> Under the old gum-tree ... Peta said, `Taihoa, wait a bit. It's not midnight yet.' <i>(1938,Brown Man's Burden,25,1973 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>exclam.</i> (informal) wait on! [ORIGIN: Maori, Anglicised pron. usually [tai|hou] (occas. esp. in Northland, [|tai ou]) or [tai|hou ]: R. Mason notes [taihou] heard in Christchurch in the 1950s; [taihou] heard in Northland only]



 
 <i>n.</i> delay, procrastination [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>GRACE</b> There is too much taihoa about you Maoris. <i>(1910,Hone Tiki Dialogues,4</i>)

>  <b>RUTHERFORD</b> We talk of `taihoa' in connection with our Native Department. The real home of unadulterated `taihoa' is Italy ... <i>(1912,Impressions of a NZ Pastoralist on Tour,162</i>)

>  <b>BARTLETT</b> A raupo cottage was much warmer than an unlined wooden cottage and could be quite comfortable if the Maori builders were allowed to take their time with plenty of Taihoa - `What's the hurry, O pakeha!' <i>(1974,Emigrants, The,41</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (Environment) <i>Cassytha paniculata</i>, a Northland creeping plant parasitic on (or associated with) the tea-tree: a back-translation from WAIT-A-WHILE [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>MATTHEWS</b> This was his first introduction to the New Zealand bush but, though interested, he had little time for observation of nature, being hard put to it to keep the pace set by his guide. There was no path, part of the way was very steep, and the thorny vines of the `bush lawyer' or, as Pene called it, <i>Taihoa</i> (Wait-a-bit) made travelling difficult. <i>(1940,Matthews of Kaitaia,17</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> Closer inspection disclosed a curious creeper, common hereabouts [in Northland], its favourite habitat a clump of scrub, amongst which it contrives to involve itself in an inextricable tangle. ... This creeper bears the appropriate name of Taihoa (wait-a-while), ... <i>(1946,Farthest North,28</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> The stringy tendrils of the aptly named <i>taihoa</i>, or `wait-awhile' native creeper, tough enough to trap and hold a horse. <i>(1948,Gumdigger, The,53</i>)</i>

### taihoa policy
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Dr Pomare.. is wisely adopting the "taihoa" policy as far as a pronouncement of his intentions goes. <i>(Jan 13, 1912,NZ Free Lance,4</i>)

>  The extreme difficulty of preserving interests and fixing individual titles has fed the "taihoa" policy until it is almost too fat to waddle. <i>(Jan 18, 1913,NZ Observer,2</i>)

>  <b>MALCOLM</b> The locals cheerfully consented to a 'Taihoa' policy. <i>(1982,Where it all Began,129</i>)

>  It was, of course, impossible to grant the Maoris power to deal with their lands through their own committee, for this would have meant the triumph of the "Taihoa" policy and a complete bar to the progress of settlement in the North Island. <i>(Oct 6, 1894,Star, The,4</i>)

>  Some of the old chiefs apparently being in the belief that the proverbial "Taihoa" policy of <i>the powers that have been</i> in this district, will cause a postponement of the work to a future generation. <i>(Jun 2, 1885,Bay of Plenty Times,2</i>)

>  The Rev. J. E. Ward (chairman) proceeded to show how all forward steps taken by the Council had been frustrated at almost every turn either by the apathy of the Government or the "taihoa" policy of the Native Land Board. <i>(Mar 10, 1909,Wanganui Herald,4</i>)

>  A `taihoa' policy administered on the spot by a competent psychologist (a humble edition of Ta Timi) with a [63] light S.O.S. line to a wise Native Dept here would have suited Samoa better than the regime that has obtained since our `conquest' in 1914. [fn] Carroll, as Native Minister, was often accused of a taihoa (by and by) policy. <i>(Feb 9, 1928,Na To Hoa Aroha,1/62</i>) [NOTES: 1928 Feb 9 Na To Hoa Aroha (1986) (Ngata-Buck Correspondence) 1/62-63 (Ngata) [fn] 1986 Sorrenson Na To Hoa Aroha (Ngata-Buck Correspondence) 1/62]

>  <b>COWAN</b> It was just as well that Sir Donald Maclean was of a calmer temperament; had he lived in a later day his methods would probably have been described as a `Taihoa policy'. <i>(1940,Sir Donald Maclean,125</i>)

>  The taihoa policy of the Maori seems to have somewhat inoculated the people of the North, and especially in matters political. <i>(Aug 12, 1875,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> Taking twelve years instead of five ... was described as a Taihoa policy. <i>(1921,At The Front,188</i>)

>  The slump finds most of the good undeveloped lands still in Maori ownership a justification of the taihoa policy; also finds the Maori youth impatient of the old excuses for not working these lands. <i>(May 15, 1931,Na To Hoa Aroha,2/148</i>) [NOTES: 1931 May 15 Na To Hoa Aroha (1987) (Ngata-Buck Correspondence) 2/148 (Ngata)]

### taihoaism
 
 <i>n.</i> procrastination

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> Waipeha was equal to any amount of taihoaism, and Toangata Maori loved him accordingly. <i>iIbid.</i> Bk 4/2/243 ... I knew I was not starting on a wild-goose chase without a certainty of being able to get back to the island within a given time - always making due allowance for Maori <i>taihoa</i>-ism. <i>iIbid.</i> Bk4/5/277 ... we began to have misgivings that the very potatoes we had planted might, by the virtue of taihoaism, be ready to dig before the dining-room was finished in which we hoped to have eaten them. <i>(1881,Poenamo,Bk2/5/88</i>)

>  <i>taihoaism,</i> procrastination. `Waipeha was equal to any amount of taihoaism, and tangata maori loved him accordingly.' J. Logan Campbell, <i>Poenamu</i> (1881), p. 88. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/157</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

### Taihoaite
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The Far North is the land of.. the Taihoaites and the "never tires". <i>(Jun 9, 1921,NZ Observer,9</i>)

### taihoism
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The King Country.. is languishing under the fell disease, "taihoaism", and its associate Maori landlordism. <i>(Jan 23, 1912,Dominion,4</i>)

>  To-day there was a repetition of the ever recurring blocks in the goods-shed on the wharf, the Storm having to discharge her cargo on to the foreshore again. This position of affairs is anything but satisfactory; in fact, is absolutely unwarranted, and the sooner the efforts at present being made, to have the proposed additions commenced at once, are heeded, the better for the shippers, the town, and the district, which are suffering unnecessary inconvenience by the "taihoism" of the Department. <i>(Jul 3, 1908,Wanganui Herald,4</i>)

### taihoropi
 
 <i>n.</i> dabchick

>  <b>BULLER</b> PODICEPS RUFIPECTUS. (NEW-ZEALAND DABCHICK.) .<i> Native names.</i>- Weweia, Totokipio, Taihoropi (Hokianga), and Taratimoho (Waikato). <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,2/280</i>)

### taike
 
 <i>n.</i> an occasional confusion of tiaki and tieke

### taiko
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a rare and endangered petrel [ORIGIN: Maori, Chatham Is taiko rediscovered 1978.]

>  Buller-based DoC biodiversity manager Martin Abel said the two new colonies of Westland Petrel (Taiko) were found during a recent survey of the burrows... <i>(Jan 8, 2006,Sunday Star Times,A6</i>)

>  It's just another night in Taiko Town where conservation staff, scientists, Ornithological members and volunteers gather in summer, every two to three years in a bid to save the world's rarest seabird - the mysterious taiko... The taiko was rediscovered on New Year's night1978 on the windswept southern end of Chatham Island by David Crockett and a band of keen volunteers. <i>(Jan 4, 2000,Dominion,6</i>)

>  During her first night at the taiko camp, she had the privilege of seeing and holding her first taiko. <i>(May 2004,Footnotes,No. 21/15</i>)

>  The world's rarest seabird, the endangered taiko, had had its most successful breeding season since it was rediscovered in 1978... The taiko, also known as the magenta petrel, was thought to have been extinct for more than a century till school teacher and amateur ornithologist David Crockett and a group of volunteers rediscovered it. <i>(May 18, 2000,Dominion,7</i>)

>  The taiko is still so scarce that the only photograph that could be found shows a rather unhappy specimen perched on a table, surrounded by coffee cups. <i>(Mar 17, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12</i>)

>  ... the most important [Chatham Island] edible sea-birds: AS WRITTEN AS PRONOUNCED ENGLISH hopo ho[macron]p' albatross hakoakoa ha[macron]ko[macron]k' mutton bird taiko tchai[macron]k' pied shear water tupoupou tchu[macron]poup' pied blue billie reoreo ro[macron]ro[macron] Mother Carys chicken turiwati tchu[macron]riwat' dottrel [sic] kuaka ku[macron]k' snipe Notes 1928 Baucke Manuscript in Skinner and Baucke The Morioris (Bishop Museum Memoirs 358 <i>(1928,Bishop's Guide to Wellington,Vol. IX No. 5 pp. 343-382) 358</i>)

>  In that great battle, the two birds, the <i>tiitii (Haladroma urinatrix </i>= petrel), and the <i>taiko</i>, were made prisoners by the river-birds ... [fn] Of this bird, the Taiko, I have formerly heard, particularly at the northern parts of the North Island, but have never seen one. <i>(1879,TrNZI,11/103</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso)]

>  The Natives are very careful, when taking the <i>young</i> taikos, <i>not to disturb the burrows.</i> <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/89</i>) [NOTES: (Reischek)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> PROCELLARIA \{MAJAQUEUS\} PARKINSONI. (BLACK PETREL.) ...<i>Native names</i>.- Taiko and Kuia. This species, which appears to be peculiar to the New- Zealand seas, is by no means uncommon on the Hauraki Gulf ... <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,2/242</i>)

>  <b>SHAND</b> The young of many sea birds ... were used as food [on the Chathams], such as <i>Kuaka</i> (plover), young gulls (<i>Ngoiro</i>), shags (<i>Kuau) ...Ho[macron]po</i> (the albatross), <i>Hakoakoa</i> (mutton bird),<i>Taiko</i>[macron] (a smaller-sized mutton bird of slatey blue colour), <i> Titi</i>[i's macron] (a still smaller size) ... <i>(1911,Moriori People of the Chatham Islands, The,6</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Many of the offshore islands [of Stewart Island] are the breeding-places of the petrel called the mutton-bird (<i>titi</i> or<i> taiko</i>), ... <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol II South Island) 167</i>)

>  Procellaria parkinsoni Gray, 1862 <i>Black petrel (Taiko) </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,1980 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1970 Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ [1980] 263654.]

>  <b>LOCKLEY</b> `Taiko' is the name for any large muttonbird, once abundant on all mountain ranges from the Waitakeres to the Rimutakas of the North Island. In the `good old days', each Maori village had it own taiko colony, which was visited in the autumn to collect the fat `titi' (young birds). <i>(1980,House Above the Sea,188</i>)

>  <i>Black Petrel Procellaria parkinsoni </i>GRAY, 1862 ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Taiko, Parkinson's petrel</i>. ... This was the largest type of muttonbird available to North Island Maoris, ... <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,90</i>)

>  For several years now they've been hunting a bird that the rest of the world has been telling them was extinct, the taiko, or the magenta petrel, to give it its European name. <i>(Dec 16, 1987,Dominion,15</i>)

>  In a military-like operation, telemetry operators in tents on hilly vantage points around the Tuku Valley pick up the signals of taiko returning from the sea... For all the hours spent in hot pursuit of taiko, just one or two burrows are found by the end of the operation... Going on taiko expeditions was the "thing to do" for birdwatchers in the post-rediscovery days, says Mr Cotter. <i>(Jan 17, 2004,Dominion Post,E 12</i>)

>  Conservation workers say their hopes for the recovery of the world's rarest seabird, the taiko or magenta petrel, have been buoyed by a record crop of chicks. <i>(Mar 21, 2002,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  The first steps have been taken towards the creation of a new colony of Chatham Island taiko, with the translocation of eight chicks to a predator-free area on Chatham island. Taiko are considered a critically-endangered species, with fewer than 15 pairs breeding each year and a total population of approximately 100-150 birds. <i>(Jun 2007,Conservation Action,35</i>)

### tail
 
 <i>v.</i> cut tails off lambs

>  <b>BUTLER</b> ... you must tail your lambs, in which case every lamb has to be caught, and you will cut its tail off, and ear-mark it with your own ear-mark; ... <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,x/153</i>)

>  I tailed about 600 lambs. <i>(Apr 28, 1864,Chudleigh Diary,130,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <i>23rd.</i> Fine. Tailed 1500 Forest Creek Lambs. McLeod and I planted potatoes putting a Lamb's tail to every potatoe. They ought to grow well. ' <i>(Oct 23, 1865,Chudleigh Diary,202,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> <i>23rd.</i> Fine. Tailed 1500 Forest Creek Lambs. McLeod and I planted potatoes putting a Lamb's tail to every potatoe. They ought to grow well. ' <i>(1940,Te Waimate,114,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <i>1. To dock lambs' tails. 2. To move a mob of sheep or cattle along by following behind them. </i> <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),403</i>)

>  <i>tail ... NZ & Austral</i>. dock tail of (lamb). <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,782</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 490]]



 
 <i>v.</i> a two up call, esp. TAIL'EM



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  WANTED - A Steady MAN, with good Dog, to Tail Sheep. <i>(Apr 28, 1882,Timaru Herald,4</i>)

>  I found Swan [Thompson's] overseer at the lower station ... It was very late before the cattle got to the station ... Swan went back to the home station. <i>24th.</i> Fine. We tailed the cattle all day and Swan took delivery of them in the evening. <i>(May 23, 1863,Chudleigh Diary,88,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPS</b> W.P.P. tailing sheep from the Woolshed ... 1867 May 15 <i>Ibid </i>103 Slight norwester Spud digging, T.A.P. & Jack, Jim tailing <i>(Feb 3, 1867,Journal Kept at Rockwood and the Point,93</i>)

>  On arriving at the yards I find all the sheep out ... I counted [the sheep] and found 129 missing. I tailed them all day and yarded them at night. Two of old Jims dogs are slain on suspicion and two other Maori dogs are tied up to die as soon as their owner returns. <i>(Feb 20, 1868,Chudleigh Diary,216,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> ... the horses, after being `tailed,' or shepherded, all day by one of us [soldiers] `told off' for that purpose, were tied in rows ... for the night .. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,133</i>)

>  <b>ELWELL</b> It was necessary to `tail' several flocks, to prevent their mixing with other flocks. <i>(1878,Boy Colonists,110</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Tail, v.</i> to herd and tend sheep or cattle: lit. to follow close behind the tail. [1844] <i>(1898,Austral English,453</i>)

>  <i>tail v.t.</i> to tend cattle, sheep, etc.-tail'ing n. pl. in mining, the refuse part of crushed ore. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,11</i>)

>  <i>Tail</i>.- (1) The t. of a mob is the last part of it; hence the weakest and worst sheep. (2) To keep sheep or cattle together in a mob allowing them to move ahead slowly as they feed. (Homebush Station diary, February 24 185(2)) 3. To cut the t. off a lamb. Euphemism for <i>cut and t. </i>([1898 Morris Austral-English], which quotes 2 as used in 1855; C) <i>(Dec 23, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> <i>TAIL</i>: To `tail' a block means to muster it, and tail and mark the lambs. <i>(1949,High Country Days,197,1950; 1966 p. 190 ed.</i>)

>  <i>tail v.tr.</i> to herd and tend sheep and cattle. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),626</i>)

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> `I'd rather you helped me tail a mob if you've got a few hours.' <i>(1984,Day Hemingway Died, The,16</i>) [NOTES: (Takahe Man)]



 
 <i>n.</i> of a sawbench

>  WANTED, for a Bush Saw-mill, a Circular Hand, and a Man for Tail of Saw. <i>(May 14, 1872,Daily Southern Cross,1</i>)

>  <b>BAINES</b> Suppose a piece of timber - a flitch nine inches by twenty-four and sixteen feet long - is on the carriage, and that we intend cutting nine by three-quarter boards. After taking off a `face' cut, setting the gauger rollers, and placing the flitch in position, the man at the head starts the machinery. `Kreash,' says the saw. and in twelve seconds she is out at the other end. The man at the `tail' at that instant reverses the motion of the table, which gigs back. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,180</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> of a coin (See [heads and tails](hea-hom.md#heads-and-tails))



 
 <i>n.</i> formerly, the tail (usually of a deer) as a token on which a bounty was paid, or as proof of an animal killed [(cf token)]

>  During the last few months upwards of 1000 wild pigs have been slaughtered upon the Four Peaks and adjoining runs, the killers getting so much per tail for their labor - and the 300 skins are from the choicest, and as to distance, most convenient of the animals killed. <i>(Nov 1, 1872,Timaru Herald,3</i>)

>  During the last few months upwards of 1000 wild pigs have been slaughtered upon the Four-Peaks and adjoining runs, the killers getting so much per tail for their labor - and the 300 skins are from the choicest, and as to distance, most convenient of the animals killed. <i>(Nov 1, 1872,Timaru Herald,3</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> YOUNG MAN: Deerstalker. Live in Hoky. ... Up on the tops, all on my own. Fit as a flea, no beer, no gut, no birds, come down, cash my tails, live it up. <i>(1969,Solo,210</i>) [NOTES: 1969 Mason Television Script in 1981 Solo 210]

>  Just as he reached the burn he spotted a spiker ... He really wanted ... a decent head - he wasn't interested in tails. <i>(Sep 19, 1971,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> One year during the eighties we tailed 28,000 lambs from 32,000 ewes. The tailing of so many lambs was a long business, and sometimes it lasted for three or four weeks. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,114,1954 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> of NZ flax, the inferior, less valuable, or refuse part of anything

>  On exposure to the atmosphere a golden tint is produced by the formation of the brown chlorophyllan from chlorophyll, which tones with the brown colour of the tails and portions of unstripped leaf. <i>Ibid.</i> 238 One drawback that is very apparent with chemical bleaching is the fact that while the clear fibre is given a good white colour, the green tails and unstripped portions are left unchanged and show up very unfavourably ... <i>(1928,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,10/236</i>) [NOTES: (Aitken)]



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a breech birth of a lamb, with a leg or rear end presenting first.

>  <b>DAVIS</b> Just my luck. At the end of the first day on my own and a tail into the bargain. <i>(1962,Mutton on the Menu,112</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the poorest quality stock on sale or on a property.

>  There were some good quality sheep, but the 'tail' was a fearsome collection. <i>(Feb 17, 1911,Hawke's Bay Herald,19</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> We took endless trouble with the 'tail' of the mob which was specially nursed [against lung-worm.] <i>(1921,Tutira,226</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to follow or watch a mob of sheep or cattle, as in boundary-keeping.

>  I have had to tail the cattle on foot this five weeks ... <i>(1852,Homebush Journal,154</i>) [NOTES: Homebush Journal in DEANS (1964) Pioneers on Port Cooper Plains]

>  TN tailing cattle from 10 till 4 o'clock. <i>(Sep 27, 1855,St Leonard's Station Diary</i>)

>  Welch tailing out scabby sheep. <i>(Jun 30, 1860,St Leonard's Station Diary</i>)

>  Morley tailing sheep. <i>(Oct 23, 1860,St Leonard's Station Diary</i>)

>  Two or three men tailing sheep for the next two days. <i>(Feb 13, 1867,Castle Station Diary,256</i>) [NOTES: in Vance High Endeavour (1965)]

>  <b>MONEY</b> .. the horses, after being 'tailed' or shepherded, .. were tied in rows ... <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,133</i>)

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Tailing the ewes between Acheron and Saxon. 1969 NEWTON Big Country of the North Island 199 There were 3,000 ewes in the mob and he had been 'tailing' them on the road for the last two months. <i>(Jun 14, 1910,Alexander Mowat Diaries</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> When I was tailing a mob of sheep through a dense patch of tea-tree, the firebug hit the bottom. <i>(1973,Boot in the Stirrup,23</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> tail: (v): to move along quietly behind a driven mob, as often as not whilst they are grazing the long acre. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,82</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to mate some dairy cows in a herd late.

>  The herd was tailed with Hereford and Angus bulls. <i>(May 19, 2001,Hawke's Bay Herald,18</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to finish off a whip lash

>  I got my old whip from McDonald. He has tailed it very nicely indeed and it is now a very valuable whip. <i>(Sep 20, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,59,1950 ed.</i>)

>  I left my 14 ft. whip with a Mr Pitt to be tailed with Canqurew hide. <i>(Oct 13, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,62,1950 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to remove the tails of slaughtered deer as tokens of culling.

>  <b>FORRESTER</b> In that year we were told to stop skinning deer and just 'tail' them, so that we could kill more. <i>(1983,Chopper Boys, The,20</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to remove the tails from stock, particularly lambs.

>  Cutting and tailing lambs. <i>(Sep 5, 1855,St Leonard's Station Diary</i>)

>  <b>HOBHOUSE</b> .. the lambs must be tailed and branded with the station-mark. <i>(Nov 4, 1859,Hobhouse Letters and Papers</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> You must tail your lambs, in which case every lamb has to be caught, and you will cut its tail off, and ear-mark it with your own ear-mark. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,153</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> One year during the eighties we tailed 28,000 lambs from 32,000 ewes. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,114</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> tail: (v) to remove lambs' tails. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,82</i>)

>  "The younger lambs are cut and tailed the less they feel it." <i>(Nov 13, 1901,Otago Witness,10</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> sc of a mob

>  <b>ACLAND</b> <i>(1933,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: [see v 1933]]

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> <i>Tail</i>. The last part of a mob; often the worst sheep to shear. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,98</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [cf TAIL(ER)-OUT]

>  <b>BAINES</b> The man at the `tail' at that instant reverses the motion of the table which gigs back. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,180</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  I broke out a dishful of stuff from this part of the claim, and on washing it at the creek below I was much pleased to see a very nice "tail" of gold in the pan. <i>(Oct 2, 1883,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  <b>'H'</b> By a peculiar motion of the wrist, Old Grit separated the lighter iron-sand from a smaller quantity of heavier copper pyrites, and draws the last into a long string into the bottom of the dish, called `the tail'. <i>(1898,Grain of Gold, The,12</i>)

>  When the battered dish flowed empty, bar a tail of new-chum gold,/ Or it gave a ring of `colour' that betokened wealth untold. <i>(1985,Penguin Book of NZ Verse,93</i>) [NOTES: c1890-1900 Edwin Edwards `Waitekauri Every Time' in 1985 Penguin Book of NZ Verse 93]

>  ... a `tail' of very finely divided gold (electrum) could be obtained by dish washing. <i>(1912,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No. 15(NS)/139</i>) [NOTES: (Bell and Fraser, Waihi-Tairua)]

>  <b>HEINZ</b> ... the quantity of small pebbles and sand in moss is surprising, and at times a good `tail' of gold is obtained. <i>Ibid.</i> 22 An experienced miner can ... judge approximately the amount of gold to the ton of quartz by the tail of gold left around the bottom of the pan. <i>(1952,Prospecting for Gold,18</i>)

>  <b>WOOD</b> If the quantity [of gold-bearing concentrates in the gold-pan] is down to a few spoonfuls, try swirling it around <i>very gently</i> ... using only about half a cupful of water in the pan - this will form a `tail' around the edge of the bottom with the light sand going ahead, the black sand ... following, and behind that the gold flakes forming the point of the tail. <i>(1970,Gold Trails of Otago,80</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> When he was tailing [sc. the sheep], the girls were sent away, and I wanted to see what tailing <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves</i>)

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> You muster them [sc. the lambs] for tailing. <i>(1967,Farm & Station Verse,184</i>) [NOTES: Original from Stronach 1949]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> What with stragglers and late lambers we finished up by tailing just over 4,000 all told. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,160</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the last in a group, or the rear end of a mob of sheep or cattle.

>  A driving dog is a heading dog, and therefore he should always look after the lead of the sheep, the tail usually looks after itself. <i>(May 1933,NZ Farmer,317</i>)

>  <b>COOP</b> It is perhaps significant that the normal 'tail' of mismothered lambs is greatly reduced. <i>(1950,Shearing Ewes before Lambing,18</i>)

>  <b>SHARPE</b> Seemed to me that Jim had enough worries, battling along at the tail of the mob ... <i>(1966,Fiordland Muster,115</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> We wandered along trying to keep the tail sheep up. <i>(1970,Many a Glorious Morning,156</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> Invariably the driver, noting I had a smart horse would ask me to hold up the tail of his mob while he rode through to the stock agents to find where his mob was to go. <i>(1973,Boot in the Stirrup,4</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> tail: (n): the back end of a mob of driven sheep, containing the weak, lame, and generally slow walkers. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,82</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Tails.</i> 3. Batmen completing a party of horsemen of high rank: New Zealand soldiers; in the G.W. Opp. <i>the heads</i>, those in authority: ex the game of two-up. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,861</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>tails </i>batmen serving with high ranks, WWI soldiers; perceived as the opposite of heads, in the head and tail sides of the coin in the game `two-up'. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,111</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> to follow or watch

>  <b>BAKER</b> ...to tail, to follow or watch... <i>(1941,NZ Slang,31</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Tail. v.</i>, 2. Recorded by C. L. Money in <i>Knocking About in NZ</i> <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1192</i>)

>  <i>tail v.</i> follow and watch. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),19</i>)

### tail chain
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The stock comprise .. 1 stack oats, 200 empty sacks, 1 s.f. plough .. tail chains ... <i>(Apr 4, 1908,Bush Advocate,8</i>)

>  M.H.R's in single file, ex-M.H.R,'s [sic] and ex-mayors following two by two; next follow unemployed three abreast, white badge on left arm; next follow the ponies fully harnessed, drivers leading with right hand, tail chain over left shoulders. <i>(Jan 14, 1889,Grey River Argus,4</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> `The bullock team was attached to the log,' says D.J. Finlayson, `by a pair of grips on either side of a long chain with a ring in the centre to take what was called the `tail chain' ...' <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,180</i>)

### tail cow
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a poorly producing cow within a herd.

>  In any herd, genetic quality is raised by replacing the tail cows for better animals. <i>(Jun 1982,NZ Dairy Exporter,5</i>)

### tail in
 
 <i>v.</i> with (somebody), to sleep head and tail (with) (cf heads and tails) [ca1938 (and probably much earlier) J. Morison ca1950 used by trampers, Wellington]

### tail out
 
 <i>v.</i> [cf TAILER-OUT n[see quot MILLHAND]]

>  <i>tail out vb. (tr., adv.) N.Z.</i> to guide (timber) as it emerges from a circular saw. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,1191</i>)

### tail race
 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining; aka tailrace, tail-race]

>  The rights of an extension of a water-race, a tail-race, and for the erection of a dam in the same locality were also granted. <i>(Feb 8, 1912,Marlborough Express,5</i>)

>  Medical evidence was heard by the Arbitration Court to-day in respect to the claim of Arthur Clifton, miner, of Rimu, from the Rimu Sluicing Company, for GBP541 13s 1d, as a result of an accident on the defendant company's suice tailrace, whereby the plaintiff represented he had suffered total disability. <i>(Mar 23, 1925,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  <i>Right of Road over Claims &c.</i> - Upon any four or more persons applying for a road or crossing-place over any gully, creek, quartz reef, tail race, or other obstruction, and guaranteeing that they will make the same, if sanctioned, the Commissioner shall determine whether or not such road or crossing-place is necessary, and shall make such order relative thereto, as he may deem requisite ... <i>(Oct 19, 1861,Otago Witness,10</i>)

>  When the water is suddenly turned off after running off stuff, every miner must have noticed that little beaches have formed alternately on the opposite sides of the tail race, and that a scour channel has run down the centre of the tail race. <i>(May 15, 1890,Otago Witness,14</i>)

>  I have also examined the banks of the Pigburn for the same distance, as well as a tail-race, one mile long ... <i>(1875,TrNZI,7/132</i>) [NOTES: (Booth)]

### tail sample
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>Mc ARA</b> <i>Tail samples</i>: Regular samples of the tailings were taken to check that maximum recovery of precious minerals was being obtained. The values contained in the tailings did not usually exceed half a pennyweight of gold per ton of ore, except in the concentrate tailing, which it was uneconomic to grind to the degree of fineness required to recover the last six pennyweights (0.3 oz) per ton. Overall recovery was usually over 90% of the values. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),334</i>)

### tail shepherd
 
 <i>v.</i> [(cf. tail v.1)]



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to look after or follow stock, to keep boundary.

>  <b>DUPPA</b> men looking for employment - hired one to tail shepherd ... <i>(Jun 8, 1860,St Leonard's Station Diary,2</i>)

>  <b>DUPPA</b> Welch tail shepherding. <i>(Jun 26, 1860,St Leonard's Station Diary</i>)

>  <b>DUPPA</b> hands tail shepherding shorn and woolled sheep. Principal work to be done ...The rams during this time should be tail-shepherded. [tail] <i>(1860,St Leonard's Station Diary,2</i>)

>  J.S. and Billy tail shepherding. <i>(Nov 10, 1860,St Leonard's Station Diary</i>)

### tail-box
 
 <i>n.</i> [gold mining (cf tail-race)]

>  At present they are removing a portion of the terrace, to enable them to get a straight line for their tail-boxes, to a part of the creek where there is a good fall, which is indispensable in ground like this, for the debris removed in a week might be estimated at thousands of loads. <i>(May 5, 1870,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  [Dunedin] persons convicted of larceny from `tail-boxes' and alluvial claims. <i>(Mar 23, 1875,Evening Post,2</i>)

### tail-cutting
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Better stripping and tail-cutting is essential to get rid of these faults if high-class fibre is to be obtained. <i>(1928,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,10/238</i>) [NOTES: (Aitken)]

### tail-dirt
 
 <i>n.</i>

### tail-drag
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>AYSON</b> 99 From a little bush nearby they cut all the posts, wall plates, rafters and joists required for the hut, and by night had them tail-dragged on the ground. <i>(1937,Thomas</i>)

### tail-dragger
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Today the problem is finding people who want or have experience in tail-draggers (aeroplanes with a tail wheel or a skid) to fly them. <i>(May 6, 2000,Dominion,20</i>)

### tail-trim
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to crutch only at a sheep's rear end.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. about 22,000 are shorn, and of these anything up to 18,000 are first tail-trimmed over the board. <i>(1973,Big Country of the South Island,136</i>)

### tailend
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a part of a flock or herd containing the poorest producing animals.

>  Another aspect is using Murray Grey bulls on heifers and the tailend of the herd. <i>(Jul 1983,NZ Dairy Exporter,17</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a poorly producing animal.

>  Tail-end ram lambs are castrated with emasculators... <i>(Jul 14, 1983,NZ Farmer,10</i>)

>  If feed is severely limiting now and likely to remain so until lambing time or after, farmers could consider selling a proportion of the tail-end ewes, whether in-lamb or dry. <i>(Jul 14, 1983,Dominion Post,15</i>)

>  Stock sales: Store sheep: tailend ram lambs $36. <i>(Sep 12, 2002,Dominion Post,A6</i>)

### tailender
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a bad doer or low producing farm animal or a lamb or calf born late in the lambing/calving season. (See [tail cow](t-ten.md#tail-cow); [tailend](t-ten.md#tailend))

>  <b>DU FAUR</b> It is of course necessary to see that the ewe lambs are not penalised, while the 'tail-enders' of this mob could be drenched, and either run with the wether lambs, or in a separate 'tail-ender' mob. <i>(1966,Sheep Farming for Profit,115</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The wether lambs are also carried through the winter and, bar for the tailenders which go into the wether flock, these are shorn and sold fat as 2-tooths. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,63</i>)

>  Often, though, tailenders left when the fastest gainers have gone to the works are the ones used for breeding. <i>(Aug 10, 1972,NZ Farmer,11</i>)

>  They switched to dairying on this farm with 250 cows from three herds. Inevitably, they bought a liberal sprinkling of tailenders and older animals. <i>(Jul 1983,NZ Dairy Exporter,2</i>)

>  Lambs are not sent off until May. Then the crop is picked over every three weeks and the tailenders (about 80 or 90) finally go off by July. <i>(Aug 23, 1984,NZ Farmer,21</i>)

>  .. a large number of smaller sorts making $5 to $6.60 down to $3 for tailenders and very late lambs. <i>(Dec 7, 1988,NZ Farmer,36</i>)

>  Last weekend at Stortford I saw good store lambs sell from $35 to $45 and tailenders from $28 to $30. <i>(May 19, 1993,NZ Farmer,6</i>)

>  .. they'd normally expect to cull about 10% of light tailenders. <i>(Jun 15, 1994,NZ Farmer,21</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> Medium, top, tail-ender ... <i>(1996,Trouble with Stock Agents, The,39</i>)

>  He recently sold the tailenders of a large flock of lambs, making more money than on any of the others sold through last season. <i>(Jun 15, 2001,Dominion,6</i>)

>  Store sheep: New-season lambs $70.20 - $76.25 .. tailenders $33 - $49 ... <i>(Nov 17, 2001,Dominion,18</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [sheep] the last at the back of a mob or in a catching pen during shearing.

>  <b>MASTERS</b> When the leaders of the huge mob began to arrive at the station, the tail enders were still seven miles away. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,14</i>)

>  Another interesting observation at the time was that on the 11km trek to and from the homestead, the numbers of tailenders were recorded. They consistently had short legs! <i>(Jun 11, 1981,NZ Farmer,121</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> The others told me that tomorrow Roger would force me into shearing all my good ones first and then he would open up while I was struggling with the tail-enders. <i>(1989,Pioneer's Life, A,44</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> At this stage I was able to put my good leading dog out to the front to control the pace of the leaders as any successful drive must be governed by the speed of the tail-enders. <i>(1989,Pioneer's Life, A,85</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> Jim, who was on the top side, came and joined me at the tail and the tail-enders, including the halt and the lame.. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,85</i>)

>  <b>HARGREAVES</b> I got back to the beach after my run and my mate Jock came along with the tailenders.. [tailender] <i>(2000,To the Back and Beyond,125</i>)

### tailer
 
 <i>n.</i> [Flaxmilling]

>  It is on the principle of the scutcher, but the inventor claims that it scrapes instead of beating, as does the tailer in use in some North Island mills. <i>(Dec 20, 1905,Marlborough Express,1</i>)

>  For several months nothing has been heard of the patent tailer, but owing to the largest miller in this district having had several tons rejected as not first quality, the machine is to be re-installed in the mill. <i>(Oct 12, 1907,Marlborough Express,5</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [sawmilling]

>  The Conciliation Council sat to-day and dealt with the timber and coal yard employees, including carters, agreement being reached on all points, as follows: Working week 45 hours, plus attention to horses by carters; wages .. tailers 1s, per hour ... <i>(Aug 16, 1911,Colonist (Nelson),2</i>)

>  The top man pulls the [pit] saw up and guides it along a chalk line while it is being pulled down by the "tailer" below. <i>(1941,Lower Hutt Past and Present,53</i>)

### tailer out
 
 <i>n.</i> [also tailer-out, incl tailer off, tailer-off]

>  <b>LOCKE</b> I began as a slabbie, then a tailer-out, then a sawyer. <i>(1984,Kauri & The Willow, The,153</i>)

>  WANTED, for Waimate, a CIRCULAR SAWYER; also, a TAILER-OUT. <i>(Dec 28, 1871,Otago Daily Times,1</i>)

>  WANTED, one tailer-out for breast bench. <i>(Nov 18, 1887,Southland Times,1</i>)

>  WANTED immediately, 1 Breast benchman, 1 Tailer-out; good time made. <i>(Dec 3, 1892,Bush Advocate,3</i>)

>  TAILER-OUT and Slabby-docker wanted for our Petone Log Mill. <i>(Sep 10, 1945,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  WANTED, TAILER-OFF, AT THE Glenlady Saw Mills. <i>(Jan 24, 1872,Otago Daily Times,1</i>)

>  WANTED, Tailer-out, also Tailman for breaking down saw and two Yardmen. <i>(Feb 17, 1911,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  Stratford, Blair and Co. - Tailer out for Arnold Siding. <i>(Sep 2, 1919,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  The mutual agreement entered into by the Sawmill Society and the employers under which a week's work is a 48 hours' mininum [sic]; wages .. middle bench tailer out, 9s ... <i>(May 29, 1901,Grey River Argus,3</i>)

>  <b>MALFROY</b> The sawyer and tailer-out trams must be solidly ... laid, absolutely parallel with the saw ... <i>Ibid.</i> 28 The tailer-out (breast bench) has charge of all the bearings at his bench ... <i>Ibid.</i> 39 Tailer-out .. The man who controls the flitches and boards at the back end of a saw-bench. He works in conjunction with the sawyer as the timber passes throught he saws. <i>(1923,Small Sawmills,23</i>)

>  <b>SMYTH</b> She came upon the sawyer and his mate, the tailer-out ... <i>(1930,Wooden Rails,32</i>)

>  The planer ... spits out faced boards for the tailer-out to stack by the goose-saw. <i>(1950,Landfall,14/125</i>) [NOTES: (Middleton)]

>  TAILER-OUT The sawmill worker feeding out from a breastbench. <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)

>  <b>CHARLES</b> During our years at Leigh in Northland one of our neighbours had a small sawmill, and I often gave him a hand - in fact I became quite an efficient `tailer-out'. <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,69</i>)

### tailing
 
 <i>n.</i> the docking of a lamb's tail

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> We completed the tailing of the hill lambs about midday. <i>(1982,Blue Cliffs,190</i>)

>  Lambing.- The tailing did not reveal many exceptional percentages. <i>(Dec 18, 1918,Otago Witness,27</i>)

>  <b>WELD</b> About a week after the lambing is finished, the operations of castration, tailing, and ear-marking are performed. <i>(1851,Hints to Intending Sheep-Farmers in New Zealand,10</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> The operation of tailing is generally performed with a knife, and care must be taken to have as uniform a dock as possible. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,80</i>)

>  <b>AYSON</b> ... as there were wild dogs about I had to follow the lambs about all day, bringing them home at night to camp them near the homestead and sometimes in the yards. That is what they call in Australia `tailing', as the dingoes were very bad in the early days. <i>(1937,Pioneering in Otago,40</i>)

>  <b>BURDON</b> Quite apart from the long, expensive business of dipping scab-infected sheep, the lack of wire fences entailed constant vigilance in tailing and boundary keeping. <i>(1938,High Country,85</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> One year during the eighties we tailed 28,000 lambs from 32,000 ewes. The tailing of so many lambs was a long business, and sometimes it lasted for three or four weeks. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,114,1954 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [cf. tail v.1]

>  <b>ELWELL</b> This watching of sheep and keeping them together was called `tailing'. <i>(1878,Boy Colonists,47</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) 1. the removing of lambs' tails with knife, searing iron or runner ring. [Used loosely to include ear-marking and castration of male lambs at the same time and used mainly in the South Island.] 2. the quiet following of stock as they graze while being driven. [docking two senses in one entry here] (See [docking](dig-dou.md#docking))

>  WANTED, at Elephant Hill Station, SHEPHERDS, with good dogs , for Mustering and Tailing. <i>(Nov 15, 1872,North Otago Times,5</i>)

>  TAILING LAMBS. <i>(Dec 21, 1904,Otago Witness,33</i>)

>  <b>WELD</b> About a week after the lambing is finished, the operations of castration, tailing and ear-marking are performed. <i>(1851,Hints to Intending Sheep-Farmers in New Zealand,10</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> The operation of tailing is generally performed with a knife ... <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,80</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> The tailing of so many lambs was a long business, and sometimes it lasted for three or four weeks. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,114</i>)

>  <b>DREAVER</b> For several years I went back to the Wisp to work on a casual basis, mustering and tailing. <i>(1994,Catlins River Wisp Run,57</i>)

>  <b>BARDSLEY</b> tailing: (South Is) docking, castration and earmarking, known as 'marking' in some South Island areas. 2. <i>(2000,Land Girls, The,171</i>)

>  <b>MCGREGOR</b> One of the early tasks which was assigned was 'tailing', not cutting the lambs' tails as the word implies today but driving the flock from one place to another and watching the sheep and keeping them together in wide-open spaces where there were no fences. <i>(1980,Dog's Life, A,3</i>)

### tailing count
 
 <i>n.</i> numbers estimated from animals "tailed"

>  [He] said preliminary figures from Meat and Wool Innovation's annual tailing count suggested a drop in lamb numbers by 1.5 million head this season to 31.6 million. <i>(Nov 13, 2003,Dominion Post,C6</i>)

### tailing ground
 
 <i>n.</i> [cf tail v1, tail shepherding]

>  <b>ELWELL</b> He had caught sight of a native hanging about the `tailing ground'. <i>(1878,Boy Colonists,67</i>)

### tailing muster
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a gathering of ewes and lambs for castration, ear-marking, and the docking of lambs' tails.

>  The effect upon the lambing in the higher districts will no doubt be very serious, and the 'cutting and tailing' muster far short of what, in a more favourable season, might be expected. <i>(Nov 4, 1882,Otago Witness,14</i>)

>  <b>COOP</b> Apart from the tailing muster, which is done on the sheep blocks, ewes and lambs are not handled or brought into the yards ... <i>(1950,Shearing Ewes before Lambing,27</i>)

>  <b>DOMINY</b> During a tailing muster, in 1990, the runholder noted that the hired man was a 'natural musterer' ... <i>(2001,Calling the Station Home,174</i>)

>  <b>DOMINY</b> The Glenaan tailing muster in 1990 when we brought in the ewes and their lambs from the steep sunny north-facing 'lambing blocks' to the yards ... <i>(2001,Calling the Station Home,195</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> No time is wasted in a tailing muster. If a ewe with a young lamb drags behind, leave her; she'll come in at shearing, and the little lamb is better out of the crush of the yard. <i>(1951,High Country,24</i>)

### tailing out
 
 <i>n.</i> [includes verb & attrib]

>  WANTED two BENCHMEN, two Men for TAILING-OUT; also BUSHMEN and some other Men, for Messrs MALCOLM, RUSSELL & CO.'S, MAKINO STEAM SAW & PLANING MILLS, FEILDING. <i>(Nov 13, 1878,Manawatu Times,3</i>)

>  WANTED, a young MAN accustomed to tailing-out at sawmill. <i>(Aug 7, 1888,Grey River Argus,1</i>)

>  WANTED, two Sawyers (circular and breaking-down); 1 Tailing-out Man; 6 Bushmen (first-class men); Engine-driver, for mill; Man Cook, for mill; General Servant, for East Coast; Canvasser for town; General Servant, country, 15s; Lady to travel, salary and commission. <i>(Oct 24, 1896,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  WANTED - For Motu Sawmill, Competent Man, for Tailing-out; also Man for Breaking-down Bench. <i>(Feb 24, 1919,Poverty Bay Herald,1</i>)

>  WANTED, SMART BOY For Tailing-out Machine; also 2 EXPERIENCED TIMBER STACKERS. <i>(Jun 2, 1920,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  WANTED, Smart Boy for Tailing-out, city sawmill, good wages. <i>(Jan 7, 1930,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  MEN AND YOUTHS REQUIRED for tailing-out and yard work, good conditions, weather protection. <i>(Aug 22, 1945,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  WANTED - A bullock-driver; tailing-out man; several farm hands and milkers; married couples. <i>(Sep 26, 1904,Bush Advocate,3</i>)

>  It was then cut into fire sized blocks, pulled away from the saw and thrown in the big heap. This was called `tailing out'. <i>(1987,Province: New Nelson Writing,60</i>)

>  <b>JACKSON</b> [He] shouted greetings to men in black singlets ..., tailing out. He was answered by the ... drone of the breaking-down saw as it bit into a pine log. <i>(1988,Rainshadow,90</i>)

### tailing paddock
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a fenced area in which lambs are tailed/docked, earmarked and where male lambs are emasculated.

>  <b>STEVENS</b> If there are many ewes with twin lambs the dog and the shepherd can spend a profitable half-hour while the yards are being transported and erected in the next tailing paddock. <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,59</i>)

### tailing pen
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a temporary paddock yard made with scrim or netting for the tailing, marking or docking of lambs.

>  At the narrow end the tailing pen is made. <i>(1919,Livestock in New Zealand,46</i>)

>  Do not erect a tailing pen in the same spot as last year. <i>(1919,Livestock in New Zealand,47</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> Despite its forlorn expression, this dog is actually serving a useful purpose by being placed a the back of a temporary tailing pen. where it keeps the sheep from pushing against the weak fence. <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year,  A,26</i>)

>  <b>DREAVER</b> My favourite memories were of how pleasant it was to boil the billy in a sheltered gully at the top tailing pens at Shingly Creek, and after a day's tailing to sit back and relax for a while. <i>(1994,Catlins River Wisp Run,57</i>)

### tailing tally
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a total number of lambs docked on one property [known otherwise as a lambing percentage.]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Tailing tallies on the two places now exceed 12,000 and the woolclip is just on 500 bales. <i>(1973,Big Country of the South Island,167</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. of a normal tailing tally of about 1700 they marked less than 100. <i>(1973,Big Country of the South Island,233</i>)

### tailing yard
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a temporary yard, made of scrim or wire netting and standards, for tailing or docking lambs in a paddock.

>  <b>HALL</b> A typical tailing yard, built on the block in just half an hour. [tailing-yard] <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year,  A,29</i>)

>  It is built on Allotment No. 629, and has a frontage to the Tararu Creek Road of 31ft., with side frontages of 99ft., and adjoins the property of J. WILSON, Esq., and Brown's Tailing Yards. <i>(Apr 14, 1885,Thames Star,3</i>)

>  Portable Tailing Yards. <i>(Jun 9, 1920,Ashburton Guardian,8</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> While Ron bought sheep-dip, coils of cyclone netting for temporary tailing-yards, belt dressings for the saw bench and other essentials ... <i>(1966,Over the River,29</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> They [sheepyards] would never mean yards for a special job such as the dip yards or the temporarily erected tailing yard. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,72</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> On most sheep stations tailing yards are only temporary affairs. They are erected for the day only, and when the job is finished are pulled up, and used on the next block to be tailed. <i>(1949,High Country Days,197,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> On most sheep stations tailing yards are only temporary affairs. They are erected for the day only, and when the job is finished are pulled up, and used on the next block to be tailed. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,190</i>)

>  <b>TOMLINSON</b> We always got on well. Next [41] day we mustered down to the tailing yards. <i>(1968,Remembered Trails,40</i>)

### tailing-band
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> There was nothing in it [sc. the farm utility] except a ten-pound tin of honey, and some tailing-bands scattered on the floor. <i>(1984,Day Hemingway Died, The,141</i>) [NOTES: (Sully's Country)]

### tailing-iron
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a searing-iron used to burn through lambs tails.

>  <b>DAVIS</b> Without moving his feet, he leaned down to his right and pulled a long-handled tailing-iron from the perforated oil drum that served as a brazier. <i>(1962,Mutton on the Menu,155</i>)

### tailings
 
 <i>n.</i> the waste material removed during the processing of mineral ores [(cf TAIL v)]

>  Owing to the tailings of the sluicing claims being swept down into the Buller River the original bed with its deposits of gold has been covered over and it is now practically useless for private working. <i>(Jun 27, 1929,Evening Post,14</i>)

>  All through Central Otago huge piles of tailings could be seen that would not grow anything for hundreds of years. <i>(Mar 4, 1941,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  It saves all the gold and mercury; none can be found in the tailings by the most delicate chemical test. <i>(Dec 21, 1852,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  Tailings : The discarded portion of the ore after treatment. <i>(Sep 1999,Ohinemuri Regional History Journal,43 : 14</i>)

>  The Phoenix Syndicate's first return under the cyanide process shows that 60 tons of tailings were treated and gave 42oz 10dwt of bullion of the value of GBP3 10s per ounce. <i>(Feb 17, 1908,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  We saw on Monday four ounces of very fine gold, the result of the labour of six men in five days, who had washed the tailings left by the diggers three years and a-half ago. <i>(Oct 5, 1859,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,2</i>)

>  The blanket tailings or blanketings, as they are otherwise called, consist mostly of iron pyrites and other sulphides, cobines with quartz, and contain a fair proportion of gold ...that had escaped over the silver tables. These tailings are treated in berdans with extra quicksilver and ground up. The berdans now in use at the Thames ... are generally five feet in diameter ... At one time a couple of rotating balls were considered sufficient for the amount of crushing required; now, the general practice is to have a loose ball as well as a stationary one attached to a chain, and it is called a drag ball; this drag does more work than a loose ball ... for the drag grinds the bowl as much as the tailings. <i>(1876,TrNZI,8/179</i>) [NOTES: Goodall Thames Gold-fields]

>  <b>BLYTH</b> <i>Tailings</i>: Useless material discarded after most of the metal has been extracted by treatment plants. <i>(1935,Gold Mining Year Book,11</i>)

>  <b>HODDER</b> ... a group of women were round the pool washing the `tailings' in tin dishes. <i>(1862,Memories of NZ Life,166</i>)

>  Kurunui Battery ... of six stampers, and one stamper specimen battery with Berdans at the end of tables to grind the tailings. <i>(1868,Thames Miner's Guide, The,93</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> The goldminer in his sluicing operations, spreads out de[e-acute]bris, drift, alluvial sludge, or tailings in strata all over the plain. <i>(1873,TrNZI,6/329</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> A company formed to work these `tailings,' as the are technically termed, has been in operation some time, with payable results. <i>(1887,History of the Early Gold Discoveries  in Otago,140,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>`1891 The detritus</i> carried off by water from, a crushing machine, or any gold-washing apparatus.' (Brough Smyth, `Glossary of Mining Terms.') Not limited to Australia. <i>(1898,Austral English,454</i>)

>  The waste dirt and stones carried off by water from the gold-washing in some kinds of mining in Australia and New Zealand <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),403</i>)

>  <b>EVANS</b> Some of the early tailings at the Sandy Knowes ... where they show a gentle southward dip. <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,5</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> TAILINGS: all the detritus left after gold-saving. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,528,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>KEENE1967</b> When we were about Waipiata, and pushing up to Hamilton's station Ethel became very excited, and exclaimed, `The Tailings!' We were pushing through heavy gravel at the time. I looked about for a heap of lambs tails, the only kind of `tailings' I knew. The language of gold mining was unknown to me, and Ethel had to explain that tailings were the heaps of gravel left after the soil had been sluiced away. <i>(1967,Tang of The Bush</i>) [NOTES: (Robert McLatchie 1875-1963)]

>  <b>McARA</b> <i>Tailings</i>: The discarded portion of the ore after treatment, usually seen on the banks of the Ohinemuri River as fine, whitish, quartz sand or as muddy water in the river. The Ohinemuri River was an officially-declared sludge channel for tailings disposal. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),334</i>)

>  The tailings from stamper-batteries are as fine as sand. <i>(1896,AJHR,I-4A/6</i>)

>  The creek was named after.. Mary Boyle, who.. complained bitterly when the stream was used as a sludge channel for tailings. <i>(1959,Women of Westland,10</i>) [NOTES: From 2nd edition]

### tailings-dump
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> My only dividend from the flood, the ample tailings-dump, made a great difference to work on the claim and returns took a turn for the better over the new few paddocks. <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,119,1963 ed.</i>)

### tailman
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [bush clearing] a man employed to pull on the tail ropes of a saw.

>  <b>WASWO</b> Sometimes an extra man stood at each end of the saw, they stood behind the men who held the saw, and pulled on the tail ropes that were tied to the saw. handles. Four men on the two man saw showing how the tailman pulled on the tail ropes of the saw. <i>(1996,Farming Progress in NZ 1814-1995,11</i>)

>  Sawkeepers, per day of eight hours, 10s; tailmen, 8s ... <i>(May 26, 1879,Evening Post,2</i>)

### tailor
 
 <i>n.</i> elliptical for tailor-made

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> 1937 Partridge Dict Slang Rare in sg: Austr ca1920: B1942: current in NZ by 1925. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Rare in sg: Austr ca1920: B1942: current in NZ by 1925. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang</i>)

### tailor-made
 
 <i>n.</i> a manufactured cigarette (opp. to roll-your-own)

>  A boy in the big house down the road had a mother who smoked tailor-mades. <i>(Aug 8, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),62</i>)

>  <b>IRELAND</b> [He] invited me to sit don beside him in the shelter of the ridge and he'd show me 'how to roll a fag so you can't tell the difference with a bloody tailor-made.' <i>(1998,Under the Bridge & Over the Moon,91</i>)

>  <b>GIBBONS</b> Since only a limited number smoked tailor-mades, collecting sets [sc. of cigarette cards] was a long business. <i>(1989,Recollections (various authors/titles),38</i>)

>  "I've even got some tailor mades," boasted Alf, producing the package. <i>(Dec 29, 1956,Weekly News,36</i>)

>  Alterations in the taxation on tobacco caused a swing back to tailor mades <i>(Sep 11, 1935,NZ Free Lance,19</i>)

>  Though there had been a big increase recently in sale of roll-your-own tobacco it did not compensate for the decline in tailor-made cigarettes.. . We have introduced mild and menthol variants because they want the options they used to get with tailor-made. <i>(Apr 28, 1992,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  Meanwhile, the contrast with TV3 was the contrast between tailor-mades and roll-your-owns. <i>(Dec 19, 1999,NZ Catholic,19</i>)

>  Give us a 'tailor-made.' <i>(1935,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,iv/36</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> (of cigarettes)

>  There are but a few pipes, and 'tailor-made' cigarettes are only a luxury. <i>(Apr 1945,NZ Geographer,I/23</i>)

>  New Zealanders are drinking less and switching their smoking habits from tailor-made cigarettes to roll-your-owns, Statistics New Zealand says... New Zealanders were increasingly smoking cheaper roll-your-own cigarettes instead of the tailor-made variety. <i>(Nov 27, 1999,Dominion,9</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> I smoke a bit, not very much; intend later on to `roll my own' as tailor-made are rather biting. <i>(Apr 9, 1944,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1944 April 9 Letter in 1990 McKay James K. Baxter 68]

### tailpaint
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [dairying] to tag cows with waterbased paint in coded colours in order to detect and leave a record of oestrus.

>  The whole herd is tailpainted again during morning milking. <i>(Oct 2000,Waikato Times,24</i>)

>  Cows are tail-painted three weeks before mating. [tail-paint] <i>(Mar 22, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a water-based paint used to tag cows for detection of bulling at oestrus.

>  <b>EDGAR</b> The use of tail paint is a major aid to the detection of heat. <i>(1959,NZ Farmers' Veterinary Guide,7</i>)

>  To identify cows which are treated or three titted, paint them on the legs with red tailpaint, stays on a lot longer than spray markers. <i>(Feb 10, 2002,RD1.com,Farm Management</i>)

### tailpainting
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the tagging of cows with waterbased paint in coded colours to aid detection and recording of oestrus.

>  Tail-painting involves applying a strip of paint along the backbone of a cow and over the point described as the 'head of the tail.' <i>(Apr 1980,NZ Dairy Exporter,49</i>)

>  Is visual selection picking up all the cows on heat, or could aids such as tail painting, recording pre-mating heats, or using a vasectomised bull with a chinball harness help? <i>(Jun 1982,NZ Dairy Exporter,2</i>)

>  Tailpainting is not used until three days before mating starts. <i>(Jul 1983,NZ Dairy Exporter,21</i>)

>  Tailpainting is an aid to heat detection. [tail-painting; tail painting] <i>(Nov 1987,NZ Dairy Exporter,11</i>)

### tainter
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a pasture weed or plant that taints the flavour of milk or butter.

>  Land cress, also known as twin cress, is one of the worst milk and butter tainters. <i>(Apr 1968,NZ Journal of Agriculture,7</i>)

### tainui
 
 <i>n.</i> a North Island shrub, scarce in the wild, related to kumarahou. (See [also golden tainui](go-gra.md#golden-tainui))

>  <b>FEATON</b> POMADERRIS TAINUI (<i>Hector.</i>) ... Its specific name is accorded to it by the discoverer in honour of the great canoe, as that by which it is known by the Maoris. <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,92</i>)

>  53 Lithograph of tainui foliage. <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,16</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>[Pomaderris apetala, Lab.] Tainui.</i> ... A common Australian plant. The Maoris assert that it sprang from the rollers or skids that were brought in the canoe `Tainui' when they first colonised New Zealand. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,99</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Pomaderris apetala (The Tainui).</i> ... Maori name <i>Tainui. </i> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,236,1940 p. 249 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LYSNAR</b> There is a plant the Maoris call `Tainui'. It is interesting because of its association with a tradition that a specimen in the North Island sprang from a green timber used in the building of the `Tainui', a canoe which brought some of the Maori immigrants to New Zealand between five and six hundred years ago. <i>(1924,New Zealand (various authors/titles),69</i>)

>  <b>WODZICKI</b> Kirk (1896) says that the tainui (<i>Pomaderris apetala</i>) has been completely destroyed at Kawhia by goats [Kirk TrNZI 1896 XXVIII 1-27] <i>(1950,Introduced Mammals of NZ,162</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>POMADERRIS APETALA, tainui</i>. An erect, rather narrow shrub growing to 5m tall. ... Known as a wild plant only in a few North Island sites, it is widespread in Tasmania and nearby parts of mainland Australia. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,88</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [Ma.] a New Zealand-bred cultivar of wheat.

>  One further 16-acre crop of Tainui was sown during the last week of September on strong loam soil on the property of Mr Alec Stuart, Heaton Park. <i>(Sep 16, 1940,NZ Journal of Agriculture,199</i>)

>  Tainui is popular in the north, but its weak straw renders it more liable to lodging .. Tainui is essentially a spring wheat. Taiaroa, which is similar to Tainui, is occasionally grown in Southland and parts of South Otago. <i>(Jul 16, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,75</i>)

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> I have not seen it [the new wheat cultivar] yet, but it is a cross between Tainui and Tuscan. <i>(1951,Canterbury Tales,84</i>)

>  Tainui and Gabo are recommended for the Rangitikei-Wanganui district. <i>(Jul 1959,NZ Journal of Agriculture,27</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Pomaderris elliptica</i>

>  (And) <i>Pomaderris elliptica</i> dwarf tainui <i>(1917,Duncan & Davies' Catalogue</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> kumarahou

>  <b>MATTHEWS</b> <i>Golden Tainui</i> - see <i>Pomaderris elliptica </i> <i>(1943,NZ Gardening Dictionary, The,79</i>)

>  <b>LOCKE</b> Botanical discoveries were spice to the dish. I shan't forgot the first time I saw [near Auckland] ... masses of kumarahou adorning a barren outcrop of white clay. (A cultivated variety is now sold as golden tainui.) <i>(1981,Student At The Gates,73</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Pomaderris phylicaefolia</i>

>  (And) <i>Pomaderris phylicaefolia</i> heath-like tainui <i>(1917,Duncan & Davies' Catalogue</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Pomaderris elliptica</i>

>  (And) <i>Pomaderris elliptica</i> yellow tainui <i>(1916,Official Trade Register of Nurserymen</i>)

### Tainui
 
 <i>n.</i> an iwi

>  His ancestry is Swiss on his father's side and Tainui on his mother's. Tainui wooed him in 1992 from Auckland to Huntly's Waahi Pa, residence of the Maori Queen, to be the first GP for their pioneering health trust, Raukura Hauora O Tainui... Tainui are the iwi of the Waikato and by oral legend the descendants of the immigrants of the canoe of that name. <i>(Nov 1999,North & South,60</i>)

>  The Tainui people moved in as far back as the 14th century, followed by Europeans in the mid 1820s. <i>(Nov 2002,AA Directions,No. 54/19</i>)

>  [Hdg] Tainui faction tries to sell Defence Force land... Under the deal, Tainui would have bought the site for $975,000. <i>(Jan 12, 2001,Dominion,7</i>)

>  So, it hasn't been a matter of Tainui suddenly being deluged with undeserved riches. <i>(Apr 1998,Mana,No. 21 : 37</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (the migration canoe)

>  <b>MILLER</b> To the north of the River Mokau, the northern boundary of Taranaki, the land was occupied by four tribal groups whose ancestors came to New Zealand in the Tainui canoe. These were the Waikato, the Ngati Maniopoto, the Ngati Toa and the Ngati Raukawa. <i>(1958,Early Victorian NZ,14</i>)

>  Mokau claims the honour of being the landing-place of the "Tainui," one of the original pioneers. <i>(Aug 7, 1890,Otago Witness,36</i>)

>  Come, I will drag the Aotea and Tainui (canoes) to the shores of the sea. <i>(Mar 15, 1873,Auckland Weekly News,14</i>)

### taioma
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [Ma] a poor class of soils.

>  .. most of the clean tussock country falls into the Wehenga class, and that which is invaded by manuka scrub is mainly of the Taioma class. <i>(Mar 1955,NZ Journal of Agriculture,277</i>)

### taiore
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> The next improvement was a similar clearance of the little fenced compartments which each family possess, and which ... were universally surrounded with planted flax, generally <i>Tehori</i>, which they used for making their mats, nets, and for other economical purposes; ... <i>(1959,Early Travellers,165</i>) [NOTES: 1847 Johnson Notes for a Journal in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 165]

### taipa
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [taiepa](t-ten.md#taiepa))

### taiparu
 
 <i>n.</i> first-born female

>  <i>taiparu</i>, first-born female in family of high rank, who was invested with special mana and special tapu: noblesse oblige strictly observed. <i>(Jun 1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/158</i>) [NOTES: J.C.Andersen]

### taipo
 
 <i>n.</i> ?taniwha

>  <b>TOWNSEND</b> There are no quadrupeds except the green lizard - which has 5 golden spots on its back - the natives are in great fear of it & call it "Syphou" - the Devil. <i>(Nov 16, 2004,Letter,9</i>) [NOTES: In <i>Absolutely Positively Wellington</i> 0/9/2004, p. 9]

>  <b>HAAST</b> On the 11th of June we started from the cave, called by the natives Te-ana-o Matuku, respecting which they tell a very interesting tale of a large ngarara, a monster in the form of an enormous lizard, which formerly lived here, and devoured all who attempted to pass. Since they have become Christians, however, they have called it by another name, Te whare Taipo (the Devil's house), probably referring the formation of the cave, of which I shall speak more fully in the geological part of my report, to supernatural agency rather than to the hand of nature. <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,50</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> We kept together, in the middle of the road, as we half expected the `taipo' to spring out of the edge of the bush and grab us at any moment. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,44</i>)

>  <i>taipo n. NZ </i>evil spirit. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,782</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <i>taipo n. NZ </i>evil spirit. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,782</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>MIKAERE</b> [Mitai Tuture records] Te Maiharoa went ahead to Awakino to kill the `taipo' of that place and I think he killed two there. Taipo is the name used now but we called [47] those evil spirits atua then [c1860s]. <i>(1988,Te Maiharoa,46</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a madman

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> `Taipo, female dreamer; a prophetess; an evil spirit.' <i>(1848,Leaf from Natural History,43</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> At Karatia I noticed a pole with a cross bar from the extremity of which was suspended a rag ... I was told that an insane man living there had told them that the influenza was caused by the anger of the spirits of the dead and that this [134] was to appease their wrath. We were soon saluted by the Taepo (madman) himself ... addressing us in an English accent in complete gibberish. <i>(Dec 3, 1853,Journal,13/133</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> `There is the <i>Taringa-here</i>, a being with a face like a cat; and likewise another, called a <i>Taipo</i>, who comes in the night, sits on the tops of houses, and converses with the inmates, but if a woman presumes to open her mouth, it immediately disappears.' <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,49</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a) evil spirit; devil. b) in weakened use, of a naughty child: `you little taipo'. [probable Maori taipo goblin] Maori <i>taipo</i>, goblin [HWO notes:8 not in Williams 3rd edn 1871, 4th edn of 1892 (or as taepo); nor in English-Maori of 1871 at goblin, etc; 1892 English-Maori: 269 <i>Goblin</i>, n. <i>tupua;</i> kehua.<i>First appears in 1917 5th edn </i>could <i>taipo</i> have been generated in English hearers by a mishearing of<i> tipua</i>, tupua `goblin, demon' etc - of same meaning.]

>  <b>MAY</b> Kehu, a first-rate bushman and worth his weight in tobacco, dispelled all feelings of loneliness by chanting the Wesleyan service mixed with incantations to the <i>taipo</i> of lake and river, and diversified by some whaling station epithets. <i>(1962,West Coast Gold Rushes,33</i>)

>  <b>REES</b> The old superstition of "Atua" and "Taipo" (exacting spirits of disease) pass away whenever Christian doctrines are received. <i>(1851,GB Parliamentary Papers (Irish Univ. series),IX 1779/33</i>)

>  <b>JOLLIE</b> [of a trick scaring the Maoris at Otaki with a turnip lantern] Most of them considered it a spirit of some kind, chiefly favouring the `Tipo' or devil, but Mrs Taylor our host's Maori wife boldly asserted ... that it was the `Atua' or good spirit, ... <i>(1873,Reminiscences,xvii</i>) [NOTES: [c1843 late] 1873 Ed Jollie Reminiscences [MS] xvii]

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> Occasionally they [sc Maoris] travel at night in a party; but they always sing <i>waitas</i> (native dirges) to keep the<i> taipo</i> (devil) away. <i>(1935,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,72</i>) [NOTES: c1875 Meredith Adventuring in Maoriland (1935) 72]

>  <b>WELLS</b> `The similarity in sound and meaning of the Egyptian word <i>typhon</i> with that of the Maori <i>taipo</i>, both being the name of the Spirit of Evil, is also not a little remarkable.' [Ingenious, but worthless.] <i>(1878,History of Taranaki,3</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> ... the natives having a great antipathy to be abroad after sunset, being superstitious and afraid of the evil spirit Taipo. <i>(1879,Waikato War,49</i>)

>  <b>POTTS</b> `His wife became seriously affected, declaring that <i>Taipo </i>had entered into her. Reasoning was wholly useless. She declared that Taipo was in the smoke of the wood, which smoke she had inhaled; soon she became prostrated by illness and was expected to die.' <i>(1886,Out in the Open</i>) [NOTES: 1886. T. H. Potts, `Out in the Open,' `New Zealand Country Journal,' vol. x. p. 262:]

>  <b>CRAWFORD</b> `After dinner Watkins requested the loan of a tomahawk to defend himself on going up to the Pa on the hill above. He said he knew that there was a <i>taipo</i> (devil) about; he felt it in his head.' <i>(1887,Travels in NZ,107</i>)

>  <b>BARLOW</b> ' They were making the noises I heard to drive away the `Taipo,' a sort of devil who devoted his attention exclusively to Maoris, over whom, however, he only possesses power at night.' <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,48</i>)

>  <b>ROBERTS</b> `They believed it was the principal rendez-vous of the fallen angel (Taipo) himself.' <i>(1891,Southland in 1856,72</i>)

>  <i>Taipo</i>, for instance, of course one knows its meaning, though it has been adopted chiefly as a name as common as `Dash' or `Nero' for New Zealand dogs; all the same the writers upon Maori superstitions seem to have no knowledge of it. Polach [sic], Dieffenbach, Nicholas, Yates, call their evil spirits <i>whiros</i> or <i>atuas</i>. Tepo, the place of darkness, is the nearest they have come to it. I think myself it is South Island Maori, often differing a little in spelling and use; and so very much the larger proportion of New Zealand literature is the literature of the North.' <i>(1896,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1896. Modern. Private Letter (May): [Morris]]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> a New Zealand word for devil, often applied by settlers to a vicious horse or as a name for a dog. There is a dangerous river, the Taipo, on the west coast. There is considerable dispute as to whether the word is true Maori or not. The Rev. T. G. Hammond of Patea says - -->`No such Maori word as taipo, meaning devil, exists. It would mean evening-tide - tai-po. Probably the early sailors introduced attached meaning of devil from the Maori saying, `Are you not afraid to travel at night?' referring to the danger of tidal rivers.' -->On the hand, Mr. Tregear says, in his `Maori Comparative Dictionary,' s.v. - -->`Taepo, a goblin, a spectre. C.f. <i>tae</i>, to arrive; <i>po</i>, night.' The Rev. W. Colenso says, in his pamphlet on `Nomenclature' (1883), p. 5 - -->`Taepo means to visit or come by night, - a night visitant, - a spectral thing seen in dreams, - a fancied and feared thing, or hobgoblin, of the night or darkness; and this the settlers have construed to mean the Devil! - and of course their own orthodox one.' <i>(1898,Austral English,454</i>)

>  <b>DOBSON</b> `Tae-po, the evil spirit, now comes. He comes in the form of a little pig. He often comes like that. ...' Terapuhi was still shaking with fear, but gradually he recovered and said, `Tae-po does sometimes come like that, and not do any harm, but I told you this is a bad place.' <i>Ibid.</i>96 Tae-po, an evil spirit, from the verb <i>tae</i>, to arrive, and <i>po</i>, night or darkness. An evil nocturnal visitor, incorrectly (as a rule), put as one word, Taipo, which is of course meaningless. <i>(1930,Reminiscences,95</i>)

>  <b>AYSON</b> That evening the boys held the floor, telling all sorts of Maori stories about Taipo. <i>(1937,Thomas,53</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Taipo.</i> occ.<i> taepo.</i> A vicious horse; as name for a dog: New Zealand coll.: mid-C. 19-20. Perhaps ex Maori: but see Morris. -2. Among Maoris, a s. term for a theodolite, `because it is the `land-stealing devil' (Morris). Ex <i>taepo</i>, Maori for a goblin. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,861</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> <i>mangumangu taipo </i>If she [a Maori woman] stayed on till dark she was frightened to go home alone as she was afraid of the `taipo' ... <i>Ibid.</i>20 (as a nickname c1850s-1860s) We did not get very far with our Maori, but I used to go to Maori services with Taipo (Joe Benson) and picked up quite a lot of it. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,13</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> Taipo, it may be added, was noted by Morris as applied by the Maoris to `a surveyor's theodolite because it is a land-stealing devil,'... <i>(1941,NZ Slang,56</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> ... <i>taipo</i>, devil (from Maori <i>taepo</i> - if indeed this word was <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,93</i>)

>  <i>taipo,</i> supernatural being; goblin: used by the Maori believing it to be Pakeha, and by the Pakeha believing it to be Maori; often spelt taepo, which also is not a Maori word: so taipo is a word coined by no one knows whom... <i>taipo</i>, see the remarks following the word in list 2. There is a Maori remark that may be quoted here: Haere ra! E tama E! Mou te tai ata, moku te tai po (Farewell! O son! lit. Yours the morning tide, mine the evening tide, meaning You pass with the morning tide, as I will pass by the evening tide). The saying was often used in war, when a man saw a young relative or friend slain; and the words were often prophetic of his own fate. The meaning is quite destroyed if the last two words are run together - taipo instead of tai po: that results in the word under discussion, making the meaning: You pass on the morning tide, while I go to the devil! - naturally a modern interpretation. A Pakeha using the expression once confused that last words and made them taepa; when it meant: You pass on the morning tide, while I get over the fence. So near is the approach of the ridiculous to the sublime. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/150</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>taipo</i>: devil <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)

>  The Maori word for `devil', literally a night-visitor, but some Maori scholars have denied that it is a Maori word at all. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),403</i>)

>  <i>taipo n</i> -s [Maori <i>taepo] NewZeal</i> : a demon, devil, or other specter appearing at night <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,2329</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> taipo: a European name for a weta, a word taken by Europeans to be Maori for <i>devil.</i> (To Maoris, so far as they use the word, it is a term of abuse, and is thought by them to be borrowed from English.) <i>(1964,Collected Stories,303</i>) [NOTES: 1964 Pearson Glossary to 1964 Sargeson Collected Stories 1935-1963 303]

>  <b>WEBBER</b> Rushing out of the bushes Archie gave such a convincing imitation of an angry taipo that the [Maori] poachers hurriedly decamped ... originally Maori; it may have been used by Maoris believing it to be English and by Englishmen believing it to be Maori, being originally applied, according to Morris, to the theodolite-tripod because it is the `land-stealing devil'); .. <i>(1965,Try Again Friday,81</i>)

>  <i>taipo n. </i>New Zealand word for devil, hobgoblin, evil spirit; Maori name for a surveyor's theodolite [Orig. doubtful]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),626</i>)

>  <i>(Mar 15, 1968,Listener (New Zealand Listener),6/5</i>) [NOTES: [check sense]]

>  <i>taipo ... n. </i>N.Z. word for devil, hobgoblin, evil spirit; Maori word for a surveyor's theodolite [Orig. doubtful]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),19</i>)

>  <b>LEE</b> I was reared on Tapu and Taipos when the Maoris were around [= `resident here'], and I don't think I'll ever get rid of a scary feeling ...I wouldn't go alone [to the Maori burial ground]. The taipos would multiply if I were alone. [=? ghost, ?devil, ?night goblin] <i>(1970,Mussolini's Millions,11</i>)

>  <b>McCARTHY</b> Now the Taipo is something older Maoris never discussed. It was a mythical spirit, which, when it made its presence known, was a fore- runner of death. <i>(1973,Listen...!,36</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> taipo <i>devil </i> <i>(1982,Selected Poems,189</i>)

>  <b>SANSOM</b> Smugglers and poachers were good solid flesh but taipo was an intangible. `Look out! Taipo will get you' frightened me off dark or forbidden places when I was a child. Taipo is [120] a hybrid word, a mixture of pakeha-maori for a mythical fiend or demon. <i>(1982,In the Grip of an Island,119</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> 206 Taipo = demon, night goblin (a word of dubious origin) <i>(1983,Bone People, The,467</i>)
