>  She would roar and whistle and play every tune on the bagpipes, shifting boulders would vibrate the hillsides; terrific, weird, madmen, helplessness the evil one - Taipo. <i>(1933,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,II/85</i>)

>  <b>ELKINGTON</b> The uneducated ones [sc. Maoris] still have a superstition that the Tipu (devil) lurks there (sc. in the bush) and prowls about at night ready to pounce on the unwary. <i>(1906,Adrift in NZ,46</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> They [sc. the Maoris] were much excited, and shouted "Taipo!" <i>(1915,Reminiscences of Earliest Canterbury,27</i>)

>  When we first came to New Zealand the Maoris would retire to their raupo huts as soon as it got dark, being frightened of the 'taipo' or devil. <i>(May 29, 1935,NZ Free Lance,18</i>)

>  I not 'fraid of te taipo... No doubt many builders possess taipos of this sort. <i>(Jul 15, 1923,Aussie (NZ Section),XII</i>)

>  They will have much idle time on their hands, and are likely to be.. a temptation to their 'taipo.' <i>(Mar 5, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  The natives are frightened to venture into the middle [sc. of Lake Rotomahana] for fear of Taipo (the devil) who they believe lives there. <i>(May 10, 1873,Auckland Weekly News,20</i>)

>  It was a haunted country, they [sc. the Maoris] said, full of "taipos." <i>(Oct 9, 1890,Otago Witness,31</i>)

>  <b>PAYTON</b> It is sometimes the custom to extort money from tourists by telling them that if a small sum of money is not left on the rock as a sort of bribe to the taipo, or devil, the boat will be upset! <i>(1888,Round about NZ,121</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Taipo, n. <i>Goblin</i>. Note.- This word is used by Maoris believing it English, and by Europeans believing it Maori, it apparently being neither. Colenso suggests <i>tae-po</i>, but this is not used by the Maori. <i>(1971,Dictionary of the Maori Language,364</i>)

>  As for the term taipo, lexicographer H.W. Williams says it is one of those words used by the Maori believing it English and by Europeans believing it Maori, it being apparently neither. We have come to accept it as a goblin, a mischievous creature of folklore. <i>(Mar 1, 2003,Dominion Post,F13</i>)

>  Uncle named him 'Taipo", which means black devil. <i>(1985,Hyacinths & Biscuits,147</i>)

>  When I arrived at the Koro Koro, I met another maori going after him; I asked him where he was going; he said, "to the hill:" I told him <i>napenap, te taipo haere mai;</i> he answered, he did not care about the <i>taipo</i>; I did not observe a dog with the white man; he had his clothes on; I believe he had on a short jacket, but did not take much notice. <i>(Dec 22, 1841,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)

>  <b>BARLOW</b> .. they were making the noises I heard to drive away `the Taipo,' a sort of devil who devotes his attention entirely to Maoris, over whom ... he only possesses power at night. <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,48</i>)

>  <b>COLENSO</b> Taepo means to come or visit by night, - a night visitant, - a spectral thing seen in dreams, - a fancied or feared thing, or hobgoblin, of the night or darkness; and this the settlers have construed to mean the Devil! - and of course their own orthodox one. <i>(1883,Three Literary Papers,5</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> <i>Taepo</i> `a goblin; a spectre' [cf. tae `arive'; po `night'] <i>(1891,Comparative Dictionary,7</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Taipo</i>, n.<i> Goblin. </i> NOTE. - This word is used by Maoris believing it English, and by Europeans believing it Maori, it being apparently neither. Colenso suggests <i>tae-po,</i> but this is not used by the Maori. <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,425</i>)

>  In Maori contexts `anything to be feared'; a devil, ghost, evil spirit etc.; Also figurative, a surveyors' theodolite'. [check this: ??paraphrase] <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/150</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words see also Morris for quotations 1848-1896]

>  Why? no one shall hurt you, they have no reason to do so, as the boy died a natural death. The evil spirit (or Taipo killed him). <i>Ibid.</i> 113 devil - ta|i|po [taipo] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,95,1986 ed.</i>)

>  `Why ? no one shall hurt you, they have no reason to do so, as the boy died a natural death' (or Taipo killed him). [FN] The evil spirit.[check if this or following cit to be used???] <i>(1979,Boultbee Journal,214,1979 Begg ed.</i>) [NOTES: See also p. 285]

>  Why? no one shall hurt you, they have no reason to do so, as the boy died a natural death. The evil spirit (or Taipo killed him <i>(1986,Journey of a Rambler,95</i>)

>  Why? no one shall hurt you, they have no reason to do so, as the boy died a natural death. The evil spirit (or Taipo killed him <i>(1986,Journey of a Rambler,95</i>)

>  <b>JAMES WATKINS</b> The inhabitants of this Island [sc. southern South Island] in common with some savages are very superstitious, their faith in the power of their priests is slavish, and all sickness is ascribed to supernatural or perhaps infernal agency. Taipo being the supposed author of the disease whatever it may be. Taipo is a foreign word, its native place and etymology I cannot trace, but as it appears to mean the Devil and is of universal use I shall not disturb it. <i>(Jun 5, 1840,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1840 June 5 James Watkins Journal in 1913 McNab O[ed fn 96: cites Watkin Journal 1840 June 5 QMS ATL; and Beattie's being told by aged Maori friends that it was `a whaler's word' (typhoid); Colenso Best JPS v30, 1921 p.48; Tregear]]

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> [The Maori] commenced chanting his Wesleyan missionary service, mixing ... special incantations to the <i>taipo</i> of the lake and river for propitious weather and easy fords ... [fn] Goblin; this word is used by Maoris believing it to be English, and by Europeans believing it Maori, it being neither. <i>(1846,Account of an Exploring Expedition</i>) [NOTES: 1846 Heaphy Expedition Southwest Nelson in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 195]

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> Proceeded this morning ... when Epike's old wife was suddenly missing. ... She said she had been struck by the <i>Taipo</i>, and did not know what she was doing until she came in sight of ... our last night's fire. <i>(1848,Account of an Exploring Expedition</i>) [NOTES: 1848 Brunner Expedition Middle Island in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 263]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [Ma.] 1. a saddle horse that is recalcitrant or badtempered, from the Maori ''devil.'' 2. an intimidating natural feature such as a river. 3. a native weta found in caves and native bush. 4. a jagged hill of many near to the Wairarapa coast at Te Wharau and near Tinui.

>  .. he rode the big chestnut - old Taipo we called him - and I rode the bay. <i>(1867,Station Ballads,47</i>) [NOTES: in MCKEE WRIGHT (1898)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Taipo .. a New Zealand word for devil, often applied by settlers to a vicious horse, or as a name for a dog. <i>(1898,Austral English,454</i>)

>  Just you look after that bucket for a few minutes and I'll ride the little 'taipo's' tail to Hades and back. <i>(Sep 17, 1901,Hawera Star,2</i>)

>  RYAN Orbis 26 355 taipo, a word often used by settlers for a vicious horse ... <i>(1977</i>)

>  <b>MCGILL</b> taipo .. a vicious horse ... 2. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Modern NZ Slang, A,111</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> Though the Taipo was still high, we were enabled to ascend along its banks. 3. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,116</i>)

>  They are such big dreadful-looking things; some call them taipos but the real name is weta. <i>(Oct 2, 1912,Otago Witness,77</i>)

>  <b>PICKERING</b> .. the ghostly cave weta, which can also live in forest and under stones. This cave version seems most deserving of the colonial name of wetas as taipos or devils. 4. <i>(1988,Hills, The,35</i>)

>  From here we climbed up into rougher country, on to the Taipos or Razorbacks, a sharp-edged rocky range about 2000 ft high. <i>(Feb 20, 1963,Weekly News,44</i>)

>  <b>FEARON</b> There were many Maoris living in this area in earlier times and there is a legend of a warrior who was banished to the wild taipos by his tribe. <i>(1980,Te Wharau,54</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a geological formation;a sharp-pointed, steep hill; a range of steep, sharply-serrated hills [Jim Cameron writes: This word is in DNZE but a more recent citation suggests a slightly enlarged meaning of sense 3c. <i>Wairarapa Branch Royal Forest & Bird Protection Society, Newsletter 1995</i> refers to "taipo cliffs" and "former taipo flora and geography". 1953 R. Mason quotes Map N162 Moore's taipo 412558 N162 Taipo minor Taipo is the name given to a sharply pointed steep hill in the eastern Wairarapa district.]

>  A hawk riding the thermal uplift circles its nest on the cliff edge of the middle taipo .. DOC has recorded the taipo as "three high pinnacles of indurated Cretaceous-sandstone .. The taipo are certainly the most prominent landmark on the skyline. There are other taipo peaks in this district. <i>(Mar 1, 2003,Dominion Post,F13</i>)

>  There are fantastic views of the taipos & valley from the sunny deck & sloping lawn. <i>(Mar 8, 2007,Property Brokers Newsletter,3</i>)

>  <b>Fearon</b> There were many Maoris living in this area in earlier times and there is a legend of a warrior who was banished to the wild taipos by his tribe. <i>(1980,Te Wharau,54</i>)

>  The Brocken Range is composed of a group of these `taipos', so-called by the Maoris, who held them in superstitious dread as the dwellings of evil spirits. <i>(1943,TrRSNZ,72 (4)/347</i>) [NOTES: (D.A.Brown, Brocken Range and the Kaiwhata Valley, East Wellington)]



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> weta, sawyer

>  They are such big, dreadful-looking things; some call them taipos but the real name is weta. <i>(Oct 2, 1912,Otago Witness,77</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>taipo</i> a weta; on the West Coast almost universal usage, says Glenn Johnston. <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,98</i>)

>  <i>Note on</i> Hemideina megacephala <i>Buller</i>. - These remarkable insects (the taipo, or weta, of the Maori) are generally said to feed only on vegetation. <i>(1932,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,13/146</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> With the exception of the Open Bay Island `Typos' (wetas) which are `swarming all over and appear to burrow in the stalks of the geigei ...' <i>(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas,81</i>) [NOTES: c1899 I.T.G. MS in 1957 Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas 81]

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> The very apogee of excitement would be reached when a `typo' was discovered. Especially if it happened to be a big fat male. <i>(1928,Dawn Beloved,47</i>) [NOTES: [reference West Coast]]

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> `A taipo. What is it really?' ... `It is really a weta.' <i>(1930,Bushman Burke,296</i>) [NOTES: [reference West Coast]]

>  <b>SARGESON</b> But the wetas came out at night and he laughed. The Maoris [in the Waikato] call them taipos. <i>(1946,That Summer,176</i>) [NOTES: alson in `Gods Live in Woods' in New Directions 9 296]

>  On the West Coast of the South Island, `taipo', or what is supposed to be the English translation of that word, `bush devil', is the name generally given to the common tree weta, Hemideina megacephala. `Weta' is the real Maori name for this and related species of large, long-horned wingless grasshoppers; `taipo' is a word of unknown origin and obscure meaning. <i>(Nov 8, 1957,Christchurch Star-Sun</i>) [NOTES: (I.W.T. Munro) could be Nov. 8 or 9]

>  On the West Coast of the South Island, `taipo', or what is supposed to be the English translation of that word, `bush devil', is the name generally given to the common tree weta, Hemideina megacephala. `Weta' is the real Maori name for this and related species of large, long-horned wingless grasshoppers; `taipo' is a word of unknown origin and obscure meaning. <i>(Nov 8, 1957,Christchurch Star-Sun</i>) [NOTES: (I.W.T. Munro) could be p. 8 or 9]

>  <b>PASCOE</b> Charlie always admitted that he hated, almost to the point of fear, two things: taipos (wetas) which he spells `typos', and those dark brown bush creeks the bottom of which is hard to see. <i>(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas,xii</i>) [NOTES: [reference West Coast] 1957 A.P. Harper in Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas xii]

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> The former [family Henicidae] includes the tree or ground wetas and the `taipos' of the West Coast of the South Island, the name of which to the Maori means `the devil who comes by night'. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/636</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> a train

>  <b>COWAN</b> Now the white man was coming and presently his iron rail and his steam `taepo' would lay the trail that was to conquer ... the mana of the Maori. <i>(1916,Bush Explorers,2</i>)



 
<b>5</b> <i>n.</i> esp. (?West Coast) a name for a (?dangerous) river (see also 1957 citation in 3. below: and get Haast etc. citations)

>  At Taipo, at Tata, at Takaka, at Aurere, at all points, in fact, of this extensive district, coal is known to abound, and by cropping out, as it does in several seams, at West Wanganui, or on the other western side of the coast, there can be scarcely a doubt but that the whole of that part of the island is a vast coal-field. <i>(May 25, 1850,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,50</i>)

>  The extent of flat land is considerable, chiefly wooded, and these districts are opened up for a short distance by their respective rivers; limestone is to be obtained in any quantity; coal at the Taipo, and Tata, at the Motupipi, and the Takaka. <i>(Sep 27, 1842,New Zealand Colonist & Port Nicholson Advertiser,2</i>)

>  A grass grown hill immediately behind Jackson's, not far west of the Otira mouth is Rangi Taepo (goblin sky), generally wrongly spelt Taipo. <i>(Jun 17, 1908,Grey River Argus,1</i>)

>  The following tenders have been received for the Taipo Bridge contract, Hokitika Christchurch road ... <i>(May 13, 1885,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> (West Coast) We afterwards learnt that the Maories had named this river [flowing into the Teremakau, Westland] `Typoo', which means the devil, and there certainly was a spice of devilry about its raging waters that suggested bad spirits in a perpetual state of frenzied agony <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,49</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> ... though the Taipo was still high, we were enabled to ascend along its banks ... <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,116</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>(1898,Austral English</i>)

>  <b>ROBERTS</b> Mr Wyatt was shepherd on the Taipo run, and lived with his wife ... in the Taipo whare, near to where Teschemaker's railway siding is now. <i>(1914,History of North Otago,26,1978 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1906 W.H.S.Roberts in 1914 History of North Otago (1978) 26]

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> This crossing [of the Papakiri] has always been known in my time as the `Taipo' - goblin-crossing, a name probably given because of a totara block which used to lie there hewn roughly to the similitude of a man's head. <i>(1926,Tutira,91</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> One of the shepherds ... lived in a hut at Taipo [Central Otago]. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,152</i>)

>  <b>VON HAAST</b> [On the Midland Railway to West Coast] ... numerous bridges were erected, the most remarkable of which was the bridge over the legendary Taipo [a branch of the Taramakau], in which many a tall fellow found his doom. <i>(1948,Julius Von Haast,437</i>)



 
<b>5</b> <i>n.</i> in exasperation, or humorous endearment, applied as an epithet, nickname, term of address, etc. (possibly shift 1 above here) [ca1935 J. Morison (Havelock)e.g. You little taipo, come here!]

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> Oh Mary, don't be a taipo. <i>(1930,Bushman Burke,300</i>) [NOTES: [West Coast reference]]



 
<b>5</b> <i>n.</i> a children's nickname, or abusive epithet, for an unattractive Maori [ca1935 Havelock Mungamunga taipo (or `tarpot'), probably from mangamanga. black. [Note that [wi:rou] also used in a similar context at that time and place: ?Maori <i>whiro</i> 1. evil, bad 2. the proper name of a malevolent atua, personification of evil, and of darkness and of death.]]

>  <b>BAKER</b> We ... use the doggerel Maori mungimungitaipo! as a ferocious expression. Taipo, it may be added, was noted by Morris as applied to `a surveyor's theodolite because it is a land-stealing devil,' ... <i>(1941,NZ Slang,56</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> ...shaking showers of white quince blossom down upon herself, she yelled, <i>`Maunga manga taipo!'</i> at Mauri in an unreasoning, provocative impudence, but he did not mind being called a black devil and cuffed good-naturedly at her head with a big brown paw. <i>(1951,Witch's Thorn, The,127</i>)

>  ... the happiest days of our lives. Young white gods yelling <i>mangumangu taipo!</i> without knowing what it meant, only that it was something to be yelled at the Maori kids in the swamps on Sunday afternoon. <i>Mangumangu taipo</i>, nigger-man devils! And it didn't much matter that you yourself weren't a Maori, ... <i>(Jul 1954,Numbers,1/4</i>) [NOTES: (Mincher)]



 
<b>5</b> <i>n.</i> ?from a rough temper, or ? a rough frightening exterior; or merely as a <i>bushman</i>, humorously from <i>taipo</i>, the tree weta. Possibly, however, <i>whero (i) or (ii), but not likely) </i>

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> [West Coast reference] ... nicknamed Taipo Burke. <i>(1930,Bushman Burke,12</i>)



 
<b>5</b> <i>n.</i> usually from the primary Maori sense `devil; ghost' figuratively used, or transferred to `a fearsome person or thing' (e.g., a weta); thence in various weakened uses, as a nickname (possibly an `Old Nick' name) mainly for animals, in familiar address to (usually naughty) children pron [|taipou] spg occasionally <i>typo</i>

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Taipo</i>, for instance, of course on knows its meaning, though it has been adopted chiefly as a name as common a `Dash' or `Nero' for New Zealand dogs. ... Often applied by settlers to a vicious horse. <i>(1898,Austral English</i>)

>  <i>taipo n</i>. the devil; a vicious dog or horse; sl. the theodolite of the surveyor, because it is thought to rob the natives of their land. [Maori tae, come, and po, night=a spectre of the darkness] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,11</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> `Now for the old girls,' he says, and Taipo yaps with joy. Uncle Ted heads for the cow paddock ... <i>(1948,Tidal Creek,28,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> `Don't mind Taipo', she said as she ushered me in. I froze. ...`What the devil is it?' She chuckled. `A Beardie'. `Does it bite?' ... [123] I had said it before. Taipo meant devil, or goblin. He was some kind of distant cousin of an Old English Sheepdog. <i>(1985,G'day Country,122</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>taipo</i> dark dog; kiwi colloquialism from mid-C19 and extant (Ref. guard dog at Fyffe House, Kaikoura); or a vicious horse. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,111</i>)



 
<b>6</b> <i>n.</i> [ca1956 W.J. Orsman popular New Zealand name for the game of <i>Bagatelle. </i>; a NZ made table game (1930s-40s)using ball bearings and enclosures marked out with steel pins (DEH]

### taipo-master
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  An old Maori chief... approached Mr Crawford [sc. an engineer] with great reverence, saying "You taipo-master?" meaning thereby to ascertain whether Mr. Crawford could work and control the machinery. <i>(Oct 28, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

### Tairoa trousers
 
 <i>n.</i> [Probably misspelling of Tairua on the Coromandel. More likely than Taiaroa on the Otago heads or a non-existent Tairoa, especially since it was apparently a prime gold-mining site.]

>  2 pairs Drill or Tairoa Trousers (state size). <i>(Nov 11, 1893,Northern Advocate,4</i>)

>  Tairoas.. are a sort of white drill trousers affected by outdoor workers in summer time. <i>(Oct 10, 1917,Te Aroha Times,2</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> The customary dress was the old Blucher boots... and Tairoa trousers, which were made of a kind of heavy white duck; these were tied with bowyangs below the knees. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,155</i>)

### Taitai
 
 <i>n.</i> (pron) early pron. of Taita

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> Now they were passing the little cemetery at Taitai ... <i>(1934,Winds of Heaven,43</i>)

### taiwharu
 
 <i>n.</i> kokopu

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Galaxias brevipinnis</i> Guenther. GUDGEON; TAIWHARU or KOKOPU. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,13,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Galaxias brevipinnis</i>: [European Name] `Gudgeon' [Maori Name] Taiwharu or kokopu. <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,73</i>) [NOTES: 1936 Handbook for NZ (ANZAAS Conf. Jan. 1937) 73]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> GUDGEON <i>Galaxias brevipinnis</i> Gunther ... This is the taiwharu of North Auckland and the kokopu of the Arawa tribe who also call the young (together with the young of Retropinna retropinna) inanga in Lakes Rotorua, Rotoiti and Taupo. <i>(1940,Fishes NZ,21</i>)

### taiwhenua
 
 <i>n.</i> [ORIGIN: Maori, = land, district (P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 233)]

>  As you'll know, due to the collapse of the Wairarapa Taiwhenua - we haven't had an umbrella organisation representing Ngati Kahungunu. <i>(Jul 26, 2005,Wairarapa Midweek,32</i>)

### Taj Mahal
 
 <i>n.</i> a men's convenience in Courtenay Place, Wellington, later converted to restaurant and later again to a bar, often abbrev. to TAJ, pron [ta:d3] [ca1941 at least [LOST IDENTITY] ?66-67 Never mind that ... it earned the title of the Taj Mahal of Amnesty Cove only from those people who had really [67] been caught short there.]

>  A recent example of such a demonstration was the attempt by a number of students to drink the Taj Mahal dry. <i>(1966,Cappicade,9</i>)

>  <i>the Taj Mahal</i>: Wellington public toilet <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <b>LAY</b> And when she rode past the propositions and obscenities would have covered the walls of the Taj Mahal. <i>(1988,Fools on the Hill, The,85</i>)

>  The bar sits on an island between Kent and Cambridge Terraces, in a building still affectionately known to many Wellingtonians as "Taj Mahal". <i>(Nov 2, 2000,City Voice,16</i>)

### takahe
 
 <i>n.</i>



 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) also called notornis a rare, flightless, native bird. [Maori takahe[e-macron]] [[Ma. <b>takah&#275;</b> (<i>Notornis Mantelli</i>), flightless gallinule] cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 233.]

>  Maud the mauled takahe is on the way back .. Takahe are territorial and trouble for Maude started when her partner Albert died. <i>(Apr 3, 2000,Evening Post,14</i>)

>  The skull of a notornis, or takahe, a native bird which has not been seen since the end of last century, has been found at Whakaputu, by the director of the Southland Museum, Mr. J. H. Sorenson. <i>(Nov 16, 1938,Evening Post,20</i>)

>  The takahe .. is a true grassland bird, plucking tussock tillers and seeds for food and laying eggs in a raised bowl of grasses under the shelter of tussock leaves. <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/47</i>)

>  'A very large step for the takahe,' (for they have very big feet), "a giant leap for conservation." <i>(1991,NZ Geographic,10</i>)

>  This bird was called Takahe, and was of a black colour, destitute of wings, and had a long bill, which, as well as the legs, was of a bright red colour. <i>(Jul 23, 1851,New Zealander, The,4</i>)

>  A nearly complete skeleton of the famous takahe was found a few years ago not far from here [sc. Lake Te Anau). <i>(Mar 6, 1890,Otago Witness,35</i>)

>  A nearly complete skeleton of the famous takahe was found a few years ago not far from here [sc. Lake Te Anau). <i>(Mar 6, 1890,Otago Witness,35</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> The Takahe (<i>Notornis Mantelli</i>), a bird about the size of a turkey, with imperfect wings, was discovered in Dusky Bay, in the Middle Island, in 1850, by sealers. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/27</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> NOTORNIS MANTELLI. (MANTELL'S NOTORNIS.) .. <i>Native names</i>. Moho, Takahe, and Tokohea. [191] Mr. Walter Mantell states that .. It was known to the North-Islanders by the name of `Moho', and to the South-Islanders by that of `Takahe'. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,189, 191,{1888 2nd edn 2/85} ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> However, nothing was known of the exact habitat of the Takahe, (Notornis) till .. Grey .. obtained the information. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,449</i>)

>  I was informed .. that the skeleton of a Takahe had been found .. near lake Te Anau .. As is well known, the only recent remains of <i>Notornis</i> hitherto obtained are the two stuffed specimens in the British Museum ... <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/78</i>)

>  <i>Notornis mantelli.</i> - Takahe; Moho. Resolution and Secretary Islands; and at Barepatch, between Maruia and Upokororo Rivers, east of Lake Te Anau. <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/113</i>) [NOTES: (Martin)]

>  By a strange ... coincidence, the first two specimens of the <i>Notornis,</i> or <i>takahe </i>as it was called in the South island, were secured ... <i>(1889,TrNZI,21/226</i>)

>  <b>PARKER</b> The Takahe is the rarest of existing native birds, if indeed it is not already extinct. <i>(1889,Catalogue NZ Exhibition,116</i>) [NOTES: (Morris)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Takahe, n.</i> Maori name for an extinct New Zealand Rail, <i>Notoris mantelli,</i> Owen. See<i> Notornis. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,455</i>)

>  <i>Notornis hochstetteri</i> Meyer. Takahe. One of these birds was reported to have been seen in full view on a track near Lake Te Anau so recently as 1914. <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/81</i>) [NOTES: Myers Endemic Birds]

>  <i> takahe</i>, a bird (Notornis hochstetteri), first found as a fossil (N. mantelli) by W. B. D. Mantell: found alive only six times in over 100 years, and confined to the rugged south-west corner of the South Island. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/150</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>POWELL</b> The Takahe (<i>Notornis hochstetteri</i>), long believed extinct ... <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,82</i>)

>  <i>takahe</i>: extinct bird <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,176</i>)

>  The Maori name for a large kind of bird of the rail family (notornis) were found again in 1948 by a party led by Dr Orbell in the Lake Te Anau district. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),404</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> <i>takahe</i> Small flightless bird (Notornis hochstetteri) <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,302</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> On being informed the prisoner had been killed, Tai Kawa said the Ngatimomoe would be banished into [293] the haunts of the Takahe and would only be safe in the depths of the forest. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,291</i>)

>  <i>takahe n</i> -s [Maori] : NOTORNIS 2 <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,2329</i>)

>  <b>DURRELL</b> <i>(1966,Two in the Bush,103</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> TAKAHE <i>Notornis mantelli Other name</i>: Moho. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,109</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> <i>takahe</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <i>takahe n</i>. see notornis <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),626</i>)

>  <b>168</b> <i>(1966,English as it is spoken in NZ</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> The takahe, as every New Zealand schoolboy knows, was thought to be extinct, until <i>Notornis mantelli</i>, rediscovered in 1948 to the west of Te Anau .. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,268</i>)

>  <i>takahe</i> .. n. See notornis. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),19</i>)

>  <i>138. Notornis mantelli</i> Owen, 1848 <i>Notornis (Takahe) </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,43,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>takahe</i> n. a rare flightless New Zealand rail. Also called: <i>notornis</i>. [< Maori] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,119</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 782; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 490]]

>  <i>Takahe Notornis mantelli</i> OWEN, 1848 .. OTHER NAMES: <i>Moho, pass bird.</i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,170</i>)

>  <i>My first note refers to the</i> Notornis mantelli, <i>the rara avis</i> called by the Maoris the <i>`takahea'.</i> The name of this bird is spelled <i>`takahe'</i> in Buller's `Birds' ... The Maoris inform me that this is wrong; there should be a final <i>a,</i> as I have spelt it here. <i>(1906,TrNZI,38/337</i>)

>  first appears in 1917 5th edn 428 (MOHO 1. a bird. appears in 3rd 1871, 85, and 4th 1892, 99) <i>Takahee</i> (ii), <i>takahea|</i>, n. <i>Notornis hochstetteri</i>; a large and rare species of rail. = <i>moho.</i> <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,428</i>) [NOTES: 1954Williams[1957/434 ... a large and rare flightless bird closely related to the pukeko.]]

>  There are just 260 surviving takahe left in the world. <i>(Jun 15, 2006,NZ Herald,A8</i>)

>  We watched nectar-eating stitchbirds visiting wayside feeders, a tui taking a bath and a shy takahe fossicking in long grass. <i>(Sep 26, 2004,Sunday Magazine (Auckland),38</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [Ma.] a New Zealand-bred cultivar of wheat.

>  Tiritea grains are compared with takahe. In trials in Southland, the new wheat cultivar yielded 6.25 tonnes per hectare, which was more than 7 percent more than what Takahe yielded. <i>(May 1982,NZ Journal of Agriculture,23</i>)

### takahe man
 
 <i>n.</i> ?an old-timer, one who prefers the old ways

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> Beneath the new order the takahe men still lived on, diminishing in quiet, unfettered contempt, unaware that they were no longer typical. Ibid. 20 Passion and prejudice were strong among the takahe men: reticence an unconscious disguise. <i>(1984,Day Hemingway Died, The,16</i>)

### takahea
 
 <i>n.</i> variant rather than a spg of takahe

>  <b>COWAN</b> These men in their young days made periodical [122] expeditions up into the lakes region, hunting for the <i>weka</i> ..., the <i>kakapo</i> or ground parrot,<i> kiwi</i>, and the rare and elusive <i>takahea,</i> the blue-plumaged notornis which is now believed to be extinct. 133 [caption] The <i>takahea</i> or Notornis Hochstetteri ... <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol II South Island) 121</i>)

### takahi
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>COWAN</b> No doubt they would have been discovered had the Arawa from Rotorua <i>takahi'd,</i> or trodden down, the fern, as was the practice in cases of the kind, but [they] carefully refrained from doing so, knowing doubtless that some of their unfortunate kinsmen lay trembling there. <i>(1922,Travel in NZ,2/170,1983 ed.</i>)

### takahikahi
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DIFFENBACH</b> <i>47. Charadrius xanthocheilus. Wagl.</i> ... Tuturiwhatu of the natives. <i>Miss Stone</i>.Takahikaki[check?] of the natives. <i>Yate. Tuturuata</i> of the natives of Cook's Straits. <i>Dieffenbach. </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/`195</i>)

### takahikaremoana
 
 <i>n.</i> storm petrel

>  <b>SALE</b> ... [The storm petrel] has many other names - skipjack, Dancing Dolly, frigate petrel - from the Pakeha; and takahikaremoana from the Maori. <i>(1982,Four Seasons,38</i>)

### Takaka marble
 
 <i>n.</i>

This link comes from one of the house's previous owners, Leonard Mills, who was a partner in.. the contracting firm that built Parliament Buildings using kairuru marble from its Tasman Bay quarry. <i>(Apr 10, 1999,Dominion,28</i>)

### takakau
 
 <i>n.</i> flat unleavened bread

>  He used to help his parents, who were staunch Ratana followers, to gather kumara, bake fried bread and takakau (a flat, unleavened bread) to sell and raise funds for Labour. <i>(Jun 1996,Mana,No.13: 25</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> SOME MAORI WORDS ... <i>takakau</i> hard bread (`wagon-wheels') <i>(1963,Piece of Land, A,192</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> Knives and forks were passed around [by the Maori family], and a plate of buttered takakau. <i>(1969,Green River,29</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> ... the remains of a meal: mussel shells, bones of fish-heads sucked clean, pork bones, stalks of watercress, and a few crusts of takakau. <i>(1976,Send Somebody Nice,154</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> Takakau, also called WAGON WHEELS a flour-and-water dough, no leaven, rolled out flat and baked in the oven; a sort of oven-damper. A good maori dish to feed a crowd. <i>(Sep 1988,Personal Communication</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> <i>Takapu (tah-kah-poo)</i>: The gannet. <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,186</i>)

>  Takapu <i>n.</i> the common gannet. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,11</i>)

>  <i>takapu</i>, a bird (Moris serrator), gannet. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/157</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>POWELL</b> GANNET (<i>Morus serrator</i>), Takapu of the Maoris, is common around the coasts of the North Island. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,79</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> AUSTRALIAN GANNET <i>Sula serrator Local name</i>: Takapu. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,60</i>)

>  <i>takapu ... n</i>. in N.Z., a gannet [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),19</i>)

>  <i>76. Sula bassana serrator </i>Gray, 1843 <i>Austral[as]ian Gannet (Takapu) </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,31,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Australasian Gannet Sula bassana serrator </i>GRAY, 1843 ... OTHER NAMES:<i> Takapu, Australian gannet, solan goose. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,110</i>)

>  <i>takapu n.</i> NZ name for an Australian variety of gannet. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,782</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

### Takapuna oyster soup
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>TAKAPUNA OYSTER SOUP </i>... milk ... onion ... pepper ... fry ... flour ... Allow the whole to boil ... Put in the oysters and simmer ... <i>(1913,Australasian Cookery Book, The,213</i>)

### Takapuna Russet
 
 <i>n.</i> a variety of apple

>  I was quite aware of the origin of the Takapuna Russet, having some years ago seen the tree (Reinette du Canada) that threw out the sport from one of its branches. <i>(Sep 17, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,28</i>)

### Takapuna surprise
 
 <i>n.</i> a steak stuffed with oysters

>  <i>TAKAPUNA SURPRISE</i> top-side steak ... Prepare a forcemeat ... oysters ... sweet herbs ... cayenne pepper ... breadcrumbs ... fill the steak with the mixture ... Sew up ... slices of bacon over the top, and bake ... <i>(1913,Australasian Cookery Book, The,213</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>Takapuna surprise</i> steak stuffed with oysters. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,111</i>)

### Takapuna/Takapuna class
 
 <i>n.</i> a former (class of) small sailboat, designed in Takapuna; aka Z class, Zeddie

>  All the clubs have added a race for the new Takapuna one-design class... The first boat is now ready for launching... Some difficulty is being experienced in getting the sails, owing to a shortage of the necessary silk cloth. <i>(Oct 29, 1921,Auckland  Star,18</i>)

>  The latest class, the Takapuna, 12 ft 6 in flatties, are very handy little craft, and ideal for racing or knocking about the sheltered bays and beaches of Takapuna. <i>(Nov 26, 1921,Auckland  Star,18</i>)

>  <b>AVERY</b> &#65279;I'm going to.. Drink a handle or a schooner as I tack my Takapuna. <i>(1974,Ken Avery Songbook, The,Unpaged</i>)

>  <b>CARTER</b> Next to the X class. is the Z class, or what are often referred to as the Cornwall Cup boats. They owe their existence to a small band of Takapuna yachtsmen who planned the first boat, with much the same idea in their minds as did George Honour when he built the first of the 14-foot square bilge boats, namely, low first cost, ease of construction, and an ever-watchful eye on speed and performance under Auckland harbour weather conditions. The yachts were originally designed for the Takapuna Boating Club but owing to their popularity from the outset the type was quickly adopted by many other yacht clubs throughout New Zealand... From small beginnings the Takapuna one-design class rapidly expanded. <i>(1944,Little Ships: NZ's Yachting Fleet,127</i>)

>  <b>TITCHENER</b> A good example.. was the design of the famous but ill-fated Z-class, which became known as the Takapuna class throughout New Zealand. <i>(1978,Little Ships of NZ,64</i>) [NOTES: [Specifications - 3.8 m yacht, designed by R B Brown of Northcote. 1.5m beam,m square chine vee-bottom principle. The whole of the sailing area carried on a gunter mainsail, on a short mast forward, no bowsprit or headsail]]

>  <b>DAVIS</b> The Z class, or "Zeddie" as it was affectionately known, was another class which was designed as a low cost sailing boat to get the boys into something safer than the tin canoes or derelict dinghies in which they cruised Shoal Bay. Ibid. 114 The Takapuna Boating Club which was then situated at Bayswater, was the sponsoring club and the yachts were often referred to as the "Takapuna Class". <i>(1989,Kohimarama,113</i>)

>  <b>MANDER & O'NEILL</b> On the other hand to be in a fleet of Takapunas and come about the weather mark, in a cloud of blossoming spinnakers and fly away before the breeze in a surge of foam was a roundabout which made up for the swing. <i>(1964,Give a Man a Boat,35</i>)

 <i>n.</i> hoiho

 <i>adj.</i> [may yet prove to be other than NZ lexeme, one further citation found at Australaian web site: ... ash. ### takatapui
 
 <i>n.</i> a homosexual [ORIGIN: Maori, http://www.learningmedia.co.nz/nz/online/ngata/m2edictionary]

>  Yes there are deep seeded [sic] issues about Takatapui/tootika/taerarua in every society/community/religon [sic] and all of these issues need to be addressed in a forum such as this ... <i>(Dec 11, 2007,Internet - see Notes</i>)

>  Every four days one gay, takataapui or bisexual man in New Zealand is being diagnosed with HIV. <i>(2006,Jack,issue 4 Dec 2005 / Jan 2006, p31</i>)

>  In the first half of this year, one gay, takataapui or bisexual man in New Zealand was diagnosed with HIV every four days. <i>(Nov 2005,Express (Gay & Lesbian Express),16</i>)

>  The inclusion of the world takataapui in William's nineteenth century dictionary, and the story that accompanies its presence there, alerts us to the fact there is indeed another reading. <i>(Aug 24, 2005,Express (Gay & Lesbian Express),4</i>)

>  .. But still I would like to see more takatapui online and in life ... <i>(Jan 24, 2008,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: www.maori.org.nz/papa_panui/forum_topic.asp?]

 <i>n.</i> matters to do with homosexuality [ORIGIN: Maori]

### takawaru
 
 <i>n.</i> a fish

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> T|aka w|adu, <i>ss</i>. A fish so called. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,206</i>)

### take
 
 <i>n.</i> [(muttonbirding)]

>  ... but this presumption has been rebutted by the admission that he had no `take', therefore he has no right to this island. <i>(Aug 1911,Gazette</i>) [NOTES: 1911 Aug Gazette in 1979 Wilson Titi Heritage 41]

>  <b>WILSON</b> `Take', and all references thereto, include taking, catching, killing, or pursuing, by any means or device, ... <i>(1979,Titi Heritage,171</i>) [NOTES: 1912 Regulations under Land Act in 1979 Wilson Titi Heritage 171]

>  <b>WILSON</b> Down the generations it had been observed that this happened about the same time each year, so the wise old chiefs set the season for the `take' for that certain time of the year which became known to the pakeha as half of March, all of April and May, allowing no trespassing on the island at any other time of the year lest the birds, refusing to nest on an island `touched' by human presence, should fly away and never return. <i>Ibid.</i> 27 Especially when the pakeha took a Maori wife; he could then share her journey to her island, and help her to `take' enough birds for their family needs. <i>Ibid.</i> 39 Some of these descendants actually visited the disputed islands and attempted to `take'. <.i>Ibid.</i> 55-56 Ever since the beginning, to `take' meant journeying to the islands and living there for the season and using hands or any device to catch the birds. ... to `take' also includes an `attempt to take'. During the torching season the birds run down the paths to the sea at night. Paths are used as boundaries by the [56] supervisors and in some cases the dividing line is the middle of the path. If one pursues a bird and grabs it over the other edge of the path one can be prosecuted, even although the bird came from the pursuer's manu. <i>Ibid.,</i> 66 In the season 1921 the Colac Bay and Riverton Maoris caught 24,000 birds, In 1922 the `take' was 14,000. <i>(1979,Titi Heritage,25</i>)



 
 (two syllables) <i>n.</i> purpose, cause [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  The academy's <i>take</i> (purpose) is about changing kids' attitudes in order to make them more employable... Rangihuna.. is passionate about the academy's real <i>take</i> and frustrated by the fixation on its sporting success. <i>(Oct 1997,North & South,77</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> Now he arranged with General Cameron to reoccupy the European land at Tataraimaka despite the repeated Maori warnings that they would regard this action as a <i>take</i>, a just cause for war. <i>Ibid.</i> <i>take</i> Root, cause <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,134</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [land-breaking] the germination and growth of a plant crop.

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> Between the isolated plants of the miserable 'take' of seed there was ample space left for the germination of undesirables. <i>(1921,Tutira,165</i>)

>  <b>TAIT</b> The grass seed being sown in the ashes as soon as possible after the burn to ensure an early take of grass with the first rains. <i>(1955,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol I,131</i>)

>  <b>GASCOIGNE</b> Either from this factor or an indifferent grass "take" much of this large paddock had always been disappointing and now quite obviously was in poor shape. <i>(1965,From the Top of a Hill,129</i>)

### take (up) the burnt chops
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) to go mustering.

>  <b>BAKER</b> Here a few [farming terms] that appear to have originated this century: to take the burnt chops (said of a person) to take on work as a musterer. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,54</i>)

>  In the old days, they used to say that a man who had gone mustering had 'taken up the burnt chops.' <i>(Nov 15, 1983,NZ Journal of Agriculture,20</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> These [mustering] gangs were made up of men who'd taken up the burnt chops, ate 'hockey sticks', wore 'Bill Masseys' slept in a 'scratcher' and rode pigskin ... <i>(1983,Other Side of Farming, The,1</i>)

>  .. while mustering they are doing a dog job, taking up the burnt chops or bringing in the wool. <i>(Nov 22, 2002,Dominion Post,C6</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i> Take the burnt chops.</i>. To work as a musterer of <i> sheep:</i>. New Zealand rural: C. 20. B., 1941. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1194</i>)

>  <i> burnt chops, to take the (Phr.)</i>. take on work as a muster. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),5</i>)

### take a beat
 
<b>1</b> <i>phr.</i> (Rural) to have a position in a mustering gang.

>  <b>MACNICOL</b> Whether working in the shed, 'taking a 'beat'on the hill, or simply washing dishes, Jim was enjoying himself. <i>(1965,Beyond the Skippers Road,146</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Jack Wilson first mustered at Mesopotamia in the early years of the First World War and he was still taking a beat during World War II. <i>(1975,Sixty Thousand on the Hoof,19</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Ian at nearer seventy than sixty is still taking a beat on walking country .. that autumn muster he took the outside beat right over the top. <i>(1975,Sixty Thousand on the Hoof,112</i>)

### take a cut
 
<b>1</b> <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [stock management] to move into a mob of stock and take out particular animals.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> to take a cut: to take out this portion. It needs a good man on a good horse to cut cows from their calves at foot, and, an even more delicate operation, to take a cut of ewes without mismothering them from their lambs. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,23</i>)

### take a fit
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>THATCHER</b> Tell him to take a fit and `bust,' / Ask him if he gets his boots on trust, / And you'll be a leary boy. <i>(1875,NZ Songster,No.5/63</i>) [NOTES: The Leary Boy]

### take a jerry
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  Halfway through the piece he [sc. the Premier] "took a jerry", and in his hasty endeavour to remove the offending bowler, he dropped it. <i>(Aug 2, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,V/49 : 5</i>)

### take a powder
 
 <i>phr.</i> to escape

>  <b>MacKENZIE</b> <i>powder</i> to take a powder, to escape <i>(1980,While We Have Prisons,98</i>)

### take a pull
 
 <i>phr.</i> to pull oneself together: often in imper. stop! [cf. P: `desist, discontinue': Aust: 20c: In imper. occasionally: Take a pull on oneself!]

>  On again, then through Napier, the Patea country, in and out till I came to Maraekakaho station: when I took a pull I was by this time heartily sick of the game, and made up my mind to give it best, and came into Hastings. <i>(Jan 7, 1911,NZ Truth,289/4/5</i>)

>  <b>HAUGHEY</b> Look here -, it's about time you took a pull. Just shake yourself up a bit quick. <i>(1946,Railway Reminiscences,21</i>)

>  <i>take a pull</i> (slang phrase) to reform (oneself). <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),626</i>)

>  <i>take a pull (Sl. phr.) </i>to reform (oneself). <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),19</i>)

>  His face was clear, his eyes were bright, and he had evidently been taking a pull at himself, as the horsey men say. <i>(Feb 10, 1875,Thames Star,3</i>)

>  When you feel anyhow and life seems just one blessed thing after another, don't get dispirited. Take a pull on yourself. <i>(Sep 20, 1924,Evening Post,21</i>)

>  "We have got to take a pull on these street collections; the public is getting sick of them," remarked Councillor W. Appleton. <i>(Aug 16, 1935,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  It is time the sprinter took a pull and kept away from the store. <i>(Jan 23, 1897,Observer (Auckland),22</i>)

>  'Come, now, take a pull! You're coming too strong! ...' <i>(Nov 21, 1901,West Coast Times,4</i>)

>  IT is about time that Lord Charles Beresofrd [sic] should either take a pull on his unbridled tongue or be made to prove some of the wild assertions that he has been making of late in order to show that the Navy is and has been neglected. <i>(Jan 29, 1910,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  It is time New Zealand took a pull at herself in the matter of football. [i.e., people are mad over football and should calm down] <i>(Sep 15, 1906,NZ Truth,65/4/2</i>)

### take a shingle off
 
 <i>phr.</i> to become angry or offended; to hit the roof, to blow one's top

>  He takes his offers too in such a manner as he would make you believe he really don't want a drink, but he "doesn't like to take a shingle off." <i>(Apr 26, 1873,Taranaki Herald,5</i>)

>  There had been a raffle for a horse, and as the winner was going out to lead him away the landlord of the bush hotel, always keen for business, said, "Shure'n you'll shout for all hands after that lump o' luck; you're not the wan to take a shingle off a poor man's roof, I'll be bound." <i>(Oct 26, 1894,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> Gilbert, thinking it best to be civil, thanked the [digger], but declined his offer [of a shout]. `You must not take a shingle off,' said the landlord addressing him, `that won't do.' <i>(1881,Waitaruna,132</i>)

### take a tumble to
 
 <i>phr.</i> to understand something suddenly [ca1945 Wellington, Victoria University students]

>  (<i>Slang phrase</i>). to realise suddenly, to become aware of. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),626</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Although, he said, he was the white-haired boy with the trump he was due for a shake-up and he had to take a tumble to himself as to how he gave the company-rations away. <i>Ibid.</i> 288 Hey, turn it up and stop all those gloomy forecasts or you will find that I can go very hostile, and it's time we all took a tumble to ourselves and discussed something pleasant, say a little party. <i>(1935,Slang Today,287</i>) [NOTES: A.E. Strong WW1 scene in Partridge Slang Today 287]

>  <i>(1979,Heinemann NZ Dictionary</i>)

>  <i>tumble ... 3 take a tumble to NZ & Austral. sl.</i> understand suddenly. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,829</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 1261; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 516]]

>  ".. There have been too many ducks kissing and slobbering over me for the last five years, and I think it's time to take a tumble and get a sure grip on one." <i>(Oct 16, 1886,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  And then "I took a tumble" - the Opera House is to be in the same street, and on the same section of ground, so the adjoining buildings - even if some little distance away - must be in keeping, outwardly at any rate, with our up-to-date Opera House. <i>(May 17, 1899,Wanganui Chronicle,2</i>)

>  Taking a line through Saturday's performances at Miramar, a few of our senior players ought to take a tumble to themselves and give the game best. <i>(Jun 6, 1908,NZ Truth,3</i>)

>  By-and-by they take a tumble. They begin to see that there is nothing humanitarian about the methods of these humbugs. <i>(Feb 8, 1913,NZ Truth,8</i>)

>  "Cambist" hopes that this review will enable shareholders to "take a tumble" to the need for a complete overhaul of affairs. <i>(Jun 20, 1925,NZ Truth,17</i>)

### take down
 
 <i>v.</i> to beat (not necessarily by cheating) ; in various uses deriving from an original sense <i>swindle</i>

>  <i>take down v.tr.</i> cheat, deceive. <i>take down n. </i>a deception, fraud. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),626</i>)

>  <i>take down v.t.</i> cheat, deceive. <i>take down n.</i> a deception, fraud. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),19</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> to cost [ca1952 H.S. Gajadhar, Wellington My holiday took me down for twenty pounds.]

>  <b>COOZE</b> A doughy concoction labelled cake `took us down' for seven and six. <i>(1944,Kiwis in the Pacific,20</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>v.</i> ?take down from where they are hanging

>  <b>BOWEN</b> As there are several different types of handpieces there is not space to set out how each of them should be taken down and experted. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,79</i>)

### take it away
 
 <i>phr.</i> to begin

>  <b>MASON</b> GUEST 1: ... Give us a haka, Johnny. JOHNNY: Aw, now. Don't feel like it, eh. GUEST 2: Yes! Ka mate, ka mate! Take it away, boy! <i>(1963,Pohutukawa Tree,45</i>)

### take it on the toe
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  I recognise this bloke as a dumper, so I take it on the toe out the gate and up the frog and toad. to take it on the toe....to run <i>(May 21, 1963,NZ Truth,19</i>)

### take on
 
 <i>v.</i> to engage in a fight; to challenge to a fight [ca 1935]

<b>1</b> <i>phr.</i> (Rural) to work as a shearer.

### take one's coat off
 
 <i>phr.</i> get serious, get down to hard work
