Counsel acknowledged that he didn't think that the expression `take your coat off' referred to immorality. Crown Prosecutor Russell: `It is a very hearty expression.' <i>(Oct 3, 1908,NZ Truth,172/5/4</i>)

### take one's hook
 
 <i>phr.</i> to leave, get away (from) (cf. sling one's hook)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Take one's hook</i>. To decamp; run away: New Zealanders': C. 20. Ex <i>sling one's hook.</i> <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,862</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Fritz landed a daisy-cutter and the transport driver done his block and took his hook. <i>(1935,Slang Today,287</i>)

>  <i>(Apr 4, 1938,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: 1938 McNab NZ slang [Slang of the N.Z.E.F.]]

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> `I certainly wish I could take my hook from everybody in Gladstone' she said. <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The,132</i>)

>  ... not staying on here if you all take your hook. <i>(1948,Voices I,7</i>) [NOTES: 1948 Voices I (ed Martin) (D Ballantyne)]

### take one's horse for a gallop
 
 <i>phr.</i> to urinate

>  take your horse for a gallop: urinate <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

### take out the back teeth
 
 <i>phr.</i> to neuter (a cat)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>take out the back teeth</i> neutering the domestic tomcat. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,111</i>)

### take over
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to inherit, occupy or buy land from a former owner or occupier.

>  When the farm was taken over ten years ago, the 200 acres in grass supported browntop and rough feed. <i>(May 1, 1945,NZ Dairy Exporter,14</i>)

>  Because this man had recently taken over the farm and lacked the finance to purchase feed, the herd was faced with virtual starvation over the winter-spring period. <i>(Feb 2, 1953,NZ Dairy Exporter,39</i>)

>  <b>TAIT</b> The youngest son took over the property. <i>(1956,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol I,36</i>)

>  <b>TAIT</b> The present owner took over the farm when his father died. <i>(1956,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol I,40</i>)

>  They took over the farm from their father, Mr John Searle. <i>(Jun 10, 1958,NZ Dairy Exporter,28</i>)

>  Before Tom took over, the farm's top production season was 50,200 lb of fat from about 150 cows. <i>(Nov 11, 1963,NZ Dairy Exporter,4</i>)

### take the back track/back trail
 
 <i>phr.</i> to retrace one's steps; occas. to retire (from a difficult situation).

>  <b>WHITWORTH</b> There was nothing for it but to turn back ... so I determined to take the back track as soon as possible. <i>(1870,Martin's Bay Settlement,34</i>)

>  <b>ANZAC</b> Our little party now came to the conclusion that it was time to take the back trail. <i>(1916,On Anzac Trail,105</i>)

>  <i>(Apr 19, 1971,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>) [NOTES: see bushed]

### take the blanket
 
 <i>phr.</i> of a Pakeha, to live as a Maori

>  Then came the agony between a young European wearing the tartan name of Stuart Mclvor and his erring half-caste Maori missus called by the festive name of Pehipa, who has, to use a current term, returned `to the blanket' in preference to Stuart's manly buzzim. <i>(Aug 17, 1912,NZ Truth,373/5/7</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Sometimes renegade white men joined with the Maoris in the ceremonies around the <i>niu</i>. One of these runaways from civilization who had `taken the blanket' ... <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/14</i>)

### take the day off to carry bricks
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>take the day off to carry bricks</i> a working holiday. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,111</i>)

### take the dog collar
 
 <i>phr.</i> (obsolete) take the prize, the cake

>  ... we find in proportion to population ... New Zealand takes the dog collar [for incidence of lunacy]. <i>(May 19, 1906,NZ Truth,48/6/6</i>)

### take the gate
 
<b>1</b> <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [stock-drafting] to operate the drafting-gates in a race.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> Oddly enough, though he [the drafter/buyer] may handle them expertly he rarely bothers to take the gate. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,28</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> gate: to take the gate: to operate the gates of a drafting race. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,34</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> take the gate: to do the actual handling of the drafting gates. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,83</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> <i>(1933,Press, The</i>)

### take the iced biscuit
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  For right down, cool, blasted cheek the announcement of the New Zealand selectors that they could not decide between Stubberfield and Mckay for a place in the team for Hobart, takes the iced biscuit. <i>(Feb 8, 1908,NZ Truth,138/3/3</i>)

### take the knock
 
 <i>phr.</i> accept the loss?

>  The `bookie' will simply, to use their own vulgar parlance, `take the knock' and go on as before. [This quote is the next sentence to SUGAR and must imply not paying out, doing a bunk or something like that] <i>(Sep 23, 1911,NZ Truth,326/2/4</i>)

### take to
 
 <i>v.</i> to attack (esp. with the fists)

>  Take to him, Bill. <i>(1911,Kiwi on the Swag,9</i>)

### take to burnt spurs
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) to go mustering.

>  burnt spurs, to take to: to go mustering <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,14</i>)

### take to one's scrapers
 
 <i>phr.</i> to take to one's heels

>  <b>CRUMP</b> Tonker took to his scrapers and hid in a hedge. <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,63</i>)

### take to the boards
 
<b>1</b> <i>phr.</i> (Rural) to begin a shearing run.

>  At a woolshed party a few days after John Lennon died, they took to the boards and played air microphones with the handpieces. <i>(May 12, 2001,NZ Farmer,64</i>)

### take to the burnt spur
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) to go mustering.

>  <b>ACLAND</b> burnt chops: an expression somewhat akin to the expression 'burnt spur' which denoted the business of a high country mustering: 'So-and-so has taken to the b.s.', meaning he has taken on mustering. <i>(1951,Early Canterbury Runs,366</i>)

### take to the road
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [itinerant workers] to depart on foot or to shoulder one's swag.

>  <b>CHINN</b> The shearing was in full swing and those who had not been fortunate enough to obtain employment shouldered their swags and took to the road again. <i>(1963,Packtrack to Highway,62</i>)

### take to the track
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> I took to the track, I came up north and swagged with a character that was well known on the track at that time. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,68</i>)

### take up
 
 <i>v.</i> occupy (land, for farming or mining)

>  Further concessions to soldiers who take up land were urged at the meeting of the Advisory Board of the War Relief Societies yesterday. <i>(Nov 17, 1916,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  ... this had been forest ... long before the run had been `taken up' or stocked. <i>(1908,TrNZI,40/518</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> It was the first station ... to be taken up in South Cantebury ... <i>(1946,Early Canterbury Runs,160</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> My companion and myself have found a small piece of country, which we have just taken up. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,v/66</i>)

>  <b>WALKER</b> (Central Otago) Prospected about 10 miles up river, every place worth having seems to have been taken up. ... [Jan 12-13] ... we went up the gully to see if there was room to put in anywhere, but found it was all taken up, so we went up to the top of the hill ... <i>(Jan 10, 1863,Journal and Letters (Walker)</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> Taking up the Run - ... I had waited to see how the land was allotted before I took it up. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,viii/114</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> The land up here 110 miles from the sea coast (Oamaru) was taken up in 1858, and all the habitable parts in of New Zealand have now been taken up ... These men are perhaps safer than these men down here [near the coast] as no one can take their land up for a long time. <i>(Feb 2, 1873,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1873 Feb 2 G.M.Wilson Diary in 1973 Wierzbicka Wilson Family 164]

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> (country) freehold or lease Thus [168] fresh country was continually taken up and active explorers went further and further afield, ... <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,167</i>)

>  I shall never forget the small dark man who took up a run at the back of beyond, carted a piano up there on a bullock dray, and passed his solitary evenings playing Bach's fugues; ... <i>(Aug 19, 1912,Lesson Outlines for Christian Education</i>) [NOTES: 1912 Aug 19 Sir Joshua Strange Williams letter in 1919 Jones Samuel Butler 1/85]

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> I took the section up in the first place through ignorance. <i>(1927,Follow the Call,111,1975 ed.</i>)

>  The original occupier of country in the early days was said to have <i>taken it up</i>. `Who took up this station?' means `Who was the first occupier?' Nowadays people tell you that Mr Jones had just `taken up' 1000 acres of Brown's run. (C.) The old sense of the world was understood everywhere on stations up to about 1900. <i>(Dec 23, 1933,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> When these stations were first taken up the access was poor, or the soil was infertile, and so they were not suitable for subdivision. 1868. <i>(1981,Both Sides of the River,8</i>)

>  <i>take up ... 2. N.Z.</i> to occupy and break in (uncultivated land):<i> he took up some hundreds of acres in the back country. </i> <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,1192</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1986 NZPOD; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 490]]

>  sc a claim BRICKMAKERS', near Punga Flat. Five men's ground, taken up in March <i>(1868,Thames Miner's Guide, The,83</i>)

>  <b>SCOTTER</b> (sc. country: cf mining use) In April, 1853, six months after his arrival in New Zealand, he bought 820 sheep in Nelson and drove them to Canterbury in search of land and it was only after he saw that all the open country was taken up ... that he ... decided to cross the Waitaki River. <i>(1948,Run, Estate and Farm,1</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to occupy, lease or purchase land for the purpose of farming, usually as the first occupant.

>  <b>HOBHOUSE</b> Every bit of the valley to the highest mountain-top has been greedily taken up for runs ... <i>(Nov 10, 1860,Hobhouse Letters and Papers</i>)

>  My companion and myself have found a small piece of country, which we have just taken up ... <i>(1860,BLANK ENTRY,66</i>) [NOTES: in BUTLER First Year (1863)]

>  .. was munificently rewarded for his bravery and sacrifice by being allowed to take up this section of virgin bush ... <i>(Oct 1918,NZ Farmer,1339</i>)

>  The original occupier of country in the early days was said to have 'taken it up.' <i>(Dec 23, 1933,Press, The  (Acland Glossary),15</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> When some of the North Island runs were first taken up, dogs were so destructive that it was hard to stock up with sheep. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,42</i>)

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> When the MacKenzie Country was discovered and all available land taken up ... <i>(1951,Canterbury Tales,143</i>)

>  <b>TAIT</b> The property had changed hands three times and had reverted to bracken, manuka and blackberry since it was first taken up in standing bush in 1910. <i>(1956,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol I,74</i>)

>  <b>FEARON</b> Ashton had also taken up his block in April 1919, and built a house close to the road. <i>(1980,Te Wharau,182</i>)

>  I have a nine acre section, and finding I could do with another small one, I applied for it, and was informed that I could only hold one deferred section at a time. Now sir, on applying I understood by the declaration I could apply for more, as long as I did not exceed the 320 acres. On this condition, I took up the small section I now occupy. <i>(Oct 3, 1884,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

### take-out
 
 <i>n.</i> (Sport) [cf. put-in]

>  Toward the end these paddlers did decide to walk a rapid or two, but we finally got to the take-out (bridge) soon after... <i>(2005,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.huttkayakers.org.nz/tripIndex.php *Accessed 17/04/06. No date for site construction or posting.]

### take/be put down the track
 
 <i>phr.</i> be fired, sent down the road

>  <b>18/14/2</b> ... and a `drummer' is the man with the lowest tally - perhaps because he is likeliest to take and keep the track with his `load.' The general applications of these fresh significations may be doubted. <i>(Jul 7, 1911,Maoriland Worker</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> The lamb would have to be killed. I felt embarrassed and upset because I was sure I would be `put down the track' <i>Ibid.</i>115 I was certain I would be put `down the track', and I was terrified by the thought. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,98</i>)

### takeke
 
 <i>n.</i> garfish, <i>Reporhamphus ih</i>i or (? earlier) <i>Hemirhamphus intermedius, H.australis </i> half-beak, ihe, piper, taheke [check that Colenso spg not mistranslit.: misprint or typo]1970 Pictured and named on the definitive issue, of the NZ 7d atamp]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> The whole of the long range of the New Zealand Coast is abundantly supplied with beautiful fish. Of this variety of 78 species I will name a part, viz. ... takeke, Guardfish; ... [<i>Glossary</i> 120 takeke Guardfish, garfish, or piper (<i>Hemirhamphus intermedius</i>)]. <i>(1843,NZ Journal,40,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COLENSO</b> A small but graceful species of garfish ... [55] flow, or rather sprung into my boat. ... Here [near Bay of Islands, the natives] call them Taheke; but among the southern tribes, Ihe. <i>(1844,Excursion in the Northern Island of NZ</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Colenso Excursion Northern Island in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 54-55]

>  English Name Native Name ... ... Guardfish Takehe [sic, but check] <i>(1849,NZ Journal,9/125</i>) [NOTES: quoting Anglo Maori Warder Oct]

>  HEMIRAMPHUS INTERMEDIUS Cantor. <i>Garfish</i>, or <i>Piper; Takeke</i>. Occurs in abundance around all the northern coasts. Common, Otago, in spring and early summer. ... Distribution: New Zealand coasts. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/120</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Hemirhamphus intermedeus</i> Cantor. GARFISH or PIPER; TAKEKE. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,21,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> GARFISH or PIPER (TAKEKE) <i>Reporhamphus ihi </i>Phillipps. This fish is known in Auckland as Piper, although in Otago it is called Garfish or Guard Fish. ... It is on account of this peculiarity [a long bottom jaw] that they are sometimes called Halfbeaks. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,157</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> THE GARFISH <i>Reporhampus ihi (Phillipps) </i>... Maori name: TAKEKE or IHI. <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,33</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> GARFISH ... <i>Other names: Reporhamphus ihi</i>; piper, half-beak; ihe, takeke (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,206</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK3/705-712</b> Garfish (piper) - ihe, takeke, <i>Reporhamphus ihi </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> GARFISH [<i>Reporhamphus ihi</i>] ... Also called piper, halfbeak, ihi, and takeke. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,16</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> smelt

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> My boys were disappointed of their eels ... the only fish they caught were a few takekes which is remarkable for the large roe it has though only about 4 inches long. <i>(Apr 10, 1847,Journal,5/20</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> <i>Awa</i>, is a small tidal river fish, resembling the roach; the settlers call it the herring, which it only resembles in shape. It is synonymous with <i>takeke.</i> <i>Ibid.</i> 414 <i>Takeke</i>, a small fresh-water fish, syn. <i>Tikihemi.</i> It is an oily fish. <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,411</i>)

### takeketonga
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Makaira mazara</i> (Jordan and Snyder). BLACK MARLIN or SWORDFISH; TAKETONGA. [check] <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,47,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> STRIPED MARLIN ... <i>Other names: Makaira audax</i>; takeketonga (Maori). Universally called striped marlin in English-speaking countries. <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,273</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> STRIPED MARLIN [<i>Makaira audax</i>] ... The Maori name takeketonga is not often heard. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,54</i>)

### takeovers panel
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ... I repeatedly called for an increase in the commission's power in this respect, but the government of the day, the stock exchange and professional bodies resisted conferring that role on the commission along with the other areas of needy regulation, such as appointment of a Takeovers Panel. <i>(Nov 13, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)

### taker-up
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) one who takes up, purchases or leases land for farming purposes.

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> Mount Cook Station is now managed by Catherine Burnett's grandson, Donald Mount Cook Burnett, and is one of four stations in South Canterbury that have been held continuously by the 'taker-up' and his descendants. <i>(1988,Tales of Pioneer Women,257</i>)

### takeyahways
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>takey-ah-ways</i> take-away food, pronounced as if a Maori word. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,111</i>)

### taki
 
 <i>n.</i> a ritual challenge [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  For the young Tai Tokerau men dressed as warriors for the taki, or ritual challenge, it's been a long wait. <i>(Feb 28, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),10</i>)

### Takitimu
 
 <i>adj.</i> Geol. classification

>  Recently completed theses and projects include: Bruce Houghton (PhD) Geology of the Takitimu Group and related intrusive rocks, Central Takitimu Mountains, western Southland, New Zealand. <i>(Nov 1978,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,46/18</i>)

### Takitimu gecko
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a rare gecko found from southern Fiordland to the Richardson Mountains; well camouflaged by bright herringbone patterning. [<i>Hoplodactylus cryptozoicus</i>]

>  [Hdg] Takitimu gecko. In 1966, A Department of Conservation field worker, Tony Jewell, found a very unusual gecko on a scree in the Takitimu Mountains in western Southland... Tony Jewell captured three more Takitimu gecko in 2000, and another in 2001, all from the same scree. <i>(May 2002,Forest & Bird,No. 304/17</i>)

>  DOC Biodiversity ranger Ros Cole confirmed that this was the first Takitimu gecko to be found in the southern Fiordland area. "The find follows a 2005 discovery, which was the first indication that Takitimu geckos are capable of living in both alpine and forest environments." <i>(Jun 2007,Conservation Action,6</i>)

### takiwa
 
 <i>n.</i> territory [ORIGIN: Maori] (See [see also taurahere](../S#see-also-taurahere))

>  In 1997, the tribe won all the island's pounamu in their Treaty settlement - every bit within their takiwa, or territory, effectively all the greenstone in New Zealand. <i>(May 29, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>)

### takiwai
 
 <i>n.</i> teardrop greenstone (See [tangiwai](t-ten.md#tangiwai))

>  [Milford Sound's] waters were familiar to [Maori] from their travels to mine the takiwai, or teardrop greenstone, found at the entrance to Milford Sound. <i>(Nov 5, 2002,Dominion Post,C8</i>)

### takoha
 
 <i>n.</i> tax, contribution

>  Purchases by gratuities, more regularly called 'takoha', were introduced to extinguish such Maori possessory rights as had been assumed or were claimed in the Waimate Plains or in the south. <i>(1996,Waitangi Tribunal Report,143 : 193</i>)

### taku tama
 
 <i>phr.</i> my son [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  A quiet breeze reveals to him a pause in animosity of storm upon the forest in the war of ancestors, where brother battles brother for the land beneath his feet, whose mauri even now rises within him and calls him taku tama. <i>(2007,Best New Zealand Fiction, Vol 4, The,36</i>) [NOTES: 'The Good and the Gentle', Ben Brown, 2007]

### takuta
 
 <i>n.</i> doctor [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  When he left the battalion in 1945, Bill Ngata son of Sir Apirana, said of Moore: "You were not the most academic of our takuta (doctors). You were not (quite) the bravest, but you were the most Maori". <i>(Feb 2005,North & South,107</i>)

### talamea
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [taramea](t-ten.md#taramea))

### talc
 
 <i>n.</i> greenstone, usu. green talk (1770) [also jasper talc(obs: early use)]

>  At the conclusion of the lecture, Mr. Lyon put us in possession of a curious fact connected with the development of the mineral wealth of the southern island, namely that the greenstone, or <i>poonamor</i>, of the natives, a species of talc slate, is in such demand in the Chinese market for the manufacture of their idols, ornaments, &c., that as much as GBP1500 per ton can be had for it. <i>(Sep 9, 1842,New Zealand Colonist & Port Nicholson Advertiser,2</i>)

>  As we have in New Zealand many of the conditions under which gold is found in other countries, as well as most of the gold-bearing rocks; such as crystalline rocks composed of quartz and felspar [sic], with a grey base, sometimes approaching to the character of porphyry; together with dykes of quartz in talc and mica-slate schist, &c. ... <i>(Jul 13, 1852,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  On the latter part of this journey Dr Shortland gained from an intelligent Native named Huruhura the first specific and reliable information regarding the interior of the island, and of the lakes which had always vaguely figured in the earliest maps under the title of Water of Green Talc, Greenstone Lake, Te Wai Pounamu. <i>(Oct 7, 1887,Otago Witness,14</i>)

>  An important discovery of mica ore and talc was made near the mouth of the Paringa River, in South Westland. <i>(May 8, 1891,North Otago Times,2</i>)

>  The tools ... are adzes or axes some made of a hard black stone, and others of green Talk. ... The green talk axes that are whole and good they set much value upon. [ed. fn] i.e. talc; pounamu, greenstone or nephrite, ... <i>(Mar 31, 1770,Cook's Journals,1/284,1955 ed.</i>)

>  ...this man had a human tooth hanging at his ear- and a piece of green talk about two and half inches long, and an inch and half broad, flat, and carved into the figure of a most uncooth [sic] animal of fancy <i>(Oct 18, 1769,Cook's Journals,1/581</i>) [NOTES: 1769 Oct 18 W.B. Monkhouse in 1955 Cook Journal 1/581]

>  ... one seizd his weapon of Green talk ... <i>(Oct 9, 1769,Banks Journal,1/402,1962 ed.</i>)

>  [see Te Wai Pounamu] <i>(Jan 31, 1770,Cook's Journals,1/243,1955 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COLLINS</b> <i>(1798,Account of the English Colony in NSW, An</i>)

>  <b>SAVAGE</b> There is a green semi-transparent talc, brought from the interior, of considerable hardness, with which they make their tools, and a number of ornaments. [transfer to TALC ?] <i>(1807,Some Account of NZ,9</i>)

>  <b>CRUISE</b> They also wear, fastened round the neck by a cord, and hanging on the breast, a piece of green talc, carved to represent what cannot be deemed human. <i>(1823,Journal,183,1957 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MATHEW</b> [Patamouri] presented the Governor with one of their splendid Green Talc Hatchets or `Mares' expressly for Queen `Wikitoria'. nephrite, ...] <i>(Feb 6, 1840,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Feb 6 F.Mathew Journal in 1940 Founding of NZ 41 See also Mathew (?Matthew)1842]

>  <b>POLACK</b> The Meri is ... formed of the green talc, jade, serpent-stone, and jasper (for it has been called by all these names). <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,2/30</i>)

>  <b>SAVAGE</b> There is a green semi-transparent talc, brought from the interior, of considerable hardness, with which they make their tools, and a number of ornaments. <i>(1807,New Zealand (various authors/titles),9</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> Singular to relate, the central island has existed hitherto unnamed; as a distinguishing mark it has been called <i>Te Wai Poenenamu</i>[a-acute], or the Waters of Green Talc (<i>rather</i> an anomalous same for <i>terra firma</i>), from the time of Cook's survey; ... <i>Ibid.</i>1/194 ... a dark-gray and black-coloured granite, termed <i>onewoa</i> by the natives was made use of in its stead, or the green talc, <i>poeanamu</i>[a2-acute] and pieces of red jasper chipped off adroitly, resembling in shape our common gun-flints. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/1/5fn</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> greenstone

>  <b>CRAIK</b> [They] make certain of their weapons, and carving tools, of a green talc, or jasper stone, which is found only in the southern island ... <i>(1830,New Zealanders, The,184</i>)

>  <b>CRAIK</b> [They] make certain of their weapons, and carving tools, of a green talc, or jasper stone, which is found only in the southern island ... <i>(1830,New Zealanders, The,184</i>)

>  It is called <i>pounamu</i> or <i>poenamu</i> by the Maoris, and `jade,' `jadeite,' or `nephrite,' by various writers, while old books refer to the `green talc' of the Maoris. <i>(1892,TrNZI,24/481</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> greenstone

>  <b>MONKHOUSE</b> He had a narrow slip of Jasper talk formed somewhat like a french bean, hanging to his ear- ... <i>(Oct 18, 1769,Cook's Journals,1/581,1955 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1769 Oct 18 W.B. Monkhouse in 1955 Cook Journal]

### talc-stone
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>COLLINS</b> They ... carry on a traffic for flax and green talc-stone, of which they make axes and ornaments. <i>(1804,English Colony in NSW,344</i>)

### talent quest
 
 <i>n.</i> a public entertainment/competition with the object of discovering talented amateur singers, musicians, etc. [Jim Cameron writes: In use for many years. Neither this compound nor <i>song quest</i> (as in Mobil Song Quest) is recorded in OED or other dictionaries. OED has an entry for <i>talent show</i>.]

### talk
 
 <i>v.</i> (of a tree) to make sounds indicating it is about to fall [ca1951 R. Gilberd Okaihau That means that a tree is about to fall and the weight of the top is breaking the fibres of wood. They crackle and squeak and groan - `talking'.]

>  `Don't you hear her talkin'?' as a gentle ticking would indicate the act of partition had commenced. Then the wedges were `backed up,' and a few solid blows from the heavy mauls would complete the operation. <i>(Dec 1904,NZ Illustrated Magazine,178</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>talk the leg off an iron pot</i> you talk too much. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,112</i>)

### talk a glass eye to sleep
 
 <i>phr.</i> to talk boringly

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>talk a glass eye to sleep</i> boring; ref. Stevan Eldred-Grigg, <i>Oracles and Miracles.</i> <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,111</i>)

### talk and puha
 
 <i>phr.</i> (a heading) punning on pork and puha q.v.

### talk boots
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) to argue.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> talking boots: a musterers' term for arguing the point. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,83</i>)

### talk bullock
 
 <i>phr.</i> to use colourful language (See [bullock language](bri-byo.md#bullock-language),[bullocky](bri-byo.md#bullocky))

>  In modern Australia ... we hear of men on up-country stations who can do nothing but `talk bullock;' and so all men did to a great extent in days when both word and idiom had origin in cattle-speech. [fn] For ancient use of expression `talking bullock' see Bible (Apocrypha), Ecclesiasticus, xxxix, 25. [HWO: Jerusalem and Vulgate references don't seem to be quite apt.] <i>(1889,TrNZI,21/450</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> .. as long ago as 1864 ... Thatcher ... used the expression <i>to talk bullock. </i> <i>(1941,NZ Slang,47</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>talk bullock</i> bad language bullock drivers were noted for; ref. Sam under `bullocky'. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,111</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Talk bullock.</i> To use much-and picturesque-bad language: New Zealand coll.: 1846, Charles R. Thatcher (cited by B., 1941). Both bullock-drivers and their beasts have much to put up with. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1194</i>)

### talk oneself around
 
 <i>v.</i> to deny (something)

>  Here is a man who took advantage of a school situation to flog off a narcotic. Talk yourself around that one. <i>(Apr 3, 1979,Evening Post,5</i>)

### talk the leg off an iron pot
 
 <i>phr.</i> to talk excessively [ca1910 W.H.B. Orsman]

>  (Loud cheers, and "Oh, don't ask, he'd talk the leg off an iron pot.") <i>(Jan 28, 1870,Colonist (Nelson),3</i>)

>  My word, Dot, she would talk the leg off an iron pot! <i>(Dec 18, 1907,Otago Witness,83</i>)

>  Among all the pots and dishes among the motley assortment of goods strewn round the place there can surely be none with legs still adhering for if anyone could "talk the leg off an iron pot" it is this auctioneer. <i>(Sep 15, 1923,NZ Truth,6</i>)

### talk to one's saddle
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [boundary shepherds and outstation shepherds] to become disturbed from isolation.

>  <b>MACFARLANE</b> Men became so strange and quiet in the back, so much alone, begin 'talking to their saddle' as the old saying goes. <i>(1946,Amuri,97</i>)

### talkback
 
 <i>n.</i> shortened form of "talkback radio"

>  Talkback can get a bit hairy sometimes. <i>(Sep 2, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),62</i>)

>  <i>talkback n. NZ & Austral.</i> broadcast programme in which listeners participate by telephoning the studio<i> (an incensed caller on Radio Avon's Sunday night talkback). </i> <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,783</i>) [NOTES: HNZD1 [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 490]]

### talkback radio
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  It could be argued that talkback radio is democracy at work - even in action. <i>(Aug 15, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),62</i>)

### talkbacker
 
 <i>n.</i> someone who phones in to a talkback show

>  The cricketers have gone on to win the acclaim of the radio talklbackers. <i>(Dec 27, 1999,Dominion,37</i>)

### Talking Shop
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HYDE</b> `Vox populi, vox dei.' You won't hear it at the Talking Shop, ... <i>(1934,Journalese,40</i>)

### Talky-Talky
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Mr Jerningham Wakefield was christened "Talky-talky" because of his ability to talk any one of them to a standstill in half an hour. <i>(Dec 23, 1890,Otago Witness,18</i>)

### Tall Blacks
 
 <i>n.</i> the NZ men's basketball team

>  By sheer luck, I spotted him in the crowd at the Tall Blacks versus Canada basketball test in Auckland. <i>(Jan 2000,North & South,78</i>)

>  Basketball's Tall Blacks emphasised the folly of making such lists when they won their first two matches in the world championships in Indianapolis, performaces regarded as among New Zealand's finest sporting achievements. <i>(Apr 4, 2002,Dominion Post,D9</i>)

>  The apt Tall Blacks is recorded from 1995 for the men's basketball team. a men's ice hockey team played in 1998 under the name of Ice Blacks, and the Wheel Blacks travelled to Canada for the second wheelchair rugby World Cup in June 1998. <i>(Jan 1999,NZWords,5</i>)

>  The international series sees the Tall Ferns and the Tall Blacks take on India in three matches. <i>(Mar 10, 2006,NZ Herald,Super Sport, D21</i>)

>  [Winston's] so on the margins of the action these days, that even in Parliament - his natural habitat - it's like watching Ronnie Corbett playing Piggy in the Middle with a couple of Tall Blacks. <i>(Apr 3, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

### tall country
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) high altitude mustering land.

>  <b>MCMILLAN</b> .. the legendary musterer, gone from the tall country. <i>(1975,More From the High Country,44</i>)

>  <b>MCMILLAN</b> I went to Glenorchy at the start of the week to muster tall country. <i>(1981,Tales of the High Country,45</i>)

### Tall Ferns
 
 <i>n.</i> the NZ women's basketball team (cf. Tall Blacks)

>  Meanwhile at least one more example established itself in 1999; the women's basketball team predictably blended the existing <i>Tall Blacks</i> and <i>Silver Ferns</i> to be known as <i>Tall Ferns</i>. <i>(Aug 2000,NZWords,4 : 1</i>)

>  She and Colling both made the Tall Ferns (the New Zealand Women's basketball team) before they made the Silver Ferns together in 1996. <i>(Sep 1999,North & South,74</i>)

>  The international series sees the Tall Ferns and the Tall Blacks take on India in three matches. <i>(Mar 10, 2006,NZ Herald,Super Sport, D21</i>)

### tall poppy
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  What this conference shows is you have a lot of interest in entrepreneurship. Don't tall poppy it, let them go, let them do their own thing. <i>(Feb 20, 2000,Sunday Star Times,E1</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> someone who stands out above others [aka taller poppy; OED online: b. Allusively, spec. as tall poppy: in Australia, an especially well-paid, privileged, or distinguished person; also transf.]

>  He was asked how he governed his people, and he went into his garden, taking a stick; he found there a tall poppy, and struck off its head, and said that was how he ruled his people. <i>(Mar 27, 1886,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  The usually objective and witty Rosemary McLeod indulged in some tall poppy pruning recently in her criticism of Sandra Coney in <i>North and South</i> (July 1988). <i>(Jan 7, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),50</i>)

>  The so-called tally [sic] poppy syndrome is a condition invented by celebrities to deflect the criticism they usually deserve. <i>(Jan 21, 2001,Sunday Star Times,A8</i>)

>  New Zealand is famous for cutting down tall poppies by means of the great clobbering machine. <i>(Feb 28, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

>  When Team New Zealand won the America's Cup, not only did they get the keys to Auckland City, and for one or two entry to the rich list, they were also granted free life membership of the order of the tall poppy. <i>(Jun 1999,Metro,39</i>)

>  The present social system (he says) must go; the principal of authority must be pulled up by the roots; the tall poppies must be lopped. <i>(Apr 12, 1900,NZ Tablet,17</i>)

>  We most of us know, from reading or hearsay, the old story of the cutting off of the heads of the taller poppies, and, of course, understand the gist of the moral conveyed in the action. <i>(Aug 22, 1874,Otago Witness,8</i>)

>  The defendants include several "tall poppies," such as the millionaire J. Ogden Armour, Louis F. Swift, Edward F. Swift, and Edward Tilden. <i>(Dec 23, 1911,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  Undue exposure of their bodies to the enemy's fire, the more striking colours or adornments of their uniform, the nature of their work, and the well-known policy of lopping off the taller poppies in battle, has usually led to a higher relative mortality among officers than among men in almost every war. <i>(Oct 26, 1899,NZ Tablet,1</i>)

>  Shortish and sharp, Macdonald sees himself as one of the tall poppies of Otaki. <i>(Mar 13, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),41</i>)

>  Mary Cull, consultant with Tall Poppies, says people should now be encouraged even within organisations to see themselves as "Me Inc." <i>(Feb 22, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

>  Some say she is a martyr to the feminist cause, a tall and brilliant poppy cut down by grey males. It's nonsense, but the nonsense will endure. <i>(Aug 5, 2001,Sunday Star Times,A8</i>)

>  One of the things that drives me nuts about this place, and I love it to bits, is the tall poppy. <i>(Apr 8, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),71</i>)

>  There's no such thing as a tall poppy playing rugby. <i>(Aug 23, 1992,Dominion Sunday Times,11</i>)

>  As a result, even the most devoted tall poppy potshotters could not quite get him clearly in their sights. <i>(Jan 9, 2004,Dominion Post  Sport & RaceForm,2</i>)

>  They're so low-key about how good they are. It would be impossible for them to be tall poppies. <i>(Nov 10, 2002,Sunday Star Times,F2</i>)

>  Some people just can't stand you being better'n them. Tall rose syndrome or something. <i>(Apr 11, 2002,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  At some stage in this affair, I'll warrant, the terms "tall poppy syndrome" and "politics of envy" will get an airing. <i>(Mar 6, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),5</i>)

>  This tall poppy business is tedious. If Martin is a poppy, he's no taller than John Kirwan.. Grant Fox and John Walker have been over the past few years. <i>(Jun 25, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),61</i>)

>  The tall poppy sydrome is a cliche used by many self-congratulating achievers in New Zealand. It seems to have become part of the ladder to the top to complain about how tall poppies are being chopped down. <i>(Feb 11, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),5</i>)

>  (From the "SATURDAY REVIEW," September 10) .. It [The Order of the Garter] is a kind of public proclamation that you are a tall poppy; and that, as in these days your head cannot be struck off, it is worth while to buy you. <i>(Dec 27, 1864,Daily Southern Cross,5</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [attrib]

>  You name it, New Zealand has an award for it. There's the incongruously named Tall Poppy Business Excellence Award to start with. <i>(Aug 23, 2004,Dominion Post,C4</i>)

>  Political correctness, tall poppy culling and - my favourite - the belief that being entitled to an opinion is an excuse for ignorance. <i>(Sep 5, 2004,Sunday Star Times,C10</i>)

 <i>n.</i> person who attempts to bring down the cleverest, most powerful, or most successful person in a group

>  anyone who earns above the poverty line here is always a target of the tall poppy syndromers for being overpaid <i>(Nov 14, 2004,Sunday Star Times,C13</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### tall tussock
 
 <i>n.</i> = snow tussock

>  Most tall tussocks mast synchronously throughout their range, from red tussock on the valley flats to the slim snow tussock (<i>C. macra</i>) in the alpine zone. <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/35</i>)

>  The genus <i>Chioonochloa (chion</i> = snow;<i>chloa</i> = grass) includes the snow tussocks, or tall tussocks - up to 2 metres high - that make up the grand, golden army that dominates our subalpine grasslands, furring the hills a tawny gold and forming a magnificent backdrop for beer and car commercials. <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/28</i>)

>  ...the larvae of tussock butterflies (<i>Argyrophenga</i> spp.) feed on silver and tall tussocks, whereas Butler's ringlets (<i>Erebiola butleri</i>) sticks to low alpine <i>Chionochloa</i> species in the wetter western South Island mountains. <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/43</i>)

### tall-poppy cutter
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The sooner we get rid of the poppy-cutting machine and the gun we hold to shoot ourselves in the foot, the better. <i>(Jan 23, 2002,Dominion,13</i>)

>  These carping, tall poppy cutters will doubtless dismiss any likening of the passing persecution of newsreaders with the decades-long detention of the living symbol of the antiapartheid movement. <i>(Jun 27, 2004,Sunday Star Times,C10</i>)

### tall-poppy lopper
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  In typical New Zealand fashion the tall poppy loppers have started whinging about being bored seeing the confident, professional, remorseless way the <i>Black Magic</i> boat crew goes about its business. <i>(Apr 2000,North & South,8</i>)

### tall-poppy puller
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  He says his humble background helps lessen the attacks from tall-poppy pullers. <i>(Aug 10, 2004,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

### tall-poppy topper
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Your correspondent.. appears to be one of these tall poppy-toppers with loppers in hand. <i>(Feb 12, 2001,Evening Post,4</i>)

### tall-poppy-bashing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  There's a part of me, that cynical, tall-poppy bashing bit so characteristic of New Zealanders that assumed I'd take a few swipes at the clothes, at the exhibition, at some little discordant thing. But I can't. <i>(Apr 9, 2001,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  It's joined our alltime favourite [art form] of tall-poppy-bashing, which has given many a lot of pleasure for years - chopping down those who dare to rise above the grey clouds of normality only to draw attention and criticism upon themselves from the unadoring crowd below. <i>(Feb 12, 2002,Evening Post,17</i>)

### tall-poppy-chopping/-scything
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  I'll take negative things that happen around me in this tall-poppy-chopping country and thrive on them. <i>(Aug 13, 2000,Sunday Star Times,F3</i>)

>  And that, I think, is why I'm pleased that Mr Fossett failed in his mission. Plus, of course, a deep-seated, tall-poppy-scything, thwarted, splenetic, mean-spirited pleasure in someone else's disappointment. <i>(Aug 22, 2001,Evening Post,10</i>)

### tall-poppy-lopping
 
 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

For many years., the "talleyman", whom the Society engaged to do just that, was an important figure. ### tally
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a hundred sheep.

>  .. in the happy camaderie of shed life, where toil is plenty and the little rest doubly appreciated, the general remarks revealed ambitions for 'tallies' in the afternoon. <i>(Mar 1, 1946,Ex-Pupils Budget,11</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> a hundred sheep. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,83 (i)</i>)

>  <i>(1) A hundred sheep.</i> When large mobs are counted the man counting calls out `T[ally].!' every time he gets to 100, and then starts again. Another man answers `T.' and cuts a notch in a stick. When the whole mob is counted the number of notches gives the hundreds and the odd number completes the total, which is also (2) called the t. 3. The total number of sheep shorn by each shearer in a day. 4. Hence <i>t.book,</i> in which the man who counts out, writes them down. 5. The <i>ts.</i> for the day before are written up each morning on a <i>tally board</i> kept in the shed. (C.) <i>(Dec 23, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> He had left the tally to Guy while he ransacked in the store for a spark plug .. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,33</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>



 
 <i>n.</i> [meat works; cf TALLY-SPEED] (See [cut tally](../C#cut-tally))

>  <b>COLIN GORDON</b> <i>Tally</i>: You <i>cut tally</i> if you get 100 per man, and you drop so many off the tally for stags (one-ball sheep) and rams (the penises and balls of both these are cut out, and this is how one counts them; <i>pizzles</i> (undeveloped penises) are cut off wethers. <i>(May 17, 1951,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: Feilding Freezing Works terms p.c.]

>  <b>WILSON</b> It is a mechanical business ..., slaughtering [62] by thousands, ... tinning the tongues, freezing the carcases, and tying them up in their little, white muslin shrouds, to be `tallied' and shipped to England, ... <i>(1894,In the Land of the Tui,61</i>) [NOTES: 1890 Feb 8 in 1894 Wilson Land of the Tui 61-62]



 
 <i>n.</i> the amount of work done in a given time

>  The demand is even more urgent than usual as the threshing season has been fine and short straw has enabled mills to make quick work. Record tallies for a day's work have been made. <i>(Mar 22, 1911,Grey River Argus,1</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> ... any good tallies, boys. <i>(1913,Sodger Sandy's Bairn,71</i>)

>  <b>LEE</b> They would work with a will for an hour or two at the outside [at gorse-cutting]. And their tally was good. <i>(1944,Shining with the Shiner,136</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> He will help our tally along if we have a bad spell; ... <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,21</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> Some of the high tallies taken in the early days of the so-called `culling' operations in the great snow-grass basins of the southern Alps and elsewhere show that the shooters learned the wisdom of concentrating their fire on the leader hinds .. <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,69,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> Stan said that a dog that chases deer on your block can cost you half your tally because it makes them more wary and difficult to catch. ...[25] I had a tally of 185 deer. <i>(1961,Good Keen Man, A,21</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [shearing] to reach a total.

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> By the end of the day Bluey had shorn 285 and I tallied 303. <i>(1991,Off the Sheep's Back,83</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> The silly creatures run and jump through [the gate], being counted or `tallied' whilst they are skipping into another enclosure. <i>(1894,In the Land of the Tui,243</i>)

>  Packs and loads of skins tallying over 100lbs. are far from easy to carry over vestigial tracks. <i>(Apr 8, 1953,Freedom,5</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [nf AND] spec. uses of OED vb ? (in the general sense of `counting a number of objects individually and entering the numbers up, or marking them off': e.g. tally cargo, etc) esp. by shearers, freezing workers, storemen; cf also EDD 7. A specified number or weight; in hop-picking, the number of bushels for which the picker receives a shilling. Glo Lon Sur Also vb. [(cf TALLY n1)]

>  <i>(1953,Short Stories (various authors),215</i>) [NOTES: a1927 1938 Antony Me and Gus in 1953 Short Stories 215]

>  <b>BOWEN</b> <i>(1956,Wool Away!</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> <i>Tally: </i>To tally is to count sheep. When counting big mobs, one man does the counting, and at each hundred he calls `Tally'. Another man keeps the tallies - at each call he may cut a notch in a stick or pick out a small pebble. The tally book contains a record of sheep counted during the year. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,190</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [count of prisoners]

>  <b>HYDE</b> .. hot contests ranging between gangs of soldiers as they scored up their tallies. <i>(Aug 1936,Passport to Hell,94</i>)

>  <b>MacKENZIE</b> <i>tally</i> count of prisoners <i>(1980,While We Have Prisons,98</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to count sheep.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Carefully the mob was 'tallied' through the gateway. <i>(1949,High Country Days,114</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> With the sheep tallied and safely paddocked for the night, dogs, horses and riders faced a tiring ride home. <i>(1991,Off the Sheep's Back,53</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a total number of sheep shorn by a shearer in one day

>  A shearer's tally is not just the basis on which he is paid, it affects his ranking in the gang. <i>(Mar 2003,NZ Geographic,84</i>)

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> The pumice in the wool played up with the gear, blunted the cutters and combs, ruined a man's tally .. Better beat the gong if he wanted a good tally. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,17</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> all a shearer's sheep shorn in a day. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,83 (iii)</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> Bluey and I were determined to try and equal his tally if we got some good cutting sheep. <i>(1991,Off the Sheep's Back,82</i>)

>  "That will do," said Brice, quite quiet; "close your shears and leave the shed." / Dick stopped swearing, took to sneering - "You don't mean it now," he said! / "I came here to get a tally, not to knuckle down to you; / You may take it how you fancy, but I mean to see them through " <i>(Sep 3, 1896,Otago Witness,41</i>)

>  Under the heading of "Large Shearing Tallies" one of your correspondents, signing himself "Manager," makes such a gross misstatement <i>re</i> the tallies on this station that I cannot allow it to pass unchallenged. <i>(Feb 9, 1884,Otago Witness,14</i>)

>  And they'll be talking up at Laghmor of the tallies that were shore,/ <i>(Oct 15, 1896,Otago Witness</i>) [NOTES: 1896 Oct 15 Otago Witness in 1967 Bailey and Roth Shanties 131]

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> For Barcoo Ben will run him and half a dozen more/ Of the lank Australian crush upon the board,/ And it ain't no use to tell us of tallies that he shore,/ There'll be records broke this year, you take my word./ <i>(1950,Farm & Station Verse,33</i>) [NOTES: 1897 McKee Wright Station Ballads `Shearing' in 1950 Woodhouse Farm and Station Verse 33]

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> I've scored my last tally, I've done my last dip / ... there's no crutching aboard of a ship. <i>(1903,Reuben & Other Poems,85</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> Climatic conditions, the treatment sheep have had in the months before shearing, and the breeds of the sheep all play a big part in a shearer's tally. For this reason a shearer's tally can fluctuate considerably, depending on the type of sheep he is shearing. It is a mistake for young shearers to make `tally' paramount in their ambition to progress in shearing when quality of work would have to be the first point taken into account. <i>Ibid.</i> 157 TALLY. The number of sheep a shearer does in a day. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,2</i>)

>  <i>Tally</i>: The number of sheep shorn during a run or other specified period. However, the general term is also used to describe a shearer's capacity to shear in a day. E.g. `He has a tally of 120.' <i>(Feb 26, 1965,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>) [NOTES: (H.A.L. Laing)]

>  <i>TALLY: </i>The number of sheep shorn during a run or other specified period. <i>(Feb 21, 1968,Straight Furrow,20</i>)

>  <b>CHARLES</b> When the shearing season is over you will hear the ringers boast/ About their shearing tallies and the big cheques they have made/ <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,28</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> He knocked up excellent tallies on neighbouring shearingboards, . <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,15</i>)

>  <i>tally ... 6. Austral. & N.Z.</i> the number of sheep shorn in a specified period. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,1193</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 784]]

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> <i>Tally</i>. The total number of sheep shorn by a shearer in a day. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,98</i>)

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> ... `Close your shears and leave the shed.'/ Dick stopped swearing, took to sneering - `You don't mean it now!' he said./ `I came here to get a tally, not to knuckle down to you;/ <i>(1950,Farm & Station Verse,31</i>) [NOTES: 1897 McKee Wright Station Ballads 34 (?35) `The Bloke That Ran across a Snag' (also in 1950 Woodhouse Farm and Station Verse 31]

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> I expected immediately to ... shear big tallies as my first step towards becoming a top shearer... I had plenty of time to stroll down the board to watch Fred's movements and still cut my tally <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,75</i>)

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> Reg was our fastest shearer. For a few days George ... put up some high tallies, but ... he dropped back to 200. <i>(1959,Stone, The,43</i>)

>  <i>tally</i>: The term for the number of sheep shorn. Each shearer has his personal tally for the `run', the day, and his final tally for the shed. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/28</i>) [NOTES: Wallace Shearing Glossary]

>  <b>PEACHE</b> ... 100 sheep a day is quite an ordinary shearer's tally ... But the machine does far better work and neither cuts the sheep nor the fleece (i.e. second cut, or cutting the fleece, or staple of the wool). <i>(Apr 20, 1890,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1890 Apr 20 letter Peache Jnl in 1970 Gray Quiet with the Hills 82]



 
 <i>n.</i> count



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a number of stock in a count

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> ... he had only to make a mark on a rail each time 'tally' was called. <i>(1881,Waitaruna,70</i>)

>  <b>SWANTON</b> .. the shed boss who looks after everything, takes the tally ... <i>(1892,Notes on NZ,97</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> The total, i.e. all the hundreds and part-hundreds counted. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,83 (ii)</i>)

>  <b>ASPINALL</b> Short tallies entailed another trip loooking for stragglers. <i>(1993,Farming Under Aspiring,183</i>)

>  Cut and tailed 130 Ewe ... lambs drafted off Hoggits - tally of whole flock ... 1813. <i>(Aug 30, 1860,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> <i>Tally</i>: To tally is to count sheep. When counting big mobs, one man does the counting, and at each hundred he calls `Tally.' Another man keeps the tallies - at each call he may cut cut a notch in a stick or pick out a small pebble. The tally book contains a record of sheep counted during the year. <i>(1949,High Country Days,197</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> during a run, or for a specified period (often a day), or for the whole of a woolshed job [1965 see cit. a. above]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [number of sheep]

>  <b>CASEY</b> I had a tally of eight hundred when I crutched them June last year, ... <i>(1963,As Short a Spring,161</i>)

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> He pushed his completed, naked sheep through his legs and out the porthole then paused to change his cutter. The pumice in the wool played up with the gear, blunted the cutter and combs, ruined a man's tally; ... <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,3</i>)

>  Number of sheep shorn by a shearer or a gang in a day, or the number of sheep in a group. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,60</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [the record of number of sheep, usually but not exclusively shearing context]

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> As I was finishing the counting-out, the boss came towards the shed with a couple of towels in his hand and invited me to come for a swim. I said I would join him as soon as I had all the tallies. The two ringer contestants then called to him, `We're going.'. <i>(1935,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,144</i>) [NOTES: c1875 Meredith Adventuring in Maoriland (1935) 144]

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> ... Bob Hadfield was asked to make up the tallies. When these were completed and each shearer's account had been made up, John produced his chequebook ... <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,68</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> [to number]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> to shear (and get paid for) a number of sheep [to sort incompatible word class and defn]



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> . [(playing) truant; ?or n1 f. teachers taking a `tally' of attenders]

>  She said she had great difficulty in getting the eldest boy, Thomas, to school, and he frequently played "tally" when she had sent him there. <i>(Sep 14, 1891,Star, The,3</i>)

>  He couldn't run about and play with other boys, or bowl a hoop through the window of the grocer's shop at the corner, or fight Billy Ah Sin for "shaking" his alley tawr, or land home from an orchard expedition with the rear guard of his pants cut to ribbons, or play tally or anything else worth while. <i>(Mar 16, 1912,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  "I were fishin' yesterday," he said slowly, "and I met Mr Crayley, and he sez, sez he, 'If I catch you playing tally again I'll inform yer parents!' sez he." <i>(Apr 1, 1903,NZ Illustrated Magazine,36</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Playing the wag was very common. We made for the bush, but it was difficult to time one's arrival at home correctly (1875; Dunedin) We called it `playing tally.' (1875; Wakefield) <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,41</i>)



 
<b>7</b> <i>n.</i>



 
<b>8</b> <i>n.</i> number of animals trapped

>  A Tawa Flat settler has so far got a tally of over 200 fish for the season, most of which were caught in the Porirua stream. <i>(Nov 21, 1908,Marlborough Express,14</i>)

>  Some very good slaughtering tallies have been put up at the Longburn works this season. <i>(Feb 21, 1914,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  <b>HELMER</b> No enormous tallies of deer shot; no fantastic shots pulled off a fantastic ranges; no shrieking blizzards or raging floods to battle for survival in; no life-risking bravery... <i>(1964,Stag Party,6</i>)

>  <b>CURRIE</b> And the fork tines flash as the sheaves are turned on the frame of the one-horse dray; .. the long trap-line; / The tallies are big on the rock-strewn spur ... <i>(1949,Centennial Treasury of Otago Verse,58</i>)

### tally board
 
 <i>n.</i> [(cf TALLY n 1.) 1933 Acland]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a woolshed blackboard on which all shearing tallies or totals to date in a season are recorded.

>  <b>MEEK</b> With age for age there's few could top that on the tally board ... <i>(1952,Station Days in Maoriland,71</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> tally board: each morning the shearers arrive and see, hanging up in the shed, the tally board. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,83</i>)

>  Seventeen clauses will be referred to the Arbitration Court for settlement. Summarised, they are as follows .. 34. Supply of tally board ... <i>(Dec 1, 1910,Poverty Bay Herald,5</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> A very good practice is to have the tally board hanging up on the wall where all the shearers on that particular board can see it, allowing them at any time of the day to know how their tally is progressing. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,97</i>)

### tally book
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>tally book</i>: The official record of what every man has shorn in each run of the day. It is made after the run by checking each shearer's counting out oen before clearing for the next session. Each shearer's tally for the day is then transferred to the tally book. In earlier times a `tally board' was used with the previous day's tallies chalked up. No matter whether the book or the board is used, the tallies must be displayed in the shed. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/28</i>) [NOTES: Wallace Shearing Glossary]

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> I said nothing, put my tally-book in my pocket, and followed the boss. ... I told him I had docked a few sheep [from the tallies] for bad shearing and cutting. (station) <i>(1935,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,144</i>) [NOTES: c1875 Meredith Adventuring in Maoriland (1935) 144]

>  (also Newton) <i>(1933,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: ACLAND]

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> The shearers untied the greasy, sacking slippers they wore on the board, ... then gathered around for a look at the tally book. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,13</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> When Fred looked at the tally book at lunch time, I was only eight behind Thornton. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,76</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a small notebook used by farmers or shearing-gang contractors to record stock numbers.

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> As I was finishing the counting-out the boss came towards the sheep. I said nothing, put my tally-book in my pocket and followed the boss. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,144</i>) [NOTES: (1935)]

>  <b>BARNS-GRAHAM</b> Dad goes out with the 'tally' book for the final count of the day. <i>(1950,Sheep Station New Zealand,62</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> In 1885, which is as far as this particular tally book goes ... <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,30</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> There were the tally books, covering over 100 years of shearing ... <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,33</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> tally book: the sheep are counted out after each shearing run, and each man's tally is written down. At the end of the day these are added up to make his day's tally .. copied out of the tally book, everybody's total for the previous day. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,83</i>)

>  <b>SALISBURY</b> soon as the last sheep of the run was shorn, Mr Williams would count the sheep from the counting-pens, recording each man's tally in the shearers' tally book. <i>(1994,I Was So Lucky,26</i>)

>  The next question as to the number shorn and wages earned, was soon ascertained by reference to the tally books, which Mr Fox produced. <i>(Jan 7, 1892,Otago Witness,6</i>)

### tally clerk
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  He finished his working life as a tally clerk at the Westfield freezing works. <i>(Jun 1998,Cuisine,No. 22: 6</i>)

>  The special holiday provisions under existing legislation for waterside workers and tally clerks are extended to apply to shipwrights and ships' carpenters engaged in the preparation of ships for cargo or in the loading or unloading of cargo. <i>(Aug 8, 1945,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  The employers had once again complained about an increase in wharf thefts and the Court wanted tally clerks, whose duty it was to detect and prevent pilfering, to be free of any union control or interference. <i>(1993,Wharfie,p26</i>)

>  Some of the tally clerks employed on the wharves declined to start work this morning under the present pay of 1s 3d an hour, considering it worth as much as the waterside workers, who under the new award, will receive 1s 5d per hour from Thursday. <i>(Feb 6, 1912,Grey River Argus,5</i>)

>  Tally clerks are steadily increasing in numbers on the Queen's Wharf, and the latest step has been the formation of a union under the title of the Wellington Tally Clerks' Association. <i>(Jan 29, 1907,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  A tally clerk named Pollock working on the breakwater received severe injuries by a heavy sling. <i>(Jun 22, 1885,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  A well attended meeting of tally clerks connected with shipping in the port, was held last night, when it was decided to form a New Zealand Federated Marine Tally Clerks' Union, and to apply to the Maritime Council for affiliation. <i>(Jul 16, 1890,Timaru Herald,3</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> When `runs' are finished the tally clerk should be ready to tally sheep out, for when the run is finished, a shearer likes to know how he has been going. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,97</i>)

>  <i>tally clerk n. N.Z. & Austral.</i> a person, esp. on a wharf, who checks the count of goods being loaded or unloaded. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,1193</i>)

>  The use of waterside workers as tally clerks should be discontinued. <i>(Sep 4, 1952,North & South,8</i>)

>  He was working.. as a tally clerk on the wharf. <i>(Oct 21, 1924,Otago Witness,54</i>)

 <i>v.</i>

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a mechanical counter attached to the outside of a shearer's catching-pen.

### tally out
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  Taking into account the number of sheep shorn, the clip may be regarded as a fair one, though as in former seasons, taking the district through, a considerable number are missing compared with those tallied out last fall. <i>(Feb 28, 1885,Otago Witness,12</i>)

>  Witness said he could shear if he liked: he shore a few sheep but they were tallied out to the other shearers. <i>(May 14, 1897,Clutha Leader,5</i>)

>  Docking is now well over, and the percentage of lambs is fairly good, considering the rough nature of the country on which many of the flocks of this district run. I heard of two lots tallying out at 100 per cent., but generally the figures range from 80 to 94 per cent., this in most cases slightly exceeding the percentages of last year. <i>(Nov 4, 1909,Hawera & Normanby Star,6</i>)

>  Members cannot be yarded like cattle, and tallied out just at the pleasure of the "whips" at the critical moment of a division. <i>(Oct 12, 1878,Tuapeka Times,2</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> When `runs' are finished the tally clerk should be ready to tally sheep out, for when the run is finished, a shearer likes to know how he has been going. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,97</i>)

### tally pen
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a counting-out pen used in drafting or shearing.

>  <b>BOWEN</b> This is why a lot of tally pens and yards holding freshly-crutched sheep present the appearance of a fall of snow ... <i>(1955,Wool Away!,65</i>)

>  .. pushed the shorn sheep from between my legs and down the short, steep race leading to the tally pen. <i>(Jan 2, 1963,Weekly News,32</i>)

>  It is an internal chute shed with tally pens under the floor. <i>(Dec 31, 1964,NZ Farmer,6</i>)

>  The building is conventional in floor layout, with the woolroom and catching pens elevated to give full headroom to tally-pens and night-pens underneath. <i>(May 8, 1969,NZ Farmer,2</i>)

>  It is also so arranged that it may be easily filled with machines by the addition of a beam over the tally pen doors. <i>(Nov 5, 1897,Hawke's Bay Herald,4</i>)

>  The Commissioner of Crown Lands notifies that the Starborough woolshed, men's hut and tally pens will be sold by auction in the township on the 1st May. <i>(Apr 21, 1900,Marlborough Express,2</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> Tally pens should be large enough, where possible to hold one run of sheep. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,97</i>)

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> In his tally pen ... [the roughly-shorn sheep] looked a speckled mess, their injuries daubed with tar to keep the flies off. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,13</i>)

### tally stick
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HARPER</b> The sheep came out with a run through a narrow gate in the yard, and, after the manner of sheep, hesitating, rushing, jumping over each other, whilst on one side I stood, with a smooth stick and a knife, ready to make one notch for every ten, and two for every hundred, as they passed me. Opposite me the purchaser's shepherd did the same. Both our tally sticks were found to correspond, to my no small satisfaction. <i>(Sep 1, 1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,23,1914 ed.</i>)

### tally up
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>THOMSON</b> On tallying up our kill we found we had shot seventeen beasts ... <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,185</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a count of stock.

### tama
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a New Zealand-bred cultivar of ryegrass (Lolium multiflorum). [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The present practice is to grow about 250 acres of Tama and this is supplemented with lucerne hay. <i>(1973,Big Country of the South Island,242</i>)

>  Ken Brown (of the DSIR Grasslands Division) propounded the philosophy that the heavier the weight in a bushel of tama, the better the establishment of the resultant crop. <i>(Mar 22, 1979,NZ Farmer,25</i>)

>  Northland dairy farmer Doug Taylor includes tama with nui ryegrass when oversowing paddocks. <i>(Jul 1983,NZ Dairy Exporter,5</i>)

>  Annual grasses, such as Tama and Moata, with high metabolisable energy values, are grazed throughout the winter. <i>(Apr 12, 2001,NZ Farmer,31</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> son

>  <b>MASON</b> PERA. ... There's two or three little tamas round here can call him Dad if they want to. <i>(1969,Awatea,22,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BUCK3/340</b> <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The</i>)

### tamaiti
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McCRAE</b> Pomarre[acute] .. .. Rungatyeeda no te Kaaingha. E Wharre[acute] .. .. Whaheene[acute] no Pomarre[acute]. Poaka .. .. Tamyte[acute] no Pomarre[acute]. ... E Po .. .. Koeteedo no Pomarre Whaheene no Tenana. <i>(1820,Journal,27,1928 ed.</i>)

>  child - tama|i|t [tamaiti] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,109,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: c1826-1827 list of Maori words in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 109]

>  <b>MOSER</b> ... the journey itself would at least cost him three days travelling, on acount of the tamaitis (children), who were not strong enough to walk ... <i>(1863,Mahoe Leaves,30</i>)

>  I started for New Plymouth, got medecine for Roka and a small tomite and sent it back from Waitara. <i>(Aug 8, 1878,Chudleigh Diary,271,1950 ed.</i>)

>  I called out to the man. Have you seen my Tomaiti. Yes we have she is in the house. <i>(Dec 10, 1889,Chudleigh Diary,398,1950 ed.</i>) [NOTES: check date]

>  <i>child; root tama, son. </i> <i>(Jun 1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/157</i>) [NOTES: J.C.Andersen]

>  <b>HULME</b> 178 Tamaiti = child <i>(1983,Bone People, The,467</i>)

>  <b>ROSIER-JONES</b> She is enveloped by Aunty's flesh. Warm, sweet-smelling. `You are my darling tamaiti, my darling child.' <i>(1985,Cast Two Shadows,52</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a child, a boy [ORIGIN: Maori, cf. tama]

>  AS A TAMAITI, 25-YEAR-OLD MANU BURKHARDT MACRAE WAS ALWAYS INTERESTED IN BUILDING THINGS AND FINDING OUT HOW THEY WORKED. <i>(2005,Te Mana  Taiohi,issue 18 summer 2005 / 2006, p12</i>)

>  <b>Ihimaera ed.</b> 'Oh my boy,' she sighed forlornly, rocking him. 'What are you doing to yourself, eh? What are you doing, tamaiti?' <i>(2007,Get on the Waka,120</i>) [NOTES: (Kingi McKinnon)]

>  <i>tamaiti</i>, child; root tama, son. <i>(1956,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/157</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

 <i>n.</i> children [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Due to recent expansions we now have vacancies for *Tamariki aged 0 - 2 years & *Tamariki aged 2 - 5 years <i>(Jan 17, 2006,Whakatane Beacon,10</i>)

>  <b>ELDER</b> I told you when you were at Parramatta I would send you a gentleman to teach your tameheekes (tamarikis) and koctecdos (kotiros) to read. <i>(1934,Marsden's Lieutenants,58</i>)

Parents responded by wrapping their tamariki up warm, and keeping them moving as the sky cried above. <i>(May 3, 2004,Salient,8/52</i>)

>  His Worship the Mayor Sir Barry Curtis, Deputy Mayor Anne Candy, Manukau City Coucillors, City Manager Colin Dale, Director of City Services Wayne Goodley and council staff, extend all their aroha and awhinatia to John's whanau, especially his hoa wahine Raiha, tamariki and mokopuna. <i>(Dec 6, 2005,NZ Herald,C8</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a child (See [tama](t-ten.md#tama),[tamaiti](t-ten.md#tamaiti))

>  Warriors, Wahines, Kuri, Poaka, Tamariki, Kumeras, Karos, Interpreters (1st, 2nd and 3rd grade), Poets Laureate, Poi Dancers, Guides, &c. on application to the Registrar. ...WAIPIRO - - An acursed drink ... TAIHOA - - The Police have the matter in hand POAKA - -a pig PUKEKO A bokakker out of season KORERO A long talk UTU - - The spike HAPU - - A Tribe KUMERA - - A sweet Spud <i>(1913,Auckland University Carnival Book,27</i>)

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> Seeing a plug of the Major's best tobacco on the table, Nene suddenly descended from his dignity. `A chew, now, Mahore, and I will take the tamariki home.' <i>(1938,Promenade,58</i>)

>  <b>GVRIEVE</b> `I think the <i>tamariki</i> burn him.' [fn] <i>Tamariki</i> - children. <i>(1939,Sketches from Maoriland,110,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HOWE</b> Tamariki, Tamarikitanga - children were traditionally openly cared for, and shared particularly by both parents and grandparents; they had the advantage of being able to expect similar care and protection from not just close kin, but other parents within the hapu (larger kinship group). They were encouraged to become independent and responsible as quickly as possible. One detrimental effect of urbanisation on the economic realities of today is the breakdown evident in such caring. <i>(1985,Towards Taha Maori,22</i>)

### tamarikitanga
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [tamariki](t-ten.md#tamariki))

### tamarillo
 
 <i>n.</i> (formerly in New Zealand called tree tomato) a bright red, subtropical fruit grown on a small tree. [pl. -os]

>  The tamarillo, it seems, is still a fruit New Zealand has largely to itself, for though it is grown in private gardens around the world in warm frost-free places like California, South Africa, Asia and Africa, only here is it cultivated systematically on a commercial scale... In the 1880s, Mr D Morris, director of the Botanbical Gardens in Jamaica, sent seeds to the hill districts of India and Sri Lanka, and it was from northern India that the first seeds were sent to New Zealand, to Hay and Sons, nurserymen of Parnell in Auckland. The Hay's 1891-1892 catalogue states that the nursery then had "plants of strong and healthy appearance" for sale... The name "tamarillo" officially came into being on January 31, 1967, after New Zealand exporters decided a more romantic, exotic name was needed than "tree tomato". The strategy worked, and today tamarillo seems to have become the accepted name internationally. <i>(Jun 12, 2004,Dominion Post,E8</i>)

>  He cites the economic potential of the pepino on a par with the tamarillo. <i>(Jun 1982,Orchardist NZ,143</i>)

>  On February 1, 1967, the name tamarillo officially replaced tree tomato. <i>(Feb 1967,Orchardist NZ,23</i>)

>  The tamarillo or tree tomato, <i>Cyphomandra betacea</i> Sendt.. is a native of Brazil and Peru. <i>(Jun 1970,Orchardist NZ,149</i>)

>  <b>YEREX</b> Tamarillos have been around for a long time and do quite well on the local market. <i>(1988,Best of Both Worlds,109</i>)

>  We're [sc. the Rural Bank] also financing orchids, carnations and roses for export.. even sub-tropical fruits such as tamarillos, persimmons, avocados and kiwifruit. <i>(Apr 6, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),72</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> A native of the Andean region of Peru and of southern Brazil, the tamarillo is the only one of the 30 species of its genus, Cyphomandra, to be widely known...It was from northern India that the first seeds were sent to New Zealand, in 1891, to D. Hay & Sons, nurserymen of Parnell, Auckland. <i>(1985,Delectable Fruits Cookery,145</i>)

>  Gently peel the tamarillos then carefully slide them into the simmering syrup. <i>(Sep 1999,Cuisine,No. 76 : 86</i>)

>  The Sex Kitten uses perfectly ripe tamarillos, blended with homemade vanilla sugar syrup and extra vanilla scraped from the pod, strained and frozen into ice cubes. <i>(Sep 11, 2003,Dominion Post,D1</i>)

>  Although it sounds Spanish, the name 'tamarillo' is another New Zealand invention. The former name 'tree tomato', was considered inappropriate for use overseas where people buying the fruits for the first time might expect them to taste like tomatoes. In 1967, orchardist Bill Thomson took the Maori word 'tama' added 'tillo' (taken from tomatillo, a complete unrelated Mexican fruit) and then (just to make the sound more mellifluous) he replaced 't' with an 'r'. Thus, 'tamarillo' was born. <i>(Apr 1991,NZ Geographic,58</i>)

>  So if you are a true New Zealander who enjoys cutting a tamarillo in half and scooping out the juicy flesh straight into your mouth, there is still plenty of time for this refreshing tonic. <i>(Aug 20, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),58</i>)

>  <i>(Oct 12, 1966,NZ News,6/2</i>)

>  <i>(Oct 9, 1968,NZ News</i>)

>  The currency of <i>tree tomato</i> (p. 263) might also owe something to New Zealand, where the tree is well known and has always been called <i>tree tomato </i>(until in 1967 the growers decided to change the name to <i>tamarillo</i>, thought to be a better name for publicity). <i>(1968,Anglia,86/359</i>) [NOTES: G.W.Turner review Pitcairnese Language]

>  <i>(Jul 23, 1969,NZ News,4/5</i>)

>  <b>MITCHELL</b> So the N.Z.B.C. was treated like the tamarillo (nee tree tomato): the name was changed and the creature kept the same. <i>(1972,Half-Gallon Quarter-Acre Pavlova Paradise, The,124</i>)

>  ... other subtropical fruits such as passionfruit, Chinese gooseberries, feijoas, and tamarillos (formerly called `tree tomatoes'). <i>(1974,NZ Agriculture,126</i>)

>  Supplies of tropical fruits were curtailed because of the Japanese threats to the sea lanes, and tamarillo (or tree tomato as they were then known) growing began in earnest. [WWII] <i>Ibid.</i> 141 An uncultivated native of the Andes region of Peru, the tamarillo is also found wild in India, Sri Lanka and parts of South East Asia. It was from the hilly regions of northern India that New Zealand's first seeds came, introduced by the Auckland nursery of D. Hay and Sons in 1891. At first only the yellow and purple varieties were grown and it was not until the end of World War I that the now almost universal red variety was bred at Mangere, Auckland. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,31</i>)

>  Two well-known New Zealand exports, neither of them native, are tree-tomatoes and Chinese gooseberries, but neither is called that by the exporters. The tomatoes are a South American fruit, a rich source of vitamin C and long popular in New Zealand. About twenty years ago, the name <i>tamarillo</i> was deliberately contrived, much as a brand name is. On the basis of <i>tomato</i>, a diminutive<i> -ill-</i> was added; then <i>tomatillo</i> was altered, in honor of a legendary Maori tribal hero, Tama-te-kapua, to <i>tamatillo</i>; and then a change to <i>tamarillo</i> was considered more euphonious. And so a new word was born. <i>(1985,Verbatim,2/11</i>) [NOTES: (G.W.Turner)]

>  <i>tamarillo n.</i> (pl. <i>tamarillos</i>) subtropical tree with bright red fruit; this fruit. [invented word] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,784</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 490]]

>  Too late in the day for kiwifruit of course but there was still a European market out there for tamarillos. <i>(1987,Metro Fiction,13</i>) [NOTES: (O'Sullivan Wonderland)]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  How she must have shuddered when newspapers referred to the Crusaders' red of her suit as trendy tamarillo, when obviously the imagery she wanted was sav in tomato sauce. <i>(Sep 2, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

>  <b>LAW</b> Spiced Tamarillo Mould. The strong colour and flavour of tamarillos make an exciting jelly to serve on its own or with plain mousse or ice-cream. <i>(1988,Dessert Cookbook, A,31</i>)

>  Nowadays, having sold his engineering business, Mr Brown runs his property company largely from the office in his home on a tamarillo orchard. <i>(Jul 15, 2000,Dominion,20</i>)

>  [Hdg] Regrets over tamarillo trial. A Northland anti-genetic engineering group leader says she never wanted to sign a deal silencing protesters over a tamarillo trial. <i>(Jul 11, 2002,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

>  There was a tamarillo tree - it never used to be there, but I do remember our dad taking an axe to a row of peach trees just before he left home. <i>(Oct 6, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

### tamure
 
 <i>n.</i> snapper
