>  English Name Native Name Rock Cod Whapuka Snapper Tamuri ... ... <i>(1849,NZ Journal,9/125</i>) [NOTES: (quoting Anglo Maori Warder Oct 5)]

>  <b>LUSH</b> <i>(Nov 19, 1850,Auckland Journals,38,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DRURY</b> FISH [MET WITH IN PELORUS]. Rock Cod - Pakiri of the natives, red and brown, or black, very numerous. Snapper - Tamuri, not abundant. ... <i>(1855,Sailing Directions,67</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> ... the Snapper (Pagrus unicolor). This name, by which it is best known, is adopted from Australia, its Maori name Tamure being seldom used by Europeans. ... The Snapper is not reputed to occur south of the Kaikoura Peninsula, the fish which usually goes by that name in the Dunedin market being the Tarakihi. <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,106</i>)

>  <b>BAINES</b> ... but big Tamuri or Schnapper, by which name it is `best known' to Europeans, is not very good eating ... <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,163</i>)

>  [Native or Common Name] Tamure, Snapper .. [Scientific Name] <i>Pagrus unicolor </i> <i>(1877,TrNZI,9/485</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]

>  [Native or Settlers' Name] Tamure, Snapper .. [Scientific Name] <i>Pagrus unicolor </i> <i>(1879,TrNZI,11/382</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]

>  <b>HAY</b> In the bays and tidal rivers are the mango or sharks - the most highly-prized food-fish - the tamure or schnapper, the whapuka or rock-cod, the kahawai or mackerel, the porahi or herring, the kanae or mullet, the patiki or sole, and many others. On the shores are oysters, mussels, cockles, mutton-fish, crabs, and other shell-fish in profuse abundance. In the fresh-water creeks are eels (tuna), lampreys (pipiharau), and whitebait (inanga). <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/151</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Ta[acute]mure, n</i>. the Maori name for the New Zealand <i>Schnapper</i> fish (q.v.). [1820, 1845 Wakefield, 1855, 1879] <i>(1898,Austral English,456</i>)

>  <i>Ta-mui.re</i> (ta.-mui.ra.), <i>n</i>. [Maori.] The Australian schnapper. See SCHNAPPER. [Local, N. Z.] <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2032</i>)

>  PAGROSOMUS AURATUS (Forster). <i>Snapper; Tamure.</i> ... Distribution: Known as far south as Jackson's Bay and Port Molyneux. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/116</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  Other fish such as kahawai(<i>Arripis trutta</i> (Forster)), tamure (<i>Pagrosomus auratus</i> (Forster)), snapper and moki (<i>Latridopsis ciliaris</i> (Forster)), are also caught. <i>(1926,TrNZI,58/615</i>) [NOTES: (Buck)]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Pagrosomus aratus</i> (Forster). SNAPPER; TAMURE. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,35,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BEST</b> The following fish were caught with hook and line: araara, maomao, moki, mango, ngoiro, pakirikiri, kahawai, tamure, tarakihi, and warehou. The kanae, kehe, kopipiro, moho, nanu and other species were taken by net. <i>(1929,Fishing Methods,43</i>)

>  <i> tamure</i>, a sea-fish (Pagrus unicolor), a brightly-coloured vigorous fish, a favourite in the market: often called snapper, and as often and wrongly as schnapper (as in Supplement). <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/150</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>LYON</b> The class of fish [available] varied with the locality - in the north the tamure or snapper, and the patiki or flounder, were the most common. <i>(1952,Faring South,131</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> SNAPPER <i>Chrysophrys auratus (Bloch & Schneider) .</i>.. Maori name: TAMURE The Snapper, one of the most valuable food fishes both in New Zealand and Australia, was described in 1801 from a specimen caught in Queen Charlotte Sound. <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,98</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> SNAPPER ... <i>Other names: Chrysophrys auratus</i>; `schnapper'; bream (`brim'); tamure (Maori). Term bream is applied to small specimens in Australia and North Auckland. <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,239</i>)

>  <i> ~ n</i>. Maori name for the snapper. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),626</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> SNAPPER [<i>Chrysophrys auratus</i>] ... The name bream is applied to small examples, and the Maori name tamure is still in use about northern coasts. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,42</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Family SPARIDAE. ... the coppery, turquoise-dotted snapper or tamure, <i>Chrysophrys auratus</i> ... , commonest north of Cook Strait. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,221</i>)

>  <i>tamure ... n. </i>Maori name for the snapper [Maori] <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),19</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>SNAPPER </i>(a) Scientific name: <i>Chrysophrys auratus.</i> (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Golden Snapper (c) Other common names: Schnapper; Brim (small snapper at Nelson); Tamure. (d) Principal localities where taken: From Nelson northwards to both coasts of the North Island. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,45</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> Snapper, or <i>tamure</i> in Maori, is our most popular food fish and also one of the most plentiful, particularly in the waters around the top half of the North Island. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,79</i>)

>  [species name] snapper .. [scientific name] <i>Chrysophrys auratus </i>.. [other name(s)] schnapper, tamure. See also premium snapper <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

Don't confuse these sawdusts with tanalised sawdust (stained green) or you will have a gardening disaster on your hands. <i>(Feb 13, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

### tandem cowshed

### tandem shed
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also herringbone](hea-hom.md#herringbone),[rotary](roa-ruv.md#rotary),[walk-through](../W#walk-through))

>  <b>McMEEKAN</b> The tandem shed consists of a set of bails which the cows stand in line, head to tail... on a platform 30 inches above the milker's level. <i>(1960,Grass to Milk,149</i>)

>  <b>McMEEKAN</b> There are three commonly used types of milking shed in New Zealand - the walk-through, the tandem and the herringbone. ... [149] The walk-through shed is strictly a New Zealand invention. ... It was born of the ingenuity of farmers faced with the task of milking large herds on their own, or with little help. ... [150] The herringbone is a New Zealand development of the tandem type. ... In this type of shed the cows are angle-parked in two rows separated by the milking `well' which is thirty inches below the cow platforms. <i>(1961,Grass to Milk,148,149,150</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a model of cowshed where cows stand in line, head to tail, to be milked.

>  .. the Christies say that the tandem shed allows them to treat their cows as individuals. <i>(Jun 10, 1958,NZ Dairy Exporter,39</i>)

>  <b>MCMEEKAN</b> Some tandems have a special washing bail attached .. If a farmer decides to build a tandem shed, it is much better to scrap the washing bail idea and to wash and stimulate in the bail. <i>(1960,Grass to Milk,153</i>)

>  <b>MAF</b> Tandem : Cows stand in a straight line for milking at the side. They can enter and leave each unit individually. [tandem bail] <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,39</i>)

### tanekaha
 
 <i>n.</i> an indigenous pine characterised by lacy leaf appearance. [ORIGIN: Maori, <i>Phyllocladus trichomanoides</i> aka parsley leafed pine, parsley-leafed pine, parsley-leaved pine, parsley pine, celery pine, found in upper tip of South Island and in the North Island north of a line from Mokau to Mahia Peninsula - latitude 39 degrees.]

>  Not so many know the curious philocladus, the tanekaha, or celery-pine, so odd in details of growth, so shapely in whole, brilliantly clothed in yellow and red,with a bark that brings in a high price for the tanning of ladies' gloves. <i>(Oct 12, 1935,Christchurch Star-Sun,14</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Mr Fairburn tried the <i>Tanakaha</i> to split for shingles ... <i>Phyllocladus trichomanoides</i>, =CELERY PINE Maori <i>taanekaha </i> <i>(Jul 23, 1828,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,139,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Went with the Sawyers up to Waikino; felled one <i>Kauri</i> and one <i>Tanakaha</i>; ... <i>(Jan 28, 1834,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,359,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McDONNELL</b> A great variety of hard-wood grows at New Zealand, admirably adapted for the timbering of any sized ships; among them is the boride[e-acute], rattar[as-acute], taraide[ase-acute], mai[a-acute], totara[a1-acute], koi katoa[a2-acute], toa[a-acute] toa[a-acute], tani raha[as-acute], to wai[a-acute], reiva[a-acute] reiva[a-acute], tana[as-acute], and many others. <i>(1834,Extracts From a Journal,19,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> Tarnicar [<i>tanekaha</i>] resembles Coudie in size but is more perishable wood and more yellow. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),33,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> Tanekaha (<i>Podocarpus asplenifolius</i>, or <i>Phyllocladus trichomanoides</i>) - This regular, beautiful, and highly-ornamental tree, is found on hilly lands, or in dry shaded woods. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),38</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> Many other trees abounded in Pamaki [a's-acute], such as the kaikatea [a2-acute], towai, rewa-rewa, totara [a's-acute], puriri, rata [a's-acute], kahika [a2-acute], tipow, tanikaha [a2-acute], rimu, and various kinds of akkas, all of which will be found described in the Appendix (Note 7). <i>Ibid.</i> 2/391 <i>Tanikaha</i> [a2,a3-acute] <i>(pinus asplenifolius)</i> g. coniferae, is a very valuable species of pine, very serviceable to the shipwright and general builder. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/109</i>)

>  <b>MATTHEWS</b> Tane-Kaha (a pine) <i>(1840,Reports and Essays,15</i>)

>  ... tanakaa[a-acute] <i>(1841,NZ Journal,2/31/81</i>) [NOTES: (G. Bennet)]

>  <b>HODSKIN</b> Tanekaha is a valuable species of pine ... <i>(1841,Narrative of Eight Months Sojourn in NZ, A,27</i>)

>  <b>BIDWILL</b> The Tanekaha [fn <i>Phyllocladus trichomanoides</i>] is also occasionally to be met with, but only on the steep sides of gullies; ... <i>(1841,Rambles in NZ,37 and fn,1952 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> ... I must especially mention [from the Middle island] a new species of the parsley-leaved pine (Phyllocladus trichomanoides), which the natives call E Hutu, to distinguish it from the tanekaha, another species of the same genus. <i>Ibid </i> 1/427 ... Tanekaha ... Phyllocladus trichomanoides ... one [pine] is called hutu by the natives, and is a Phyllocladus; ... <i>Ibid.</i> 2/385 Tanekaha - a pine-tree (<i>Phyllocladus trichomanoides</i>) <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/188</i>)

>  <b>COLENSO</b> [Maoris] used the tough, forked branches if the Tanekaha <i>(Phyllocladus trichomanoides)</i> and Kahikatoa <i>(Leptospermum scoparium)</i> for the hooks for the fish. <i>(1844,Excursion in the Northern Island of NZ</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Colenso Excursion in the Northern Island in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 6]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> 15. Tanekaha, (<i>Phyllocladus trichomanoides.</i>) This tree is not very common in the central and southern parts of New Zealand; ... <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,143</i>)

>  <b>McKILLOP</b> The tanekaha tree yields a black or brown dye, and the tuhuhi a blue-black dye from the wood and bark, as does the mako; the whakou also yields a handsome blue. <i>(1849,Reminiscences,162</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Phyllocladus <i>trichomanoides,</i> Don. ... Nat. names, `Tanekaha' north of the Thames, and `Toa Toa' south of that river, <i>Col. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants  235</i>)

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> 5. Taneka .... (say Pitch Pine.) <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/141</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Tanekaha, <i>Col[enso]. Phyllocladus trichomanoides.</i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  ... the graceful fern-plumaged Kawaka, ([<i>Libocedrus Doniana, Hook: f.</i>]), the handsome celery-leaved Tanekaha, <i>(Phyllocladus trichomanoides,)</i> ... the ornate Tawhai, <i>(Fagus Menziesii,)</i> and ... the ... Tawhai-rau-nui, <i>(Fagus fusca,). </i> <i>Ibid..</i> 34 The channelled stems of the Neinei (<i>Dracophyllum latifolium)</i>, and the ... saplings of Toatoa, or Tanekaha <i>(Phyllocladus trichomanoides)</i>, made valued walking-sticks. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/4</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  <b>LASLETT</b> <i>(1875,Timber and Trees,306</i>) [NOTES: (Morris)]

>  <b>KIRK</b> TANEKAHA.- <i>(Phyllocladus trichomanoides.) </i>... more abundant in the Province of Auckland. <i>(1875,Durability NZ Timbers,12</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Tanekaha ... is a larger tree again. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/191</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> <i>Phyllocladus trichomanoides</i>, Don. Tanekaha, Celery-leaved Pine.--A slender handsome tree 60 feet high; trunk rarely exceeds 3 feet in diameter: <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,125</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>P[hyllocladus] trichomanoides, D.Don. ... Tanekaha; Toatoa; Celery-leaved Pine. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,658</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Phyllocladus trichomanoides (The Celery-leaved Pine [The Celery Pine). </i>The native name of this tree - <i>Tanekaha</i> - is said to signify <i>virile</i>, or <i>strong in growth. </i> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,76,1940 [79] ed.</i>)

>  <i>tanekaha n, </i>-s [Maori] : CELERY-TOPPED PINE <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,2336</i>)

>  <i>tanekaha n.</i> New Zealand tree, with straight-grained wood, variety of pine. [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),626</i>)

>  <i>tanekaha ... n</i>. N.Z. tree, with straight-grained wood [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),19</i>)

>  <i>tanekaha n. NZ</i> celery-topped pine. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,784</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  tanekaha, a tree (Phyllocladus Trichomanoides), celery-leaved pine. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/146</i>) [NOTES: Notes Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  The other and unspecified timbers include poplar, miro, rata, tanekaha, taraire, mangeao, blue-gum, pukatea, rewarewa, silver-pine, hinau, puriri, kamahi, kawaka, maire, macrocarpa, yellow-pine, and willow. <i>(Jan 11, 1929,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  It has been found that kowhai, puriri, kauri, houhere, and rimu thrive in these unusual surroundings, but tanekaha, birch, and a few other trees cannot get sufficient water or are affected by the dust and smoke. <i>(Aug 17, 1935,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  The trade of the place is confined to the export of timber.. and sometimes small quantities of tanekaha bark and gum. <i>(Oct 11, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,7</i>)

>  <b>Crowe</b> In its slender, mature form, kaikomako makes a good specimen tree, well suited to small gardens. <i>(2009,Which Native Tree?,31</i>)

>  The association decided on a most comprehensive display, which will take the form of two large easels containing eight to ten pieces each of the various timbers produced in the districts, such as rimu, totara, kahikatea, maire, miro, tanekaha, rewa rewa, black birch, rata, manuka[,] puriri, etc. <i>(Aug 11, 1906,Grey River Argus,1</i>)

>  The land to the water's edge abounds with Pohutukawa, Tanekaha, Kauri timber, while the facilities for building and launching are not to be surpassed. <i>(Feb 4, 1874,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>tanekaha</i>: moderate-sized tree 50 ft. to 70 ft. high. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,194</i>)

>  The price of tanekaha bark in Auckland is usually about forty dollars per ton. * <i>(Dec 15, 1883,Auckland Weekly News,21</i>)

>  The plant is correctly named.. the tanekaha, but is far better known as the celery-leaved pine. <i>(Nov 4, 1924,Otago Witness,17</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [taniwha](t-ten.md#taniwha))

### tanga
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Called Te Atakura, the programme was designed to provide people strong in Maori language and tanga, who could act as role models for Maori pupils. <i>(Dec 2, 1987,Auckland  Star</i>) [NOTES: (Race Conciliator puts onus on teachers) (p.c. Ross Clark)]

### tangahangaha
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Pseudolabrus celidotus</i>, spotty, pakirikiri

>  <b>BUCK</b> <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,2/215</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [ORIGIN: Samoan]

### tangao
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [tangeao](t-ten.md#tangeao))

### tangaroa
 
 <i>n.</i> ?? [ORIGIN: Maori, citation unclear; Tangaroa guardian of the sea; waning moon (Ryan) ???]

### Tangaroa
 
 <i>n.</i> god of the sea, guardian of the sea, Neptune

>  <b>COLLINS</b> He is a native of the district of Ho-do-doe, (which is in Doubtles bay,) of which district Too-gee's father is the Etang-a-roa[macron]h, or high priest; and to that office the son succeeds on his father's death. <i>(1804,English Colony in NSW,342</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> <i>Tangaroa (Tah-nga-ro-ah)</i>: God of the Sea. <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,50</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> <i>(1938,Brown Man's Burden,21</i>)

>  Place them on the stone. In that way Tangaroa is placated and the offering still lives for when the tide comes in. <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/31</i>)

### Tangaroa Accord
 
 <i>n.</i> a group of 16 iwi, including most tribes north of Taupo

>  The tribes of the Tangaroa Accord support the [Waitangi Fisheries] commission's Ahu Whakamua proposal, based on the best of tribal traditions, which is to suppress differences for the good of all... The 16 iwi of the Tangaroa Accord, which includes most tribes north of Taupo, represent more than 54 per cent of the Maori population, but these tribes will get just 32 per cent of the Ahu Whakamua allocation. <i>(Nov 13, 2002,Dominion Post,B9</i>)

### tangata
 
 <i>n.</i> a human being [ORIGIN: Maori, Maori tangata person (either sex), pl. tangata humankind, people (Ryan 239)]

>  <i>English New Zeland South-sea Islands</i> A Chief Eareete Earee A Man Taata Taata A Woman Ivahino Ivahine <i>(Mar 31, 1770,Cook's Journals,1/286,1955 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> A Comparative Table of the Various Languages in the Isles of the South-Sea ... [English] A Man ... [New-Zeeland] Tangata [a1-macron, a2-macron grave, a3-macron] <i>(1778,Observations Made During a Voyage,284</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> T|angata; man; male or female. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,207</i>)

>  man - ton'gata [tangata] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,109,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: c1826-1827 list of Maori words in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 109]

>  What was this country to him now, with its spirits of old tangata haunting the tree-tops ... <i>(1966,NZ Short Stories,93,1976 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Wilson `End of the River')]

>  <i>tangata n. NZ</i> a human being, a person of either sex. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,784</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 491]]

>  tangata, man; human being. In Hawaii dialect `t' becomes `k' and `n'" `n'" whence tangata becomes kanaka, the vulg. Pakeha for `man' in the Pacific, heavily accented on the second "a": ta'ata in Tahiti: all three `a's' are equally light in accent. (In OED under kanaka.) <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/158</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]



 
 <i>n.</i> foreigner

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> T|angata ke,<i>s</i>. A foreigner, another person. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,207</i>)

### tangata kaitiaki
 
 <i>n.</i> [ORIGIN: Maori, lit. 'people caretaker']

>  The following have been nominated as the Tangata Kaitiaki for managing customary food-gathering from Mahurangi to Bream Tail extending 130km in an easterly direction: ... <i>(Nov 21, 2006,NZ Herald,C7</i>)

>  The taking of fish, aquatic life or seaweed (ie, customary food-gathering) may not take place in the area/rohe moana without an authorisation from a Tangata Kaitiaki. <i>(May 4, 2007,Whakatane Beacon,26</i>)

### tangata Maori
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori person [(Orsman)(Maori)... very frequently used to distinguish objects from others having special characteristics; thus, ... <i>tangata maaori, man, human being, </i>as opposed to a supernatural being: ... later, <i>man of the Polynesian race</i>, not a foreigner, the distinction not being confined to colour]

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> One of Cook's peculiarities was to run down the Maoris. ... Waipeha, on the other hand, always stood up manfully for Tongata Maori. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/6/103 Fish in plenty, fowl but scanty, flesh none, save a rat, so poor Tongata Maori had to fall back upon himself when the craving for animal food seized him, . <i>(1881,Poenamo,2/3/70</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> Waipeha was equal to any amount of taihoaism, and tangata Maori loved him accordingly. <i>(1881,Poenamo,88</i>)

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> A considerable number of natives including about thirty of the Tangata Mauri, or heads of tribes, ... <i>(1834,Narrative of Two Visits,108</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> `<i>Plurry pakeha</i>, eh? You got the skin like the Maori, but you're not <i>tangata</i> Maori. There isn't any Maori who doesn't eat the <i>hou-hou</i>.' <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,148</i>)

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> Tangata maori is the native in contradistinction to Tangata pakeha, the stranger. <i>(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter),00</i>)

>  I. <i>Maori,</i> a. 1. <i>native</i>. 2. He wai maori; <i>fresh water. </i> -->A. The original Maori adjectival use is recorded in early English texts(as is the substantival), translated as `native' COPPER MAORI (q.v.) would fall within this use (contrast MAORI COPPER), as would PAKEHA MAORI. -->[J.C. Andersen Maori Names p.30 notes "<i>Korari Maori</i>: Here McCrae uses the word Maori in its usual sense signifying `normal, usual, ordinary. Not until long after this time did the word come to mean what Euopeans assume it actually means, and in which the natives themselves now aquiesce - that is the name of the people, the New Zealanders. It had no such meaning in the mind of the natives until a comparatively recent date, nor was it apparently so used by them to any extent until about 1850, though Wakefield [? which one ?] and some English writers had begun the use some years before. In earliest times <i>tangata maori</i>did not mean man of the Maori race but human being as opposed to supernatural being; <i>wai maori</i> was `fresh water'; <i>Whetu maori</i> `inferior stars' Cook spoke of the inhabitants of NZ as <i>Indians,</i> early Europeam writers as <i>natives</i> or New Zealanders. [Andersen is paraphrasing Williams Dictionary here.] <i>(1871,Dictionary of the Maori Language,75</i>)

>  New Zealander - ton|g|ata ma|u|ree [tangata maori] <i>(1826,Boultbee Journal,109,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: c1826-1827 list of Maori words in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 109; ALSO 1826-28 Boultbee Vocabulary in 1979 Begg 282]

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> [contrast tangata ma] Mr Hamlyn is so good a Linguist that ... the people got up ... to look at him closer as the[y] said he was a `Tangata Mar', or white Man but that he had the Tongue of a `Tangata Mouri', native; ...[`native' is a marginal note] <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),71,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> They [sc. Maoris] were all exceedingly pleased with `Ko te pakeha [a1-acute] pai, kaore [a/e-acute] ano redi e na [a-acute] tangata [a2,a3-acute] maori,' (the excellent white man, who permitted the natives to do what they pleased, without being angry in return). <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/75</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> ... kainga maori or native village <i>Ibid.</i> 123 ... the national nomenclature is that of na tangnata [sic] maori or native people .... the word maori representing the term of native or indigenous. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,2/47</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> They call themselves <i>Maori</i> which means<i> indigenous, aboriginal</i>; or <i>Tangata Maori</i> indigenous men; in opposition to Pakea which means stranger, or Pakea mango mango, a very black stranger, a negro. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/370 Maori - indigenous, native, natural; haere maori - walking, as a native [sic: not `maori'] has no other chance[?check]; wai maori - fresh water <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/7,1963 ed.</i>)

>  The pas, villages, and houses of the people are not visible, not actually to be seen by mortal (Tangata Maori) eyes. <i>(1909,Journal of the Polynesian Society,III/3</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> When the Bar was crossed Canoes or in the Native Mourie [Maori] Tongue, Walker [<i>waka</i>] Mouries, or Native Boats, boarded us, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 30 [here TANGATA MOURI felt as a noun with Mouries pl cp. NATIVE] Crow made all hands of the Natives (or Tangata Mouries) give us some of their Dances, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 53 The Sawyers tell the Natives when in Anger, They will exterminate the `Tangata Mouries' as the `Rah Parkieah' has the `Rah Mourie'. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),29,1963 ed.</i>)

>  ...he will find that originally "maori tangata" meant an "ordinary man" or a man native to the place where he was living. <i>(Jan 18, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),6</i>)

>  Before that can be done, we must try to remove the tap or tappers which lawless chiefs for the most part put on the principal points of rivers; they are traps in fact to catch the unwary and extort payment from them; they are, as some of the Maories said to me, only the mau mingering of the tangata Maori, the lying for the purpose of which is to have pretexts for roads and forays, and keep the rest in subjection and slavery: it keeps the land locked up, however, and you may travel fifty to a hundred miles on the banks of some of our rivers without seeing a native. <i>(Jul 12, 1853,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a European person (contrastive with tangata Maori) [ORIGIN: Maori]

 <i>n.</i> (humorous)

### tangata tiriti
 
 <i>n.</i> Pakeha (literally 'people of the Treaty')

>  Does he agree that by that definition, while Pakeha cannot call ourselves 'indigenous' or 'tangata whenua', we do belong here by right of Te Tiriti o Waitangi, and could justly call ourselves 'tangata tiriti'. <i>(Aug 4, 2004,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

>  Pakeha sometimes feel they miss out. Who are we? Are we second-rate? No, we're not, and the reason we're not is because, as Mason Durie so beautifully put it, we're tangata tiriti. We're legitimately here by virtue of the Treaty. <i>(Jul 16, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),33</i>)

>  Paradoxes swam out of the creation: culture as art, the exotic as workaday and, miraculously, exiles creating connections with and between tangata whenua and tangata tiriti. <i>(Dec 14, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),41</i>)

>  Two cultures signed the Treaty of Waitangi - one the tangata whenua represented by many iwi chiefs, the other the tangata tiriti represented by the British Crown. <i>(Feb 21, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)

>  Debate on GM issues ... is made unique by the partnership between tangata whenua and tangata tiriti created by the Treaty of Waitangi. <i>(Aug 11, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

>  .. the first indigenous people had made a treaty with the Crown that permitted colonisation and gave us two streams of people with rights to be here, tangata whenua and tangata tiriti, to use Eddie Durie's characterisation of them. <i>(Sep 2007,English in Aotearoa,73</i>)

### tangata whaiora
 
 <i>phr.</i> a mentally ill person [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  The Like Minds, Like Mine movement to promote respect and equity for people with experience of mental illness/tangata whaiora has taken off and there is great community support. ... Tangata whaiora in the Eastern Bay report a greater feeling of acceptance and less discrimination than when we started five years ago, but there is still some way to go. <i>(Oct 5, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,2</i>)

>  I have experience of mental illness. Twenty-eight years ago as a teenage university student, I began the long haul as a tangata whaiora (patient). <i>(Jan 10, 2006,Whakatane Beacon,4</i>)

### tangata whenua
 
 <i>n.</i> . [ORIGIN: Maori, earliest people in NZ, as distinct from later boat-people; apparently Maori, but not exclusive of Moriori]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Peace and prosperity marked the time when there was cooperation between the newcomer and the <i>tangata whenua. </i> <i>(1963,Ratana,2,1972 ed.</i>)

>  In addition, the concept of tangata whenua (people of the land) remains intact despite the alienation of the land. <i>(1984,Te Maori,31</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> Somes Island was Matiu and Ward Island was Makaro ... in the days when the Ngai Tara, the tangata whenua, first lived there. <i>(1985,G'day Country,114</i>)

>  Statement on the Status of Maori people as Tangata Whenua of Aotearoa New Zealand ... The Commission recognises the importance of Maori culture ... to all people of Aotearoa New Zealand. <i>(Mar 1988,Human Rights Commission Focus,1</i>)

>  Tangata Whenua as a concept requires some explanation. It is often translated as people of the land, but such a translation doesn't correctly convey the deeper meaning. I prefer to translate the phrase as `people who are the land'. <i>(Mar 1988,Human Rights Commission Focus,1</i>) [NOTES: (H. Kaa)]

>  "I have cited a long list of great authorities, all agreeing that the continent of Asia was the land from which the Maoris came .. They are one people - Tangata Maori in India, in Indonesia, and in the Pacific ..." <i>(May 2, 1913,Poverty Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  Data concerning the previous arrivals on these shores had gradually accumulated until we were in a position to state that the ancestors of the so-called tangata whenua (aboriginal people) landed on these shores about thirty generations ago. <i>(Jul 15, 1930,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  NGATI POROU IN AUCKLAND Your Taura Here seeks a Kaitiaki A Rohe .. *To foster relationships with Te haukaenga, tangata whenua, matawaka ... <i>(Jul 17, 2006,Dominion Post,D12</i>)

>  There were many different tribes of these tangata whenua ... <i>(May 14, 1896,Otago Witness,42</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [ORIGIN: Maori, people of the land, not necessarily Maori or NZ]

>  <b>McGILL</b> Despite that, readers were assured, folk were friendly and you could find Maori and Islander values here parallel to the strong clan-like traditions of the Presbyterians; it sounded like the Presbyterians were to be seen as the tangata whenua, the Maori and Islanders as late arrivals. <i>(1985,G'day Country,174</i>)

>  In recent months I have drawn two arrows, as it were from my own Maori lexicon. I used the word tangata whenua to describe locals. I used the word putiputi to describe freeloaders. This last word is not to be found in any standard Maori dictionary but was in common usage a good while ago. <i>(Nov 28, 1986,Contact (Wgtn),2</i>)

>  The paradox that still besets Australia is that it has learnt to live and prosper with its many racial migrant communities while remaining brutally ignorant ... about its tangata whenua. <i>(Dec 12, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori person or persons [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Not a tangata whenua in sight, either, which is one of the rich ironies of this whole business. <i>(Aug 30, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),5</i>)

>  They go down into the east in great numbers - a vast horde of stalwart warriors, prepared to force their way through the tangata whenua who may oppose them. <i>(Jul 30, 1896,Otago Witness,48</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> . [Moriori, Chatham Island Moriori]

>  <b>SIMPSON</b> Meanwhile at Chatham Island the <i>tangata whenua</i> - or Morioris as we now call them - found that their new home boasted no timber.... <i>(1950,Chatham Exiles,26</i>)

>  A story runs that the first armed clash between the emigrants and the tangata whenua or people of the soil, arose from Muaupoko treachery, as a result of when, two of the children of Te Rauparaha were slain and he himself barely escaped with his life. <i>(Apr 12, 1932,Evening Post,14</i>)

>  Unfortunately, New Zealand's earliest people, whoever they were, have left no permanent relics, being ignorant of of metals; but further research in the North may, even with this drawback, still throw some light on the traditional "tangata whenua" - "The People of the Land." <i>(May 14, 1910,Evening Post,4</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> "people of the land", the indigenous people; the local people, the local residents of a particular place [ORIGIN: Maori, NZ context; usually Maori; general and also with specific reference to a particular place within NZ]

>  <b>MACRAE</b> `That Lillian bird better watch which of the tangata whenua she mouths off like that in front of,' ... <i>(1988,Awful Childhoods,89</i>)

>  "The land wars are over, so the consent of the tangata whenua is required before customary title can be extinguished." <i>(Jul 5, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14.</i>)

>  The grey mists wreathe through the ivory towers as benighted geologists scutte around searching for goals on which to impact, for end-users with whom to be relevant, and for an ecological niche in which to embed cyclotherms amongst the legitimate aspirations of the <i>tangata whenua<i/>. <i>(Mar 1997,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,112/73</i>)

>  If some councillor made a racist statement I don't think we would put them on a committee that dealt with the tangata whenua. <i>(Dec 20, 1992,Sunday Times (NZ),3</i>)

>  We.. have reached a point where the Union Flag appearing in the canton has little significance for non-Britons, and even less for the tangata whenua. <i>(Aug 16, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),9</i>)

>  At large Maori gatherings when... there are visitors from other tribes whom the "tangata whenua," the sons of the soil, delight to honour, songs are chanted.. that date back centuries. <i>(Dec 1, 1928,NZ Free Lance,6</i>)

>  Up at Waitangi the tangata whenua of Aotearoa discuss their colonisation and whether to celebrate it. <i>(Mar 2, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),29</i>)

>  I took care, however, to arrive some time earlier than the Premier that I might be regarded as distinct from his party, "He tangata whenua," not "He Manuhiri," i.e., a resident and not a guest. <i>(Aug 3, 1895,Wanganui Chronicle,3</i>)

>  Later, climb previously uncharted peaks with the gorgeously craggy Mark Whetu - the first tangata whenua to climb Mt Everest under the terms of the Treaty of Waitangi. <i>(Oct 14, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),69</i>)

>  Labour MP Dover Samuels stood up to respond to the greetings of the tangata whenua. <i>(Jun 6, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),35</i>)

>  <b>Baucke</b> ...for the `tangata whenua' (resident) took precedence on his own soil; ... <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,54</i>)

>  <b>Howe</b> Tangata whenua - `whenua' (q.v.) is the placenta which was usually buried in a special place which would not be walked over and thus defiled by anyone. Thus a person's spiritual well being was linked with the land belonged to. `Tangata whenua' thus refers to the `people belonging to the land', who have therefore the right to live there according to their own traditions and values. <i>(1985,Towards Taha Maori,22</i>)

>  <b>Ihimaera</b> We are the tangata whenua, the people of the land. Ibid. 28 I want us to be in full view of the pa, so that the tangata whenua can see that we are here. <i>(1986,Matriarch, The,4</i>)

>  Paul Temm feels essentially unchanged by his experience on the tribunal, sitting at marae around the country, listening to the elders vent their frustrations in Maori, hongi-ing with both tangata whenua and manuhiri (visitors), chatting over tea and kai. <i>(Jun 21, 1986,Evening Post</i>)

>  [Hdg] Capitalism v tangata whenua... Clearly the ideologies of capitalism and tangata whenua are in conflict, so accommodations have to be made between Maori and Pakeha if they are to inhabit the same social space. <i>(Jul 24, 1982,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>)

>  And yet the tangata whenua of this country have to scrape, have to make do, have to put up with second-best in health, education, jobs and housing. <i>(May 12, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21</i>)

>  There were about fifty canoes containing the visitors, and about fifty had already arrived conveying the "tangata whenua." <i>(Jun 5, 1857,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  About 11 a.m. Nga ti Haua and the Tangata Whenua (tribes who called the meeting and provided the food) mustered their fighting men, in war costume, <i>i.e.,</i> armed with muskets or guns, and naked except a bandage or other covering round the middle of the body: in all about 300 or 400 marched on to the ground, where they formed in column. <i>(Jun 5, 1860,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  Again, from their behaviour they were a nuisance, not only to the Europeans, but also to the natives, the "tangata whenua" themselves. <i>(Mar 29, 1883,Waikato Times,2</i>)

>  <b>Zimmerman</b> And here again the club gave expression to a fundamental idea of Maori life, that of the obligation of hospitality of the tangata whenua, the man of the country, to whoever might chance to be his guest. <i>(1946,Where the People Sing,37</i>)

>  <b>Henderson</b> The taua muru party ... crouched with their weapons ready. The tangata whenua ... now sprang to their feet and danced a furious war dance. <i>(1948,Taina,43</i>)

>  tangata whenua the local people (e.g. as opp. to nga manuhiri) the visitors) (knowing the invaders were approaching, the tangata whenua had hidden their heirlooms). [M, = people of the land] <i>(1896,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,784</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  The Maori Land Court will then be asked to vest the reservation in a body corporate, which will include representation from the tangata whenua (local people), appointed by the court in trust for the community. <i>(Jun 29, 1988,Dominion,2</i>)

>  How a project ... with the blessing ... of all the tribes, including some but not all the tangata whenua Ngai Tahu, plus Pakeha-dom ... could have become so unstuck ... is a complex tale of human foibles. <i>(Mar 5, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  He has enormous trouble with certain words; for instance, he can't pronounce "tangata whenua" or "socialism" witrhout breaking out in hives and experiencing a gagging and choking sensation. <i>(Mar 13, 1982,Listener (New Zealand Listener),9</i>)

### tangata whenua-ness
 
 <i>n.</i> [[Ma. /"taNata"fenua/: Williams 494 <b>Tangata whenua</b>, people belonging to any particular place, natives.]]

>  "So what of Toi Kairakau, of Rauru, my history, my tangata whenua-ness, my 1000 missing years?" <i>(Nov 17, 2005,NZ Herald,A1</i>) [NOTES: Pita Sharples' maiden speech in parliament.]

### tangata whenuan
 
 <i>adj.</i> of the tangata whenua; Maori

>  As "Enid Barker, children's librarian, beautiful, witty, Tangata Whenuan" [she] looked beautiful in her sweeping crinoline skirt. <i>(Jun 8, 1993,Salient,22</i>)

### tangatiaki
 
 <i>n.</i> food guardian [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  .. a Kaikoura man was caught with 90kg of paua and the tangatiaki (food guardian) who issued the permit was charged with altering it to make the catch appear legal. <i>(Jul 12, 2005,NZ Herald,A7</i>)

### tangeao
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> T|ange |o; A tree used for fire-wood. <i>Litsaea calicaris</i>, a tree (Williams 379) <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,207</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> There are many other woods of much service to Europeans, differing in quality, among others: the 30 <i>Warangai</i> - 31 <i>Pata </i>-32 - <i>Niho</i> - 33 <i>Tangio,</i> - 34 <i> Maihoi</i> - 35 <i> Nene</i> [e's-acute] - 36 <i> Taraiti </i>- 37 <i>Kohihihu </i> - 38 <i>Waihupuku</i> - 39 <i> Kaikamoko</i> - 40 <i>Pukapuka - </i> - 41 <i>Karanghu</i> - 42 <i>Utuhutu</i> - 43 <i>Angiani</i> - 44 <i>Akkas </i> of various kinds. 45 <i>Parekireki</i>, &c. Of all these woods there are abundant quantities. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/400</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Tetranthera <i>calicaris</i>, Hook. fil. ... Nat. name, `Tangao'. [check spg] <i>(1853,Flora NZ,216</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Tangeao, <i>Col[enso]. Tetranthera calicaris. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  Of our shrubs and smaller timber trees, several are of strikingly beautiful growth ... (<i>e.g.</i>) the Houhere, <i>Hoheria populnea</i> ...; the Horopito <i>Drimys axillaris</i>; the Manuka-rau-riki, <i>Leptospermum ericoides</i>; the Kohuhu.. , the Tangeao, <i>Tetranthera calicaris.</i>... <i>Ibid.</i> 46 The Tangeao or Mangeao, (<i>Tetranthera calicaris</i>,) is a small tree, also confined to the Northernmost parts of the Island ... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/5</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i> L[itsaea] calicaris, Benth and Hook.f. </i>... A perfectly glabrous closely branched leafy tree 30-40 ft. high ... NORTH ISLAND: ... to Rotorua and East Cape. ... <i>Mangeao; Tangeao</i>. ... wood strong, tough, and elastic ... <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,603</i>)

>  The mangeao (or tangeao) is seldom used. It is useful for boat-building purposes, as it possesses the property attributed to the oak, of closing up instantly should the wood be pierced by a bullet. <i>(Nov 5, 1861,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  57 Lithograph of tangiao foliage. <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,17</i>)

### tangelo
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Tangelos are citrus hybrids obtained by crossing the tangerine and the grapefruit. <i>(1964,Duncan & Davies' Catalogue,231</i>)

>  New Zealand-grown mandarins and tangelos are gaining steadily in popularity with the public. <i>(May 1968,Orchardist NZ,102</i>)

>  Two kinds of citrus which have adapted well to New Zealand's relatively cold growing conditions.. are the so-called New Zealand grapefruit and, more recently, the seminole tangelo. <i>(Jan 1972,Orchardist NZ,25</i>)

>  Tangelos are a group of citrus fruits derived as hybrids of mandarins and grapefruits which are more or less intermediate in character between the parent species. <i>(1974,Orchardist NZ,239</i>) [NOTES: Check month of issue]

>  <b>MALCOLM</b> Today there are still orange trees similar to the modern Tangelo. <i>(1982,Where it all Began,41</i>)

>  With the large number of Seminole Tangelo trees in New Zealand a considerable number of bud mutations should be expected to occur. <i>(Aug 1986,Orchardist NZ,252</i>)

>  <b>MATTHEWS</b> Tangelos are the result of hybridising tangerines and grapefruit. <i>(1986,NZ Garden Book,244</i>)

>  With the large number of Seminole Tangelo trees in New Zealand a considerable number of bud mutations should be expected to occur. <i>(Aug 1986,Orchardist NZ,252</i>)

### tangi
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Tangihanga leave [headline] The CSU is to lodge a claim for the recognition of tangihanga leave. The claim is based on the belief that tangihanga is a grieving process, and that as a consequence, extended bereavement leave should be granted those attending a tangi. <i>(Jun 18, 1986,PSA Journal,6</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [ORIGIN: Maori, lament; one cit: tangitary]

>  <b>MACKIE</b> Standing by our inn I heard a noise like the howling of dogs and asking a man who was passing what it was, he told us that it was the Maories at their pa .. howling or crying on acount of the death of one of their number, they call it `tangitary', wailing for the dead. <i>(Apr 8, 1853,Traveller under Concern,99,19I73 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> weakened or trivialised in European perceptions to the feeding of the guests at the TANGIHANA; then = feast (thence ? below confused with HUI or weakened to a drunken party) cf 19 cit.



 
 <i>n.</i> a crying or weeping as a ceremony of welcome, or meeting

>  <b>MUNDY</b> .. a friend who had been for some time in the colony pointed out this scene to me, as `a good case of tangi' <i>(1852,Our Antipodes,121</i>)

>  <i>(1840,Journal of Ensign Best</i>) [NOTES: [see cit. 1a. above]]

>  <b>ANGAS</b> On entering one of the enclosures .. these visitors commenced their salutations by pressing noses with each in succession, and then sat down in silence; the women setting up a <i>tangi</i>, or `crying of welcome'. <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/237</i>)

>  <b>POWER</b> Muskets were laid aside, hands shaken, noses rubbed, news talked over; and, while the feast was preparing, a considerable majority sat [32] down to indulge in a `<i>tangi</i>', as an expression of their pleasure at meeting. <i>(1849,Sketches in NZ,31</i>)

>  <b>SMITH</b> We .. were enlivened by a good hour's <i>tangi</i> between our guide and the woman .. at the end of which our guide .. knocked off, and fell upon a kite of potatoes ... <i>(Jan 13, 1858,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Jan 13 Smith Notes of a Journey in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 360]

>  <b>MANING</b> .. our chief advanced towards him, fell upon his neck embracing him in the most affectionate manner, commenced a <i>tangi</i>, or melancholy sort of ditty, ... and ... shed floods of tears. <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,3/56</i>)

>  <b>MOSER</b> As the reader may not know what a `tangi' is, I may briefly say, that when Maories meet after a long absence, they invariably have a good cry ..., and that the same `tangi' is likewise held over their dead ... <i>(1863,Mahoe Leaves,32</i>)

>  <b>GORST</b> All the old women turned out to `tangi' over him, according to the Maori fashion of saluting an old friend, ... [fn] The old native women always show affection by a sort of whimper, which is often prolonged for hours. This is called a `tangi'. <i>(1864,Maori King, The,322</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> When friends meet, who have been separated for some time, a `tangi,' or crying, takes place, the parties sitting in silence opposite to each other for perhaps half an hour, exchanging no words, but uttering melancholy wailings; then, all of a sudden, they approach, press noses, and commence laughing and chatting in the most lively manner possible. <i>(1866,Polynesia,150</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> Our college graduate arrives; the home-coming tangi and nose-greeting is over; .. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,278</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a funeral, a farewell [metaphorical, anglicised egs]

>  There have been merry Irish wakes, and tangis running beer, / <i>(1893,College Rhymes,36</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> Starkie thought he would like a bonfire, .. or one of those great native tangis, kumaras and shark and eel and sucking-pig cooked under the wet sacks on hot stones, ... <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,41</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> [Masses of grub and long orations] were inevitable at their <i>tangis</i> and <i>huis. </i> The <i>tangi</i> was a funeral, the hui a feast; but there wasn't much difference. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,121</i>)

>  <b>POTTS</b> These birds [sc gulls] .. when they meet together, after a few deep-toned barks or growls hold a regular `tangi', and utter most dismal wails and yells. <i>(1874,On Recent Changes in the Fauna,9</i>)

>  As to his gratuitous advice of burying it and holding a tangi at Kennedy's, I firmly believe the settlers will nurse it to maturity, and hold a meeting of rejoicing over its vigorous manhood, for getting rid of the baneful influence Rutland Chambers seems to exercise in road matters. <i>(Jul 13, 1883,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  If a night is missed, that is the night [the slugs] seem to hold a "tangi"and devour all the choice seedlings in sight. <i>(Feb 10, 1916,Feilding Star,2</i>)

>  Man-o'-war officers and men.. held a "tangi" on board last Saturday night over the ashes of the British team. <i>(Aug 27, 1904,NZ Observer,7</i>)

>  So in that sense, was the recent Labour leadership struggle the real death of Rogernomics - a convulsion the party had postponed, but needed to endure? "Yes," says Helen Clark, "we'd never held the tangi." <i>(Dec 18, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),38</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [no citations; ca1935 R. Mason The day after Parliament opens there is a short recess in memory of members who have died in the previous session. This is [still c1953] popularly known among M.P.s as a <i>tangi</i>, a time of mourning.]



 
 <i>n.</i>



 
 <i>n.</i>



 
 <i>n.</i> espec. a booze-party

>  <b>MILLER</b> A barrel of beer arrrived at the cookhouse after tea time, so the section settled down to a large evening. I missed most of the tangi. <i>(1939,Camps, Tramps and Trenches,131</i>) [NOTES: 1917 Sep 5 in 1939 Miller Camps Tracks and Trenches 131]

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> They'll be having a <i>tangi</i> [after the wedding] in the evening I understand. <i>(1926,Butcher Shop, The,60</i>)

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> Duke had a drink and went off, but the remainder set to for the usual tangi <i>(1928,Dawn Beloved,227</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Tangi</i>. n. and v. <i>Tangi,</i> a maori word for `a tribal gathering at a funeral: a dirge': `Now very commonly adopted here [New Zealand] by the upper classes, especially as an equivalent of the outmoded `beano'. E.g., Harold and I were on the tangi (or were tangi-ing) last night; or there is a big tangi on to-night,' Niall Alexander, letter of Oct. 22, 1939. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang,1194</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> I spent Christmas at the Bealey [hotel] for five consecutive years, and they were `tangis' that are worth remembering. <i>(1952,High Country Journey,132</i>)

>  <b>ROGERS</b> Hal's teeth flashed ... `Hislops are holding a <i>tangi</i> tonight. Tiare invited all of us. ... I'm taking Concertina Charlie over with me. Special invitation to bushwhackers.' <i>(1960,Long White Cloud, The,13</i>)

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> .. I had often fooled around with girls .. at a local hui or tangi. <i>(1964,Walk on the Beach,160</i>) [NOTES: (Not for a Seagull) [check if correct category???]]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> But I was mighty curious all the same - that must have been a real bottler of a tangi. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,100</i>)

>  <i>tangi ... n.</i> Maori for a lamentation, cry, dirge [Maori]; European New Zealanders use the word <i>tangi</i> to mean a celebration. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),19</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> Michael came back. They'd had a terrific tangi [sc a student party], he said. He drank a cup of black coffee .. and went to bed. <i>(1972,Brides of Price,170</i>)

>  Our occasional carousals were tolerated .. `What's that appalling racket?' Alexander had asked. `Oh, that's just Des having one of his tangis.' <i>(Jun 1973,Meanjin Quarterly,32/3/134</i>) [NOTES: (Dan Davin) [This or foll. cit. to be retained - correct date???]]

>  <b>DAVIN</b> `What's that appalling racket?' Alexander asked. `Oh, that's just Des having one of his tangi's.' <i>(1975,Breathing Spaces,131</i>)

>  <b>LAMBERT</b> Now, there are races, shows, ...football, ... dances, cinemas, <i>tangis, huis</i> and boxing ... <i>(1936,Pioneering Reminiscences of Old Wairoa,186</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> wailing

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> Their two boys upon hearing of the projected departure had set up such a howling <i>tangi</i> (shedding tears into all his possessions, so Toby declared) that to silence them the Twins promised they should also go. <i>(1930,NZ Memories,88</i>)

>  During the whole night the poor wretch kept up a horrible "tangi." <i>(Feb 1, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,21</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a mourning ceremony; lament. [also tangie, taki]

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> New Zealand colloquialisms which are of Maori origin include: .. <i>hold a tangi</i>: to hold a feast, party or meeting <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),116</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> This [91] had caused, he said, <i>nuee nuee tanghee tanghee</i> (a good deal of crying) among them ... <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/90</i>)

>  <i>tangi n.</i> Maori for a lamentation, cry, dirge [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),626</i>)

>  <b>POWER</b> All day long there had been a considerable howl over the dead and wounded, and to-[28]morrow the bodies will be carried to Waikanahi to have a tangi there ... <i>(1849,Sketches in NZ</i>) [NOTES: 1849 Power Sketches in NZ 27-28 Aug. 21st [1846].]

>  The Taiaroa Tangi / The Last Tribute to the Remains of a Great Chief [headlines] <i>(Apr 19, 1905,NZ Truth,9/5/6</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> When a messenger comes to your gate and cries, `Tama died last night,' the men unyoke their teams .. Ailing children are made into bundles, and away you rush to the tangi. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,130</i>) [NOTES: also 1905 Baucke White Man Treads 34, 128]

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> <i>Tangi (Tang-ee):</i> The Maori <i>wake</i>. Literally, `a crying.' <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,44</i>)

>  Gone are the days when the Maori was summoned to many momentous meetings to discuss real or fancied wrongs, or to attend a tangi to the departed. <i>(Apr 20, 1912,NZ Truth,356/4/1</i>)

>  <b>BEAGLEHOLE</b> <i>tangi</i> funeral celebrations and rites <i>(1946,Some Modern Maoris,xxii</i>)

>  <b>ASHTON-WARNER</b> I'll wash my grief away as the Maoris do after a tangi. [fn] Maori funeral. <i>(1958,Spinster,152</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> Then Mrs Te Weu died .. and was buried with Grace absent from the tangi. [fn] tangi - Maori funeral rites; lamentation. <i>(1960,Maori Girl,28</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> <i>Tangi</i> [title] <i>Ibid.</i> 448 Matui Jackson fell from the face of the dam, / .. / They buried him without a tangi. <i>(1979,Collected Poems,400</i>) [NOTES: [check name spg???] (1967) 1979 Baxter Collected Poems 400]

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> <i>tangi</i>: a wake, a ceremonial of mourning and farewell before burial. For an important person it may last two or three days. As a verb, the word means to cry or mourn. <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden,142</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> The coffin, covered with a beautiful kaitaka, was carried to a whaleboat and rowed across to a Maori [143] burial ground [at Stewart Island] .. then the women sat by the stream and began the low chanting .. of the Southern taki. <i>(1978,Acheron Journal,142</i>)

>  <i>tangi n. N.Z. 1.</i> a Maori funeral ceremony. <i>2. Inf.</i> a lamentation. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,1194</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 785; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 491]]

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> The time was opportune for another feast-day. .. there had not been a marriage .. for months now; and among the long-lived little community, neither had the occasion for a tangi arisen, altough it was evident that poor old Honi Tawa was not far now from the road to the Reinga. <i>(1938,Brown Man's Burden,3,1973 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Wedding Gift)]

>  <b>HULME</b> I have never seen someone die. What do you do? Pray? Tangi? Listen. <i>Ibid.</i> 449 Tangi = weep, mourn. <i>(1983,Bone People, The,365</i>)

>  The intelligence brought back by this messenger, who visited Rangihaeata's pah, is to the effect that that savage will be able to collect from Otaki, Wanganui, and other places, two hundred men, who will arrive at Porirua under pretence of holding a <i>tangi</i> over a chief lately dead, and will, afterwards, proceed to the Hutt and establish themselves at the Taita. <i>(May 16, 1846,New Zealand Spectator & Cook's Strait Guardian,2</i>)

>  In December of the same year, a large concourse of natives, from different parts of the Nelson Province, were assembled, on one of their periodical visits, at Porirua to hold a tangi or lamentation over some of their relatives recently deceased. <i>(Jul 17, 1860,Colonist (Nelson),3</i>)

>  He said he was obliged to hold a tangi; it was native custom. <i>(Nov 16, 1895,Hawke's Bay Herald,4</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> As a widow, a mother, they wail, at a death, at a tangi. <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,4</i>) [NOTES: Check whether page is 4 or 41]

>  Lahood feels <i>Numero Bruno</i> is like a continuing tangi for Lawrence. <i>(Jul 17, 2000,Dominion,14</i>)

>  To hold the doeful <i>tangi</i> or, / shed for him a tear <i>(Nov 21, 1860,Taranaki Punch,5</i>)

>  The drunken debauchery at Maori tangis is much to be deplored. <i>(Aug 13, 1904,NZ Observer,3</i>)

>  Some 200 came down the Waipa in their canoes, and were of course received with the usual "haeremais" and "tangis". <i>(Mar 8, 1873,Auckland Weekly News,14</i>)

>  <b>PAYTON</b> Then they collected all the bones into a heap, got up a <i>tangi</i> over them, and departed to look out for their next debauch, among some tribe that was not related to them. <i>(1888,Round about NZ,93</i>)

>  <b>PAYTON</b> <i>Tangi</i> literally means "a weeping", and is a ceremony of joy, or sorrow.<i>Ibid.</i> 332 Most of them got out of doing any real tangi business, that being generally left to several old women. <i>(1888,Round about NZ,328</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> At a tangi, of course, the body would be lying in state on the porch of the meetinghouse, and as we advanced there would be the karanga, calling to us to come and weep for the dead. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,109</i>)

>  I always felt sorry for the widows at those early tangi. <i>(Apr 1991,NZ Geographic,109</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> Nobody had seen a tangi as big as Uncle Pihopa's. He was a great chief, that old man who ate bishops. <i>(1951,Witch's Thorn, The,80</i>)

>  <b>BEAGLEHOLE</b> On the first day of the tangi kinsmen and tribal members, together with tribes related to that of the dead person, are notified of the death.<i>Ibid.</i> 258 The climax of the tangi is reached on the fourth day, the day of the burial. <i> Ibid. </i> 259 The abiding impression that one receives as one follows the course of a full-scale Maori tangi is that death is a very matter-of-fact event for the people. <i>(1946,Some Modern Maoris,257</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> In the old days tangis had gone on for so long that sometimes the deceased had tricked away through the cracks in the coffin. <i>(1951,Witch's Thorn, The,80</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> T|angi, <i>s</i>. A cry, lamentation; <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,207</i>)

>  cry - takki [tangi] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,112,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: c1826-1827 list of Maori words in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 112; ALSO 1826-28 Boultbee Vocabulary in 1979 Begg 285]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> .. an old lady put forth her miserable strains in a <i>tangi</i> ... <i>(Feb 19, 1835,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,413,1961 ed.</i>)

>  At daylight we were awakened by the howlings of some Mauries .. who were making the `Tangie' .. The high note of the `Tangie' is like a dog Baying at the moon .. there is the Tangie for the dead or sick - the `Tangie' of welcome or after a long absence and [sic] the `Tangie' at separation. <i>(Aug 1840,Journal of Ensign Best,233,1966 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POLACK1</b> When the dance ceased, the lamentation (<i>tangi</i> ) commenced, in which this singular assemblage dissolved into [89] tears ... <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/88</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> A <i>tangi</i> by all hands lasted nearly an hour. <i>(Feb 20, 1841,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1841 Feb 20 E.J. Wakefield Journal in 1842 Heaphy Residence in NZ 57]

>  <b>McKILLOP</b> THE `TANGI', OR WAIL. [heading] <i>(1849,Reminiscences,253</i>)

>  [The Maoris] were going to have a <i>tangi</i> over the grave. <i>(Aug 7, 1854,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/153</i>) [NOTES: (C.W. Richmond)]

>  <b>HARPER</b> His wife had lately died, and a number of Maori women, from their settlement close by, were holding a `Tangi', or lamentation, to bewail her death, ... <i>(Nov 4, 1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,43,1914 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MANING</b> He mocks the war-song, but <i>I<i> see your fall, at least one Pakeha Maori shall raise the <i>tangi</i>; and with flint and shell as of old shall the women lament you. <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,1/3</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> This was the first occasion upon which I had ever heard the `tangi,' or melancholy wailing, which is performed by [58] the women of the tribe on the departure or return, as well as on the death, of one of its members. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,57</i>)

>  <b>MARRYAT</b> `Why do they weep and lament in that distressing way?' `.. it is what they call <i>tangi</i> in their language.' During the time that the women tangied, the men stood silent. <i>(1874,Among the Maoris,113</i>) [NOTES: 1874 Emilia Norris (Marryat) Amongst the Maoris 113]

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> Perhaps some old woman did a quiet tangi over his grave. <i>(1881,Poenamo,191</i>)

>  <b>RENWICK</b> 'Tis the tangi floats on the sea-borne breeze,/ In its echoing notes of wild despair. <i>(1883,Betrayed,41</i>) [NOTES: [Morris]]

>  <b>PAYTON</b> It is always the custom in New Zealand, especially in the North Island, to use Maori words frequently in referring to the natives and their doings; a colonist would no more dream of calling a Maori <i>whare</i>house,' or a <i>tangi</i> `a ceremony of weeping' than he would of saluting Tawhias [sic check -o?] as King of New Zealand. <i>(1888,Round about NZ,viii</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> The old Bible words, `He lifted up his voice and wept,' become a reality to any who have heard the shrill Maori ta-ngi. <i>(1884,Our Maoris,18</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Tangi, n.</i> (pronounced <i>Tang-y</i>) Maori word for a lamentation, a cry, or dirge. <i>(1898,Austral English,457</i>)

>  <i>tangi n</i>. lamentation; mourning. [Polynesian] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,11</i>)

>  Tokotoko says she is not going to Kawa. She can't be bothered going to the `backblocks' she says, and in any case .. there will be plenty more tangis to go to before her own. <i>(1945,Journal of the Polynesian Society,54/97</i>) [NOTES: (Beaglehole)]

>  <i>tangi</i>, sound; cry; lament, as in a wake; sound of birds: in place-name Waitangi, it means "sounding water." <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/146</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  dirge or lamentation <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,176</i>)

>  The Maori word for weeping, wailing, lamentation, a ceremony on the death of a distinguished Maori. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,405</i>)

>  <i>tangi n</i> -s [Maori, lit., to mourn, cry] : a Maori funeral rite; <i>also</i> : a lamentation or dirge that accompanies it <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,2337</i>)

>  <b>PEARSON</b> I'm a full Maori and proud, too. .. Old Mum she meant I'm still a savage, that's what she meant. Just quietly, eh? <i>Haka</i> and <i>tangi</i>. <i>(1963,Coal Flat,43</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to mourn or weep [as in Maori funeral]

>  <b>BAKER</b> Everybody tangi'd over the grave, so many people dead, seven in one hole. <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,89</i>)

>  <b>M. WILLIAMS</b> I .. decided to send Joseph to Turanga. I could not think how I had consented and tangied over his preparations. <i>(Oct 17, 1844,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Oct 17 M. Williams Journal in 1960 Drummond Married and Gone to NZ 39]

>  <b>ANGAS</b> .. she [a Maori woman] begged .. that she might be permitted to <i>tangi</i> over it [a sketch] .. saying, `it was her brother and she must <i>tangi</i> till the tears came ... <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/47</i>)

>  [The Maori] was Komene's wife. .. She sat down, began tangi-ing, waving her arm occasionally; so they remained for some minutes. <i>(Apr 19, 1864,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/107</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  <b>MARRYAT</b> `Why do they weep and lament in that distressing way?' `.. it is what they call <i>tangi</i> in their language.' During the time that the women tangied, the men stood silent. <i>(1874,Among the Maoris,113</i>)

>  <b>MANING</b> He was .. consoled by hundreds of friends who came .. to condole and <i>tangi</i> with him ... <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,(iv) 74</i>)

>  The great-nephew of the chieftainess, Mr Joe Karaka, her namesake, Mrs Tuaiwa (Eva) Rickard and relatives followed Mrs Rongo into the tomb to tangi over the remains. <i>(Sep 25, 1972,Marlborough Express,6</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden,142</i>) [NOTES: [see noun 1.above]]

>  <b>WEBSTER</b> Pemia went first, pulling the black shawl over her head and adjusting it before she began to cry out, tangi-ing towards the corpse for a few minutes and then getting closer, and crying out again. <i>(1979,Rua,24</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> 375. Tangi = weep, mourn <i>(1983,Bone People, The,468</i>)

>  <b>'K'</b> Here we stop for a few minutes to enable our native companion to be tangi'd over. <i>(1885,Visit to Lake Rotoaira, A,4</i>)

>  To establish and maintain all forms of amusement and entertainment, and in particular schools and colleges and whare taperes of poi-dancing, and hakas and tongue stretching, and dismal tangi-ing, living pictures, and abdominal contortions. <i>(Dec 6, 1912,Wanganui Chronicle,2</i>)

>  Tito Kowaru has emerged from his retreat .. and, at the last accounts, was visiting and tangi-ing over the scenes of his former exploits. <i>(Oct 4, 1870,North Otago Times,3</i>)

>  Who was the young lady who 'tangied' when she heard of the boat capsizing and dear Ralph was nearly drowned? <i>(May 20, 1899,Observer (Auckland),21</i>)

>  The poor wounded woman was there tangi-ing for her lost child. <i>(Jul 13, 1912,Evening Post,13</i>)

>  "I don't want to tangi and all that crap," he says. <i>(Apr 27, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),23</i>)

>  A chief died in Te Karaka once, and they tangied for over a year on end. <i>(Jul 22, 1924,Otago Witness,71</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> The<i> tangi</i> band knelt on the ground, ... leant forward, and placed their hands on the ground. <i>(1876,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,111,1935 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> Since the introduction of measles this district has been a continual travelling route of tangi parties. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,128</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> The <i>tangi</i> custom was also a source for the spread of infection. <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [weeping, not necessarily over death]

>  As the march began, Eva Rickard, organising Kotahitanga (unity council) president, admitted: `I had a big tangi (cry), didn't I? <i>(Jan 29, 1984,NZ Times,1</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>tangi</i>: dirge, lamentation; to mourn <i>(1963,O Te Raki,194</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a post-mortem meeting or discussion [fig]

>  <b>BAKER</b> When we refer to holding a tangi about a setback or problem we are putting another Maori term into colloquial use,... <i>(1941,NZ Slang,56</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Tangi, holding a.</i> Faced with a problem or a set-back: New Zealand: C. 20. B., 1941. Ex Maori tangi, `a wake'. Cf. prec. entry. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1194</i>)

>  I hear that Miss Lelia Williams, who was pronounced by the verdict of those present as being the belle, in view of the feeling displayed by some of the disappointed ones, does not intend to accept the kind offer of Mr Foy, of having her photo enlarged, but intends instead to hold a tangi at Kopu over the remains of 'blighted hopes' of those who thought they were entitled to the coverted [sic] honour, and for the occasion she purposes [sic] wearing the same costume, so that even the Maoris will not think she is anything but a princess of their own blood, as the majority of those present at the ball did. <i>(Oct 28, 1893,Observer (Auckland),22</i>)

>  One Councillor said that they had better make the final payment first and hold a tangi over the work afterwards. <i>(Jul 26, 1909,Feilding Star,3</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a squabble, a fight

>  <b>DAVIN</b> And if she was angry enough she'd go in and give him a tongue-lashing ... Now it had never struck me that I might get involved in one of these tangis. <i>(1959,No Remittance,170</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [recitation]

>  <b>BUDDLE</b> Here he recited a native tangi, see p. 17, of Sir George Grey's collection. [`Tera in tai o Ngamotu'] <i>(1860,Maori King Movement,56</i>)



 
<b>7</b> <i>n.</i>



 
<b>9</b> <i>v.</i> lament [one cit, to be tangi'd over]

### tangi leave
 
 <i>n.</i> leave from work for the purpose of arranging or attending a tangi; funeral leave

>  We hear also that one should not hire a Maori employee because they are able to take unlimited tangi leave. The law is absolutely clear that all Kiwis have the opportunity for bereavement leave, whatever their colour, race or creed. <i>(Feb 24, 2004,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  The Council of Trade Unions has explained that he's got it wrong about tangi leave. <i>(Feb 18, 2004,Northern Courier,5</i>)

>  Statements by the National Party in recent weeks.. about a fictitious concept called "tangi leave", need to be addressed. Every employee has the same entitlement under the law to bereavement leave. <i>(Feb 28, 2004,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  The controversial bereavement provisions allowed for a day's bereavement leave if employers accepted an employee had cultural responsibilities towards the deceased, a clause interpreted by National as amounting to "unlimited tangi leave". <i>(Oct 10, 2004,Sunday Times (NZ),D1</i>)

>  Margaret Wilson (Feb 28) seems determined to misrepresent the effects of Government employment law change as it applies to the so-called tangi leave. <i>(Mar 5, 2004,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

### tangi-er
 
 <i>n.</i> someone attending a tangi

>  A coterie of mourners was selected to tangi for the deceased, a master of ceremonies was appointed whose principal duty was to see that the `tangi-ers' kept up to concert pitch. <i>(1935,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,111</i>) [NOTES: c1875 Meredith Adventuring in Maoriland (1935) 111]

### tangiao
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [tangeao](t-ten.md#tangeao))

### Tangier pea
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [contrast everlasting pea](ens-eye.md#everlasting-pea))

>  <i>pea, Tangier </i>[s] <i>Lathyrus tingitanus</i> L. <i>Ibid.</i> 77 <i>Tangier pea</i> [s] <i>Lathyrus tingitanus</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,57</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> TANGIER PEA (<i>Lathyrus tingitanus</i>) Beautiful biennial ... often mistaken for everlasting pea but flowers are red-purple and fewer in bunch. <i>(1981,Weeds of Roadsides,47</i>)

### tangihanga
 
 <i>n.</i> mourning; mourning party [ORIGIN: Maori, aka tangihana]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> The natives were busy preparing food for the expected Tangihana ... [July 3] About ten the fleet of canoes containing the <i>tangihana</i> or crying party appeared in sight .. <i>(Jul 2, 1846,Journal,4/70</i>)

>  <b>FITTON</b> .. a great meeting of natives at Te Wairoa on the occasion of a grand `tangihana' for Apatri, an old chief drowned last year. <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),106</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Taraia died in November 1871 ... and the Governor ... went up ... to Ohinemuri to attend the <i>tangihana. </i> <i>(1940,Sir Donald Maclean,112</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> ... all those taking part in the <i>tangihana</i> who were psychic or receptive hoped to catch this gift from the departing spirit. <i>(1948,Taina,181</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> 238 Tangihanga = funeral, and the ceremonies connected with it <i>(1983,Bone People, The,467</i>)

>  <b>HOWE</b> Tangihanga - this word refers specifically to the ceremony which takes place when a person dies. It is closely linked with the idea of `whanaungatanga' (q.v.). People go to the tangi to share their grief with the bereaved family, to reaffirm family ties, to recall the living past. <i>(1985,Towards Taha Maori,22</i>)

>  Tangihanga leave [headline] The CSU is to lodge a claim for the recognition of tangihanga leave. The claim is based on the belief that tangihanga is a grieving process, and that as a consequence, extended bereavement leave should be granted those attending a tangi. <i>(Jun 18, 1986,PSA Journal,6</i>)

>  Tangihanga aren't exempt either. It's not uncommon to farewell the tupapaku the night before burial with songs and jokes - and booze. <i>(Jun 1996,Mana,No.13: 56</i>)

>  A very large number of Natives have gone to the Tangihanga. <i>(Dec 9, 1874,Star, The,3</i>)

>  Tangihanga leave [headline] The CSU is to lodge a claim for the recognition of tangihanga leave. The claim is based on the belief that tangihanga is a grieving process, and that as a consequence, extended bereavement leave should be granted those attending a tangi. <i>(Jun 18, 1986,PSA Journal,6</i>)

>  From the Anglican cup of tea to the wailing of the tangihanga, where the deceased are never left alone, we find out why funerals are changing. <i>(Sep 28, 2004,Dominion Post TV Week,3</i>)

>  Tangihanga to be held at Poutu Pa Shannon-Foxton Highway Friday 10 February 2006 at 11am. <i>(Feb 8, 2006,Dominion Post,D9</i>)

>  By means of a long stick and noose they succeeded in raising the body, which was then quite black, from the fatal cave, and the messenger arrived here as above to invite the Ngatipikiaos to attend the "tangihanga." <i>(Sep 9, 1880,Bay of Plenty Times,3</i>)

>  TANGIHANGA AT SOUTH WAITAKI. <i>(Aug 6, 1897,North Otago Times,3</i>)

>  When that chief lost his life by the great land-slip which totally destroyed Te Rapa, Iwikau assumed the patronymic <i>Te Heu Heu</i>, and fearing lest another <i> ta niwha</i> should destroy his abode at Te Hihi he removed to Pukawa, and there he received the grand <i>tangihanga</i>, which came to do honour to his brother's memory. <i>(Dec 3, 1862,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,3</i>)

>  A large tangihanga will be held at Papawai.. in memory of the Hon. Mr. Wi Pere. M.L.C. <i>(Mar 2, 1916,Wairarapa Daily Times,5</i>)

>  Marae spokeswoman Moko Tini Templeton said the tangihanga was a huge logistical exercise. <i>(Aug 17, 2006,NZ Herald - The College Herald,A1</i>)

>  Tangihanga at Otawhare Marae, Omaio. <i>(Aug 30, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,10</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> funeral [ORIGIN: Maori, [Ma. <b>tangihanga</b> mourning] cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 239.]

### tangiing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HUNT</b> Here we stopped, and the canoes were drawn up in front of the <i>pa</i>, amidst much <i>tangi</i>-ing, and shouts of `<i>Haeremai, haeremai, haeremai</i>!' - the usual amount of shaking hands and rubbing noses being over, a long <i>korero</i>, or talk, ensued. <i>(1866,Chatham Islands,17</i>)

>  The night ... was spent in speechifying, tangeying, going through the war dance ... <i>(1879,Waikato War,15</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> After each new arrival had performed the proper [179] amount of tangi-ing wailing at the semi-circle they retired to feast, and to gossip - ... <i>(1881,Poenamo,Bk3/5/178</i>)

>  There has been great feasting and tangiing going on for the past two or three weeks. <i>(May 14, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,22</i>)

### Tangiwai
 
 <i>n.</i> rail tragedy at Tangiwai on Christmas Eve, 1953, during Queen's visit, when a lahar from Mt Ruapehu destroyed a rail bridge with loss of 151 lives.

>  At this precise time National Radio will air a two-hour documentary, <i>Tangiwai ... A River of Tears</i>, detailing what happened that night in 1953 and exploring the ongoing significance of what became known as the Tangiwai - translates as "river of tears" - disaster. <i>(Dec 20, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),66</i>)

>  Yes, the memories flow throughout the reunion, not least of that terrible Christmas Day as the news of Tangiwai spread. <i>(Mar 27, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)

>  There are many reasons why, half a century on, the name Tangiwai has an emotional resonance for more than one generation of New Zealanders. Most obviously, it was the country's fifth worst disaster and, at the timne, the eighth biggest railway disaster the world had ever known. Then there was the timing, Christmas Eve. <i>(Dec 20, 2003,Dominion Post,B2</i>)

>  "We've heard about you and your bloody Tangiwai. You didn't even get your feet wet." <i>(Apr 20, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),72</i>)

>  At the end of the day word filtered through to the New Zealand team of the Tangiwai rail catastrophe back home. <i>(Jan 11, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

### tangiwai
 
 <i>n.</i> a translucent variety of greenstone.(bowenite

>  <b>MONRO</b> There are two kinds of greenstone, that which is commonly seen, and which is named the ponamoo, and another sort more glassy and transparent named tuggewai. The ponamoo is exceedingly hard, and has an irregular fracture. The tuggewai is much softer, of a more transparent green, and divides easily into plates. <i>(1844,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson Examiner July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 261]

>  <b>MANING</b> 1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson Examiner July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 261 <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,321</i>) [NOTES: [that is, the notes check date]]

>  Serpentine occurs in two forms - as common Serpentine ..., and as noble Serpentine (<i>Tangiwai</i>) in thin veins associated with Jade. ... Jade - ordinarily known as Maori Greenstone (<i>Poenamu</i>) occurs <i>in situ</i> on several parts of the West Coast ... <i>(1868,Province of Otago in NZ, The,39</i>)

>  With the exception of the <i>tangi-wai</i>, the various kinds of greenstone are all found in a restricted locality on the west coast of the South Island. <i>(1892,TrNZI,24/481</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Taking the dead warrior's gun and <i>whakakai </i>pendant of <i>tangiwai</i> greenstone as trophies, Mair hurried back to the scene of the fight. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/158,1983 ed.</i>)

>  Amongst the most valued objects in the Cook relics ... is a bowenite (tangiwai) ear-drop. <i>(1924,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,7/181</i>) [NOTES: (Thorpe)]

>  <b>COWAN</b> High above on the slopes of the Mitre Peak range there are deposits of the famous <i>tangiwai</i> or bowenite, the greenstone sought after so much by Maori and <i>pakeha</i> alike. ... The discovery of this <i>tangiwai</i> reef is described in ... the legend of Tama-ki-te-Rangi. <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol II South Island) 148</i>)

>  <b>FIRTH</b> Among the Maori, considerations of aesthetic interest helped quite materially ... to decide the relative worth of objects made of the <i>pounamu</i>, the nephrite so much prized for pendants, <i>mere</i>, and adzes. Most prized was the <i>kahurangi</i> stone, bright green, translucent, hard and clear; the <i>inanga</i> variety perhaps comes next, with its white milky-veined appearance, [389] followed by the <i>kawakawa</i>, dark olive-green, dull and opaque. ... <i>Tangiwai</i> (`tear drop'), <i>bowenite</i>, a softer stone of a clear pale green, with the appearance of drops of water in the texture, was less esteemed, though more beautiful in the European eyes, whilst <i>kahotea</i>, green, spotted or streaked with black, was an inferior type, and was used for small chisels, and for objects of subordinate interest. <i>(1929,Primitive Economics of NZ Maori,388</i>)

>  <b>ELDER</b> Bowenite was used, but genuine specimens in this material are excessively rare. <i>(1932,Letters & Journals of Samuel Marsden,432</i>)

>  <b>ZIMMERMAN</b> Each variety has its own Maori name, but the two most prized are <i>inanga</i>, which is heavily flecked with white, and <i>tangiwai,</i> the deep, clear kind. <i>(1946,Where the People Sing,149</i>)

>  New Zealand greenstone is either the mineral nephrite (Maori: pounamu) or bowenite (Maori: tangiwai). <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,1/875</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> A material similar in appearance to greenstone, but known to the geologists as bowenite and to the Maori as tangiwai, is widely used in the same manner as greenstone. Some pieces are quite translucent while others show the characteristic yellow-brown colouration. Bowenite is a softer material than greenstone. <i>(1966,Maori Life and Custom,98</i>)

>  <b>HALL-JONES</b> McKenzie picked up many Maori artefacts at this source of the translucent tangiwai type of greenstone (<i>bowenite</i>) which the Maoris used for ornaments. <i>(1968,Early Fiordland,32</i>)

>  <b>DUFF</b> Closely resembling nephrite to the inexpert eye, and closely associated with it in Maori tradition, the greenish translucent bowenite (tangiwai) is again recorded only from the remote south-western coast of the South Island, namely Anita Bay in Milford Sound. <i>(1977,Moa-Hunters of NZ,231</i>)
