>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Greenstone was picked up at Anita bay - takiwai, not the esteemed pounamu. <i>(1978,Acheron Journal,159</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> <i>(1983,Bone People, The</i>)

>  One of the party, who in past years had seen a great deal of work by the Maoris in greenstone, and had a good knowledge of the stone, disputed somewhat hotly Captain Hutton's ruling. The Maoris call this kind tangiwai, an allusion to the water contained in it. <i>(Mar 21, 1874,Wellington Independent,2</i>)

>  There are two kinds of greenstone, that which is commonly seen, and which is named the ponamoo, and another sort more glassy and transparent named tuggewai ... the tuggewai is much softer, of a more transparent green, and divides easily into plates. <i>(Oct 5, 1844,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,3</i>)

>  One of the chiefs who spoke - a tatooed, grey-haired warrior - wore a very beautiful pendant ear-drop, made from the tangiwai, or tear-water greenstone. <i>(May 25, 1904,Otago Witness,49</i>)

>  Tangiwai is clear like glass, and ranges from olive-gree to bluish green. Most tangiwai is sourced from two isolated areas at Piopiotahi (Milford Sound). Koko-tangiwai, the longer name for this stone, referes to a deep sorrow that is never completely healed. <i>(Oct 23, 2009,Press, The,4</i>)

### tangle fern
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Gleichenia dicarpa</i> (formerly <i>alpina, circinata</i>), having thin, spidery pinnae. [(often preceded by a descriptive epithet)]

>  Umbrella ferns are quite unlike any other fern. Rather than producing a typically divided frond, the midrib divides repeatedly into two, producing a bud at the end of each branch, with two new branches arising immediately below it. Lateral branching of this sort can be finite, producing a distinctive shape, as in the umbrella fern (<i>Sticherus cunninghamii</i>), or it can continue almost indefinitely, with the frond forming extensive thickets, as in the tangle fern (<i>Gleichenia dicarpa</i>). <i>(2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/72</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Gleichenia dicarpa</i> TANGLE FERN ... Spider fern ... Swamp umbrella fern ... Fronds wiry, forked ... often forming tangled thickets or cushion-like springy masses ... <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,432</i>)

>  <b>POTTS</b> (And) <i>Gleichenia dicarpa </i> (formerly <i>alpina</i>) alpine tangle-fern <i>(1882,Out in the Open</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And) <i>Gleichenia dicarpa </i> (formerly <i>alpina</i>) alpine tangle-fern <i>(1909,AJHR,C-12</i>)

>  <b>H.B.DOBBIE</b> (And) <i>Gleichenia dicarpa </i> <i>(Nov 21, 1921,Letters</i>)

>  <b>MARK & ADAMS</b> Several species are virtually confined to peat-bogs - the wire rush (<i>Calorophus minor</i>) and the tangle fern (<i>Gleichenia dicarpa </i>). <i>Ibid.</i> 3 <i>Gleichenia circinata</i> Swartz TANGLE FERN <i>(1973,NZ Alpine Plants,22,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Gleichenia dicarpa </i> TANGLE FERN ... Spider fern ... Swamp umbrella fern ... Fronds wiry, forked ... often forming tangled thickets or cushion-like springy masses ... <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,432</i>)

>  Lateral branching of this sort can be finite, producing a distinctive shape, as in the umbrella fern (<i>Sticherus cunninghamii</i>), or it can continue almost indefinitely, with the frond forming extensive thickets, as in the tangle fern (<i>Gleichenia dicarpa</i>). <i>(2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/72</i>)

### tanglefoot
 
 <i>n.</i> liquor

>  In prohibition districts 8 bottles of tanglefoot are known a `kit'. 2. beer, bad liquor: <i>(Aug 19, 1905,NZ Truth,1</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> obstructive vegetation

>  <b>THOMSON</b> After an hour of this the going improved considerably, the river bank being more level with less `tanglefoot' and a more defined track appearing, as if wapiti had frequented it at some earlier date. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,129</i>)

### tanglefoot paper
 
 <i>n.</i> (sticky) flypaper

>  ... to catch the bugs ... They tried tanglefoot paper at first... plastered gummy paper. <i>(Apr 7, 1906,NZ Truth,1</i>)

### tani raha
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [tanekaha](t-ten.md#tanekaha))

### taniko
 
 <i>n.</i> an ornamental border or band [ORIGIN: Maori, sometimes refers to the mat, not just its border]

>  The girls try to keep up some of the Maori arts, such as weaving and doing Taniko. <i>(Sep 21, 1927,NZ Tablet,39</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> If men ever wove they confined their attention to the weaving of the ornamental borders (<i>taniko</i>) of mats. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,228</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> The <i>taniko</i> in the embroidered borders of cloaks, is from the point of technique the latest development in Maori weaving. <i>(1929,Coming of the Maori, The,28</i>)

>  The garment was a parawai or kaitaka cloak with a taniko border. <i>(Jul 30, 1933,Na To Hoa Aroha,3/103</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata) 1933 July 30 in 1988 Na To Hoa Aroha (Ngata-Buck Correspondence) 3/103]

>  The arts and crafts section comprised tukutuku, ... plaiting (rourou [fn A small basket used for cooked food.], rough kits and floor mats), taniko work and making poi balls. <i>(Mar 28, 1936,Na To Hoa Aroha,3/212</i>) [NOTES: 1936 Mar 28 in 1988 Na To Hoa Aroha (Ngata-Buck Correspondence) 3/212 (Ngata)]

>  <b>BEAGLEHOLE</b> Some of the women ... are skilled at taniko plaiting, but such work is hardly common enough to be called a folk art. <i>(1946,Some Modern Maoris,108</i>)

>  <b>ZIMMERMAN</b> She would have been a beauty, dressed in the old fashion, in the <i>piu-piu</i> skirt, with her hair flowing free under a narrow <i>taniko</i> head band. <i>(1946,Where the People Sing,116</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,2/172, 173</i>)

>  <b>MALING</b> A warrior's cloak, or cape, woven from the finest flax fibre, bleached white, with a decorative <i>taniko</i> border. <i>(1958,Torlesse Papers,126 fn,1958 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> ... the children almost all in traditional Maori costume, the elders with a few concessions to it - some of the women wear pare round their heads, or piu-pius over their dresses, a few older men with taniko bordered cloaks. <i>(1969,Awatea,48,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> And Your Mother wears on her head the taniko band. <i>(1979,Collected Poems,449,1969 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ADCOCK</b> taniko <i>woven band or belt </i> <i>(1982,Contemporary NZ Poetry,143</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> taniko <i>woven band or belt </i> <i>(1982,Selected Poems,189</i>)

>  The following contributions were received at the Museum during the quarter ending March 31 .. Rev. James W. Stack, Kaiapoi - Taniko (flax mat), Maori chief's cloak. <i>(Apr 22, 1872,Star, The,2</i>)

>  The mat on the left is called taniko, and the other, on the right, korawai. <i>(Dec 10, 1889,Otago Daily Times,6</i>)

>  FOR SALE, Handsome MAORI MAT with Taniko Border. <i>(Mar 9, 1892,Otago Daily Times,3</i>)

>  Among the ceremonial garments are some feather cloaks and capes, while one of the garments shows really exquisite "Taniko" bordering. <i>(Jan 10, 1918,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  The mana of meeting houses and the cult symbols of the Maori millennial prophets.. combined with the triangles and diamond shapes of taaniko weaving provide the atmospherics for one of Cotton's best-known paintings. <i>(Dec 12, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),37</i>)

>  One of the kuia observed that one section had the precise colour and form of taniko weaving. <i>(Dec 14, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),41</i>)

>  During those younger years she learnt taniko weaving from a Pakeha teacher and later became a master weaver. <i>(2008,Mana,84/9</i>)

>  Maori material culture, the patterns and forms of tekoteko, taniko and tukutuku, is a tempting source of raw material for artists. <i>(Dec 14, 2005,NZ Herald,B4</i>)

>  These 100-hour courses help you learn new techniques in kowhaiwhai, raranga, whakairo, taniko and kakahu. <i>(Nov 17, 2006,Whakatane Beacon,13</i>)

### taniwha
 
 <i>n.</i> a legendary water monster; a river guardian [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> T|aniwa,<i> s</i>. A sea monster so called. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,207</i>)

>  <b>ELDER</b> `The taneewha (taniwha) whom the natives say upsets their canoes and drowns them, at other times takes them out of the canoe bodily, drowns, and eats them; ... they ascribe to their taking some old thatch from a house which was <i>tapu</i> or sacred into their canoe to sit upon. The taneewha or one of the sea-gods was angry, upset the canoe, etc. <i>(1934,Marsden's Lieutenants,255</i>) [NOTES: 1822 Aug 15 John King in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 255]

>  <i>Taniwha</i>: fabulous monster; prodigy; incubus of the imagination: might take any shape, saurian, rock, log: might be stationary, or move from place to place in air, water or land, even through the land as a fish through the water or a bird through the air. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/`158</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words [check spg saurian and commas???]]

>  <b>BAXTER</b> Growing cold in amber water, riding the logs/ Upstream, and waiting for the taniwha. <i>(1979,Collected Poems,45,1979 ed.</i>)

>  fabulous monster <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,176</i>)

>  <b>SAR GESON</b> No. No thanks. They had a big parcel of fish and chips. ...There. In the dragon [a merry-go-round car so shaped] (like the taniwha, eh!). Poppy was minding it. <i>Ibid.</i> 273 (gloss.) taniwha: water-spirit or monster in Maori mythology. <i>(1949,I Saw in My Dream,252,1974 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1949 Sargeson I Saw in My Dream (1974 ed. Rhodes) 252]

>  Aorangi dominated the view for miles as we drove back to Taihape ; a fit abode for the taniwha, Tarakumukumu, who lives in a lake on the summit - a taniwha, it is said, with a head like a cattle beast, a semi-human body and amiable disposition. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,15</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>taniwha: </i>fabulous monster supposed to live in deep water. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,194</i>)

>  <b>HINTZ</b> The Horomatangi reefs are named after a fierce taniwha - a mythical marine monster - which, the Maoris say, used to lurk in the rocks. <i>(1964,Trout at Taupo,38</i>)

>  <i>Taniwha n</i>. a mythical Maori monster [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),626</i>)

>  <i>taniwha ... n</i>. mythical Maori monster [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),19</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> ... the place at the river-bend is named/ After her tears - I accept that - I wait for/ The taniwha in the heart to rise - <i>(1979,Collected Poems,467,1969 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> 174 Taniwha = a mythical (?) water terror/monster <i>(1983,Bone People, The,466</i>)

>  <i>taniwha n.</i> mythical monster inhabiting pools of water. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,785</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 491]]

>  <b>IHIMAERA & LONG</b> Something deep inside him was let loose like a raging taniwha out of the deep cave and before his party mates on the beach realised it, Hemi had pushed Tare off the crate and was on top of him punching wildly. <i>(1982,Into the World of Light,217</i>)

>  <b>KNOX</b> A book... About taniwhas - disinherited taniwhas. They're at a loose end about what to do and feel they don't belong anymore. It's rather sad, on a sort of conservationist theme. <i>(1987,After Z-Hour,3</i>)

>  Taniwha block highway builders in Waikato. <i>(Dec 3, 2002,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  The Taniwha, fearful and fierce, / Who is clad from head to tail / In a coat of scaly mail. <i>(Feb 1, 1890,NZ Observer,6</i>)

>  Such "taniwhas" as this [sc. a 34 lb eel] account for many lf the fry liberated in the stream they inhabit. <i>(Dec 26, 1928,NZ Free Lance,42</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> The taniwha gave you part of the story and I'm giving you a bit more... It was Cally's taniwha, carved in white bone, inlaid with chips of coloured stone, with a plaited flax thing attached. <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,67</i>)

>  [The government]announces thgat the SAS will be issued with secret medals. What do they tell the other ex-servicemen on Anzac Day? That they were issued by a taniwha. <i>(Dec 8, 2002,Sunday Star Times,A12</i>)

>  Suddenly it's a place of spiritual cleansing along the greenstone trail or birthing canal of the great taniwha or something. <i>(May 15, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),33</i>)

>  'There's a sign down there,' said Marion. 'It says the harbour is guarded by taniwha.' <i>(2001,Huia 4: Contemp. Maori Fiction,16</i>)

>  I would not wonder if we had failed to record many old New Zealanders, including the Hoihois and Taniwhas. <i>(Nov 20, 1890,Otago Witness,42</i>)

>  A blue taniwha painted on a window loooks out over a small field of mud. <i>(Jul 22, 2001,Sunday Star Times,C1</i>)

>  Cool off in the firece Waikato River where jet boats patrolled to save the unwary from the clutches of the taniwha. <i>(Jan 7, 2003,Dominion Post,C1</i>)

>  An angry hoarse murmur was heard.. as though the dreaded Taniwha were coming to avenge the insult. <i>(Apr 26, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,18</i>)

>  They are seized by a large eel - a regular taniwha to ducks. <i>(Nov 5, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,7</i>)

>  Most historical accounts of taniwha have them resembling a water-borne serpent, and over the years there have been claims of sightings... "I don't subscribe to the idea these taniwha come out and cause problems because a road's going through. Taniwha are actually guardians, they have always been more helpful than non-helpful." <i>Ibid.</i> C1 In a week when a taniwha created headlines for holding up a motorway development.. business people have every right to be sceptical of the Government's latest bureaucracy-busting move. <i>(Nov 9, 2002,Dominion Post,A7</i>)

>  There lived, not far from these parts, a dreaded taniwha, a dragon who came at time from his deep mountain cavern to devour all with whom he came into contact. <i>(Oct 12, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,94</i>)

>  His answer, when it finally comes, might give those who appointed him a hint that they may have a taniwha by the tail. <i>(Nov 23, 2003,Sunday Star Times,C1</i>)

>  [Hdg] Urban Taniwha roaming... From conception to completion, the making of Urban Taniwha has been a musical journey filled with passion, rage, despair and ecstasy. <i>(Oct 12, 2000,City Voice,4</i>)

>  And this is certainly the only country in the world that has ever stopped rebuilding a lethal stretch of highway because a local hapu complained that it ran over the home of a one-eyed taniwha. <i>(Sep 13, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21</i>)

>  <b>Hochstetter</b> They sent it [sc. fire] to him through the two Taniwhas (mountain and water-spirits living underground), Pupu and Te Haeata, by a subterranean passage to the top of Tongariro. <i>(1867,New Zealand (Heaphy),391</i>)

>  [Miria Simpson's] contribution was such that she was heralded simply as "Te Reo" by the hundreds who gathered to farewell her at Victoria University's Te Reo Waka Marae on Tuesday and yesterday. Others have fondly taken the description further, calling her "te reo o te taniwha" (the taniwha of the language). <i>(Jun 13, 2002,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> A son of old Tarea who had died long ago, and turned into a <i>Tanewa</i>, God of the Sea, had appeared to him ... <i>(Jan 28, 1832,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,223,1961 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [check title and page number???]]

>  <b>YATE</b> If a ship is lost ... [at Hokianga], it is attributed to the anger of the Taniwa, sea monster, or god, who has raised himself under the vessel, and overturned it. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),94</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> (<i>Wairoa</i>[a2-acute]) another person I knew, for the same cause, was within a few moons after drowned while bathing, the river god (<i>Taniwoa</i>) took hold of him. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/125</i>)

>  ... a lovely little bay shaded by Pohutekawa trees formerly the abode of the Tuniwa, or Sea God who upsets the Canoes and then devours the people. <i>(Apr 4, 1842,Journal of Ensign Best,338,1966 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WADE</b> `Hearing us use the word <i>tapu</i>, as we looked towards it, one of our boatmen quickly repeated that the place was tapued for the tanewa (a water demon). `And I wonder,' was his irreverent addition, `what this same tanewa may be! An old pot leg, perhaps!'' <i>(Sep 9, 1842,Journey in NZ,34</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> The natives affirm this river is filled with Taniwas, which they say are spirits which assume the form of fishes and eat men. <i>(Jun 13, 1845,Journal,3/127</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> The creature spoken of in the traditions of the New Zealanders is frequently called <i>Tuatara</i>, a name also common to the iguana of the country, derived from the circumstance of its back being armed with a row of spines. It is also sometimes called <i>Taniwha</i>, a word of indefinite signification, equivalent to the English monster: ... <i>(1856,Traditions & Superstitions,75</i>)

>  <b>SMITH</b> At the southern end, the lake for some distance round is <i>tapu</i>, on account of the many canoes that have been swallowed up by a great <i>taniwa</i> called Horomatangi, which the natives say lives there. <i>(Jan 21, 1858,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Jan 21 Smith Notes of a Journey in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 370]

>  <b>MANING</b> `Take care, the pakeha is <i>taniwha!</i> the pakeha is a <i>tino tangata!'</i> <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,2/32</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> ... the New Zealanders have their legends and songs about the terrible `taniwha,' ... <i>(1866,Polynesia,76</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> Subsequent conversation elicited that the Maoris were frightened to go to this particular waterhole, because they alleged that a <i>taniwha</i> (a fabulous reptile) lived in it. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,42,1935 ed.</i>)

>  <b>NESFIELD</b> Whether the Taniwha was offended I do not know ... <i>(1881,Chequered Career,109</i>)

>  The ridge-boards are carved to represent a number of <i>ngarara</i>, or lizards, running along the roof, and the <i>maihi</i>, or gable-boards, have carvings of the mythological animal known as the <i>manaia</i> - probably a kind of taniwha. <i>(1895,TrNZI,27/674</i>)

>  Ten years ago a <i>taniwha</i> was captured in a lagoon near hamilton ..., and exhibited in a butcher's shop, and it proved to be a <i>Stenorhynchus. </i> <i>(1895,TrNZI,28/88</i>)

>  `The river at one time is reported as having been infested with taniwhas - gigantic fish that used to swallow the natives - and a Maori pointed out a deep pool under some willows, and told me his grandfather had been sezied by one of these monsters at that spot, dragged to the bottom and eaten. ... <i>(Jan 23, 1896,Otago Witness,51/2</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Maori name for a mythical monster <i>(1898,Austral English,457</i>)

>  <i>Tai.ni-wha</i> (ta.-ne.-hw&adot.), n. [Maori.] A fabulous monster, resembling a dragon, famous in Maori legend. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2032</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The Maoris had belief in the existence of huge monsters (<i>taniwha</i> ) generally of saurian character and mostly water-dwellers. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,428</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> [The Maori's] existence was burdened with the knowledge of huge reptilian monsters, ngarara on land, and taniwha in the water, who could speak, and roar, ... [39] and decoy with satanic duplicity! <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,38</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> <i>Taniwha (Tah-nee-whah)</i>: A sea-monster. <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,51</i>)

>  Taniwha n. <i>a legendary sea-monster credited with destroying human begins. [Maori] </i> <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,11</i>)

>  Perhaps the taniwha, god-devil of the ocean, would be merciful ... <i>(1930,NZ Short Stories,262</i>) [NOTES: 1930 Iris Wilkinson (Robin Hyde) in NZ Short Stories 262]

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> <i>(1938,Brown Man's Burden,66</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> There's a little old woman <i>taniwha</i> , who comes up behind the souls of the dead. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,136</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CARR</b> Going back a few years, I recall my first ever pig hunt. My brother-in-law had condescended to take a rather timid townie to tackle the taniwha. In my mind at least, the furtive inhabitants of the bush resembled monsters, leviathans, behemoths and the such like, in their inherent savagery and ferocity. <i>(1986,Diary of a Pig Hunter,35</i>)

### taniwha clause
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  If he had a niggle, it was around presentation and "MPs' use of emotive language such as the taniwha clause", which he believed many Maori would dislike. <i>(Jan 25, 2003,Dominion Post,A1</i>)

>  The perfect symbol for all this is the Meremere taniwha. This one-eyed spirit stopped work on a new highway.. and gave National leader Bill English a new slogan. A reference to the Treaty in new legislation became the "taniwha clause" - a phrase coated in political poison... Historian Michael King says the issue "has nothing to do with the literal existence of non-existence of taniwha. Taniwha are a metaphorical way of saying that place is either dangerous or has special spiritual or historical significance. The way Maori signified that was by talking about taniwha or guardian to make poeople respect it and to explain the fact that sometimes people disppeared or drown or whatever, in those places". <i>(Feb 2, 2003,Sunday Star Times,C5</i>)

>  A requirement [in the 2002 Local Government Act] labelled the "taniwha clause" by opponents also drew fire. The clause demands that council decisions have regard to the Treaty of Waitangi and allows separate council seats for Maori. <i>(Dec 28, 2002,Dominion Post,C5</i>)

### Taniwha soap
 
 <i>n.</i> a brand of soap

>  An early contender, with more than a dash of local character, was certainly Taniwha soap. A by-product of one of the country's defining features, the meatworks, its very name conjured up mythical monsters, while the label displayed a bevy of topless mermaids lathering up against a backdrop of smoking volcanoes. <i>(Jan 2000,NZ Geographic,No. 45/20</i>)

### Taniwhasaurus oweni
 
 <i>n.</i> particular mosasaur

>  Hector's two species of mosasaur, taniwhasaurus oweni and Tylosaurus haumuriensis, are regarded as valid. <i>(Dec 1970,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,29/15</i>)

### tank
 
 <i>v.</i> to booze

>  He was a terror to tank <i>(1903,NZ Illustrated Magazine,8/365</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a corrugated iron water tank for house supply [Jim writes: (In DNZE with 1902 citation... There are references to the use of house tanks before 1870 (though not the words <i>tank stand</i> or <i>tank water</i>) in Maclean.. The earliest recorded use might thus be pushed much further back.]

>  Drays arrived with tank boards &c. <i>(Apr 9, 1861,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  Some time ago the Board of Education sent up a large tank and stand for the use of the schoolmaster's residence; it now lies on the public road, some chains from the house. <i>(Dec 30, 1879,Timaru Herald,2</i>)

>  In the open space allotted to implements is erected a tank stand of considerable altitude, containing four tanks, from which the fountains will be supplied by water pumped up by force pumps worked by motive power. <i>(Apr 1, 1882,Timaru Herald,3</i>)

>  The picture is a presentment of Messrs Hungerford and M'Kay's tank stand, from which great icicles are seen hanging, while the gorse bushes close by are beaten down with the weight of ice, the whole of which was the work of one nights [sic] frost. <i>(Aug 5, 1895,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  The application for the renewal of this license was also held over for a month to allow repairs to be effected to the fire escape and tank stand. <i>(Jun 2, 1910,Hawera & Normanby Star,8</i>)

>  <b>Maclean</b> The majority of the townspeople [sc. of Wellington] were dependent on shallow wells, rainwater tanks, and some on water from running streams. <i>(1964,Challenge for Health,68</i>)

>  <b>SALMOND</b> In the 1880s big corrugated iron tanks came on the market which were cheap enough for most households, and large enough to store water for the biggest of families. The round, corrugated tank became a characteristic of villa houses, sitting out the back on its high wooden stand hard up under the eaves, so that water would flow naturally through the outlet pipe in the bottom to the house. <i>(1986,Old New Zealand,143</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a safe, a burglary involving a safe [(cf blow a tank, tank-artist)]

>  <b>MACKENZIE</b> <i>tank</i> a safe <i>(1980,While We Have Prisons,99</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> A safe: New Zealand: since ca. 1920: 1932, Nelson Baylis (private letter); by 1935, also Australian; 1945, Baker, `To blow a tank, to break open a safe with gelignite'. Ex its shape-resemblance to a small watertank. <i>(1949,Dictionary of the Underworld,713,1961 ed.</i>)

>  Now, I'd been in the cooler for a tank job and my presence is not wanted on racetracks. a tank....a safe <i>(May 21, 1963,NZ Truth,19</i>)

>  <i>Coulter</i>: [A prison escaper. `I intended to] knock off a couple of tanks and shoot through.' <i>(Jul 4, 1963,Dominion,5</i>)

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> TANK (n) A safe. Derives from British rhyming slang `septic tank' (bank). <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,255</i>)

>  Words for crimes include.. <i>tank</i> for a burglary involving a safe (presumably from the usage of <i>tank</i>, which refers simply to a safe). <i>(Oct 1999,NZWords,2(2) : 2</i>)

### tank folk
 
 <i>n.</i> people dependent on tank water supply [Jim writes: I remember a light-hearted editorial in the Evening Post in the late 40s or 50s which referred to <i>tank folk</i>, ie people living in the Paremata-Kapiti area who were dependent on tank water.]

### tank loaf
 
 <i>n.</i> a concertina loaf

>  <b>BYCROFT LTD</b> Tank loaf <i>(1934,Bread Baking in NZ,?124</i>)

### tank stand
 
 <i>n.</i> the wooden platform on which stood a household water supply tank [common usage in Taranaki in the 1930s and subsequently]

>  When it's this early, I creep back past the wash house, the tank stand, the scullery ... <i>(1986,Day and Night,5</i>)

>  TANK STANDS. - Build tank stands with 6in. x 4in. uprights ... <i>(1902,Brett's Colonists Guide,1146</i>)

>  <b>McCAULEY</b> And a handyperson is wanted to do tankstand repairs. <i>(1986,Then Again,116</i>)

>  Just as the train was nearing the station at Gore, a dog was run over, Mr. Conyers leaned over from the side of the engine and was looking down the line, when his head struck against the tank stand, which had been placed too near the rails. <i>(Feb 14, 1879,Otago Daily Times,3</i>)

>  It was lying on the ground near the house tanks, having apparently been placed on the tank stand by Mr. Westlake on his return from Foxton. <i>(Sep 23, 1929,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  An injury to the groin was sustained by J. Urwin, a carpenter, of Raumati South, as a result of jumping from the top of a tank stand on which he was working on to a stick. <i>(Apr 1, 1936,Evening Post,13</i>)

>  Fatal injuries were received by John Harold Gibson, a middle-aged farmer, of Kaimiro, when a tank stand on which he was working yesterday, collapsed and he was pinned beneath a 400-gallon tank full of water. <i>(Mar 28, 1944,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> Peter had just finished his out-door work, on my arrival, and was busy at the tank stand, having a wash. <i>(1936,Follow the Call,63</i>)

### tank water
 
 <i>n.</i> household water supply collected originally from the roof by guttering and later pumped in from streams, wells etc.; usu. rainwater collected from a roof and fed into a holding tank for domestic use [ca1960 Wellington (Pukerua Bay)]

>  This [distilled] water was found greatly preferred to the tank water embarked ashore. <i>(May 31, 1851,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,59</i>)

>  They consider that some want of proper care in the selection of the casks must have occasioned this, as the tank water was not tainted. <i>(Apr 12, 1862,Lyttelton Times,5</i>)

>  This very ingenious and effective improvement in filters will be gladly welcomed by all householders, but it must commend itself at once more especially to those who reside in the country and who are necessarily more dependent upon tank water than wells. <i>(Mar 7, 1896,Feilding Star,2</i>)

>  In various parts of the town and district the tank water has a heavy, clayey appearance, caused, it is surmised, by dust from the eruption in Samoa brought down by the rain. <i>(Nov 15, 1902,Grey River Argus,3</i>)

>  BIRDS AND TANK WATER. <i>(Jun 14, 1912,Poverty Bay Herald,5</i>)

>  CARNARVON STREET - 4 Goood [sic] Rooms; new shed for motor-car, sewerage, city water, tank water; built of kauri. <i>(Nov 26, 1920,Poverty Bay Herald,4</i>)

>  ".. The greater portion of water at present used in the town was either tank water or surface water taken from the well, into which drained, in many cases, stagnant pools, and all sorts of rubbish " <i>(Sep 3, 1870,Timaru Herald,2</i>)

>  No fresh tank water had been opened shortly after leaving there, nor had the passengers been eating tinned meat. <i>(Dec 23, 1885,Star, The,4</i>)

>  "Beachcomber" talks about the health value of tank water. <i>(Jun 20, 1931,Evening Post,12</i>)

>  TO LET, Farm Cottage, 3 rooms and kit., Normandale Rd., 2 miles from Lower Hutt Station, no e.l., tank water. <i>(Oct 17, 1945,Evening Post,4</i>)

### tank-artist
 
 <i>n.</i> a safe-blower

>  <b>SARGESON</b> 'Actually by profession he's a tank-artist... That's what they used to call a cracksman. <i>(1967,Hangover,87</i>) [NOTES: 1984 edition]

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>v.</i> a booze-up
