### tankblower
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HAMILTON</b> I'd back some of the tankblowers to get a smoke through a two foot wall. They're the best, the tankblowers, and the more boob they've done the better usually. <i>(1953,Till Human Voices Wake Us,73</i>)

### tanked
 
 <i>adj.</i> drunk; in the sense `drunk, boozed' as common in NZ as elsewhere, possibly reinforced by soldiers in WW1 (cf TANK)

>  He seldom committed that vulgar error of becoming `tanked'. <i>(1916,Anzac Book, The,45</i>)

>  All mad - or tanked. <i>(1916,On the Anzac Trail,69</i>)

>  <b>FOSTON</b> Many got `tanked' (full up with drink). <i>Ibid.</i> 187 Tim went on the bust and got tanked. <i>(1921,At The Front,128</i>)

>  MC: I'm drunk. ...Drunk. Tanked. Cut. Sloshed. Out of my tree. Shickered to beat the band. <i>(Nov 22, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>)

>  So if you're tanked as a goldfish and hurt yourself, who's to blame? <i>(Dec 21, 2003,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),7</i>)

### tanker
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to convey milk by tanker from farm to factory.

>  When milk tankers first came in, they closed down the little branches and tankered the milk to Midhurst. <i>(Jul 20, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,22</i>)

### tanker driver
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [sp. use] a driver of a milk tanker that collects milk from dairy farms.

>  <b>HARRISON</b> 'You silly bugger!' said Sid, aghast when Harry proudly showed him his little green seedlings. Anyone could spot them a mile off, tanker drivers, whoever. <i>(1986,She's a Fair Cow,66</i>)

### tanker loop
 
 <i>n.</i> driveway around which tanker can turn when collecting milk

>  Winter is a good time for essential maintenance in and around the farm dairy, tanker loop and effluent ponds and systems. <i>(Jun 2005,Farmlink,25</i>) [NOTES: -]

>  . . . the woman sitting in the back of the Government issue Ford Falcon as it swooped along the tanker loop. <i>(Dec 2003,Dairy Exporter (NZ),56</i>) [NOTES: -]

>  Dairy companies are also recommending the middle of the tanker loop should be planted. <i>(Apr 2002,Rural News</i>) [NOTES: -]

### tanker track
 
 <i>n.</i> a farm road by which a milk tanker travels to collect milk from a cowshed.

>  ..Mr Cox says the council was worried about effluent getting into water courses, which is why he confined the spreading to the tanker track. <i>(Jun 2004,Dairyman, The,15</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a road on a dairy farm on which a milk collection tanker can traverse paddocks to collect milk.

>  It [illusion of excellence] goes as far as keeping the milking shed painted, the tanker track clear of junk, and making your property attractive from the road. <i>(May 1993,NZ Dairy Exporter,111</i>)

>  Roy grades the tanker track. <i>(Sep 12, 2000,Waikato Times,9</i>)

>  Tanker track troubles. Some North Taranaki dairy farmers say the Kiwi Dairy Company is taking a bullying approach over the state of tanker tracks on their farms. The farmers - who decline to be named because they say they fear retribution from Kiwi - believe the company's grading system for tanker tracks is overly rigorous. Acccording to the farmers, the company conducted surveys of tanker tracks and entrances, which included measuring track widths, turning conditions and entrances. <i>(Nov 2, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,15</i>)

>  In the hands of its dedicated and creative family, the tanker track has become lawn, the paddock, flowing and flowering borders. <i>(Dec 22, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,14</i>)

### tankie
 
 <i>n.</i> member of the Tank Corps

>  <b>FULLARTON</b> Most of [the tanks] conked out so the tankies used them as pill-boxes. <i>(1944,Troop Target,30</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> `Where else? The show's a wash-out. You tankies have let him run all over you.' The sergeant flushed. <i>(1975,Breathing Spaces,111</i>)

>  <b>HUGHES</b> The walls of the APC bulged like a wobble board and out of the turrets popped the heads of a couple of stunned tankies, rather like startled prairie dogs. <i>(1999,Shooting from the Lip,95</i>)

### tanking
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Tanking</i>- A continuous waterproof membrane applied to a surface to precent the ingress or egress of water. <i>(1979,SANZ Glossary Building Terminology,113</i>)

### tankman
 
 <i>n.</i> a safe-blower (cf TANK n2)

>  The old-style criminal activity such as `breakmen' and `tankmen' blowing safes and so on is starting to go. <i>(Jul 17, 1978,Dominion,4</i>)

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> TANKMAN (n)C Safe-blowing expert. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,255</i>)

### tanky
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a water joey, or threshing mill crewman in charge of the water tank.

>  I joined the crew of Tozer's chaff-cutter in June 1926 .. I secure a job on the crew as 'tanky.' My mate. Gil McBride, was engaged as 'sewer' Jim Hamilton (my cousin) was to be ringer, Matt O'Driscoll the 'steerer' and 'feeder', then two stackmen. <i>(Jan 9, 1963,Weekly News,33</i>)

>  <b>EVANS</b> He later returned to the district as the 'tanky' or tank water driver <i>(1975,Silver Tussock, The,134</i>)

### tannergram
 
 <i>n.</i> [cf mod. gorillagram, stripogram]

>  Tannergrams is the somewhat apt designation which the new sixpenny telegrams have been christened in commercial vernacular. <i>(Jun 13, 1863,Oamaru Mail</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Tannergram, n.</i> very recent New Zealand slang. On 1st of June, 1896, the New Zealand Government reduced the price of telegrams to sixpence (slang, a `tanner') for twelve words. <i>(1898,Austral English,457</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> <i>(1941,NZ Slang,46</i>)

>  The most simple and expressive is "Tannergram," and will doubtless come into equal popularity with "cablegram," which has for many years held its own. <i>(Dec 9, 1885,Tuapeka Times,3</i>)

### Tanpap
 
 <i>abbr.</i> There are no proposals at present to.. [ORIGIN: acronym]

>  This is a slightly more elegant version of the previous government's Tanpap scheme. "There Are No Proposals At Present to ... confiscate solo mothers' children / send all school leavers to boot camp / sell the Symphony Orchestra to Fay Richwhite." <i>(Jun 24, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

### tansy
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i> tansy [s] Tanacetum vulgare</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,71</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> TANSY (<i>Tanacetum vulgare</i>) ... A European plant, formerly used for ornament and for tansy tea, now locally common throughout New Zealand,... <i>(1981,Weeds of Roadsides,145</i>)

### tansy ragwort
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>tansy ragwort n </i>: a common ragwort (<i>Senecio jacobaea</i>) with pinnatifid leaves and compact clusters of yellow flower heads that is native to Europe, No. Africa, and western Asia but has been introduced in No. America, New Zealand, and Australia and in some areas is a dangerous and aggressive weed and toxic to cattle when consumed in quantity <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,2338</i>)

### tantie
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) a tantrum [noun. Also tanty.]

>  There's even a special road rage concoction imaginatively titled Calm blending bergamot, mandarin and frankincense and myrrh (yes, really) to ease those traffic light tanties. <i>(Jan 20, 2002,Sunday Star Times,D2</i>)

>  After a brief attempt to find a street leading back to the city, the driver threw a tanty and pulled over. <i>(Nov 16, 2005,Capital Times,3</i>)

>  Had it been presaged even two years ago, there would have been major tanties. <i>(Dec 18, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

>  And if they're not first, then it's tanty fits at 50 paces. <i>(Oct 28, 2001,Sunday Star Times,A7</i>)

>  What is so pathetic is that he has to get the media along to film him throwing his tantie. <i>(May 20, 2001,Sunday Star Times,C5</i>)

>  Plus I wanted to hear about the tantrums and get to laugh at their artistic pretensions. As it turned out, no tanties were witnessed. <i>(Jul 16, 2001,Salient,No. 13 : 22</i>)

>  We also got an authentic Laws tanty with oathing and blinding and a lot of girly foot-stamping, after his luggage got soaked during the eight-hour journey on top of the train. <i>(Apr 2, 2003,Dominion Post,B7</i>)

>  After a brief attempt to find a clear street leading back to the city, the driver threw a tanty and pulled over. <i>(Nov 22, 2005,Capital Times,3</i>)

>  [Hdg] A tour with plenty of laughs and tanties... Tracey throws an impressive tantrum when her boyfriend Stuey turns out to have cancelled a luxury coach booking for a cheaper trip in Tronk's Vee Dub Kombi. <i>(Nov 7, 2000,Dominion,27</i>)

### tantrumatics
 
 <i>n.</i> [Term used by Jenny Shipley on National Radio speaking to Kim Hill on 13/08/98 in reference to Winston Peters' action in walking out. Probably an accidental coinage - tantrum + dramatics]

### tao cooking
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BURTON</b> There are countless ways of preparing food Maori style, but basically there were only three ways of cooking it. The first, which involves steaming in a subterranean oven called an<i> umu</i> or <i>hangi, </i>is known as<i> tao</i> cooking. It was the preferred method of cooking for groups of people, from family size up, in a village setting. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,1</i>)

### taoka
 
 <i>n.</i> treasure [ORIGIN: Maori, SI Maori = taonga]

>  Taoka, however precious to us, become forlorn junk, leftovers, on our deaths <i>(1989,Homeplaces,119</i>)

### taoka tuku iho
 
 <i>phr.</i> treasured asset of the past [ORIGIN: Maori, SI Maori taoka = taonga] (See [taonga tuku iho](t-ten.md#taonga-tuku-iho))

>  It's an opportunity for Ngai Tahu to restore a taoka tuku iho (treasured asset of the past) ... <i>(Jul 2005,Conservation Action,21</i>)

### taokete
 
 <i>n.</i> brother-in-law; sister-in-law [ORIGIN: Maori, Definition from: http://www.learningmedia.co.nz/nz/online/ngata/m2edictionary]

>  Loved Taokete of Irene Mokai. Will be sadly missed. <i>(Feb 24, 2006,Whakatane Beacon,46</i>)

### taonga
 
 <i>n.</i> (collective) goods, valuables; (in singular) a highly-prized (Maori) object or person etc.; a treasure. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Are 'taonga' (valuables) buried there, too? <i>(Dec 4, 1912,Otago Witness,89</i>)

>  The second article of the Treaty guarantees to iwi Maori the control and enjoyment of those resources and taonga which it is their wish to retain. The preservation of a resource base, restoration of iwi self management, and the active protection of taonga, both material and cultural, are necessary elements of the Crown's policy of recognising rangatiratanga. <i>(1989,Principles for Crown Action,10</i>)

>  Ta|onga, s. Property procured by the spear, &c. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,207</i>)

>  <b>DIEEFENBACH</b> Taonga - treasure, property, goods, rich ... <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/384</i>)

>  <b>MANING</b> The chief also, having made some enquiries .. such as, whether I was a <i>rangatira</i>, if I had plenty of <i>taonga</i> (goods) on board .. gave me a most sincere welcome. <i>Ibid.</i> (ii) 22 Has he not half a shipfull of <i>taonga? </i> <i>Ibid.</i> 236 Taonga - Goods; property. <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,(i) 6</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> The beautiful snow-white plumes from the back of this bird [Kotuku] have always been greatly prized by the Maoris, for the personal adornment of both the living and the dead, and their ancient poetry abounds in references to this valued <i>taonga. </i> <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,2/127</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> [The Maori] understood that by signing [the Treaty of Waitangi] he incorporated himself into a community whose stores of taonga (goods), of guns, blankets, axes, rum, and others were illimitable ... <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,113</i>)

>  .. the Maori is reacting on himself & his native culture, equipped with the `taonga' [ed fn: Property, treasures] the pakeha has laboriously gathered for himself. <i>(Jan 4, 1929,Na To Hoa Aroha,1/166</i>) [NOTES: 1929 Jan 4 Na To Hoa Aroha (1986) (Ngata-Buck Correspondence) 1/166 (Ngata)]

>  Before last election Tumate, Te Rata's brother had announced his candidature. He was persuaded after some difficulty and the use of diplomacy to retire but not before Pom had been made to declare he would only run the life of this Parliament and then hand back the `taonga' to Waikato. <i>(Jul 16, 1930,Na To Hoa Aroha,2/40</i>) [NOTES: 1930 July 16 Na To Hoa Aroha (1987) (Ngata-Buck Correspondence) 2/40 (Ngata)]

>  <b>MYERS</b> She was to be married tomorrow .. and the [Maori] children were laden with gifts. Each brought a little <i>taonga</i>; and into her apron, there fell the oddest assortment of things! <i>(1941,Valiant Love,21</i>)

>  A lump of wood of little or no great significance is thus transformed through the art process, by building words (korero) into it and by contact with people, into ting Maoris class as a taonga, or in full, he taonga tuku iho. The dictionary describes taonga as 'property', or as 'anything highly prized' (Williams 1971: 381). Thus a taonga tuku iho is a highly prized object that has been handed down from the ancestors. <i>Ibid.</i> 22 In the popular sense a taonga is a highly prized possession to which is attached korero (text or story). An example is a whakapapa (genealogy) staff. <i>(1984,Te Maori,21</i>)

>  The key element to the power of Te Maori [exhibition] has always been the presence of the elders, the supporters and the performers, that is, the people. We are moved by our taonga, by the ancestors represented in Te Maori. <i>(Jun 13, 1986,NewsVUW (Wellington),13</i>) [NOTES: S.Mead]

>  Wellington Maori, backed by Maori throughout the country, say that the Treaty of Waitangi requires the State to uphold the greatest taonga (treasure) of all, the Maori language, which, they say, has been prejudiced by several Acts of Parliament. <i>(Jun 21, 1986,Evening Post,27</i>)

>  Emotion spilled over as the Governor-General, Sir Paul Reeves, yesterday handed the taonga (treasure) back to the Tahitahi family and Te Aupouri tribe at Waimirirangi meeting house. <i>(Jul 19, 1986,Dominion,1</i>)

>  Old Hapuku .. presented him with a great <i>taonga</i> .. It is the skin of a very peculiar Huia ... <i>(1879,TrNZI,11/337</i>)

>  The head salesman .. had supplied Marama with so much taonga and "truck". <i>(Dec 1912,NZ Free Lance,Xmas Annual 30</i>)

>  A knowledge of Maori art and history and a commitment to the care of taonga is necessary. <i>(Dec 19, 1992,Evening Post,28</i>)

>  Of course the national psyche must suffer somewhat a the sight of one of our national taonga being so publicly and ritually disgraced. <i>(Feb 2006,Express (Gay & Lesbian Express),11</i>)

>  In Jon Gadsby's novel <i>The Trough</i>, the museum elevates an old Kelvinator found at the Petone tip to taonga status, after its provenance is traced back to the kai for the investiture of Dame Te Atairangikaahu. <i>(Apr 8, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

>  It followed the Ngati Kahu claim that they need not pay dog registration fees because those four-foots, which are elsewhere called flea taxis in that other language, were suddenly elevated to "taonga" or treasures and, by definition, gave the tribe a guaranteed right of undisturbed possession under the Treaty of Waitangi. <i>(Mar 29, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

>  "Ngawha to many Maori is a taonga from God... " In addition, most land in the north was of historical value to Maori and could be described as "taonga". <i>(May 19, 2002,NZ Catholic,8</i>)

>  Sarah admits she was going to chuck it all in after missing out on a taonga at the Sydney Olympics in 2000. But with the tautoko and awhi of her partner .. she decided to push on ... <i>(2005,Te Mana  Taiohi,8,Autumn ed.</i>)

>  In New Zealand, a school cannot stop their students from wearing religious symbols, taonga or faith crucial clothing. <i>(May 2005,Tearaway,13</i>)

>  The site - the Observatory Reserve at Kelburn - is highly significant: it is a taonga, a turangawaewae, to be treasured and preserved - none but a fool or a knave would willingly surrender it! <i>(Nov 1993,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,102/66</i>)

>  Taonga such as Topeora's cloak and huia feathers provide the starting point for each episode. <i>(Jun 8, 2006,Dominion Post,EG.8-14 June, p5</i>)

>  <b>Hone Kouka</b> We are now in a position to reclaim land and other taonga that had been confiscated illegally in the past. <i>(1999,Ta Matou Mangai (Our Own Voice),10</i>)

### taonga industry
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  What I now call the taonga industry indeed has the best of backing... The courts, and in particular the Privy Council, may have accepted the Waitangi Tribunal's findings, but the fact is that the taonga industry was founded on myth. <i>(Jun 2, 2004,MediaSearch</i>) [NOTES: The Press, City Edition]

### taonga puoro
 
 <i>n.</i> Maori musical instruments / music

>  Canadian composer Teresa Connors, in partnership with respected taonga puoro musicians Rangiira Hedley ... <i>(Mar 1, 2006,Porirua City News,1</i>)

>  Exhibitions of Ta Moko (body art), Hauora (health in massage), Taonga Puoro, (Maori music), Raranga (weaving), Korero Purakau (storytelling). <i>(Sep 27, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,10</i>)

>  The festival will focus on taonga puoro or Maori musical instruments, and there'll be a free concert from Anika Moa and a kapa haka performance. <i>(May 27, 2004,Wellington Contact,1</i>)

>  Traditional Maori instrument player Richard Nunns says New Zealanders are more familiar with the sounds of the instruments, or taonga puro, than they know. <i>(Sep 24, 2004,Dominion Post,B10</i>)

### taonga species
 
 <i>n.</i> treasured species (See [species taonga](spa-sys.md#species-taonga))

>  Taonga species are native birds, plants and animals of special cultural significance and importance to Ngai Tahu that are found within the Ngai Tahu takiwa, defined by the Te Runanga o Ngai Tahu Act 1996 as being most of the South Island (excluding a northern segment) and the islands to the south including Stewart Island/Rakiura and others. There are 49 bird, 58 plant and 6 marine mammal taonga species - see full list below. <i>(2010,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.doc.govt.nz/about-doc/role/maori/settlements/ngai-tahu/species/]

>  They [Westland petrels] are classified as vulnerable by the International Union for the Conservation of Nature and considered a taonga species by Ngai Tahu. <i>(Jan 8, 2006,Sunday Star Times,A6</i>)

### taonga tuku iho
 
 <i>phr.</i> treasures passed down [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  "The Maori Party is based totally on taonga tuku iho, [treasures passed down] that's come down for 1000 years in these islands and survived colonisation, just. ..." <i>(Dec 2004,North & South,64</i>)

### taonga tuturu
 
 <i>n.</i> valuable artifacts, especially those newly discovered. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  NOTICE OF TAONGA TUTURU FOUND .. Pursuant to section 11 of the Protected Objects Act 1975, the Acting Chief Executive of the Ministry for Culture and Heritage has been notified of the finding of the following taonga tuturu .. Claims for ownership of one or more of the taonga tuturu are to be lodged with the Acting Chief Executuve within 60 working days ... <i>(Feb 11, 2009,Dominion Post,C13</i>)

>  Taonga tuturu are a special category of protected objects. They have to be 50 years or older, but are also things that relate to Maori culture, history or society, or are believed to have been made, or used, by Maori. <i>(Jun 3, 2012,Sunday Star Times,C4</i>)

### taonui
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  By far the most plentiful amongst the bird community at Karewa is the flesh-footed shearwater <i>(Hemipuffinus carneipes)</i>, or taonui of the Maoris. <i>(1924,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,7/183</i>) [NOTES: (Sladden)]

>  FLESH-FOOTED SHEARWATER, or TAONUI (<i>Puffinus carneipes</i>). <i>(1925,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,8/208</i>)

### tap
 
 <i>v.</i> to ask (a person) for a favour, esp. a loan; =(cold-)bite is as common in NZ as elsewhere

>  <b>PATTERSON</b> On arrival on a new field the nearest storekeeper is `tapped' as to his willingness or otherwise to give the newcomer a `start' - that is, to provide him with a few weeks' provisions while he digs the necessary gum. <i>(1904,Kauri-Gum,5</i>)

>  Rumor [sic] has it that before leaving Wellington this youthful nobleman `tapped' a Minister of the Crown for a pony, and soon after his arrival in the northern city he suddenly became short of the `needful'. <i>(Feb 11, 1911,NZ Truth,294/7/1</i>)

>  Some tap the boss before they join,/And their record clearly shows/ By this they make their marble good/ <i>(1926,Transport Workers' Song Book,31</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> To ask for a loan. .in 4;`Perhaps you could get some help from your brother. Why don't you tap him for a loan - I think he'd very likely let you have the money.' <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],1009</i>) [NOTES: (1962-63)1974 Syder and Hodgetts Aust. and NZ English [TS] 1009]

>  <i>tap ... NZ & Austral. colloq.</i> minimum amount of work (in neg. contexts, e.g. <i>he hadn't done a tap</i>). <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,785</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]



 
 <i>v.</i> to obtain bush-bled kauri gum from the live tree

>  <b>REED</b> [Climbing for fresh-bled tree gum] he makes a cut in the bark [of the kauri] like the horizontal barb of an arrow. The cut (he calls it the `tap') is deepest at the apex. <i>(1948,Gumdigger, The,78</i>)

### tap-a-tap
 
 <i>n.</i> [[e.g. under tattoo - 1842 Bremner???]] (See [tapu](t-ten.md#tapu))

### tap-room
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ... the great number of runaway sailors that are to found generally spending every shilling in the tap-room ... <i>(Sep 13, 1849,Letters from Otago,36,1878 ed.</i>)

### tapa
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MANING</b> There was a kind of variation on the <i>tapu</i>, called <i>tapa</i>, of this nature. For instance, if a chief said, `That axe is my head', the axe became his to all intents and purposes, except, indeed, the owner of the axe was able to break his `head', in which case, I have reason to believe, the <i>tapa</i> would fall to the ground. It was, however, in a certain degree necessary to have some legal reason, or excuse, for making the <i>tapa</i>; but to give some idea of what constituted the circumstances under which a man could fairly <i>tapa</i> anything, I must needs quote a case in point. <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,(xii) 174</i>)

### tapa cloth
 
 <i>n.</i> cloth made from paper-mulberry bark

>  For years, he boldly plugged on with his belief that tapa cloth can rightly sit beside quilt-making in exhibitions he has curated - a welding of the indigenous and the colonial. <i>(Sep 2003,NZ Heritage,No. 90/25</i>)

### tapairu
 
 <i>n.</i> first-born female

>  <i>tapairu</i>, first-born female in female of high rank, who was invested with special mana and special tapu: <i>noblesse oblige</i> strictly observed. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/158</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

### Tapanui flu
 
 <i>n.</i> a common name for a group of symptoms otherwise called the myalgic encephalomyelitis (ME) syndrome. [from Tapanui, a town in Southland]

>  [Hdg] Tapanui flu remains a mystery. Right now no one really knows what ME (myalgic emphysema or Tapanui flu) is all about, despite the fervent beliefs of some doctors and many patients who have been pigeon-holed in the appropriate diagnostic box. <i>(Feb 17, 1993,Evening Post,18</i>)

>  His first real break.. was followed by three long years of a perplexing, Tapanui-flu-like condition which saw him sidelined by exhaustion three games out of five. <i>(Sep 2000,North & South,32</i>)

>  Dr Pajak also blames the explosion for the Tapanui 'flu which he believes is caused by exposure to the high levels of radioactivity that can still be found in some pockets in the Crater. <i>(Aug 1988,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,81/52</i>) [NOTES: Reproduced from NZ Truth.]

>  I first became aware of the debilitating nature of this in the early 80s when a workmate's daughter went down with what they then called Tapanbui flu just before whe was due to leave for the UK on her OE... Unfortunately no one knows what causes this chronic fatigue. It has been called many names: Tapanui flu, myalgic encephalomyelitis and immune dysfunction syndrome but it now seems that the medics in this country and Australia have settled on the term Chronic Fatigue Syndrome (CFS). <i>(Apr 2000,North & South,103</i>)

>  In 1983 the 'Tapanui Flu' was for people in Tapanui; terms like ME - myalgic encephalitis - or Chronic Fatigue Syndrome were largely unknown <i>(Mar 1991,More Magazine,108</i>)

>  For a while though it was McGregor who looked after Fergus, when he was badly debilitated by chronic fatigue which dates from a bout of Tapanui flu in 1984. <i>(Jul 1, 2005,Dominion Post,B11</i>)

>  There is a belief - though this has yet to be backed by strong scientific evidence - that a bout of glandular fever can lead to ME (myalgic encephalomyelitus), chronic fatigue syndrome or Tapanui flu. <i>(Mar 19, 2002,Dominion,12</i>)

>  Even its name depends on the direction you come from: benign myalgic encephalomyelitis, Tapanui flu, postviral syndrome, epidemic neurasthenia - or any of 30 longwinded titles. <i>(Nov 29, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

### tapatapa
 
 <i>n.</i> a curse, especially one involving a notable person [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The third curse (<i>tapatapa</i>) was to call anything by a person's name ... For instance, if a chief <i>taptapa'</i>d a spear by saying that it was one of his legs, it became his property ... <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,204</i>)

>  <b>Taylor</b> Another way of obtaining revenge was by cursing. To call any object by the name of a chief, and then strike or insult it, was regarded as a curse; this was tapatapa or tukutuku. <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,94</i>)

>  .. and it is not uncommon to hear a chief complain that he has been shot at, when on explanation it appears that he has only been shot in effigy. This form of insult is called a "tapatapa" or "tukutuku". <i>(Mar 31, 1852,New Zealander, The,2</i>)

>  .. were a chief to become aware that a chief of a neighbouring district had used some insulting expression towards him, he might tapu the track leading to that district, which would cause much inconvenience until it was reopened. That particular kind of insult known as a tapatapa might cause such an action. <i>(1907,Proceedings RSNZ,40/198</i>)

### taper off
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  Stop drinking too much (without misery). In old days station hands and others used to get `on the spree' when they visited town. when they returned to the station they drank a little less each day for a few days till they felt normal. Then they got back to tea, bread, and mutton without much suffering. (C.) <i>(Dec 23, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

### tapeur
 
 <i>n.</i> a frequent borrower (of money) (cf TAP v, and French

>  Oscar was a notorous `tapeur' borowing money from a friend and forgetting to pay it back. <i>(1913,Triad, The,20/9/19</i>)

### tapitap
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A letter might be written about the tapitap so useful for hammer handles, the hakihaki for the handles of axes, and the ramiram for ramming in the posts in fencing. <i>(Jun 15, 1851,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/97,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (H.R. Richmond)]

### Tappy
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) Mount Tapuaenuku (Marlborough) [ca1930 R. Mason Very general among trampers ca1940 common in Marlborough.]

### tappy-tap
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [tapu](t-ten.md#tapu))

### tapu
 
 <i>n.</i> [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> He remembered well how he himself had once tomahawked a Pakeha who broke the tapu of a burial ground. <i>(1938,Brown Man's Burden,36</i>) [NOTES: 1973 edition]

>  The body of Te Heuheu, who was killed by a land slip, was lying in a native tapu. <i>(May 7, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,14</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> A pretty state of affairs, he said, if a tapu tree could be desecrated by mere busybodies. <i>(1938,Brown Man's Burden,36</i>) [NOTES: 1973 edition]

>  <b>CHEVASSE</b> Well, we talked about everything except our bush walk; that was tapu. [fn] Sacred, impermissible. <i>(1990,Integrity,14</i>)

>  "Achieving a great gig occurs when you treat the stage like it's sacred, like a treasure, sort of tapu." <i>(May 8, 2003,Dominion Post,D3</i>)

>  The rock is represented by a stylised pyramidal shape with no sense of mass, power or beauty, and its central placement upstage inhibits the action horribly (being tapu, it cannot be played in front of, so the actors are confined almost entirely to the sides of the stage). <i>(Sep 21, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),42</i>)

>  If the present death rate continues most of the "fine old Maoris" of Whangaroa will be consigned to the local tapu ground. <i>(Dec 6, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  [The Brothers] was a tapu place where dwelt an atua or deity, who controlled the winds and seas. <i>(May 2, 1910,Dominion,6</i>)

>  Now the sons of those men told me that, and they believed in the end that the treaty itself ought to be left alone as a tapu document. <i>(Aug 11, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> [to render tapu, cf taboo]

>  In the parish of Omanaia, in this district, a block of land, containing several thousand acres, has been tapued on the forty-acre system; and not a single settler is residing there. <i>(May 29, 1867,Daily Southern Cross,6</i>)

>  When the Maori war broke out Wells was captured by the Maoris, but was saved from torture and death by the wife of a maori chief, who "tapued" him. <i>(Sep 24, 1889,West Coast Times,4</i>)

### tapu rakau
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  The warrior places before them a tapu rakau (leaf or similar object.) <i>(Dec 19, 1999,NZ Catholic,7</i>)

### tapu'd/tapued
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [Ma.] [of land and other features] sacred.

>  <b>HUNT</b> And so they kept going until the whole of the tapued property, potatoes, pumpkins and barley, were neatly piled up in the yard. <i>(1866,Twenty-Five Years in NZ,51</i>)

>  <b>JACOBSON</b> .. if they had reserved nothing more than the 'tapued' lands that we indicate as being those between Periga (Peraki) and the Bay of Oihoa. <i>(1940,Akaroa Mail and Banks Peninsula Advertiser,184</i>)

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> In 1923 the Waikopiro Maori block was opened up for returned soldiers and the five sections were balloted for. This land was tapu-ed by the maoris on account of the massacre that had taken place there so many years before. <i>(1988,Tales of Pioneer Women,73</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> His [harvesting] fork was his own and tapu'd an inch thick. Lord help any new chum who had the impudence to touch it. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,41</i>)

### tapu-lifting/tapu-removal
 
 <i>n.</i> [attrib.]

>  <b>MIKAERE</b> Tapu-lifting ceremonies were also carried out to [42] remove restrictions which had been imposed for specific purposes. <i>(1998,Te Maiharoa,41</i>)

>  <b>MIKAERE</b> Perhaps because St Paul had said that the old covenant was fulfilled in Christ, the tapu-removal ceremony was to be performed once only. <i>(1988,Te Maiharoa,43</i>)

### tapued
 
 <i>adj.</i>



 
 <i>adj.</i> [also tapu'd] (See [tabooed](t-ten.md#tabooed))

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> In the afternoon we were told that a large party was on their way to this place, for the purpose of stripping us ... Tikoki had stripped an Englishman on the opposite side yesterday ... out of revenge for some property of his, which had been stolen out of a <i>tapu</i>ed house ... <i>(Jan 29, 1827,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The</i>) [NOTES: 1827 Jan 29 H. Williams Early Journals (1961) 41]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> The canoe was <i>Tapu'</i>d, having conveyed the body of Hengi ... to his former place. <i>(Jan 18, 1832,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,1961 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1832 Jan 18 H. Williams Early Journals (1961) 220]

>  <b>YATE</b> A man who has touched a very young child, or has approached a corpse, or has rendered the last sad offices of friendship for a friend, is strictly tapued for several days; and is not allowed to touch food with his hands, nor in any way to feed himself, but by picking up his sustenance from the ground with his lips and teeth. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),85</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> ... such would have been Caddell's fate, had he not ran up to a chief ... who happened accidentally to be tapued at the time, and, catching hold of his garment, was saved in consequence; ... <i>Ibid.</i>1/67 She politely welcomed us, as we marched through her plantation of Indian corn and kumeras [a-acute], which were nearly ripe and in fine condition : unfortunately for us, the place was strictly tapued, or prohibited from being touched by any person, until the following harvest; ..<i>Ibid.</i>1/141 The whole of the prepared flax was tapued [a-acute], and of course safe from depredation. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/52</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> When a place is to be <i>tapued</i>, the intimation is announced to all whom it may concern (or not) by a <i>raoui</i> or grotesque carving in wood, painted with the usual red earth, which is found in certain districts, and is baked after the native method. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,2/276</i>)

>  <b>MAJORIBANKS</b> The `tapu' or tabboo is a ceremony common to New Zealand, along with all the other South Sea Islands. The power of `tapuing' seems to be vested in the principal chief alone, and may be considered the most important bulwark of his authority. This ceremony [90] renders every object, either animate or inanimate, sacred, or not to be approached, so that no person can enter tapued ground, nor touch a tapued person. Every girl promised in marriage is tapued, and married women also, until chastised by their husbands, when the `tapu' ceases, and they are then free to any one who can catch them, a remarkably good plan for securing the affections of the husband, and relieving the sex from some of those periodical chastisements in which some of the husbands in this country are so apt to indulge. Favourite pigs, canoes, and muskets are sometimes tabooed; burial ground is always tabbooed, and so is also a chief's head. <i>(1844,Travels in NZ,89</i>)

>  ... a principal chief, who it seems was <i>tapued</i>, or rendered sacred for the time, was knocked down and beaten by one of the Maori police, ... <i>(May 17, 1851,Lyttelton Times,6</i>)

>  <b>MUNDY</b> The tapued resting place of departed chieftains. <i>(1852,Our Antipodes,284,1855 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [check pagination with 1852 edn]]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> ... at certain seasons places where shell-fish abounded were tapued. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/153</i>)

>  <b>MANING</b> In some cases he would be fed by another person, who ... would manage to do it without touching the<i> tapu'd</i> individual; ... <i>Ibid.</i> (viii) 136 ... I myself once got <i>tapu'd </i>with this same horrible ... style of <i>tapu?</i> <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,(viii) 134</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> About two hours after she retired to a house, in which had been hung up a tapued gun; this she loaded, and ... discharged it. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,63</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> Some of the settlers had unwittingly caused trouble by cutting down `tapued' trees to build boats. <i>(1930,NZ Memories,80</i>)

>  <b>CURR</b> [see introd. cits] No wonder the subject is tabooed (or tapued) here [in Wellington]. <i>(1856,Waste Lands of Wellington, The,5</i>)

>  He walked a short distance to a tapu'd place and with the utmost unconcern brought me the head wrapped in a dirty bloody native mat. <i>(1999,James Preece Missionary Papers,23</i>) [NOTES: From <i>The Missionary Register</i> 1838]

>  The carrying of a road for the Company through a piece of tapued ground is still under consideration, and only requires the exercise of a little patience and pertinacity to secure it. <i>(Feb 16, 1870,Taranaki Herald,8</i>)

>  Ragwort, tutu, docks, fern, etc., outgrow those of any "tapued" native land, while fences are delapidated [sic], gates and rails require painting, and to aggravate (or is it to hide?) the offence, a belt of half-dead and unattended trees, planted by past trustees, adjacent to the fence, are a blot on the landscape and are left to shut out the sun and air from graves and road ... <i>(Jan 5, 1910,Bay of Plenty Times,4</i>)

>  Twenty men (among whom was our cook, Pirougia), headed by Hohaia te Rarau, formed the tapued party. <i>(Apr 30, 1897,Evening Post,2</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> [(see also 1827 cit. a. above)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i> [tapu'd - move to tapued + Note]

### tapuing
 
 <i>n.</i> [aka tabooing]

>  Tiroa [205] returned the knife to me, after he had taken the taboo off, by rubbing it over with the gills of a fish. This strange custom of tabooing I do not understand, further than that it is a law strictly observed, & any wilful breach of it is considered a serious offense, & in severe instances punishable by death. <i>(1827,Boultbee Journal,204</i>) [NOTES: 1827 Boultbee Journal in 1979 Begg 204-205]

>  [This or prev. cit???] This strange custom of tabooing I do not understand, further than it is a law strictly observed, and any wilful breach of it is considered a serious offence, and in several instances punishable by death. <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,88</i>) [NOTES: c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 88]

>  <b>MAJORIBANKS</b> The `tapu' or tabboo is a ceremony common to New Zealand, along with all the other South Sea Islands. The power of `tapuing' seems to be vested in the principal chief alone, and may be considered the most important bulwark of his authority. This ceremony [90] renders every object, either animate or inanimate, sacred, or not to be approached, so that no person can enter tapued ground, nor touch a tapued person. Every girl promised in marriage is tapued, and married women also, until chastised by their husbands, when the `tapu' ceases, and they are then free to any one who can catch them, a remarkably good plan for securing the affections of the husband, and relieving the sex from some of those periodical chastisements in which some of the husbands in this country are so apt to indulge. Favourite pigs, canoes, and muskets are sometimes tabooed; burial ground is always tabbooed, and so is also a chief's head. <i>(1844,Travels in NZ,89</i>)

>  <b>Campbell</b> When any article was fancied, the intending purchaser took a thrumb from the fringe of his or her mat, and fastened it on to the chosen article. If the selector happened to be wearing a blanket or shirt or mat without a fringe, or wearing nothing at all, as was sometimes the case, from which any tapu-ing mark could be detached, then a neighbouring flax bush, or piece of flax from a potato kit, supplied the wherewithal to affix the tapu. <i>(1881,Poenamo,4/6/293</i>)

>  The committtee of runanga have (I hear) agreed to allow the sale of wheat, provided it never rules at a lower rate here than 5s. cash; but as they say we have been making a profitable spec. by sending timber to your market, they have stopped that branch of trade by tapuing the sale of all timber, firewood, &c., either for sale or home consumption. <i>(Jan 29, 1859,Otago Witness,6</i>) [NOTES: Reprinted from the <i>Hawke's Bay Herald</i>]

>  There was a meeting convened for the purpose of taking into consideration the propriety of tapuing an aukati at Aotea, for the object of preventing any white traders visiting Kawhia; and it was agreed by runanga that no persons should be allowed to pass he boundary line without signing their names in a kind of pass-book, kept for the purpose by Maaka, the King's toll-keeper. <i>(Jun 13, 1867,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  The Otago Mining Stock Exchange almost unanimously carried a resolution to ask other exchanges to join them in tabooing any further flotation of dredging companies. <i>(May 24, 1900,Timaru Herald,3</i>)

>  The question of the status of counsel before the Committee was also discussed, and the general feeling appeared to be in favor of tabooing the legal gentlemen. <i>(Jul 15, 1896,Tuapeka Times,3</i>)

### tapuism
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  To institutionalise the religion of "tapuism" is an outright breach of the Bill of Rights, and the Minister responsible should be called to account. <i>(May 6, 2000,Evening Post,4</i>)

### taputini
 
 <i>n.</i> a variety of kumara [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Dell Wihongi, a leading figure in the claim, said the hutuhuti, rekamaroa and taputini were the last remaining varieties of the indigenous crop, and it was important Maori were recognised as guardians. <i>(Feb 1, 2007,NZ Herald,1</i>)

### TAR
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Traffic Accident Report

>  This figure comes from Traffic Accident Reports (TAR) filled out by the police after every accident involving injury they attend... Kippenburger says there are many corners on rural roads that drivers don't manage to negotiate even at 80km/h, and these will probably be recorded as excessive speed on a TAR sheet. <i>(Jan 15, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

### tar
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>tar</i>: Stockholm tar was once used as an antiseptic sealer on cuts, and although this has been generally replaced by improved antiseptics and special powders against flies, these new substances are still referred to as `tar'. At one time in the big sheds when any shearer called `tar' there was a tarboy to dab the cut, but now this is usually by the nearest shedhand. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/28</i>) [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  In case a shearer turns out a sheep badly cut or insufficiently tarred, he shall at once sew and tar such wounds in his pen. <i>(1906,Awards etc.,VII/625</i>)

>  In some sheds the managers insist on the cuts made in shearing being tarred, to prevent them being fly-blown; others use kerosene. <i>(Sep 28, 1878,Otago Witness,3</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to apply tar to a sheep afflicted with shearing wounds or fly-strike.

>  T.J.S rode round again to Kahikanui and tarred a ram with the fly. <i>(Dec 14, 1889,Tutira Station Diary</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Tar</i>.- In the old days t. was always put on sheep cut by the shears. Nowadays some carbolic dip, kerosene, or Condy's is used; but it is always called t. When he cuts a sheep the shearer calls out `T., boy, t. on the leg', and a fleecepicker or broomie dabs the disinfectant on with a <i>t.stick</i> and puts it back in the <i>t. pot.</i> Patterson writes of `The tar boy, the cook, and the slushy, The sweeper that swept the board', but I do not think a tarboy was ever employed specially in New Zealand sheds. (C.) <i>(Dec 25, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> TAR: In earlier days a pot of Stockholm tar was kept in shearing sheds. If sheep were cut during shearing, the cut was dabbed with tar. Today this has been mainly replaced by other antiseptics, but these are still referred to as `tar.' If a shearer cuts a sheep he calls: `Tar.' <i>(1949,High Country Days,197,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> exclam. (shearers') Proceeding just fair, but with their regularity that follows a shearer getting his back used to the arduous job, the advancement went on but with a different and a rougher cut than was usual. More frequently application of the loppies galloping down the board, to the ever increasing cry of `Tar'. The cut was becoming rougher and numbers spoilt good work, and slowly the gore of the classer, the shepherds, and certainly that of William Steuart, rose. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,49</i>)

>  <b>PINNEY</b> A tarboy would disinfect cuts made by the shearers. He came at the cry of `Tar'. For a bad cut the cry would be: `Tar, and a bag for the guts'. <i>(1981,Early Northern Otago Runs,12</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> "Tar" one yelled (when a sheep is cut a shearer calls for tar or sulphur to dab on the cut) . <i>(1975,Mary-Lou,57</i>)

>  WHOLESALE PRICES CURRENT .. TAR: Coal GBP1 15s 0d .. GBP0 0s 0d[,] Stockholm GBP2 0s 0d .. GBP5 0d. <i>(Oct 31, 1840,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,4</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> shortened form of tar-seal [ca?1939 A Havelock rhyme about a child who persisted in riding his tricycle on the newly-sealed main road Robin rode on the Havelock road,/ A motorist came from afar;/ Jim Lovell took his shovel and broom/ And scraped him off the tar.]

>  <b>RHODES</b> `Follow the tar-sealed road as far as Darfield,' Mike Foster's instructions read ... Off the tar now and on to shingle - and into clouds of dust. <i>(1952,Fly Away Peter,99</i>)

### tar pot
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a tin containing Stockholm tar, used to apply to shearing cuts on sheep.

>  <b>MCMILLAN</b> Water pots, tar pots, rosellas, bare bellies - words gone from sheds that will never come back. <i>(1975,More From the High Country,42</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> Each stand had a tar-pot nailed to the wall. <i>(1989,Pioneer's Life, A,44</i>)

>  <b>ASPINALL</b> It was always my job to turn the grindstone for them (shearers); to shed up, pick up, pen up, press up and be there pronto with the tar pot. [tar-pot] <i>(1993,Farming Under Aspiring,93</i>)

>  When the boss is cursing loudly, and the tar-pot's flying round, / When the pieces and the bellies is a coverin' the ground, / When the shears is clicking quickly, and the fleeces coming thickly, / When the rouseabouts are grumblin' - well, ye'll know it's shearin' then. <i>(Feb 17, 1904,Otago Witness,59</i>)

### Tar White
 
 <i>n.</i> an Anglicisation of Te Awa-iti

>  The spot was known by the mellifluous name of Te Awa-iti, "the little river"... How were they to know that.. the whalers and scalliwags.. would corrupt the musical name into the awful crudity of "Tar White?" <i>(1988,Tales of Pioneer Women,165</i>) [NOTES: Written of 1940]

>  The rusting try-pots are the only relics of "Old Tar White". <i>(1944,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),2(1) : 3</i>)

### tar-baby
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> Maori

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> `Those tar-babies and that fellow in the sweater - if you ask me ...' <i>(1959,New Zealanders, The,140,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Play the Fife Lowly)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> road cyclist

>  [heading] The Onslow Tar-babies. ... The name derives from a mountain-bikers' name for road cyclists. ... The Tar-babies have a "president for life", a "hunt master" to control each ride, and a self-appointed committee that meets by Internet or over a beer in the "Postie's Whistle" once or twice a year. ... The Onslow tar-babies are assembled at the old BP station in Khandallah. <i>(Oct 22, 2005,Evening Post,E18</i>)

### tar-boy
 
 <i>n.</i> a person who walks the board where sheep are prone to fly strike and puts a smear of tar on the shearing cuts made in response to the shearers' call `tar!' (Tar is now replaced by modern antiseptics and fly repellants.)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a shed-hand who applies tar to a cut on a sheep being shorn. [shearing; aka tarboy, tar boy]

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> ... there would come a shout from the other end of the board, and if he didn't come at once - 'Tar! boy, tar!' to hurry him up. <i>(1954,On the Hill,12</i>)

>  Tar-boy: the first-aid hand who in earlier days administered Stockholm tar to the wounds on badly shorn sheep. <i>(Feb 21, 1968,Straight Furrow,20</i>)

>  <b>MACNICOL & TROTTER</b> I used to pick up the fleeces and sweep the floor and be tar boy. <i>(1987,I, Duncan from Skippers,61</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> The tar-boy was kept busy applying Stockholm tar to the minor cuts but more severe cuts had to be stitched. <i>(1989,Pioneer's Life, A,44</i>)

>  <i>Tar.</i>- In the old days<i> t.</i> was always put on sheep cut by the shears. Nowadays some carbolic dip, kerosene, or Condy's is used; but it is always called<i> t</i>. When he cuts a sheep the shearer calls out `<i>T</i>., boy,<i>t</i>. on the leg', and a fleecepicker or broomie dabs the disinfectant on with a <i>t.stick</i> and puts it back in the <i>t. pot.</i> Patterson writes of `The tar boy, the cook, and the slushy, The sweeper that swept the board', but I do not think a tarboy was ever employed specially in New Zealand sheds. (C.) <i>(Dec 23, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>BOWEN</b> TAR-BOY. The hand who walks the board where sheep are subject to the fly and who puts a smear of tar of the cuts made on sheep. <i>(1956,Wool Away!,157</i>)

>  The first-aid hand who in earlier days administered Stockholm tar to wounds of sheep cut in shearing. <i>(Feb 26, 1965,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>) [NOTES: (H.A.L. Laing)]

>  <i>tarboy n.</i> woolshed hand who applies tar to cuts made on sheep by shearers. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),626</i>)

>  TAR-BOY: The first-aid hand who in earlier days administered Stockholm tar to the wounds on badly shorn sheep. <i>(Feb 21, 1968,Straight Furrow,20</i>) [NOTES: Shearer's language]

>  <i>tarboy ... n</i>. woolshed hand who applies tar to cuts made on sheep by shearers. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),19</i>)

>  <b>PINNEY</b> [Notes] 12. A tarboy would disinfect cuts made by the shearers. He came at the cry of `Tar'. For a bad cut the cry would be: `Tar, and a bag for the guts'. <i>(1981,Early Northern Otago Runs,229</i>)

>  Person who walks the board where sheep are prone to fly strike and puts a smear of tar on the shearing cuts made in response to the shearers' call `tar'. Tar is now replaced by modern antiseptics and fly repellants. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,60</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> ... I was allowed to miss school to help in the shed by picking up the bellywool and removing the pizzle stains from the wether bellywool. I was also `tar-boy', which meant putting dabs of Stockholm tar on any cuts inflicted by the shearers. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,55</i>)

>  tar: Stockholm tar was once used as an antiseptic sealer on cuts, and although this has been generally replaced by improved antiseptics and special powders against flies, these new substances are still referred to as `tar'. At one time in the big sheds when any shearer called `tar' there was a tarboy to dab the cut, but now this is usually by the nearest shedhand. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/28</i>) [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]

>  But the shearer is up and busy two or three minutes before the time, has taken his shears from the tar-boy, or "picker up," who has been practicing a little through the leisure, has given the blades a final touch with the stone, thrown the coat off his shoulders, and stripped and eager as a wrestler at the call of "time," stands at the door of his pen. <i>(Oct 20, 1887,Timaru Herald,4</i>)

>  One recently returned, per registered parcel post, addressed to a Hawke's Bay station-owner, a pair of greasy bluchers, found in a bale of New Zealand wool. Probably, a "tar-boy's" joke. <i>(Sep 19, 1903,New Zealand Free Lance,26</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> I was also "tar-boy", which meant putting dabs of Stockholm tar on any cuts inflicted by the shearers. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,55</i>)

### tar-brand
 
 <i>v.</i> tar-mark, sc. of sheep, or occas. cattle

>  <b>ELWELL</b> It took a good month to muster and tar-brand all the sheep. <i>(1878,Boy Colonists,202</i>)

>  Mr Heslop said he had had cattle in his possession which had been tar-branded for weeks, and the brand was quite plain; and, more than that, he used to tar-brand all the cattle he sent to the Witaraa works, and there would be no difficulty about it at all. <i>(Jun 20, 1893,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

>  No doubt, the sheepfarmers of the South were compelled to tar-brand immediately after shearing, and in the case of merinos this would be generally sufficient for a twelvemonths, but long-wool sheep would have to be branded at frequent intervals to keep the brand legible. <i>(Jun 21, 1889,Poverty Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  No doubt, the sheepfarmers of the South were compelled to tar-brand immediately after shearing, and in the case of merinos this would be generally sufficient for a twelvemonths, but long-wool sheep would have to be branded at frequent intervals to keep the brand legible. <i>(Jun 21, 1889,Poverty Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  Mr Heslop said he had had cattle in his possession which had been tar-branded for weeks, and the brand was quite plain; and, more than that, he used to tar-brand all the cattle he sent to the Waitara works, and there would be no difficulty about it at all. <i>(Jun 20, 1893,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

>  Some parties who had previously paid Messrs. Chalmers for twelve months, and whose term was not expired, claimed to be exempted till the end of such period producing their receipt, were informed they should have to pay just as others would have to do; that he should tarbrand all cattle on the run to prevent evasion; that all calves when six months old should be so branded and paid for ... <i>(Oct 24, 1868,Tuapeka Times,3</i>)

>  Mr Budge interjected that if you tar-branded cattle, and the cattle rubbed, together in the yard, the brands would simply be a smudge in the course of half an hour, and that certainly the brands would not last. <i>(Jun 20, 1893,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [tar mark on farm animal, as label; incl attrib] (See [tar-mark](t-ten.md#tar-mark))

>  At first sight I thought I was in this way going to escape the tar-brand business; but no turning to clause 19, I find that though I may ear-mark if I like, I am in some mysterious way to do so "without removing any part of the whole ear," under a penalty of GBP20. <i>(Jun 8, 1880,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  I could not swear to his sheep but by the tar-brand; they were not counted to me. <i>(Jul 4, 1857,Otago Witness,2</i>)

>  LOST from Messrs. Hunter and Co.'s saleyards, on the night of Saturday last, a Yellow HEIFER, springing in calf, with white stripe down the back, white tail, short horns, and a tar-brand "33". <i>(Jun 18, 1866,Daily Southern Cross,1</i>)

>  LOST from the farm of JOHN CROZIER. - One WHITE HEIFER, branded 30 (fire-brand) and 13 (tar-brand). <i>(Sep 19, 1881,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)

>  It is too late for any suggestions as to wool-branding sheep to be made use of for the benefit of this season's clip, but the complaints respecting tar-brands in wool are so strong that we repeat the buyers' remarks on the subject, so that when branding sheep after shearing some of teh many non-injurious branding materials may be used. <i>(Oct 4, 1892,Ashburton Guardian,2</i>)

>  STRAYED, from the Scotch Church, Otahuhu, a Strawberry COW, close to her calving; tar-brand 24. <i>(Apr 2, 1870,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

### tar-mark
 
 <i>n.</i> [tar mark or brand as label on farm animal] (See [see tar-brand](t-ten.md#tar-brand))

>  LOST, last week, four fat COWS; and the week previous, one fat STEER with tar-mark on shoulder. <i>(Apr 20, 1883,Otago Daily Times,1</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to mark a farm animal (espec. sheep) with tar [cf.tar-brand v]

>  <b>PHARAZYN</b> Tar marked B's and M's sheep. <i>(1847,Journal,81</i>)

>  ... tar-marking and branding cattle <i>(Aug 30, 1918,NZEF Chronicles,61</i>)

>  STRAYED from Kaikorai Yards on Wednesday last, 16th inst., THREE COWS, tar-marked near shouder and loin. <i>(Apr 23, 1873,Otago Daily Times,1</i>)

>  STRAYED from Kaikorai Yards on Wednesday last, 16th inst., THREE COWS, tar-marked on near shoulder and loin. <i>(Apr 23, 1873,Otago Daily Times,1</i>)

>  The following morning Ching and I went to draft the cattle; he .. called my attention to a calf that was not tar-marked. <i>(Dec 15, 1884,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

### tar-marked
 
 <i>adj.</i> [stock farming: marked with tar as farmer's label]

>  A large quantity of wool is used in the manufacture of carpets and similar productions, and although employes [sic] are cautioned to exercise due care and vigilance to take out any tar-marked wool, they may discover, it frequently happens that tar marks in small quantities pass through the various stages into the finished goods and cause considerable damage and loss. <i>(Jun 4, 1896,Star, The,2</i>)

### tar-pot
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SMITH</b> Prior to the commencement of shearing the board is fitted with `tar-pots', so called because in the early days tar was used to put on the cuts accidentally made by the shearers; but as tar [74] would not scour out ..., it is now usual to use an antiseptic liquid. <i>(1927,Sheep & Wool Industry,73</i>)

>  When he cuts a sheep the shearer calls out `<i>T.</i>, boy,<i> t</i>. on the leg', and a fleecepicker or broomie dabs the disinfectant on with a <i>t.stick</i> and puts it back in the <i>t. pot</i>. <i>(Dec 23, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> Get ready with tar-pot and brooms for the job,/ While the shearers stroll over to size up the mob/ <i>(1950,Farm & Station Verse,185</i>) [NOTES: 1949 Stronach `Shearing' in 1950 Woodhouse Farm and Station Verse 185]

### tar-seal
 
 <i>n.</i> a tar-sealed road, bitumenised roadway [aka tarseal]

>  <b>FRAME</b> <i>(1957,Owls Do Cry,48</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> Tar-seal gave way to a road of clay and pumice ... <i>(1959,New Zealanders, The,88,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Love Story)]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Harry ... got out saying he wanted to live and walked ahead of us along the tar-seal, a defiant figure marching along in the headlights, ... <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,15</i>)

>  <b>BACON</b> As we came nearer to Estuarine, the roads improved, and once we were on the tarseal, my spirits rose. <i>(1963,In the Sticks,172</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> <i>(1963,Piece of Land, A,91</i>)

>  <i>[tar]-sealed</i> (New Zealand), surfaced with [tar] macadam, so <i>[tar]-seal</i> n.; <i>(1964,Concise Oxford Dictionary,1325</i>)

>  <b>DAVIS</b> At the shops the tar-seal ended as though it had never been and the going became rough, two wheel tracks through loose metal, ... <i>(1964,Watersiders,129</i>)

>  <b>SEVERINSEN</b> Wilby and I were pounding up the tarseal toward Te Anau Downs with Terry. <i>(1970,Hunt the Far Mountain,123</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> There are a few no-exit offshoots near the town but once the ten miles of tarseal give on to the rough, metalled surface, one is bereft even of the comforting sighting of a friendly crossroads and a few direction signs. <i>(1971,Plekhov Place, The,9</i>)

>  <b>ARMFELT</b> So they took the tar-seal to Waitapa. <i>(1971,Catching Up,62</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> I want to go up the river road/... Past Atene where the tarseal ends, <i>(1979,Collected Poems,589,1972 ed.</i>)

>  By the time they hit the tarseal she was well out of sight. The road [271] was perfect for speed <i>(1973,Islands,5/270</i>) [NOTES: Gifkins (Winter Riders)]

>  <b>STEAD</b> He turned towards the lake. Just below the motel the tarseal came to an end and the road was of clay ... . <i>(1973,Smith's Dream,80,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>STEAD</b> He turned towards the lake. Just below the motel the tarseal came to an end and the road was of clay ... . <i>(1973,Smith's Dream,80,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> We were awfully quiet in the car, as Gil ripped her along the tar-seal at about fifty-five. <i>(1973,NZ Jack,97</i>)

>  <b>CURNOW</b> and a rubbery squeal / tells the tarseal / that a man goes home / [Check 1937 edn <i>Enemies</i>] <i>(1974,Collected Poems,31</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> bitumen road On the suburban side of the Belt all to the eye was uniform and conforming, the latest tar-seal serving the affluent middle-class and their housing : new cars breaking the speed limit ... <i>(1974,Islands,7/92</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> On the suburban side of the Belt all to the eye was uniform and conforming, the latest tar-seal serving the prosperous middle-class and their housing; ... <i>(1976,Sunset Village,31</i>)

>  <b>FRY</b> Two oppossums/ ... / on the tarseal/ hammered flat/ by singing tyres/ <i>(1978,Brushed Words,7</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Sandra presses her horn ... and the youngish man ... starts to head back to his appropriate side of the tarseal. <i>(1979,Skin Deep,39</i>)

>  <b>HOOPER</b> ... he watched cars flashing past on the wet tar-seal ... <i>(1981,Goat Paddock,159</i>)

>  She said she felt the bus go off the tarseal and shouted. The driver then whipped the bus to the right. <i>(Mar 2, 1982,Dominion,1/6</i>)

>  <b>WEDDE</b> Look out for the shellfish family licking its way/ across the fourth lane of tarseal/ <i>(1984,Georgicon,42</i>) [NOTES: (`Off/Of')]

>  [caption to a photo of a land-yacht] Taking to the tarseal <i>(Sep 1, 1986,Dominion,3</i>)

>  <i>tar-seal v.t. NZ & Austral. </i>surface (a road etc.) with tar macadam; <i>tar-seal n. NZ & Austral</i>. a tar-sealed road or area; <i>tar-sealed a. </i> <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,786</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <i>King of the Tarseal</i> [title] <i>(Jun 1987,Landfall,162/125</i>) [NOTES: (Murphy)]

>  <b>HILL</b> ... 30-odd wethers, all pattering ... along the tarseal on their way from Wally's paddock six sections down the road to Wally's other paddock eight sections up the road. <i>(1988,More from Moaville,28</i>)

>  Everything has been tried, starting with the old macadam rolled in water-bond to tar-concrete, tar-macadam, tar-sealed macadam, Bituco, Tarvia, Kortfelt, and a dozen other patent and other methods, and, lastly, plain cement concrete. <i>(Jun 17, 1914,Evening Post,14</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> [aka tarseal]

>  <b>KNOX</b> There's a long, pot-holed tarseal road between the towns ... <i>(1987,After Z-Hour,110</i>)

>  Macadam, the famed roading engineer after whom tarseal roads are named, was one of the first to use broken rock in the early 1880s. <i>(Oct 16, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),50</i>)

>  Many times during the past few years we have referred in these columns to "non-permanent" macadam roads, to "permanent" concrete roads (cushioned with suitable material), and to the various grades of "semi-permanent" tarseal or tar-macadem roads that fill the space between the "non-permanent" and "permanent" types. <i>(Jul 18, 1921,Evening Post,6</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> [aka tarseal]

>  <b>HALL-JONES</b> With the tarsealing of the road from Invercargill, Te Anau township surged ahead ... <i>(1968,Early Fiordland,180</i>)

>  <b>BOYD</b> When more money became available, a start was made by forming and metalling Heretaunga Street and levelling and tar-sealing a footpath past the town hall. <i>(1984,City of the Plains,84</i>)

>  Mossburn - Te Anau road.. has had its corners straightened, its dips filled and its surface tarsealed. <i>(Sep 7, 1956,NZ Herald,5</i>)

>  <i>(Aug 2002,NZ Memories,37/16</i>) [NOTES: There was little traffic about and many of the roads in Taranaki were tar-sealed, thanks to the revenue from toll-gates which were placed strategically around the province.]

>  In view of the number of new houses which are being erected the City Council is to be requested to have the footpath in Nicholson Road between Clutha Avenue and Cockayne Road tar-sealed. <i>(Nov 18, 1937,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  <b>LAMBERT</b> The Waimate West County achieved a New Zealand first in roading, by tar-sealing roads in their area in 1914. <i>(1983,Illustrated History of Taranaki,106</i>)

>  Of the 293 miles 39 chains of roads in the Taranaki County, 54 miles 77 chains or 18.6 per cent, are tar-sealed. <i>(May 25, 1939,Taranaki Herald,4</i>)

### tar-sealed
 
 <i>adj.</i> [aka tarsealed]

>  <b>DARE</b> When we'd gone twenty-five miles on a pretty good tar-sealed main road, we switched off into a secondary road ... <i>(1956,Rouseabout Jane,2</i>)

>  Goodbye - To the quickening kiss, and the long-drawn sigh, / And the tar-sealed roads and the open sky. <i>(Jul 25, 1928,NZ Free Lance,42</i>)

>  The anual [sic] cost of 75 miles tar-sealed ("Restar") roads and concrete bridges is estimated as follows <i>(Mar 7, 1914,Wanganui Chronicle,6</i>)

>  Everything has been tried, starting with the old macadam rolled in water-bond to tar-concrete, tar-macadam, tar-sealed macadam, Bituco, Tarvia, Kortfelt, and a dozen other patent and other methods, and, lastly, plain cement concrete. <i>(Jun 17, 1914,Evening Post,14</i>)

>  In truth it's nothing more than a booze up in a tar-sealed car park. Everyone's in shorts and jandals and they're drinking beer straight from the can or bottle. The beer is in cartons piled up on the back of a ute. <i>(Mar 1992,Metro,32</i>)

>  Mossburn - Te Anau road.. has had its corners straightened, its dips filled and its surface tarsealed. <i>(Sep 7, 1956,NZ Herald,5</i>)

>  There was little traffic about and many of the roads in Taranaki were tar-sealed, thanks to the revenue from toll-gates which were placed strategically around the province. <i>(Aug 2002,NZ Memories,37/16</i>)

### tar-sealing
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> ... Councillor Whortle was hoping that the Mayor would support a motion ... for tar-sealing the street in which he lived. <i>(1935,Little Country, The,140,1937 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [See also sealing; [dateline missing???] The tar was coming out of the tar-sealing. Tar-sealing is a dirty job]

>  Shingle for tar-sealing has been started out for the road fronting Powdrell's property. <i>(Oct 9, 1917,Wanganui Herald,8</i>)

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> Catching butcher-boys was good fun ... You chose a piece of footpath where there was no tar-sealing and looked for little round holes in the earth. When you found a hole, you pushed the soft end of a grass stalk into it ... As soon as it moved you pulled it out and caught a small white grub clinging to the end with its nippers. <i>(1959,Stone, The,29</i>) [NOTES: (The Stone)]

>  Who among the pupils of the time will ever forget the tar-sealing of the road. <i>(1957,Hurleyville School 65th Anniv.,15</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> [aka tarsealing]

>  The big tar-sealing programme is now nearly completed. <i>(Jan 10, 1941,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  The acquisition of the water synchronised with the tar-sealing of the first stretch of the main road in Pahiatua County a mile and a half past the hospital. <i>(Feb 11, 1935,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  An announcement of interest to local bodies having control of roads was made to-night, when it was stated that a company had been formed to manufacture a tar-sealing compound which promises to go a long way towards solving the roading problem. <i>(Apr 21, 1914,Poverty Bay Herald,8</i>)

>  He favoured an extension of footpaths and tar-sealing work. <i>(Apr 26, 1929,Evening Post,15</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SLATTER</b> ... I saw the England of working with a tamper ... and wheeling barrows of chips for the tar-sealing gang I was on, ... <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,37</i>)

### tar-stick
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  In the old days <i>tar</i> was always used to put on sheep cut by the shears. Nowadays some carbolic dip, kerosene, or Condy's is used; but it is always called <i>tar</i>. When he cuts a sheep the shearer calls out `Tar, boy, tar on the leg,' and a fleecepicker or broomie dabs the disinfectant on with a <i>tar-stick</i> and puts it back in the<i> tar-pot</i>. . <i>(1933,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: Acland]

>  I got out, and looked for the tarstick. <i>(Mar 27, 1909,Marlborough Express,2</i>)

### tar-water
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an infusion of tar and water, used as a stock and pasture remedy.

>  I am sorry to say that tar water has proved a failure against root fungus. <i>(Oct 1890,NZ Farmer,402</i>)

>  Mr Kirk gives the following recipoe for making the tar-water: Boil 1/2 lb of coal tar in 1 gallon of water; after boiling for sometime, dilute with 50 gallons of fresh cold water. <i>(1898,NZ Farmer,186</i>)

### Tar-white
 
 <i>n.</i> Te-awa-iti

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> The native name of this place is Te-awa-iti, or `The little Stream'; but it is more generally called `Tar-white' by the whalers. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,230</i>)

>  EASTERN ENTRANCE, OR TORY CHANNEL. Te Awaiti of the natives. Tarwhite of the whalers. <i>(1854,Canterbury Almanac, The,70</i>)

### tar-wood
 
 <i>n.</i> monoao (<i>D. sublatum</i>, a shrub

>  This species is the yellow pine or tar-wood of the Otago settlers. <i>(1877,TrNZI,390</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Tar-wood, n</i>. name given by the Otago bushmen to the tree, <i>Da[c]rydium colensoi</i>, Hook.; Maori name, <i>Manoao</i> (q.v.). (Kirk, `Forest Flora,' p. 189.) <i>(1898,Austral English,460</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> D. Colensoi, the silver-pine of Westland. <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,77</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [1889 KFF (Andersen) <i>Dacrydium Bidwillii </i>]



 
 <i>n.</i> [1875 LFN 1909 CSI (Andersen, 4 entries)]

>  <b>KIRK</b> (And) <i>(1889,Forest Flora</i>)

>  Pine, mountain (tarwood) .. Dacrydium Bidwillii .. Timber hard, and used for firewood <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,13</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>D[acrydium] biforme, Pilger </i>... Mountain districts ... <i>Yellow-pine; Tar-wood.</i> This is for the most part the <i>D. Colensoi </i>of the [Hooker] Flora ... <i>Dacrydium biforme </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,653</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And) <i>Dacrydium biforme</i> tar-wood <i>(1908,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: Report on a Botanical Survey of the Tongariro National Park]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And) <i>Dacrydium biforme</i> tar-wood <i>(1909,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: Report on a Botanical Survey of Stewart Island]

>  Of the so-called pines there were many varieties, perhaps the most common being the bog pine, or tarwood (Dacrydium Colensoi) a tree remarkable on account of its exhibiting leaves of the most opposite appearance, as even on the same branch the young leaves will be found spreading in shape and half-an-inch in length, while the older ones are so closely placed that the branchlets seem to resemble strings of whipcord. <i>(Feb 6, 1907,Otago Witness,25</i>)

### tara
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [taro](t-ten.md#taro))



 
 <i>n.</i> a fern

>  The former extent of native cultivations is attested by the common occurrence of the Tara [is this the correct location???] <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/91</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Tara, n. </i>...(2) A Tasmanian aboriginal name for the fern <i>Pteris aqulina ... 1834</i> Ross, `Van Diemen's Land Annual,' p.129: `The most extensively diffused eatable roots of Van Diemen's Land are those of the tara fern [etc.] ...' <i>(1898,Austral English,458</i>)

>  <i>Tara [marked foreign word]</i> ... sb. [? Native name in Tasmania.] The edible fern of Tasmania and New Zealand, a variety of the common brake, Pteris aquilina <i>var</i>. esculenta. <i>Also</i> tara fern. <i>(1933,Oxford English Dictionary,11/89</i>)

>  <i>tar'a(-fern)</i>, n. Edible fern of New Zealand etc. [Tasmanian] <i>(1964,Concise Oxford Dictionary,1325</i>) [NOTES: [omitted 1982 7th ed.]]

>  <i>n</i>. in New Zealand, the sea swallow or tern; variety of fern [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),626</i>)

>  <i>tara ... n. </i>in N.Z., sea swallow or tern; variety of fern [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),19</i>)

>  <i>ta[a-dieresis]ra, n. </i>a variety of bracken with edible rhizome. - also <i>tara-fern. . </i> <i>(1977,Chambers Twentieth-Century Dictionary,1380</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> female genitals [is this from an Island word: used only by females: is it a have on from Williams <i>Tara (i) .. 6. Membrum virile</i>.: or from <i>Tara (vi). Pudenda muliebra.</i>: or possibly <i>Tara</i> (iii) ... 3. ... <i>Gosip, scandal.</i>: or from a modern Maori word (HWO);I knew this use from South Taranaki in the 1940s (DEH) 1984 16 F E37 Pakuranga Coll. 11 TARA [addressin an equal] -->1984 16 F E40 Pakuranga Coll. 11 TARA -->1984 16 F E42 Pakuranga Coll. 25 TARAS]



 
 <i>n.</i> usually the white-fronted tern; but also the black-fronted tern and other varieties. [Maori] often with suffixed epithets, applied to various sea-birds:

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> T|ara, s. A gannet; <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,208</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> <i>77. Sula australis, Gould,</i> ... Tara of the natives of Queen Charlotte's Sound. - <i> Dieffenbach. </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/200</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/200]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> tern (white-fronted tern, etc.), sea-swallow, [cf hydro + progne]

>  <b>POLACK1/112</b> Puhi ... arranged the hair of the gentlemen, placing ... the various feathers of the uia and tara, sea-fowl, that assisted the decoration. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack)</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The gentlemen arranged the hair of each other, and being all sons of chiefs, placed with the <i>gout</i>[u-circumflex] of connoisseurs the plucked feathers of the albatross <i>(Uianui)</i>, gannet (<i>tara</i>[a1-acute]), and other sea-fowl in various parts. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/80</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> [Word list] Tara a sea bird Toro do a large bird <i>(Aug 1843,Journal,52</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> ... we remarked a great number of sea swallows (Tara) flying about in every direction ... <i>(Nov 10, 1845,Journal,3/166</i>)

>  <b>PRESHAW</b> eggs of a small bird called tara, or sea swallow <i>(1888,Banking Under Difficulties,105</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  Sterna frontalis. Tara. Abundant. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/161</i>) [NOTES: (Hutton Birds Great Barrier)]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 113. STERNA FRONTALIS. Gray. Sea Swallow. Tara. <i>S. LONGIPENNIS.</i> FINSCH. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,42</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> STERNA FRONTALIS. (WHITE-FRONTED TERN.) ... <i>Native name</i>.- Tara; `Sea-swallow' of the Colonists. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,281,{1888 2nd edn 2/68} ed.</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> STERNA ANTARCTICA. (GREY \{BLACK-FRONTED\} TERN.) ... <i>Native name.</i>- Tara. ... This ... Tern is very common in every part of the South Island, but is only occasionally met with \{is not so plentiful\} on the north side of Cook's Strait. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,282,{1888 2nd edn 2/71} ed.</i>)

>  STERNA - Sea swallows (<i>Tara</i>). Five species in New Zealand ...; but <i>Sterna antarctica</i> (Common Tern) I found as far as forty miles inland ... <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/99</i>) [NOTES: (Reischek)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Tara, n.</i> (1) Maori name for the birds <i>Sterna caspia,</i> Pallas, and <i>S. frontalis</i>, Gray, the Sea-Swallow, or <i>Tern</i> (q.v.). <i>(1898,Austral English,458</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,2/96</i>)

>  <i>tara</i>, a sea-bird (Sterna stricta), white-fronted tern: several other birds are known as tara with suffix, as S. nereis, fairy tern, tara-iti; Hydroprogne caspia Caspian tern, tara-nui; Larus novae hollandiae, red-billed gull, and L. bulleri, black-billed gull, tara-punga; but these are not so definitely used in general as to be adopted. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/158</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>POWELL</b> WHITE FRONTED TERN (<i>Sterna striata</i>), Tara of the Maoris, is abundant all around the New Zealand coasts and occurs in Tasmania and Eastern Australia as well. This is the graceful little `Kahawai-bird' or `Sea-swallow,' which pursues schools of small surface fish, wheeling, darting and dipping to the water, in its energetic quest of food. Since the Kahawai fish is predaceous on the small school fish also, the tern is an excellent indicator of the presence of Kahawai. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,80</i>)

>  <i>(1952,Chambers Shorter English Dictionary Supp.,805/2</i>) [NOTES: [OEDS]]

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>Sterna ... frontalis [? striata]</i> ... It pursues schools of small fish close to the coasts of New Zealand, Tasmania and Australia, and as the kahawai fish also feed on the same class of food, the tern is a useful indicator to fishermen of their presence. Hence its name of kahawai bird. Kahawai Bird. Sea Swallow, Tom Noddy. TARA. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,82</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> BLACK-FRONTED TERN <i>Chlidonias albostriatus Other names</i>: Tara, Sea Martin, Ploughboy, Inland Tern, Riverbed Tern. <i>Ibid.</i> 164 WHITE-FRONTED TERN <i>Sterna striata Other names</i>: Sea-swallow, Kahawai Bird, Tara, Blackcap, Grenadier, Swallowtail, Noddy (Chathams), Tikkitak (Stewart Is.) <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,157</i>)

>  <i>n</i>. in New Zealand, the sea swallow or tern; variety of fern [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),626</i>)

>  <i>tara ... n.</i> in N.Z., sea swallow or tern; variety of fern [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),19</i>)

>  <i>205. Sterna striata</i> (Gmelin, 1789) <i>White-fronted Tern (Tara)</i> New Zealand <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,55,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Black-fronted Tern Sterna albostriata</i> (GRAY, 1845) ... OTHER NAME: <i>Tara. </i> <i>Ibid.</i> 232 <i>White-fronted Tern Sterna striata</i> (GMELIN, 1789) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Tara, sea-swallow, kahawai bird, black cap, grenadier, swallowtail, noddy. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,227</i>)

>  hey funny! think of all the taras in there! Tara tara tara! <i>(1986,Te Kaihau,10</i>)

>  <i>tara n.</i> black and grey NZ variety of tern. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,786</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  "Tara" is a greenstone skewer. It's also a seabird... The writer of these words is lying on a hillside "near the top" ("tara" can mean the top of a hill)... "Tara" can also mean the side wall of a house. <i>(May 31, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener)</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

### Taradise
 
 <i>n.</i> Taranaki

 <i>n.</i> Taradale
