### taraire
 
 <i>n.</i> variant spellings of taraire [taraire taraide/tarairi]



 
 <i>n.</i> a native tree, <i>Beilschmiedia tarairi</i>, used in fencing and in some farm buildings (common north of Auckland). [ORIGIN: Maori, aka tarairi, taraide]

>  Taraire (Tryday) splits very easily indeed and for that reason would not hold staples as well as some other timbers. <i>(Sep 13, 1951,NZ Farmer,28</i>)

>  A mellow finishing touch inside has resulted from using varnished taraire on the wall and gates facing the board. <i>(Nov 27, 1975,NZ Farmer,14</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> T|araide,<i> </i>s. A tree so called. <i>(1920,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,208</i>)

>  <b>McDONNELL</b> A great variety of hard-wood grows at New Zealand, admirably adapted for the timbering of any sized ships; among them is the boride[e-acute], rattar[as-acute], taraide[ase-acute], mai[a-acute], totara[a1-acute], koi katoa[a2-acute], toa[a-acute] toa[a-acute], tani raha[as-acute], to wai[a-acute], reiva[a-acute] reiva[a-acute], tana[as-acute], and many others... <i>Ibid.</i> ?...taraide [acutes on a,a,e] <i>(1834,Extracts From a Journal,19,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> Torairi [sic] (<i>Laurus macrophylla</i>) - This tree grows to ... fifty to seventy feet ... Its berries are black, exactly resembling the damson in size and appearance; ... Its leaves are like those of the finest, largest and most brilliant English laurel; ... <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),42</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> <i>Tarairi, (laurus tarairi</i>,) is among the least valuable of timber trees. It is bacciferous, bearing a dark purple berry, on which the wild pigeon feeds heartily. The taste is particularly acrid. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/400 There are many other woods of much service to Europeans, differing in quality, among others: the 30 Warangai.. 36 Tarairi .. 45 Parekireki, &c. Of all these woods there are abundant quantities. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/396</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> The Taraire is a very comon tree sometimes used for fencing, splits fairly , but soon decays, it bears a large blue plum, apparently wholesome, ... <i>(1844,NZ Journal,105,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Neso daphne <i>Tarairi</i>, Hook. fil. ... Nat. name, `Taraire', <i>Cunn. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 217</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Taraire, <i>Col[enso]. Nesodaphne taraire. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Also the Tarairi, an ornamental but not valuable tree, only fit for firewood and charcoal. <i>(1868,Past and Present of NZ,205</i>)

>  The general character of the [Great Barrier Island] bush greatly resembles that of the main land north of Auckland, its ... difference being the variety of the Tarairi, <i>Neodaphne Tarairi</i> ... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/144</i>) [NOTES: (T. Kirk)]

>  Tarairi. Used for most of the purposes for which sycamore is applied in Europe. <i>(1873,Catalogue of Vienna Exhibition</i>) [NOTES: (from Morris)]

>  <b>HAY</b> The Taraire (<i>Nesodaphne Taraire</i>) is a huge and handsome tree ... <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/196</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> Laurel-tree (Tarairi) ..... O. Lauriniae; G. Neodaphne; S. N. Tarairi. `A forest tree; sixty to eighty feet high; berry one and a half inches long; deep purple.' <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,2/336</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> <i>Beilschmiedia tarairi</i>, Hook. Tarairi.--A lofty forest tree, 60-80 high, with stout branches. Grows in northern parts of North Island. <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,129</i>)

>  <b>KIRK</b> [Native name] Taraire .. [Settlers' name] Taraire .. [Family] Lauriniae <i>(1886,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,121</i>)

>  The general bush consists of Manuka ... Rata ... Taridi Tawa ... <i>(1887,TrNZI,19/182</i>) [NOTES: (Reischek)]

>  Taraire .. Beilschmiedia tarairi .. Wood close, straight in the grain, and easily split; used for firewood <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,14</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Taraire[dieresis], n. </i>Maori name for a New Zealand tree; formerly <i>Nesodaphne tarairi,</i> Hook., now <i>Beilschmiedia tarairi</i>, Benth. and Hook.,<i> N.O. Laurineae.</i> <i>(1898,Austral English,459</i>)

>  <i>Ta-rai.i-re</i> (ta.-ra.i.&emacr.-r&amacr.; <i>colloq</i>. t&adot.-r&imacr.i.re.), n. [Maori.] A New Zealand lauraceous tree, <i>Beilschmiedia (Nesodaphne) tarairii.</i> It is a lofty evergreen, confined to the Auckland district. [Local, N. Z.] <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2032</i>)

>  <i>taraire</i>, a tree (Beilschmiedia taraire), no common name, and even the science name has here adopted the Maori name as the specific one. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/146</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

### tarakihi
 
 <i>n.</i> a fine-eating fish common in New Zealand waters, the jackass fish of southern Australia. <i>Dactylopagrus macropterus</i> (formerly <i>Chilodactylus</i>), a fine food fish [ORIGIN: Maori, incl terakihi, common spelling error]

(and Leather jacket <i>(Pseudomonacanthus scaber</i>). <i>(1929,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/111</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson and Thomson)]

>  <b>COLENSO</b> They preferred the flesh of the Tarakihi (a fish which migrates towards these coasts in great shoals in the summer). <i>(1844,Excursion in the Northern Island of NZ</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Colenso Excursion North. Island in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 6]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> <i>Tarekihi [sic], a beautifully flat silvery fish, with a black spot on the back. <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,415</i>)

>  <b>DRURY</b> FISH [MET WITH IN PELORUS]. Terakehi - not abundant. Hapuka - plentiful near the entrance in deep water. <i>(1855,Sailing Directions,67</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> ... at her feet would be laid the treasures of the deep, the finest <i>pawa</i>, or the fattest <i>tarakia; ... </i> <i>(1986,Chatham Islands,32</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> TARAKIHI. ... (Chilodactylus macropteros). This is a very common fish in the market. <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,107</i>)

>  <b>BAINES</b> Truly ... presently in the bottom of the boat lay Hapuku, Tamuri, Tarakiki [sic], Rock-cod, Yellow tail, young shark, crayfish ... <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,163</i>)

>  About the end of January, a few Tarakihi, <i>Chilodactylus macropterus,</i> were brought to market,... <i>(1879,TrNZI,11/381</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]

>  The fish for eating are hapuka, <i>Oligorus gigas</i>; rock cods, <i>Percis colias</i>; tarakihi, <i>Chilodactylus macropterus</i>; moki, <i>Latris ciliaris</i>, etc.,etc. <i>(1885,TrNZI,17/198</i>) [NOTES: (Reischek)]

>  The fishing off the island is very good, there being abundance of hapuku, kohikohi, maumau, schnapper, kahawai, tarakihi, in addition to plenty of shell-fish such as koura or cray-fish, crabs, paua,etc. <i>(1885,TrNZI,17/419</i>) [NOTES: (Goldsmith)]

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> TARAKIHI (<i>Chilodactylus macropterus</i>). ... The tarakihi is found in the Tasmanian waters, where it is known as the black perch. ... In Sydney it is called the carp, or morwong, where its length varies from 12in. to 18in. <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,98</i>)

>  Of the sea-fishes that are used as food in New Zealand, we have among the constant residents on all parts of our coast the hapuku, tarakihi, trevally, moki, aua, rock-cod, wrasse, and patiki; and while the snapper, mullet, and gurnet are only met with in the north, the trumpeter, butterfish, and red-cod are donfined to the south. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,167</i>)

>  Of the sea-fishes that are used as food in New Zealand, we have among the constant residents on all parts of our coast the hapuku, tarakihi, trevally, moki, aua, rock-cod, wrasse, and patiki; and while the snapper, mullet, and gurnet are only met with in the north, the trumpeter, butterfish, and red-cod are donfined to the south. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,167</i>)

>  7. <i>Portland Island.</i> - Tarakihi, moki, frost-fish, barracouta, king-fish, john-dory, warehou, maomao, parrot-fish, flying-fish, smooth-hound, soldier fish. <i>(1892,TrNZI,24/403</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Tarakihi, n.</i> the Maori name for the fish <i>Chilodactylus macropterus</i>, Richards.; called in Sydney the <i>Morwong</i> (q.v.). <i>(1898,Austral English,459</i>)

>  <i>Ta&#769;.ra-ki&#769;.hi (ta.&#769;.ra.-k&emacr.&#769;.he.), <i>n</i>. [Maori.] The fish called <i>morwong</i> in Australia. See MORWONG. <i>[Local, N. Z.] </i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2032</i>)

>  <b>DRUMMOND & HUTTON</b> The warehou, tarakihi, and gurnet are common in the north, and the moki, butterfish, and frost fish in the south, while the kahawai, hapuku (the groper of Dunedin), blue cod, barracouta, the little sea mullet, sometimes called the herring, and the flat fish are found all round the coast. <i>(1982,Nature in NZ,71</i>)

>  <b>WAITE</b> <i>Cheilodactylus Macropterus</i> ... Tarakihi <i>(1911,Records of the Canterbury Museum,1/220</i>)

>  Its nearest allies amongst New Zealand fish are <i>Cheilodactylus macropterus</i> (the tarakihi) and <i>Douglasi </i>(the porae) <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/6</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson)]

>  Its nearest allies amongst New Zealand fish are <i>Cheilodactylus macropterus</i> (the tarakihi) and <i>Douglasi </i>(the porae) <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/6</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson)]

>  Tarakihi. <i>Dactylosparus macropterus</i> ... In the winter, spring and early summer months an abundance of tarakihi is constantly seen exposed for sale. <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/271</i>) [NOTES: (Phillipps)]

>  Tarakihi. <i>Dactylosparus macropterus</i> ... In the winter, spring and early summer months an abundance of tarakihi is constantly seen exposed for sale. <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/271</i>) [NOTES: (Phillipps)]

>  DACTYLOSPARUS [<i>Dactylopagrus</i> 1922<i> Ibid </i>V/92] MACROPTERUS (Forster). <i>Tarakihi. </i> <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/115</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

 <i>n.</i> <i>Lycopodium</i> sp.

Maori names: <i>Manono, papauma </i> <i>Coprosma robusta</i> Raoul maori name; <i>Karamu</i> (name applied to any large-leaved <i>Coprosma Coprosma acerosa</i> A. Cunn. ### taramahoe
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [Ma.] a variety of kumara.

>  Since the variety known as the "Waina" came into possession of the Natives they have gradually discarded most of the others with the exception of a few varieties, such as the Uti-uti, Taromahoe and Rekamaroa, the other kinds being mostly small, rough, and irregularly shaped, and light croppers. <i>(Oct 20, 1914,NZ Journal of Agriculture,280</i>)

### taramea
 
 <i>n.</i> speargrass (espec. <i>Aciphylla squarrosa</i>); also called spaniard, spear-grass, bayonet-grass the larger of two related varieties of spear-grass. [Maori]

>  gum - tallama'er [taramea] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,109,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: c1826-1827 list of Maori words in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 109 See also 1826-28 Boultbee Vocabulary in 1979 Begg 282 (|= macron precedes) gum, ta|lla maer [taramea]]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> ...taramea, a prickly plant, abounds here [Auckland]. <i>(1840,Journal,2/II/302</i>) [NOTES: 1840 R. Taylor Journals (1833-) 2]

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> We also found the `taramea', a small plant with fleshy sharp-pointed leaves, not unlike the American aloe in its mode of growth. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,217</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Jan 15 in 1851 Shortland Southern Districts of NZ 217 [check re initial caps for taramea and aloe???]]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> ... we came into an open grass country with the taramea growing in it as sign of our having reached the Taupo region. <i>(Mar 12, 1845,Journal,3/88</i>)

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> In the Rotoiti Valley is found a species of spear-plant, called by the natives taramea. <i>(1846,Great Journey,21,1952 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> In the Rouiti [Rotoiti] Valley is found a species of spear-plant, called by the natives <i>taramea</i>, which is much valued by them. <i>(Dec 24, 1846,Journal of an Expedition</i>) [NOTES: 1846 Dec 24 Brunner Expedition Middle Island in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 262]

>  Thick growth of kalo and talamea near the bush which was ... a black birch forest with an occasional red pine. <i>(Apr 14, 1849,Torlesse Papers,74,1958 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [see KARO quot]]

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> TARAMEA, a small stemless plant, having fleshy triangular leaves, terminating in a sharp point, all growing in a tuft from the level of the ground. From them the natives procure a scent (vide p. 217). <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,313</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> TARAMEA, a small stemless plant, having fleshy triangular leaves, terminating in a sharp point, all growing in a tuft from the level of the ground. From them the natives procure a scent (vide p. 217). <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,313</i>)

>  There are two plants, the karo (prickly thorn) and taramea (spear grass), whch are well worthy of their names, but are almost exclusively confined to the hilly regions. There generally grows near these a plant called the timuka, from the leaf of which the natives make mats and leggings, which are impervious to the sharp points of the former. <i>(Jun 28, 1851,Lyttelton Times,7</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Taramea, <i>Col[enso]. Aciphylla Colensoi. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> <i>A.Colensoi</i> Hook f. ... the `Taramea' of the Maoris, (Colenso.) <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,49</i>)

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> <i>A.Colensoi</i> Hook f. ... the `Taramea' of the Maoris, (Colenso.) <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,49</i>)

>  Taramea. Aciphylla Colensoi, Hook. f. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/3/42</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  Several of the smaller species have become comparatively rare from the repeated burnings of the taramea ... <i>(1895,TrNZI,28/7</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>[Aciphylla Colensoi, Hook.f.] Taramea; Spaniard. </i>... By far the finest species of the genus ... <i>A[ciphylla] squarrosa, Forst. ... Taramea; Kurikuri; Spear-grass. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,209</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Aciphylla Colensoi (Colenso's Spaniard)</i> ... Both islands. Maori name <i>Taramea. </i> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,321,1940, p. 339 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The hill was covered with taramea or speargrass ... <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,127</i>)

>  <i>taramea,</i> a plant (Aciphylla squarrosa and other sp.), spear-grass, bayonet-grass, spaniard: a hill and mountain plant from whose spiny blades the Maori by heat and torsion extracted a valued scent. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/158</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>MEAD</b> ... crossing first a grassy plain filled with tumatikuru ..., taramea, ... and a pretty cream-coloured star flower, .. <i>(1966,Richard Taylor,69</i>)

>  <i>taramea</i> [s] <i>Aciphylla colensoi</i> Hook.f. <i>Aciphylla ferox</i> Oliver <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,77</i>)

>  <b>HAYES</b> ... a land [at Hakataramea] of rolling downs with mile upon mile of golden tussock and yellow-green sharp thickets of speargrass or `taramea' . <i>(1978,From the Toss of a Coin,23</i>)

>  <b>MIKAERE</b> ... there was the little thing ... playing about quite happily amongst the matagouri and the taramea. <i>(1988,Te Maiharoa,78</i>)

>  He noted that there were more scented flowers in New Zealand than in any other country he had visited; and those who remember the mahoe, the euosmia, the golden akeake, the autumn earina, the taramea, will agree that it is a land of fragrances. <i>(Feb 15, 1932,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  The "Kohuhu" and the "Tarata" .. and the "Taramea" (<i>Aciphylla Colensoi</i>) [sic] were highly prized for their perfume by the natives. <i>(Aug 8, 1889,Otago Witness,20</i>)

>  Certainly something mystic would seem to have been connected with this taramea, or prickly high-growing plant; the germ of it was gathered only by young maidens, who sought its lovely haunts after certain ceremonial songs. <i>(Nov 29, 1905,Otago Witness,82</i>)

>  Backcountry farmers, trampers and others who frequent the hills know them variously as speargrass or Spaniard, while Maori refer to them as kuri kuri or taramea, which means "rough, spiky thing". <i>(Nov 27, 2004,Dominion Post,E13</i>)

>  The peculiar plants which I have noticed growing in the Mania are the taramea, a prickly plant with a root like a carrot, two kinds of fennel also with tuberous roots, a real carrot, two kinds of asters, a gentian, with several other plants, with a beautiful scarlet flowering burr, and several shrubs bearing berries in lieu of the cranberry, and a curious shrub, an aralia; the chief rocks are whiustone. <i>(Apr 7, 1847,New Zealand Spectator & Cook's Strait Guardian,3</i>)

### taramoa
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [tataramoa](t-ten.md#tataramoa))

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i> tataramoa</i> or<i> taramoa</i>: climbing bramble of the bush known as 'bush lawyer'. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,194</i>)

>  The Maori name for the prickly "bush lawyer," taramoa, or tataramoa, he learns from Tahiti is "the-cock's-spur plant." <i>(Aug 1, 1893,Timaru Herald,4</i>)

### Taranaki
 
 <i>adj.</i> of Taranaki

>  <i>taranaki adj, usu cap</i> [fr.<i> Taranaki</i>, New Zealand] : of or from the provincial district of Taranaki, New Zealand : of the kind or style prevalent in Taranaki provincial district <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,2340</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Occasionally unusual types of hiding games were played, for example All In or Taranaki. In this game there were two teams and a home base. One team, the `outs,' went and hid, while the home team, looked for the `outs.' As soon as an `in' player saw an `out,' he cried, `All in for Tommy Jones' (the `out' player who had been spotted). At that cry all the `in' players raced for home, and all the `out' players emerged from their hiding places and attempted to tag them. If all the `in' players could get home without being tagged, then the person seen (Tommy Jones), was considered captured, and he had to come and sit in the home base, And so the `in' team went on trying to sight players and [53] then get home without being tagged. <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,52</i>)

>  [Hdg] Preparing pillars and Maori mats The entrance to Government House, Wellington, will soon have a Taranaki flavour - carved pillars and woven mats made in the Te Atiawa (Taranaki) style Petone Technical Institute students carved pou out of a 100-year-old totara pile ... Te Atiawa women are still plaiting the mat to be laid at the main door. <i>(Oct 31, 1986,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  Alley bought a Taranaki farm ... He walked off the land in November 1926 .. <i>(Dec 28, 1987,Dominion,2</i>)

>  [In article <i>Feats of Clay,</i> caption to a picture of clay model of a rough wood-block with a crude `gantry' bolted to it from which is hanging a light bulb - a real Heath-Robinson outfit - entitled:] <i>Taranaki Bedside Lamp</i> by Peter Lange. A humorous approach to the manipulation of clay. <i>(1988,Pacific Way,41</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>Taranaki drive</i> a technique used in bush felling whereby one tree is felled on to another, in order to lay a group of trees down. Unlike many Taranaki references, this one is a skilled operation, a dangerous practice that is not encouraged, except when there is the need to knock down `hang ups'. <i>[G. Johnston] </i> <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,99</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [of objects or processes] makeshift, but showing ingenuity. (See [Taranaki gate](t-ten.md#taranaki-gate); [Taranaki huntaway](t-ten.md#taranaki-huntaway); [Taranaki drive](t-ten.md#taranaki-drive))

>  Referring to the use of the word 'Taranaki', Mr Loughnan says that it always seems to have suffered at the hands of South Islanders, who have used it as an adjective to refer to something makeshift, but which implies a definite degree of usefulness and ingenuity. The most common use of Taranaki is, of course, for the makeshift gate of wires and battens, which is a nightmare for the novice to open and shut. <i>(Feb 11, 1977,Press, The,19</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> Taranaki seems to have suffered at the expense of South Islanders, who have used it as an adjective to mean something makeshift. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,83</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> ... they again formed into a double line, ... the forty Taranaki's [sic] who had arrived with Patara continually running round them shouting ... <i>(1932,East Coast Historical Records,92</i>) [NOTES: 1865 Mar 25 S.A.Levy in Williams East Coast Historical Records (19??) 92]

>  The Ngatiawas had arranged to [?] the N.Z. Government, and the Taranakis objecting, marched up the slopes of the mountain, 2,500 strong, [?] sell their beloved mountain. <i>(Jan 18, 1905,Otago Witness,42</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,103</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i> often connotes `makeshift; rough-rural; country-bumpkinish; uncouth' (cf. the way Irish is used in general English: ?many Irish-pedigreed farmers in Taranaki)

### Taranaki cow
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a run cow or one in poor condition.

>  <b>BAKER</b> Taranaki cow, a nondescript beast of poor type. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,59</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>Taranaki cow</i> any nondescript or poorly conditioned cow. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,112</i>)

>  He commenced with the Taranaki cow, and by judicious selection he has suceeded in establishing a good dairy, producing first-class butter. <i>(Apr 17, 1886,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  To do justice to the Taranaki cow requires an abler pen than mine. Nothing less than a modern Homer would do her merits justice. Born as she is to "blush unseen and waste her sweetness on the desert air," but not desert fields, of fair Taranaki, she fills our pails, she fills our pockets, and by-and-bye she will fill our ships with the butter and cheese she produces ... <i>(Dec 14, 1891,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  Just a week or two ago one read that the Taranaki cow business was thriving with a forty horse-power thrive. It was a record season for butter, and farmers were getting huge milk cheques. <i>(Dec 29, 1906,NZ Free Lance,12</i>)

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a bush drive, in which several trees are felled at once.

>  West Coast bushmen call this a Taranaki drive - driving this tree onto that other one which is hung up there. I don't know where the word comes from. <i>(Sep 6, 1987,TV1</i>) [NOTES: Country Calendar]

### Taranaki exquisite
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>THOMSON</b> .. it is worthy of notice, that the colonists, soon after the formation of all the settlements, acquired distinguishing epithets; thus there was an Auckland cove, a Wellington swell, a Nelson snob, a Taranaki exquisite, an Otago cockney, and a Canterbury pilgrim. These epithets, almost already forgotten, are too characteristic to be buried in oblivion, as each originated in some peculiarity of the leading colonists. The Otago settlers derived theirs from their ignorance of the country in which they lived; the Auckland people from their extensive Sydney connexions; and the origin of the other names are sufficiently obvious without explanation. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,2/188</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> `There was an Auckland cove, .. a Taranaki exquisite... <i>(1941,NZ Slang,24</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>Taranaki exquisite</i> nickname for person from Taranaki in provincial government times. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,112</i>)

### Taranaki fern
 
 <i>n.</i> ?bracken, ?paesia

>  <b>HAAST</b> The banks of the Mawhera-iti ... were covered with small grass patches ... beyond which ... large terraces appear, partly covered with splendid grass, partly with toitoi, Taranaki fern, and stunted manuka. <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,39</i>)

### Taranaki gate
 
 <i>n.</i>



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a makeshift gate of wire and battens, difficult to open and shut. [Irishman's gate; ten-minute gate aka taranaki, Taranaki as abbreviations] (See [Irishman's gate; ten-minute gate](../I#irishman's-gate;-ten-minute-gate))

>  <b>SMITH</b> [New Plymouth] also introduced the Taranaki gate - it is a pity it didn't stop at black sand. <i>(1947,From N to Z,57</i>) [NOTES: 1959 edition]

>  "Taranaki" gates are usually a curse on any dairy-farm. They are damnable to open and damnable to shut. <i>(Dec 1, 1945,NZ Dairy Exporter,9</i>)

>  The 'Taranaki' gate is excusable only when it has not to be opened more than half a dozen times in the year. <i>(Mar 1931,NZ Farmer,217</i>)

>  To reduce the cost of fencing many farmers skimp on the material to be used for gates or erect "Taranaki" gates built out of fencing materials. A good solid gate .. will give service years after the "Taranaki" gate has become a wire entanglement and a menace to stock in the vicinity. <i>(Aug 15, 1939,NZ Journal of Agriculture,145</i>)

>  Although it is quite possible and very cheap to use an electrified 'Taranaki' gate, we go in for proper timber gates ... <i>(Jul 15, 1940,NZ Journal of Agriculture,23</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> In the saddle the track cut through a 'Taranaki' gate, on to the Glen Lyon country again. <i>(1952,High Country Journey,15</i>)

>  J of Ag June Then it was a matter of setting to to make the necessary Taranaki gates as we discussed. <i>(1986</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> It was a Taranaki gate (ie made of wire and fence battens) and the top had come loose and collapsed. The sheep just walked over it. <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year, A,25</i>)

>  <b>FISK</b> This is the secret of a good Taranaki. If the loops at this end are too short you will find it hard to close. If it is too sloppy, the gate will be too slack ... <i>(1996,Practical Smallfarming,20</i>)

>  Still got the odd Taranaki gate? <i>(Nov 2001,Dairying Today,15</i>)

>  NZ Historic Places 84 Feb 34 There is great potential for studying and interpreting how these entities worked and interrelated from .. all the way down to the Taranaki gate and the knots used to join No 8 wire. [Taranaki] <i>(2002</i>)

>  <b>HARSANT</b> There were only bridle tracks in lieu of road, but throughout the farm were several "Taranaki" gates to be opened and shut, if one wanted to go further afield. <i>(1979,They Called Me Te Maari,17</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> All of these were out to do you harm; but they were harmless compared with the Taranaki gate, a fascinating invention fit to rank with the discovery of penicillin, the breaking of the atom, or the invention of the steam engine. <i>(1971,Our Open Country,79</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> Up near the trig station was a Taranaki gate. <i>(1986,Diary of a Pig Hunter,66</i>)

>  You could say it's a bit of a Taranaki gate arrangement, but it worked very well. <i>(Oct 5, 1992,Dominion,3</i>)

>  <b>KELLY</b> The nosedive that followed the turn stopped short at a Taranaki gate. <i>(1986,Gun in the Hills,119</i>)

>  None of them seemed to know what a scrum was or understand the technicalities of a Taranaki gate. Some American students thought it was the particular way Fr Michael walked. <i>(Aug 1999,Wel-com (Wellington),20</i>)

>  <b>JACKSON</b> A Taranaki gate - barbed wire and batons [sic] - lying in long grass. <i>(1988,Rainshadow,6</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> He's satisfied that the taranaki is properly up. <i>(1948,Tidal Creek,205</i>)

>  He [sc.. Murray Ball] decided that Aloudek was only going to get it right by seeing the fine detail, right down to the way wire coils around a Taranaki gate- post, for himself. <i>(Nov 26, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),68</i>)

>  But the townies' image of wealth creation was the hardworking cockie and his make-do inventions such as the Taranaki gate. <i>(May 7, 2005,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

>  He knew which end of a Taranaki gate to smash with his front foot so that it would drop from the loop. <i>(Feb 23, 1966,Weekly News,37</i>)

>  These 22 yarns are wildly improbable, very funny, and as New Zealand as a Taranaki gate. <i>(Sep 1991,Metro,159</i>)

>  In 1910 wooden gates were being made to replace some of the Taranaki gates, those notorious barways made out of wire and battens. <i>(Apr 1961,NZ Geographer,10</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### Taranaki huntaway
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a rock driven down a hill to scatter sheep, rather than sending out a dog. (See [German doggie; Maori huntaway; Nelson huntaway](../G#german-doggie;-maori-huntaway;-nelson-huntaway))

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> Taranaki huntaway: a rock rolled down the hillside to disturb sheep, thus saving running a dog. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,84</i>)

>  There is also the delightful "Taranaki huntaway," for a rock rolled down a hillside to disturb sheep and save a dog running out. In some quarters Mr Loughnan adds this has been adapted to "Nelson huntaway" - presumably in Taranaki. <i>(Feb 11, 1977,Press, The,19</i>)

### Taranaki ironsand
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  The plant for the Taranaki Ironsand Company will be on the ground in three months. <i>(Jul 2, 1904,NZ Observer,7</i>)

>  Mr. E. Metcalfe Smith, of Taranaki ironsand fame.. is "dead agin" the early closing of public houses on occasions. <i>(Mar 5, 1904,Free Lance,12</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  In the early 'seventies, Mr. Smith had a small arsenal in Wellington, and here from Taranaki ironsand he used to manufacture breech blocks for the rifles then in use by the Colonial Government. <i>(Mar 30, 1912,Dominion,22</i>)

>  There were.. samples of the product of Taranaki Ironsand which Mr Smith laboured so long and untiringly to develop. <i>(Jul 28, 1926,NZ Observer,6</i>)

>  Taranaki ironsand consists principally of magnetite (magnetic oxide of iron), ilmenite (titanate of iron), ferro- magnesian minerals such as augite and hornblende, some olivine and quartz. <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/140</i>)

>  Mr. Smith, who has identified himself so conspicuously in the manufacture of steel from Taranaki iron sand, gave a sample of the metal in the rough , and also a razor to a gentleman on his way home to England. <i>(Mar 29, 1873,Auckland Weekly News,4</i>)

>  Mr. Smith, who has identified himself so conspicuously in the manufacture of steel from Taranaki iron sand, gave a sample of the metal in the rough , and also a razor to a gentleman on his way home to England. <i>(Mar 29, 1873,Auckland Weekly News,4</i>)

>  [Caption] Taranaki Iron-Sand. <i>(Aug 16, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,5</i>)

>  I have just witnessed copious stream of the Taranaki iron sand pouring like water from a temporary furnace, in execution of a Government order for railway plant castings. <i>(Nov 1, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,6</i>)

>  He intended to decide whether the "sponge" which could be produced from Taranaki ironsand, could be treated electrically or otherwise. <i>(Feb 9, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,20</i>)

>  <b>STEWART</b> The beach was of the famous Taranaki iron-sand, grey in the heat where we sunbaked, gleaming black where it stretched out far and flat to meet the water. <i>(1991,Springtime in Taranaki,84</i>)

>  We have on numerous occasions alluded to the iron sand of Taranaki; and pointed out what a vast amount of undeveloped wealth is lying on the shores of that province. <i>(May 22, 1871,Evening Post,2</i>)

### Taranaki method
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a basic method of making silage where a hole is dug and the silage buried.

The Taranaki method should become popular among New Zealand dairymen. <i>(Oct 17, 1903,Farmers' Advocate,5</i>)

 <i>n.</i> cow dung [ca1935 Havelock]

Taranaki Province is famous for its dairy cattle. `Among the sheep-farming communities, Taranaki is usually referred to as a land of cows and cow cockies up to their knees in mud and cow dung' (N. Alexander, l.c.). <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1195</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>Taranaki topdressing</i> cow dung. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,112</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) cow dung.

### Taranaki War
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The Taranaki War was caused by some surveyors pegging out. <i>(Dec 23, 1935,Christchurch Star-Sun,8</i>)

>  <b>BUDDLE</b> Thus commenced the Native War at Taranaki, which has continued from that day [c1854] to this, ... [He] appears to have ben one of the speakers who attended [check ben???] the meeting of chiefs at Wellington in April last - a meeting convened ... for the purpose of hearing the views of the native chiefs in reference to the Taranaki War ... <i>(1860,Maori King Movement,6</i>)

>  ... ornamental scars/ Of blue, with red ones added in/ The Taranaki wars./ <i>(1866,Canterbury Rhymes,97</i>)

>  <b>BOOTH</b> In 1860 commenced the disastrous Taranaki war, which lasted some years. <i>(1912,Five Years in NZ,[intro]</i>)

>  <b>PINFOLD</b> During the Taranaki War ... the prayer was still offered up ... <i>(1930,Fifty Years In Maoriland,81</i>)

>  Cause of Taranaki War. <i>(Jan 7, 1943,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  That was just after the Taranaki war, and the colony was soon to enter upon the Waikato war. <i>(Aug 30, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,Suppl. 1</i>)

>  He was obliged to leave Kawhia, with other Europeans, during the first Taranaki war. <i>(Aug 2, 1880,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  Our readers are reminded that Mr C.O. Montrose, the veteran campaigner, litterateur, and war correspondent, will give a narrative, in the Alexandra Hall on Monday evening next, commencing at 9 o'clock, of his personal experiences in the Taranaki war, and the Waikato. <i>(Jul 9, 1892,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  Let us suppose that the Governor, disenabled from consulting the Representatives, - with a sufficient excuse for acting <i>mero motu</i>, upon his own authority, had expended large sums of colonial money in preparation for a Taranaki war: who would then have had a right to complain? <i>(Sep 18, 1855,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  There was a large attendance (though not so large as was expected), including numerous pioneer settlers and Taranaki war veterans. <i>(Apr 12, 1909,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  There is not much known of her early life, except that she was known as a Queen's Maori during the Taranaki Wars. <i>(Dec 2003,NZ Memories,No. 45/25</i>)

>  There is not much known of her early life, except that she was known as a Queen's Maori during the Taranaki Wars. <i>(Dec 2003,NZ Memories,No. 45/25</i>)

>  It's a familiar roll-call: Northern War 1845-6, the Wellington and Wanganui skirmishings of 1846 and 1847, the First and Second Taranaki Wars, the main campaigns in the Waikato and Bay of Plenty, ending with the campaigns of Titikowaru and Te Kooti. <i>(Sep 28, 2002,Dominion Post,F9</i>)

### Taranaki wool
 
 <i>n.</i> the tree fungus <i>Auricularia polytricha</i> Hakeka; an old word for the edible jew's ear fungus collected for sale. [from its one-time commercial importance in a mainly dairy-farming area] (See [ear fungus](../E#ear-fungus))

>  <b>CROWE</b> Ear fungus <i>Auricularia polytricha</i> Hakeka This fungus has also been known as 'Taranaki wool' from the days when its collection provided a livelihood in Taranaki. <i>(1997,Field Guide to Native Edible Plants of NZ,126</i>)

>  Often when household funds were low, a few shillings were obtained by selling fungus, otherwise known as "Taranaki Wool," which was exported to China for culinary purposes. <i>(1988,Tales of Pioneer Women,91</i>) [NOTES: Writing of 1940]

>  "Chew Chong, based in Taranaki, exported Wood Ear fungus to China. In the mid-1800s there was a huge trade in fungus. It was at one stage larger than butter exports." The fungus attracted the popular name of "Taranaki wool", Yee says. <i>(Aug 13, 2003,Capital Times,19</i>)

>  <b>LOCKE</b> In a single year, 1885, seventy-two thousand pounds worth of this "Taranaki wool" was sent away. <i>(1984,Kauri & The Willow, The,91</i>)

>  The box on fungi tells us that Chinese trader Chew Chong, one of New Zealand's early dairy exporting entrepreneurs, first made his name as an exporter of the edible wood ear fungus, then known locally as "Taranaki wool". In the 1880s the trade was worth thousands of pounds. <i>(Jul 1998,NZ Geographic,No. 39/18</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR & HARREX</b> Settlers breaking in new land [sc. in Taranaki] had to rely on contract work on the roads and such trades as that in fungus (or 'Taranaki wool' as it was called) for an income during their initial years on the land. <i>(1978,Looking Back,79</i>)

>  The district has been invaded by a party of Parihaka Maoris, some 80 strong, in pursuit of that commercially mysterious product known as fungus, and once styled in derision "Taranaki wool." <i>(Mar 23, 1899,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a fungus grown in Taranaki for export to China.

>  In these days of honest endeavour to maintain Taranaki's reputation as a primary products province, it is well to remember the part played by its early settlers in establishing it. Those advance agents for long enjoyed little recompense for their labours beyond the proceeds of the abounding 'Taranaki wool' crop. <i>(Jan 5, 1929,Hawera Star,14</i>)

>  <b>BARRER</b> The children also had to work hard, picking fungus, which was facetiously known as 'Taranaki wool'. <i>(1939,Brave Days: Pioneer Women of New Zealand,141</i>)

>  It is difficult to realise what fungus - commonly known as 'Taranaki wool'- meant to the pioneer dairyman in that province. <i>(Apr 1958,NZ Journal of Agriculture,377</i>)

>  <b>MCGILL</b> Taranaki wool: a fungus the Chinese regarded as medicinal and a delicacy, exported to Hong Kong in the early 1880s by Taranaki entrepreneur Chew Chong ... <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,99</i>)

 <i>adj.</i>

 <i>n.</i> a person born in, residing in, or loyal to Taranaki

>  Taranakians <i>(1850,McLean Papers,8/272</i>)

>  Taranakians <i>(1857,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/39</i>)

>  .. a Taranakian in the Ministry ... <i>(1865,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/616</i>)

>  The Taranakians are satisfied with the present route and protest against her being omitted from the regular ports of call, in any arrangement hereafter to be made; the speech of Mr. Richmond, at the Nelson dinner, having first supplied the hint that Ministers contemplated recommending a route in which Taranaki would be passed by. <i>(Jan 26, 1859,Lyttelton Times,4</i>)

>  The Taranakian became a knight automatically after serving in the Speaker's chair for the term of one Parliament's life. <i>(Apr 19, 1924,NZ Truth,1</i>)

It cost GBP36,000, and is a monument to the faith and enterprise of the Taranakians, who in this matter have shown the same doggedness of their race as when they were called upon to defend their homes during Maori troubles. <i>(Jul 30, 1913,Grey River Argus,4</i>)

>  Water has obtained an unusual prominence in the surroundings of this earthly pilgrimage - a prominence that, however congenial to the taste of the amphibious Taranakian, does not seem to meet with the approval of recent arrivals from drier climes. <i>(Apr 29, 1892,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  Yesterday, on parade, we had a very pleasing affair; lots were drawn in order that the comforts in clothing, sent us by the ladies if Lyttleton [sic] and Wellington, might meet with an equal distribution amongst us. They were sent only to the volunteers, but we agreed to translate the word to civilian, so that every Taranakian had something of use and value. <i>(Oct 16, 1860,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a person born in, residing in, or loyal to Taranaki

### taranakite
 
 <i>n.</i> a mineral

>  A New Zealand mineralogist seeing the book is naturally interested in the minerals first described from this country, namely akatoreite, (Read and Reay), awaruite (Skey), huttonite (Palst), hydrogrossular (Hutton), motukoreaite (Rodgers, Chisholm, Davis and Nelson), taranakite (Hector and Skey), tuhualite (Marshall), wairakite (Steiner and Coombs),and wairauite (Challis and Long). <i>(Oct 1982,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,58.71</i>)

>  This mineral, which is double hydrous phosphate of alumina and potash, part of the alumina being replaced by ferrous oxide, was first discovered by H. Richmond, Esq., at the Sugar Loaves, Taranaki, where it occurs as thin seams which occupy fissures in trachytic rocks. It is described by Dr. Hector (Juror's Rep. N. Z. Ex., 1865, p. 423) as of a yellowish-white colour; amorphous and soft; readily fusible before the blowpipe and in other respects resembling wavellite. <i>(1883,TrNZI,15/385</i>) [NOTES: (Cox)]

>  <i>taranakite n -s [Taranaki,</i> provincial district in New Zealand, its locality + E <i>-ite</i>] : a mineral ... ? consisting of a basic hydrous phosphate of aluminium and potassium in compact clayey masses <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,2340</i>) [NOTES: [In OEDS in this quotation]]

>  This included the first description of the new mineral taranakite, discovered near New Plymouth. <i>(Nov 1990,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,90/44</i>)

>  I have much pleasure in acknowledging the receipt from E.B. Dickson, Esq., of New Plymouth of three interesting geological specimens:- No.1, a specimen of granite from the Sugar Loaves, showing the formation of the newly described mineral, "Taranakite;" No.2, a larger one from an outlying reef; and No.3, a smaller specimen of the Taranikte by itself. It is probable that this substance will prove to be of some commercial value, as it evidently contains much phosphate of lime, which, for agricultural purposes, is highly esteemed. <i>(Sep 19, 1866,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> RED-BILLED GULL <i>Larus scopulinus Other names:</i> Tarapunga, Mackerel Gull, Jackie (Chathams). <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,154</i>)

>  <i>194. Larus novaehollandiae scopulinus </i>Forster, 1844 <i>Red-billed Gull (Tarapunga)</i> New Zealand ... <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,53,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Red-billed Gull Larus novaehollandiae scopulinus </i>FORSTER, 1844 ... The red-billed gull is one of the commonest gulls on the New Zealand coast; it rarely lives inland. ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Silver gull, tarapunga, akiaki, mackerel gull, jackie. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,222</i>)

>  The pretty little gulls called Tarapunga by the Maoris are very plentiful on Rotorua, and have been protected for some years, but recently a number of dead and wounded birds have been seen floating on the lake. <i>(May 10, 1902,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  On a little point on the north side of the lake, some years back, the red-billed seagulls (tarapunga), from Lake Taupo, used to build their nests amongst the stones close to the waters edge. <i>(Jun 26, 1912,Evening Post,17</i>)

>  The red-billed or mackerell gull - .. the Maoris' tarapunga - is a smaller and prettier bird. <i>(Aug 13, 1908,Auckland Weekly News,38</i>)

### Tarara
 
 <i>n.</i> a Dalmatian

>  Even the Maoris learnt Nashki as they were accpeted as being ours and we were theirs. They even honoured us by giving us our special name of Tarara. <i>(Aug 2004,NZ Memories,No. 49/49</i>)

>  Thousands of people are expected to visit Waitakere City's Te Rangi Hiroa Park on March 3 for Tarara Day 2002. A celebration of the joining of Maori and Croatian cultures on the gumfields more than 100 years ago, Tarara Day provides an opportunity for people of Croatian-Maori descent to celebrate their cultures and learn more about their heritage. 'Tarara' was the name given to Croatians by Northland Maori to distinguish them from the English. <i>(Jan 29, 2002,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: Waitakere City Council Media Release www.waitakere.govt.nz]

>  In business we can't be islands unto ourselves. We've got to work with the Tararas [Yugoslavs], Hindus, the Pakehas - anyone. <i>(Sep 8, 2004,Dominion Post,C3</i>)

>  <b>BATISTICH</b> We Dalmatians got on well with the Maoris from the start. It was almost as if we were related, maybe somewhere back in time. The Maoris had a name of their own for us: tarara, from the sound of our speech, not unlike their own; the same broad vowels and rolled 'r's, the musical inflexion that belongs naturally to song. <i>(1987,Sing Vila in the Mountain,35</i>)

>  In the 1860's the first Dalmatian gum diggers arrived fleeing hard times back in their Adriatic homeland. As the diggers were exclusively men, they found wives among the Maori community who called the newcomers Tarara, a reference to the sound of their speech. <i>(Jul 2004,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: Planetfm 104.6 Newsletter of the Access Community Radio Ak Inc. July.Aug./ 2004. www/.104.6.planetfm.org.nz /Media. July - Aug 2004]

>  She also has English ancestry from her father and Tarara connections through her grandfather Mate Vuletich (on her Mum's side). <i>(May 2002,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.terarawa.co.nz/kukupa/may2002.htm]

### tarariri
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Puriri Posts, house blocks and firewood (puriri, tea-tree, matai and tarariri). <i>(Sep 1907,Wairoa Bell & Northern Advertiser,[13]</i>)

### Tararua
 
 <i>n.</i> [R. Mason notes: also other examples: of a view `a thick mist'; of weather `with mist and/or drizzle, bad, sometimes windy and wet [?d] Tramper 12(5):6 ... the weather remained typically Tararua ... [d?] ibid 16(8):9 ... the view typically Tararua ...]

>  ... Often the weather or the view is `typically Tararua' or merely `typical'. No one is under the illusion that the conditions are then good; ... <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,28</i>) [NOTES: Ruth Mason `Track and Trail']

### Tararua biscuit
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BURTON</b> [Title] Tararua Biscuits. A New Zealand mountaineers' staple, one of these biscuits lies buried in the Himalayas, on the summit or Yerupaja Norte. The gesture was the brainchild of Graeme Dingle, who was the first to conquer the mountain in 1968. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,152</i>)

>  Among the authentic local baking-terms the one I find most evocative is the Tararua biscuit. It comes in high-fibre high- calorie slabs... Two generations of Wellington dentists must have made a tidy income from its inroads. I must be one of the few of my old Mt Holdsworth gang with my own teeth, since I only once tackled a Tararua biscuit and I must confess I did not take it straight. <i>(Jun 4, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),55</i>) [NOTES: [I.A. Gordon]]

>  Among the authentic local baking-terms the one I find most evocative is the Tararua biscuit. It comes in high-fibre high- calorie slabs... Two generations of Wellington dentists must have made a tidy income from its inroads. I must be one of the few of my old Mt Holdsworth gang with my own teeth, since I only once tackled a Tararua biscuit and I must confess I did not take it straight. <i>(Jun 4, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),55</i>) [NOTES: [I.A. Gordon]]

>  <i>Tararua biscuit inf. </i>tramper's home-made biscuit <i>(1985,Collins Compact Dictionary,492</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; nf. 1986 NZPOD]]

>  <b>PICKERING</b> A classic lunch food is the Tararua biscuit. This is any sort of home-baked biscuit baked out of a mixture of rolled oats, flour, golden syrup and other ingredients, and formed into a hard flat biscuit that acts as a base for butter and spreads. The principle of Tararua biscuits is that they serve as a replacement for bread on long trips. ... The taste is not a crucial matter, but they must be firm and filling - and all of the same size, otherwise bickering will develop over who's got the biggest. <i>(1988,Hills, The,71</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> Tararua biscuits must be baked hard to withstand jostling about in a pack. <i>(1982,200 Years of NZ Food & Cookery,152</i>)

>  While on a hunting trip in the Grebe Valley the other weekend, we bumped into some members of the Peninsula Tramping Club and (I think) the Over 40s Tramping Club at Clark Hut. I peered over their shoulders and noted that their lunches included good old Tararua biscuits and one person had boiled eggs ... <i>(Nov 2011,FMC - (Federated Mountain Clubs of NZ) Bulletin,52</i>)

>  Among the authentic local baking-terms the one I find most evocative is the Tararua biscuit. It comes in high-fibre, high-calorie slabs generally baked by young males in secret when Mum is watching TV. Two generations of trampers swear by its life-sustaining qualities and two generations of Wellington dentists must have made a tidy income from its inroads. <i>(Jun 4, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),55</i>)

>  After his appearance in <i>Trog 80</i>, presiding over recipes for 'Aunt Hazel's Neutron Pudding', an improved 'Tararua Biscuit' and 'Tasty Kea Stew a la RF&B', Uncle Jacko was consigned not so much to history as oblivion. <i>(Aug 2010,FMC - (Federated Mountain Clubs of NZ) Bulletin,31</i>)

### Tararua dishmop
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Raoulia tenuicaulis</i> creeping mat plant, one of the scabweeds [ca1952 May R. Mason fide N. Elder May 1952 From its being used by trampers as a dishcloth. Originating among Wellington trampers, but long obs in Wellington. Survives in Hawkes Bay.]

>  It is unnecessary to write at length in general of New Zealand plant names, but mention may be made of the <i>Tararua dishmop</i> and the mountain mop</i>. Apparently the first is given to<i> Raoulia tenuicaulis</i>, one of the scabweeds, by Heretaunga Tramping Club. No doubt plenty of Tararuas have used it with sand or ashes to clean out a billy, little thinking that their actions might inspire a new plant name, given to it by Canterbury mountaineers apparently, from the tufts of leaves at the ends of the branches. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,30</i>) [NOTES: Ruth Mason `Track and Trail]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>Tararua dishmop Raouli tenuicaulis</i> plant, used by trampers to clean out pots and billies. (Cf mountain mop.) <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,112</i>)

### Tararua pack
 
 <i>n.</i> a special frame-pack built on a bergan frame

### tarata
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  50 Tarata (turpentine) slab. <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,16</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a New Zealand tree (Pittosporum eugenoides). also called the lemonwood, mapau a small native tree with aromatic leaves and gum. [Maori] (See [lemonwood](lay-liv.md#lemonwood))

>  <i>Ta-ra&#769;.ta </i>(ta.-ra.&#769;.t&adot.; <i>colloq</i>. t&adot.-ra.&#769;.t&adot.), <i>n</i>. [Maori.] <i>(Bot.) A New Zealand tree (Pittosporum eugenoides). </i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2032</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> PITTOSPORUM EUGENIOIDES (<i>A.Cunn.</i>) ... The `TARATA'.- ... It is known to the settlers in some parts as `Lemon Wood'. <i>Ibid.</i> 178 Tarata [Maori name] .. Lemon wood [Settlers' name] .. Pittosporum Eugenioides .. Tree <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,35</i>)

>  Turpentine (tarata) .. Pittosporum eugenioides .. Timber white and elastic; ... <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,15</i>)

>  Turpentine (tarata) .. Pittosporum eugenioides .. Timber white and elastic; ... <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,15</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Tarata, n.</i> Maori name for the New Zealand tree <i>Pittosporum eugenioides</i>, A. Cunn., <i>N.O. Pittosporeae</i>; called also <i>Mapau, Maple</i>, etc. See <i>Mapau. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,459</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>[Pittosporum eugenioides, A.Cunn.] Tarata</i>. ... The largest of the New Zealand species, ... <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,61</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Pittosporum eugenioides (The Lemonwood) </i>... Maori name <i>Tarata.</i> (Name from <i>Eugenia,</i> a genus of myrtles)... [<i>Pittosporum eugenioides</i>] it is often cultivated for its beauty, and is sometimes - though not so often as<i> P. [195] tenuifolium </i>- used to form an ornamental hedge. It is known by a variety of names amongst the colonists, such as <i>Mapau, White Mapau</i>, and even <i>Maple</i>, and <i>Lemon-tree.</i> The Maori name seems to have been <i>Tarata</i>, but it is also called the <i>Mapau. </i> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,194,1940 p. 202 ed.</i>)

>  <i>tarata</i>, a tree (Pittosporun Eugenioides), lemonwood; gum used by the Maori for scenting oil. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/146</i>)

>  <i>tarata</i>, a tree (Pittosporun Eugenioides), lemonwood; gum used by the Maori for scenting oil. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/146</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> ... such species as lancewood, karamu, toro, ramarama, koromiko, kanono, kohuhu, kawakawa, hangehange, rangiora, five finger, patete, toru, tree daisies, broadleaf, kamahi, mahoe, tarata, karo, ... Some have been merged: but merge all of these regardless. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,87,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <i>tarata</i> also <i>taratah n</i> -s [Maori <i>tarata</i>] : a small evergreen tree (<i>Pittosporum eugenioides</i>) of New Zealand having white bark and fragrant corymbs of pale yellow flowers - called also <i>white mapau </i> <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,2340</i>)

>  <i>tarata</i> also <i>taratah n</i> -s [Maori <i>tarata</i>] : a small evergreen tree (<i>Pittosporum eugenioides</i>) of New Zealand having white bark and fragrant corymbs of pale yellow flowers - called also <i>white mapau </i> <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,2340</i>)

>  <i>tarata n. </i>small New Zealand tree, called also lemon-wood. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),626</i>)

>  Tarata, the larger leafed <i>Pittosporum eugenioides</i>), does well in deeper and good soils... Tarata has sweet fragrant flowers and the crushed leaves give off a lemon-carrot scent. Both tarata and kohuhu were used by Maori, mixed with kereru fat to make a body lotion or, using gum bled form vertical slits in the trunk, mixed with oil from crushed titoki berries to make a scented body oil. <i>(Oct 2000,Growing Today,69</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> The "Tarata"... It is known to the settlers in some parts as "Lemon Wood". [Pittosporum eugenioides]. <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,35</i>)

### taro
 
 <i>n.</i> OED : a. A food-plant, Colocasia antiquorum, N.O. Araceae, cultivated in many varieties (C. esculenta, macrorhiza, etc.) in most tropical countries for its starchy root-stocks, or its succulent leaves or stems, which in a raw state are acrid, but lose their acridity by boiling. 1769 S. PARKINSON Jrnl. 1 Oct. in Jrnl. Voy. South Seas (1773) II. 97 Adjoining to their houses are plantations of Koomarra and Taro.

>  <b>PARKINSON</b> They [the natives] informed us, that there was Taro, Eape, Oomara, Yams, and also a peculiar kind of Deer, to be found upon the island. <i>(Oct 11, 1769,Journal</i>)

>  <b>MONKHOUSE</b> [Our prisoners] asked if it was Taro [circumflex a], a Species of Arum, the root of which we now learnt constituted a part of the food of these people - they spoke also of their using the Yams and Sweet Potatoes. <i>(Oct 6, 1769,Cook's Journals,1/569,1955 ed.</i>)

>  English New Zeland South-sea Islands ... ... ... Coccos Tara Taro Sweet Potatoes Cumala Cumala Yamms Tuphwhe Tuphwhe <i>(Mar 31, 1770,Cook's Journals,1/287,1955 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> A Comparative Table of the Various Languages in the Isles of the South-Sea ... [English] <i>Arum esculentum </i>or <i>eddoes</i> ... [New-Zeeland] Tallo [a-macron grave] <i>(1778,Observations Made During a Voyage,284</i>)

>  <b>NCIHOLAS</b> In the plantations adjoining this village, I observed a plant very common in our West India settlements, where it is called <i>tacca</i>, and named by the natives of this island <i>tarro.</i> It does not appear to me that this plant is indigenous to New Zealand, but must, in my opinion, have been brought hither, either by Captain Cook or some other European navigator who has visited the country. <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/351</i>)

>  <b>MARSDEN</b> They were clothed with their best mats, their heads ornamented with feathers, and had prepared an immense quantity of common and sweet potatoes and some tarra <i>(taro)</i>, a root which they are very fond of, about the size of a small turnip. <i>(Aug 1820,Letters & Journals of Samuel Marsden,283,1932 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> T|aro,<i> s</i>. A root so called; bread. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,209</i>)

>  <b>McRAE</b> Next day went to Wy Whero a fine tract of cultivated land - Peaches - Taroo Flax, Meero - return to house, - <i>(1820,Journal,26,1928 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McNAB</b> Q. Of what does their agriculture consist? A. The cultivation of yams, the potatoes (sweet and English), a native plant or root called tarra (? taro) [sic], and the calabash. <i>(1908,Historical Records,1/543</i>) [NOTES: 1821 May Evidence of Ensign McCrae before Commissioner Bigge in 1908 McNab Historical Records 1/543]

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> We had to go through Cultivation mostly all the way. Indian Corn, potatoes and Cumeras [<i>kumara</i>] or Sweet potatoes, Tarraw [<i>taro</i>] a kind of Yam. The Tarras comes I believe from the South sea Islands. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),42,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> ... between those [small] hills the large broad lotus leaf of the tarro appeared; ... <i>Ibid.</i> 1/288 The <i>taro</i> (Arum esculentum) is a well-known legume; various species are planted in the islands, especially southward; it is very farinaceous. The taro oia, or soldier taro, has a blue cast, within a thin, atramentous skin; ... <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/181</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Here [East Cape] we are sitting in our tent ... we have a large fire in front and a smaller one in which a cake I have been making of taro is baking; Williams is kneading a damper in a tin pot; <i>(Apr 30, 1839,Diary,17</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> A long lane was formed on an extensive level plain, fifty feet in width, by two high walls, formed by perhaps upwards of ten thousand baskets of potatoes, kumeras, Indian corn (<i>Holcus indicus</i>), tarro (<i>arum</i>), water melons, turnips preserved, (<i>kou</i>) steamed kernels of the native fruit, (<i>karaka</i>[as-acute]) baked roots of the <i>ti</i> palm tree, (<i>korou</i>) a quantity of dried cod, eels and other fish, also of shell-fish, bundles of fern-root, onions, peaches, grapes, &c. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/91</i>)

>  Natives ....regaled us with Taro (a kind of luscious slimy root more like the Jerusalem artichoke than any other vegetable). <i>(1840,Journal of Ensign Best</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Best Journal 1837-1843 ed 1966 Taylor 256]

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> Turnips have for a length of time been grown in the native plantations, ... and the indigenous Polynesian vegetable, `Taro'. <i>(1842,New Zealand (Heaphy),30</i>)

>  <b>COLENSO</b> Leaving Te Kawakawa ... I passed by several of the Taro (Caladium esculentum, Vent.) plantations of those natives ... [7] Of the Taro plant the natives possess two kinds (species?) <i>Taro maori</i> and <i>Taro Hoia</i>; ... the former is supposed to have been introduced with the present race of natives, whilst the latter, as they themselves state, is quite of modern introduction. [check this citation] <i>(1844,Excursion in the Northern Island of NZ</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Colenso Excursion Northern Island in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 6, 7]

>  <b>LINDLEY</b> `Whole fields of <i>Colocasia macrorhyza</i> are cultivated in the South Sea Islands under the name tara or kopeh roots.' <i>(1846,Vegetable Kingdom,128</i>) [NOTES: [Stanford]:]

>  <b>ANGAS</b> Tradition further tells us that they [sc. New Zealanders] came from the island of <i>Hawaiki</i> (which lies eastward), bringing with them [306] <i>taro</i> and dogs. The <i>kumera</i>, or sweet potato (<i>convolvulus battata</i>) ... also came from the eastward. <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/305</i>)

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> ... each [Maori] having a kit of potatoes and taro, a large quantity of the kotiro, or preserved potato, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 56 ... and tara [fn Taro, edible lily of Polynesian origin] plantations of former days [West Coast]. <i>(1847,Great Journey,54,1952 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MacLEAN</b> There are 1,200 kits of <i>kumara</i>, large baskets of <i>taro, papa</i> or bark cases of birds cooked ..., including <i>tui</i> ... <i>(Jul 6, 1849,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1849 July 16 Maclean Diary in 1940 Cowan Sir Donald Maclean 48]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Next in importance [in Maori diet] to the original sweet potato was the taro (<i>Caladium esculentum</i>). The edible part is a bulbons root, ... <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/156</i>)

>  [check this and following cit???] <i>Ibid.</i> 55 The ground-work of this feast appeared to be sweet potatoes and <i>taro</i>, but on top ... was placed either dried shark, eels, mullet, or pork, all `piping hot'. <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,49</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Taro, <i>Col[enso]. Caladium esculentum. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  These were - two roots, and one gourd-like fruit; the Kumara, or sweet potato, (<i>Convolvulus batatas</i>), the Taro (<i>Caladium esculentum</i>), and the Hue, a large kind of gourd ... <i>(1869,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),1/3/29</i>)

>  These were - two roots, and one gourd-like fruit; the Kumara, or sweet potato, (<i>Convolvulus batatas</i>), the Taro (<i>Caladium esculentum</i>), and the Hue, a large kind of gourd ... <i>(1869,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),1/3/29</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  These were - two roots, and one gourd-like fruit; the Kumara, or sweet potato, (<i>Convolvulus batatas</i>), the Taro (<i>Caladium esculentum</i>), and the Hue, a large kind of gourd ... <i>(1869,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),1/3/29</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  <b>DOMETT</b> `May a bed, That late in such luxurious neatness spread, Of melon, maize and taro - now a wreck.' <i>(1872,Ranolf and Amohia,374</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> `May a bed, That late in such luxurious neatness spread, Of melon, maize and taro - now a wreck.' <i>(1872,Ranolf and Amohia,374</i>)

>  Fascines of a tolerable thickness are ... disposed chess-board fashion (like Maori <i>taro</i> beds), and the sowing is made. <i>(1873,TrNZI,5/109</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> We all had a turn at the soup-making; some put <i>kumeras</i> into the pot, some put <i>tarro</i>, a vegetable which is like a lump of flour, and if left long over the fire boils away by slow [118] degrees and thickens the soup. <i>(1873,Poenamo,2/7/117</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> The small cray-fish in myriads bred,/ With sunk fern-bundles lifted from the Lake:/ Next pounded fern-root pounded to a cake,/ ...; and the choicer roots,/ .../ The kumara [u-acute] and taro. [a-acute] <i>Ibid.</i> 1/303 Taro ..... O. Pandaneae (Aroideae); G. Caladum; S Caladum esculentum. `A staple article of food in many parts of the Old World' A root something like the kumara - but whiter, firmer, and less sweet. <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/184</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> The small cray-fish in myriads bred,/ With sunk fern-bundles lifted from the Lake:/ Next pounded fern-root pounded to a cake,/ ...; and the choicer roots,/ .../ The kumara [u-acute] and taro. [a-acute] <i>Ibid.</i> 1/303 Taro ..... O. Pandaneae (Aroideae); G. Caladum; S Caladum esculentum. `A staple article of food in many parts of the Old World' A root something like the kumara - but whiter, firmer, and less sweet. <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/184</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>a familiar food plant, Colocasia</i> species, widely cultivated in tropical regions, especally in Polynesia. The Word is Polynesian, and much used by the Maoris. <i>(1898,Austral English,459</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>a familiar food plant, Colocasia</i> species, widely cultivated in tropical regions, especally in Polynesia. The Word is Polynesian, and much used by the Maoris. <i>(1898,Austral English,459</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> Upon the stages were piled baskets of [rest of cit???] <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,113</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> The staple cultivated foods of the ancient Maori ... were: Kumara, <i>kumara, taro, hue,</i> dried fish ... etc. ... taro, and hue. <i>Ibid.</i> 15 [rest of cit???] <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,12</i>)

>  <i>taro n.</i> a plant, a species of colocassia, cultivated in many Pacific islands for its edible roots and tubers. [polynesian] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,11</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> Potatoes, kumeras, taros, and water melons were the chief crops grown in the ... humid bush-surrounded plots ... <i>(1951,Confessions,24</i>)

>  A food plant which the Maoris cultivated, having brought it with them from Hawaiki. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),406</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> The Maoris thrived and multiplied largely because they introduced certain food plants to New Zealand, especially the <i>kumara</i> (the sweet potato), the <i>taro</i>, and the yam. <i>iBID.</i> 302 [Maori glossary]<i> taro</i> Food plant (<i>Colocasia antiquorum</i>) <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,19</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>taro</i>: food plant <i>(1963,O Te Raki,194</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> There is now little doubt that the primary migration to New Zealand ... brought the Maori food plants from an eastern Polynesian homeland, ... These plants were the island yam (Dioscorea esculenta), the bottle gourd, the taro, a dwarf type of cabbage tree and the kumara. <i>Ibid.</i> 4 Taro grows wild in a few places in the north, having escaped from old Maori gardens, and is not difficult to grow. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,3</i>)

>  <i>taro n.</i> pl.<i> -ros</i>. 1. an Asian plant, cultivated in the tropics for its large edible rootstock. 2. the rootstock of this plant. [C18: < Tahitian & Maori] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,1197</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 786; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 492]]

>  Filipo trims taro at an Otara supermarket. <i>(Oct 27, 2004,Capital Times,11</i>)

>  Plant scientists believe they are close to defeating a disease which a decade ago nearly crippled Samoa and still threatens the Pacific's vital taro plantations. <i>(Jul 16, 2002,Dominion Post,B1</i>)

>  "It doesn't matter how well you cook the taro... That doesn't pay the bills." <i>(Oct 26, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),89</i>)

>  Totally unversed in the mysteries of subsoil ploughing, and ignorant of the virtues of guano, all their field work is performed by the hoe, by use of which, however, and by not sparing their own labour, they contrive to raise abundant crops of yams, taro, potatoes, and kumeras [sic]. <i>(Oct 16, 1850,New Zealand Spectator & Cook's Strait Guardian,3</i>)

>  The land here is very fertile and productive - maize, kumeras [sic], potatoes, taro, and tobacco growing in great luxuriance, peaches also very plentiful. <i>(May 9, 1862,Daily Southern Cross,5</i>)

>  <i>My Dad</i> walked home with taro and Twisties / in his work-bag and he recalls his past with / a bottle of 'Vailima' in his hand. <i>(Aug 25, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),93</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BAKER</b> ...Hemi was waiting on the grassy bank with a taro sack he'd found in the storeroom. <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,43</i>)

### taro Maori
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a cultivar of taro, Colocasia esculenta. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>BEST</b> The great labour of growing taro maori (the old pre-European variety) caused it to be abandoned when the taro Merekena was introduced. The latter is hardy, prolific, runs wild in fact, and easily cultivated, but it is very inferior in flavour and flouriness to taro maori. I don't think I have seen taro maori for thirty years. <i>(1925,Maori Agriculture,127</i>)

### taropo
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [tarapo](../T#tarapo))

### tarp
 
 <i>v.</i> to cover with a tarpaulin

>  <b>REDMOND</b> This was really hard yakker because I had to tarp the loads. <i>(2000,Truckers: NZ Truckers on the Road,103</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) short for tarpaulin [Jim writes : In NZPOD but not DNZE. NSOD tags it as <i>NAmer & Austral.</i>; Collins as <i>US Austral and NZ</i>.]

>  <b>Geary</b> Haden lifted the tarp on his transport tray. - I brought a rope and tyre, see. <i>(2003,Man of the People, A,151</i>)

### tarpaulin muster
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,243</i>)

>  <b>WAITE</b> The crews had a `tarpaulin muster,' the result of which was a present for every man in the division of half a pound of tobacco, at a time when it was specially acceptable. <i>(1919,New Zealanders at Gallipoli,263</i>)

>  If the good old fashion of a `tarpaulin muster' among friends constitutes charity, well, what next? <i>(Sep 18, 1963,People's Voice,5</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> "I am too poor, let us make a tarpaulin muster and call in a surveyor, that this distraction be ended." <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,243</i>)

>  Tarpaulin Muster, Miramar Progressive Association meeting 17s 0d. <i>(Feb 9, 1928,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  The ceremony of dedicating at the War Veterans' Home in Narrabeen two rooms, together with furnishings, provided out of a "tarpaulin muster" by the New Zealand returned soldiers who visited Sydney for the 1938 Anzac Day celebrations [which] took place today. <i>(Apr 15, 1940,Evening Post,13</i>)

>  Tommy says there is not a sufficient number of the old Mosquito Fleet left now to raise a tarpaulin muster. <i>(Aug 13, 1881,Observer (Auckland),546</i>)

>  The Government, I think, are trying to strengthen their hands by sending over the Wellington portion of the tribe, who arrived at Te Waotu last week, bring in their pockets subscription lists for native parsons, good moral tracts, and such like remedies for the present state of darkness (mental) in which their Waotu brethren are living; but I am afraid a " tarpaulin muster" for a bottle of grog would find more favour amongst them than dubbing up for the purpose of keeping another native preacher. <i>(Aug 23, 1873,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  The great Conference of the representatives of the goldfield counties at Wellington, came to a conclusion on Monday last, just before dinner time, and so satisfied were the deputants that their deliberations would meet with the approval of the Premier, and that the Government Sugar Basin would be placed within reach of their spoons, that they got up a tarpaulin muster of a tanner a head, and indulged in a half hour's anti-teetotal recreation in anticipation of the success of their plans. <i>(Jun 24, 1891,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> Rather than cancel the trip and return, the females of the party decided to take up a "tarpaulin muster" and by shedding the odd garment here and there they soon had Pud warm and dry and dressed in a new set of finery. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,47</i>)

>  The camp was kept going by a tarpaulin muster. <i>(Sep 18, 1919,Maoriland Worker,8</i>)

>  The Waitomo County Council yesterday had a "tarpaulin muster" for a staff presentation. <i>(Dec 10, 1937,King Country Chronicle,4</i>)

>  Any organisation to which he belongs forms the ideal means for the procuring of liquor by a tarpaulin muster. <i>(Dec 20, 1937,King Country Chronicle,5</i>)

### tarpot
 
 <i>n.</i> [ca1935 Havelock schoolchildren `Mungimungi tarpot' (or `Mungimungi taipo') as an abusive catcall to a Maori]

>  <b>ADSETT</b> Isabel did not say how, when at school, she had taken a leading part in the teasing of Maori kids by chanting `Tar pot! Tar pot! Who fell in the tar pot!' <i>(1963,Magpie Sings, A,88</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SARGESON</b> <i>(1949,I Saw in My Dream,112,1974 ed. Rhodes ed.</i>)

>  <b>MORRIESON</b> `As if the crafty bitch would have it in her bag', Miriam scoffed. `All these tarpots are too cunning to turn their back on themselves. And half-castes are the worst of the lot ...' <i>(1976,Pallet on the Floor,49</i>)

### tarra
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [taro](t-ten.md#taro))

### tarring
 
 <i>n.</i>

Each bag was then tied securely with flax and twine, branded with our private brand and stacked in the storehouse to await the half time boat. <i>(1979,Titi Heritage,88</i>) [NOTES: ?f. TO-TARR(A)ing]

### tarro
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [taro](t-ten.md#taro))

### tarry soil
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a dark sticky loam soil [ esp. S I.]
