### tarseal
 
 <i>n.</i> [surface of roadway, as opposed to road itself; aka tar-seal]

>  The tar-seal of "Restar" costs about GBP150 per mile. <i>(Mar 7, 1914,Wanganui Chronicle,6</i>)

>  <b>HILL</b> In the 1930s, the Wharetapu and Pukerangi Roads were narrow strips of metal with shallow ditches on each side. In the1980s they're narrow strips of tarseal with deep ditches on either side. <i>(1985,Moaville Magic,68</i>)

>  It leads, after 45km of skinny metal, then skinny tarseal, then wide tarseal, to Taupo, via Kinloch and Acacia Bay. <i>(Apr 4, 1993,Evening Post,20</i>)

>  Basil and Trudy Meyer live on a narrow road where the tarseal runs out back of Ikamatua. <i>(Jun 23, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)

### tarsealer
 
 <i>n.</i> one who tarseals roads

>  TARSEALER HOT ON SPEEDING DRIVERS ...Tarsealing gang foreman...had some harsh words for inconsiderate motorists... <i>(May 6, 1983,Dominion,10</i>)

### tart
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> `Where you been all this time?' his mother asked. `Down the library,' he said. ... `I bet he's been tarting,' Joy said. <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The,32,1976 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a female, espec. a girl; a girl-friend, sweetheart -->(children's often jam-tart) [1951 14-17 M 14 St Bede's, Chch 28 TART [24] girl friend -->1960 16C F B5 Wellington Girls C. 28 TART -->1960 17C M B6 St Bede's, Chch 28 TART -->1960 17C M B8 Wanganui Girls C.I 28 TART]

>  A tart from Hataitai / Skips around and pokes her flamin' / Umbrella in me eye. <i>(Dec 1, 1920,NZ Free Lance,Xmas Annual 43</i>)

>  Tarts don't climb trees. Tarts don't play with footballs. Tarts don't ride bicycles. <i>(1988,Back Then..,I : 90</i>) [NOTES: Refers to around 1930]

>  he cavorted around the [ball-]room in a rather undignified manner, a number of the tarts turned their broad backs in his direction .. and snubbed him vigorously. <i>(Aug 17, 1905,NZ Truth,9/7/8</i>)

>  No matter how sweet a `tart' may be she always wants `sugar'. <i>(Jan 6, 1906,NZ Truth,29/1/4</i>)

>  <i>(Feb 8, 1913,NZ Bulletin,16</i>) [NOTES: [= girlfriend: see quot cobber]]

>  <i>(Sep 2, 1916,Hutt Valley Independent,6/294/4</i>) [NOTES: [= woman]]

>  <i>(1916,Anzac Book, The,22</i>) [NOTES: [= woman]]

>  Becos, yo' see, th' muse this side,/Is such a bonzer tart. <i>(Aug 1917,Princes Street,12</i>)

>  Bosker tart [equivalent to American `Jane'] <i>(Aug 16, 1918,NZEF Chronicles,33</i>)

>  He's only ninepence, and would like to treat/ His Sunday tart to sparkling `fizz' and oysters./ <i>(Nov 1, 1918,Quick March,23</i>)

>  When I draw'd me wind, and wus walkin' aht/ In me slommicky 'orspital blue,/ I sights a tart, who `gets me cart'/ Wiv a `glad' wot bites me through./ ... We wus photograrfed, jest me an' 'er,/ 'Ere 'ave a cook at the proof.'/ Bill `cooked' and saw RED. <i>(Jul 19, 1919,Quick March,17</i>) [NOTES: (by Harcus Plimmer)]

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> Gus was busy flying all over Taranaki on his motor-bike, taking some red-headed tart to football matches and such like, ... . <i>(1927,Me and Gus,82,(1977 Gus Tomlins ed. Sturm) ed.</i>)

>  I was standing in a public hall in Wellington one evening, when I heard one young fellow ask another to `give him a knockdown to that tart in the green skirt'. I gathered he was asking for an introduction to a young lady. <i>(May 1930,National Education,197</i>) [NOTES: (C.J. Hickson)]

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> (also jam-tart) Check that have cit. from Campbell Island to Island Classrooms were smelly places in the thirties, especially on wet mornings when small boys and girls (`tarts' or `jam tarts', we used to call them), ... began to steam in their damp clothes. <i>(1984,Island to Island,89</i>) [NOTES: [c1936]]

>  <b>HYDE</b> `Christ. Well, young or old, my old women or the next, it's all the same in the finish. A tart's a tart.' <i>(1938,Godwits Fly, The,129,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> `He can see a tall, dark woman going to cross my path, and I'm to look out, 'cos she's not the kind of tart my mother would have liked.' <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,61</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> I don't see that silly little tart fixing counterweights. <i>(1937,Vintage Murder,156</i>)

>  <b>LAWLOR</b> [children speaking] Ever been out with a tart. <i>(1938,House of Templemore, The,199</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> The slang word for girl is <i>tart</i>, used, for instance, by schoolboys; it has only lately begun to assume the derogatory sense it now has elsewhere. <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,85</i>)

>  (in Cockney) A `tart' is a common name for a girl, just like our `dame,' and does not mean a loose woman. <i>(1944,US War Dept. Short Guide to NZ,38</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> `I know why you're off to Moss's - that young tart of his. Ha-ha!' <i>(1948,Tidal Creek,102,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MUIR</b> `There was a tart used to be mad keen on me, see? ... But I notice this picture in the Weekly and I says to me cobber, I reckon I know that [251] bint.' <i>(1960,Word for Word,250</i>)

>  I had a schottische with the tart from the Butcher's,/ Got stuck for a waltz with the constable's wife;/ I had a beer from the keg on the cream-truck,/ And the cop had one, too, you can bet your life. <i>(1964,Book of New Zealand, A (J.C. Reid),259</i>) [NOTES: (Cape `Down the Hall on Saturday Night)]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> [see poppy show] <i>Ibid.</i> 230 Tank Tarrant once led his gang across the paddocks ..., kicking the puffballs and the sheep turds, throwing the dry cow pats at any tarts he met. Chanting the gang haka. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,52</i>)

>  <b>MANTELL</b> `That's my wife. Now, d'you think I'd let that silly tart [sc another woman] loll over me like that ...' <i>(1980,Murder or Three, A,65</i>)

>  <b>BARLTROP</b> A prostitute is not called a tart by Cockneys, because `tart' has always meant a girl. It is a street word, not used indoors, but it is not coarse or unpleasant. It conveys a well made up, attractive girl. One might say `Who's that ginger-haired tart,' or `Harry was talking to a tart', or even `your tart' - though the last shows a poor vocabulary and would be said only by children. The term has been going strong for at least seventy [83] years, and probably derives from something like `jam tart'; given that, why should a reference to colourfulness and sweetness be offensive? <i>(1980,Muvver Tongue, The,82</i>)

>  <b>BEARDSLEY</b> tarts girls <i>(1984,Blackball 08,245</i>)

>  He wasn't interested in girls at that stage. But `there were always the tart chasers. All the girls were `tarts' regardless of their morals I suppose. But most of the fellows weren't interested.' <i>(May 31, 1985,Evening Post,15</i>) [NOTES: [Mr Peter Rasmussen reminiscing about St Patrick's College, Wellington in 1909]]

>  <i> tart 2 1 n. sl</i>. girl, sweetheart (<i>NZ & Austral.</i>). <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,786</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  Your correspondent, in Tuesday's issue, tries to explain the meaning of the words, "Jam Tart." I fancy that his brain must have been in the same haze that your own dear brain was, respecting the sense of the term. I real meaning of the name, both from a confectionary [sic] and social point of view, is a thing with a great deal of puff, but with very little in it. <i>(May 10, 1879,Waikato Times,2</i>)

>  Thus "The Sporting Times" was sued in relation to a paragraph, in its dramatic column, in which the young ladies of the chorus of the Avenue Theatre were described as "tarts." <i>(May 21, 1887,Te Aroha News,6</i>)

>  What about the old tarts on the New Zealand bench? <i>(Mar 23, 1912,NZ Truth,6</i>)

>  At a case in the Te Awamutu Police Court recently a witness was making a statement. "Me and my wife," he said, "went ___." Then he stopped. "Pardon," he said. "I and the missus went ___." Still feeling that something was wrong somewhere he stopped again, and reddened. Then his face lit us. "Yes," he said, "The old tart and myself ___." <i>(Sep 8, 1920,Ohinemuri Gazette,2</i>)

>  [Caption] I was late for dinner, late for the tart, and she chucked me! <i>(Sep 20, 1913,NZ Observer,16</i>)

>  <b>CAPE</b> I had a schottische with the tart from the butcher's. <i>(1967,Shanties by the Way,148</i>)

>  The fact is, the <i>Herald</i>, like a number of the Remuera 'jamtarts' was disgusted at finding Lennox was a thorough Liberal, and not a sham one who would profess advanced views to humbug the working class and go to Parliament to act in the opposite interest. <i>(Jan 1, 1891,Observer (Auckland),6</i>)

>  I blushes like a 14-year-old tart. <i>(Jul 5, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,IV/47 : 251</i>)

>  The attendance was very meager, only about twenty couple [sic] putting in an appearance. This was principally owing to the fact that invitations were limited to lady friends of the members only. However, a few of the opposite sex - on whom the "jam-tarts" occasionally bestow a passing notice - managed to gain admittance. <i>(Jan 14, 1882,Observer (Auckland),282</i>)

>  Jam-tarts were a-bun-dant at the Institute ball,and were found in both supper and dancing rooms. <i>(Nov 23, 1901,Observer (Auckland),26</i>)

>  <b>BEARDSLEY</b> The tarts getting plenty too [sc. lollies] and filling their aprons, so quick they were, little hens darting on the lollies. <i>(1984,Blackball 08,62</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  She was an old tart with a high-bridged smelling apparatus. <i>(Aug 26, 1905,NZ Truth,10/7/8</i>)

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> That tart gets my goat ... that skinny old biddy. <i>Ibid.</i> 28 OLD TART, old woman He felt crook again, remembered the old tart hitting him with a broom, the kids watching. <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> No, I don't know, but there's a yarn his father was a whaler that stayed out here and got in tow with some maori tart. He's part maori for sure though, because once he got me to witness a paper and it was about maori land.<i>Ibid.</i> 116 166 But do you know what that tart did? he said. She ran up the trunk of one of the willow trees and sat up there in the branches. <i>(1949,I Saw in My Dream,117</i>) [NOTES: (1974 ed. Rhodes)]

>  <b>CRUMP</b> Y'see, we got a job working for an old tart who had a bit of a sheep -and cattle-run in the Hawke's Bay. <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,190</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> The old tart on the sideline who tripped up Fartstick Taylor with her umbrella when he was clean away for a try. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,174</i>)

>  <b>McINNES</b> Hurry up you kids. It's three tarts on bikes. The three tarts were aghast. <i>(1969,Castle on the Run,52</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> HINGAWARU ... When Rangi chooses his wahine, someone else pays, ne? RANGI: When I choose, Dad, I'll choose a <i>girl</i>, not a wahine! Hate that word! Makes me see fat slobbery old tarts waddling along the beach. <i>(1974,Hand on the Rail, The,20</i>)

### tat/tatt
 
 <i>n.</i> a tattoo (esp. prison, and often self-applied, or amateurly applied) (also as verb)

>  <b>BEATON</b> DI: I can see it now. Ginny in a pretty pink dress, hair set, face set. Tatts hidden under long sleeves. <i>Ibid.</i> <i>tatt</i> tattoo, especially one applied in prison. <i>(1982,Outside In,101</i>) [NOTES: [itals OK???]]

>  <b>BAKER</b> 'Those shop ones are just toys anyway,' said Mangu, dreads, tats and a checkled swanny... <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,126</i>)

>  Imagine, you get the tatts, and then you follow through with a genuine wahine. <i>(Jul 27, 2000,Dominion,8</i>)

### tataramoa
 
 <i>n.</i> (bush-) lawyer, taramoa, New Zealand bramble

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>tataramoa</i> or <i>taramoa</i>: climbing bramble of the bush known as 'bush lawyer'. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,194</i>)

>  The kohia (<i>Passiflora tetrandra</i>) is very abundant, ... while karioi [supplejack], mangemange, clematis, and tataramoa twine and intertwine as they unite tree to tree. <i>(1889,TrNZI,21/37</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> ... the primeval forest was often almost impenetrable, on account of the thick creepers, and the thorns, tataramoa (rubus), of which several species are to be found, and which tore our hands and faces severely. <i>Ibid.</i> ) 2/386 Tataramoa - thorns, blackberry <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/143</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> I also noticed a curious kind of rasp the <i>Tataramoa</i> [fn This is the only thorn in New Zealand.] which has a singular looking leaf ... <i>(Apr 13, 1839,Journal,2/100</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Rubus <i>australis</i>, Forst. ... Nat. name, `Tataramoa', <i>Cunn. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,II Flora Novae-Zelandiae I Flowering Plants 53</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Rubus <i>australis</i>, Forst. ... Nat. name, `Tataramoa', <i>Cunn. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,II Flora Novae-Zelandiae I Flowering Plants 53</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> Dr. Bennett says, `I accompanied a native to a place where I could see the tree-ferns growing. After passing through a dense forest, annoyed by the `tataramoa,' or New Zealand bramble, and stumbling over the lianas, or supple-jacks, which trailed upon the ground, we descended a hill covered with exuberant vegetation, and shaded by enormous trees; ... <i>(1866,Polynesia,47</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Tataramoa, <i>Col[enso]. Rubus australis. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> In this condition [<i>Rubus australis</i>, Forst.] forms one of the most ... troublesome `Lawyers,' or `Supple-Jacks,' of the forest. It is known to the settler also as the `Wild Irishman,' and to the North Island Maori as `Tataramoa'. [fn] A term defined by Dieffenbach as `Thorns, Blackberry,' - a description at least partly applicable, and with greater propriety, to <i>Discaria Toumatou. </i> <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,54</i>)

>  ... the fruit of the Tutu-papa (Coriaria thymifolia); of the New Zealand Bramble, Tataramoa, (Rubus australis);... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/31</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  Tataramoa. Rubus australis, Forst. varieties <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/41</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  Rubus australis Tataramoa <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/123</i>) [NOTES: (Armstrong Botany Canterbury)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> I have sometimes found its crop distended with the ripe pulpy seed of the tataramoa (<i>Rubus australis</i>), or with the berries of the kaiwiria (<i>Parsonia albiflora</i>) [?Parsonsia] [and kareao (<i>Rhipogonum scandens</i>); and it is said to feed also on the leaves of the thistle and wild cabbage. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,154,{1888 2nd edn 1/3} ed.</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> NOTE. - As I have no desire to mystify readers unacquainted with New Zealand flora, I append a brief description of the shrubs mentioned:- <i>Kowhai</i> - A papilonaceous flowering tree, of the acacia tribe. [17] <i>Bush-lawyer</i> - Native name, Tataramoa - A disagreeable bramble, very tenacious of grasp. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,1/iii/16,1974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Tataramoa ... is equally well-known under the designation of `bush-lawyer'. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/201</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> RUBUS AUSTRALIS (<i>Forst.</i>) ... The `NATIVE BRAMBLE'.- ... [120] The Maori name of the plant is `Tataramoa', whilst the settlers designate it as the `Native bramble', or `Bush lawyer'. <i>Ibid.</i> 178 Tataramoa [Maori name] .. Bramble [Settlers' name] .. Rubus Australis .. Shrub <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,119</i>)

>  I found his crop gorged with the berries of the tataramoa bramble <i>(Rubus australis), ... </i> <i>(1897,TrNZI,29/194</i>) [NOTES: (Buller)]

>  <b>SATCHELL</b> Supplejack vines, and the saws / And spines of tatara-moa. <i>(1900,Patriotic Rhymes,37</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>R[ubus] australis, Forst. ... Tataramoa; Bush-lawyer. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,125</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> The native name, <i>Tataramoa</i>, signifies <i>a heap of prickles</i>. The Maoris have also bestowed this name upon the English furze, and upon brambles generally. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,196,1940 p. 205 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> Over the noble lumber, the Bramble, the envious <i>Tataramoa</i> / Springing up, ... [fn] <i>Bramble</i>: Better known to New Zealanders as `Bush Lawyer'. <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,190</i>)

>  <i>tatara[a]moa, tara[a]moa,</i> a scrambling bramble (Rubus australis) bush-lawyer, wait-a-bit, both names inspired by the recurved prickles. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/158</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>MARTIN</b> The TATARAMOA or COMMON BUSH LAWYER <i>(Rubus cissoides)</i> is one of several <i>Rubus</i> species ... <i>(1929,Flora NZ,143,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Rubus cissoides </i>BUSH LAWYER ... Taataraamoa The commonest and most savagely prickly of the native brambles ... <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,40</i>)

>  Rubus Australis (Tataramoa, bush lawyer). <i>(Mar 2, 1917,Ohinemuri Gazette,4</i>)

>  Its Maori name, tataramoa, is given in Tregear's Maori dictionary as simply "the bramble." <i>(Oct 16, 1922,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  Amongst the berry-bearing trees were tataramoa, rama-rama, porokaiwhiria, mahoe, titoki, taupata, kohekohe, puriri, karaeo, and miro. <i>(Apr 19, 1934,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  The leaves of the tataramoa, or bush lawyer vine, can be boiled up to make a pleasant tasting concoction, safe and mild enough even for teething babies. <i>(Feb 2001,Growing Today,26</i>)

>  I ventured into the bramble, or tataramoa (tatter you more, rather). <i>(Dec 17, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  The Maori name for the prickly "bush lawyer," taramoa, or tataramoa, he learns from Tahiti is "the-cock's-spur plant." <i>(Aug 1, 1893,Timaru Herald,4</i>)

### tatare
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [totara](ten-tot.md#totara))

### tatariki
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>YATE</b> <i>Tatariki</i> - A small brown bird, with a white head, short black beak, black legs, and brown feet with four claws. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),161</i>)

### tatata
 
 <i>n.</i> a garment

>  <b>POLACK</b> The smallest garment woven is called the <i>patai</i>[a1-acute], principally worn by females for an inner garment; the <i>korowai </i>differs but little from the former, except in having a deep fringe of knotted flax strings. The<i> tatata</i>[as-acute] is similar in size to the former, having pending from every part, an infinity of small cords six inches long, of no use or ornament; its origin must be ascribed to the arbitrary taste of fashion, <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/177</i>)

### tatatarra
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [totara](ten-tot.md#totara))

### tatau
 
 <i>n.</i> a door slab [Williams [omit or include because of Satchell's Greenstone Door]

>  <i>tatau</i>, sliding slab for closing a door or window in a Maori house: tatau-pounamu (greenstone door), a term for an enduring peace between two tribes. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/158</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]



 
 <i>n.</i> a Samoan tattoo [ORIGIN: Samoan]

>  "We are all aware as Samoans that if a malu or tatau is tattooed without the agreement of the family, its results are fatal," Stanley told the <i> Samoa Observer </i> newspaper. <i>(Dec 6, 2007,NZ Herald,A7</i>)

>  His legs are as distinctive as any in the game, courtesy of full-length tattoos - Samoan "tatau". "These were formerly done in high chiefs, but restrictions have been loosened and just about anyone can get them. They're to do with manhood, if you're brave enough to get them"... <i>(May 7, 2004,Dominion Post  Sport & RaceForm,5</i>)

>  The first feature outing by Peter Burger and co-written by <i> Black Sheep's </i> Jonathan King .. a supernatural thriller about an American tattoo artist (Jason Behr) who unwittingly unleashes an evil spirit when he dabbles in the Samoan art of tatau. <i>(Jul 12, 2007,Time Out,5</i>)

### tatere
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> I went to see the fish which is called the Tatera its skin is rough like the shark ... 8 feet long ... 6 gills [sketch with caption] The Tatera or ururoa or Tuatini. 8 ft. long. <i>(Nov 28, 1844,Journal,3/41</i>)

### tats
 
 <i>n.</i> teeth

>  - when swift as a flash, she swished a stinging left hook on to his chin and - presto - his whole set of upper front `tats' fell into his long beer! <i>(Jul 28, 1906,NZ Truth,58/1/5</i>)

### Tattersall's
 
 <i>n.</i> an Australian lottery, popular in NZ because it offered better prizes than the local lottery

>  <b>HUNT</b> When I did she told me ... that she didn't think she would invest in any of my `tickets', as her brother had frequently taken tickets in `Tattersall's' and had never got anything for his money. <i>(1951,Confessions,67</i>)

>  Still, I could see they wanted someone outside themselves to believe in rainbows and trout and pots of gold and that Southland would win the Ranfurly Shield ... and Dad would win on his ticket to Tattersalls and take us all home to Galway where he'd come from. <i>(Aug 29, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>) [NOTES: Davin]

>  He died at the age of 58, universally lamented by a large circle of sporting men, who frequented his "Minor Tattersall's." <i>(Oct 14, 1843,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)

>  It was settling day at Tattersall's, and Palmer could not show. <i>(Sep 5, 1856,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  Most of our readers will remember the well known Tattersall's Corner, where so much betting is, in the season, transacted. <i>(Feb 18, 1864,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,3</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Here is the famous "marble bar" built out of the unclaimed funds of Tattersall's. <i>(Sep 1, 1904,NZ Observer,5</i>)

>  Formerly, the method used was for Tattersall's to post tickets direct to the customer from Hobart. <i>(Jan 21, 1953,NZ Truth,5</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>4</b> In Tattersall's Horse Bazaar at the annual Grand National sale in Christchurch of trotting and thoroughbred stock, a black mare changed hands twice within the hour. <i>(Aug 23, 1940,Taranaki Herald</i>)

### Tattersall's sweep
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Two Christchurch residents, Parish, a milkman, and Pierce, barman at the Oxford Hotel drew Glenloth in Tattersalls sweep, the monster cash prize consultation on the Melbourne Cup and they divide about GBP9000. <i>(Nov 2, 1892,Bay of Plenty Times,2</i>)

>  At the Police court to-day, Joseph Isaacs, tobacconist, was charged on four informations with breaches of the Gaming Act by disposing of tickets in Tattersall's sweeps. <i>(Nov 26, 1913,Evening Post,8</i>)

### tattoo
 
 <i>v.</i> [also tatu]

>  "It is generally supposed Te Kooti has Ririrangi tattooed up in his breast; but this is quite wrong, as cer[t]ified by those who have known him from childhood up to the fall of ngatapa, and whose information is of the most reliable kind. <i>(Oct 14, 1869,West Coast Times,3</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> . [aka tatu, tatto; derived from Polynesian dialects, was introduced to general English after the publication of Cook's Voyages (so Morris), and used by English speakers in New Zealand as the usual word for (applying) the Maori <i>moko</i> n `tattooing (on face or body)'] (See [moko](mob-mys.md#moko))

>  Both sexes paint their bodys. <i>Tattow </i>as it is called in their language. [check context and reference: ??Maori] <i>(Jul 1769,Cook's Journals,1/125,1955 ed.</i>)

>  ... in each of the Canoes were two or three Cheifs [sic] and the habits of these were superior to any we had yet seen, the Cloth they were made on was of the best sort and cover'd on the out side with Dog skins put on in such a manner as to look agreeable to the Eye. Few of these people were tattow'd or mark'd in the face like those we have seen farther to the south, but several had their Backsides tattou'd much in the same manner as the Inhabitants of the Islands within the Tropics. <i>(Nov 1769,Cook's Journals,1/213</i>)

>  `They have a custom of staining their bodies ... which they call <i>Tattowing</i>. They prick the skin, so as just not to fetch blood, with a small instrument, something in the form of a hoe. ... <i>(1773,Hawkesworth's Voyages,2/191</i>) [NOTES: >1773. `Hawkesworth's Voyages' (Cook's First Voyage; at Tahiti, 1769), vol. ii. p. 191:]

>  <b>LEDYARD</b> They are very curiuosly tatowed or punctuated in different parts of the body, particularly in the face. The food of the New-Zealanders is chiefly fish, ... <i>(Feb 1777,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1777 Feb in 1783 Ledyard Journal (1963) 14]

>  <b>FORSTER</b> ... the custom of tattowing the faces in spirals and Various scrolls, which prevails in New-Zeeland .... <i>(1778,Observations Made During a Voyage,271</i>)

>  <b>MARSDEN</b> The chisel was constantly dipped in a [172] liquid made of soot from a particular tree, and afterwards mixed with water, which communicates the blackness or, as they call it, the amoko (<i>moko</i>). I observed proud flesh rising in some part of the breech which had been cut almost one month before. The operation is too painful to bear the whole tattooing at one time. They appear to be several years before they are perfectly tattooed. <i>(1819,Letters & Journals of Samuel Marsden,171,1932 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McCRAE</b> ... as a gratifying memorial of his victory, [he] had got his nose tattooed afresh. <i>(1820,Journal,19,1928 ed.</i>)

>  tattoo - em|ook [e moko] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,111,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: c1826-1827 list of Maori words in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 111 also in Begg 1979/283]

>  <b>CRAIK</b> The term tattoo is not known in New Zealand. <i>(1830,New Zealanders, The,47</i>) [NOTES: [Rutherford's account:]]

>  <b>McNAB</b> In Witness whereof I have this day set my hand and Seal in my Tatto likeness Opposite. Middle Island or Tavai Poenammoo. <i>(1913,Old Whaling Days,90</i>) [NOTES: 1832 Nov 9 Deed in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 90]

>  <b>HODGSKIN</b> They were much tattooed about the face; ... <i>(1841,Narrative of Eight Months Sojourn in NZ, A,8</i>)

>  All [women] are tattooed that are tap-a-tap or married. <i>(1843,Letters from Settlers,74</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Thomas Bremner in 1843 Letters from Settlers 74]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> A very famous artist in tatu came with the party. <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,2/109</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> ...<i> and to mark the human body with indelible pigments. The word is Polynesian; its first occurrence in English is in Cook's account of Tahiti. The Tahitian word is Tatau </i>which means tattoo marks on the human skin, from <i>Ta</i>, which means a mark or design. (Littre.) The Maori verb, <i>ta</i>, means to cut, to tattoo, to strike. See <i>Moko. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,461</i>)

>  TATTOO BETRAYED HIM. A native named Mohi Te Kati was arrested at daylight at Petane Pah in August 1884 ... The prisoner was discovered through his having his name tattooed [sic] on his breast. <i>(Feb 18, 1886,Grey River Argus,2</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to brand or ear-mark cattle or dairy cows.

>  I've got 180 samples in this shed waiting to be tested and 16 meters to scrub and pack as well as 30 calves to tattoo. <i>(Jun 1977,NZ Dairy Exporter,38</i>)

### tattooed
 
 <i>adj.</i> [aka tatued]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> I well remember many a hard fought battle with these same<i> tatued</i> countenances <i>(Nov 2, 1834,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,399,1961 ed.</i>)

### tattooing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>ELDER</b> The face had been oiled in order to make the marks of the tattooing clear. <i>(Jul 23, 1815,Marsden's Lieutenants,87,1934 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MARSDEN</b> The chisel was constantly dipped in a [172] liquid made of soot from a particular tree, and afterwards mixed with water, which communicates the blackness or, as they call it, the amoko (<i>moko</i>). I observed proud flesh rising in some part of the breech which had been cut almost one month before. The operation is too painful to bear the whole tattooing at one time. They appear to be several years before they are perfectly tattooed. <i>(1819,Letters & Journals of Samuel Marsden,171,1932 ed.</i>)

>  Another interesting specimen of Maori art is an elaborately-carved feeding tube. This article was once used to convey liquid nourishment to persons undergoing the tattooing process, who were therefore in a very tapu condition, and debarred from handling food. <i>(May 4, 1910,Wanganui Chronicle,4</i>)

>  That the art of tattooing was a comparatively recent development among the Maoris was pointed out by Mr. R. F. Way, who gave an address on "Maori Moko, or Tattooing," to the Auckland Society of Arts, states the "Auckland Star." <i>(Sep 22, 1931,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  "Moko or Maori Tattooing." By Major-General Robley. 1896. 95s. <i>(Mar 10, 1928,Evening Post,21</i>)

>  "I love," followed by a name, is so common a form of tattooing that the record of it fills several pages. <i>(Dec 26, 1895,Otago Witness,51</i>)

>  Moko is the art of latter-day tattooing amongst the Maori. The older style was called Mokokuri, and was practised by the demigod Mani, who used to tattoo the muzzle of a dog - whence dogs went with black muzzles as we see them now. <i>(Nov 26, 1896,Otago Witness,3</i>)

### tattooing adze
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ... they fell to pieces on being touched, as did most of the bone articles - ... excepting the uhis or tattooing adzes ... <i>(1903,TrNZI,32/240</i>)

### Tatts
 
 <i>n.</i> Tattersall's lottery [aka Tatt's; incl attrib]

>  It did not require a tram ticket of an obsolete 31,456 in a Tatt's sweep to win admission. <i>(Jul 12, 1911,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  A man who was working on the new Strand Arcade in.. Auckland, won GBP5000 on "Tatts" the other day. <i>(Nov 12, 1910,NZ Free Lance,13</i>)

>  The Government says it is wrong to send a little bit over to Tatt's. <i>(Feb 11, 1920,NZ Free Lance,7</i>)

>  [Hdg] A Ticket in Tatt.'s... This Government clerk was acting in Government time as agent for Tattersall's <i>(Jun 3, 1916,Dominion,14</i>)

>  [Caption] Thou shalt not, on the pain of death, buy a ticket in 'Tatts'. <i>(Jun 9, 1912,NZ Free Lance,1</i>)

>  <b>JACKSON</b> When he failed ... to win Tatts or the Art Union, he took it as a personal slight ... <i>(1988,Rainshadow,105</i>)

>  Every member ... will have the option of keeping a `pub.' or running a `Tatts' agency. <i>(Jul 1, 1918,Quick March,10</i>)

>  ... I can't afford to ... shut up the little tabaccory and barbery because this is the flush season for my little `Tatts' business (on the side). <i>(Nov 1, 1918,Quick March,19</i>)

>  ... I was a tobacconist ... with a side-line in `Tatt's'. <i>(Jan 10, 1922,Quick March,17</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> We struck a ticket in Tatt's, and we won't be needing it sc money]. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,339</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> We struck a ticket in Tatt's, and we won't be needing it sc money]. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,339</i>)

>  <b>HISLOP</b> We were soon on the way again [after a lucky escape from accident], and on arrival at Queenstown the few locals ... came round advising me to take a ticket in `Tatts'. <i>(1943,Pure Gold,54</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> You're so lucky you ought to buy a ticket in Tatts. <i>(1951,Witch's Thorn, The,160</i>)

>  Heights of `pretty good' are reached only ... rarely by the backers of 16-16 outsiders and Tatts winners. <i>(Sep 1952,Here & Now,15</i>)

>  Heights of `pretty good' are reached only ... rarely by the backers of 16-16 outsiders and Tatts winners. <i>(Sep 1952,Here & Now,15</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> ... see you in Tatts after the war the blokes used to say and I'll shout you a beer ... <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,26</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> Was there any show of his busting out of it? None that he could see - unless he struck Tatts or a good double at the races, enough to get a stake to start himself. <i>(1960,Maori Girl,238</i>)

>  <b>McLAGLAN</b> I gave them enough money to buy a ticket in Tatts which seemed to please them. <i>(1965,Stethoscope & Saddlebags,165</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> ... perhaps his share in some winning Tatts ticket, ... <i>(1965,Memoirs of a Peon,197</i>)

>  <b>I. DAVEY (Whangarei)</b> The Turner index refers to TATTS. Why not UNCLE T? That is - or was - common when Uncle T was illegal in NZ. <i>(Jul 29, 1966,Letter</i>)

>  <i>Tatts n.</i> shortened form of Tattersall's formerly Tasmanian, now a Victorian lottery. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),626</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> The Golden Kiwi established partly to prevent sent money being sent out of New Zealand for tickets in Tatt's, a Tasmanian lottery with a name abbreviated from Tattersall's and quite unconnected with the English cant word <i>tatts</i> `dice'. It was illegal to sell tickets in Tatts' in New Zealand but many barbers' shops displayed a notice, `We communicate with Hobart', which was universally understood. . . .It was clear that in the context of a barber's shop window the notice meant `we sell tickets in Tatt's', but legally a man can announce that he writes to Tasmania. Newspapers might report the good fortune of winners but the illegal source of the winnings would not be specified. The money came from `an overseas consultation'. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,175</i>)

>  <i>Tatt's ... n. </i>shortened form of Tattersall's, formerly Tasmanian, now a Victorian lottery. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> Before leaving Raglan my mother had purchased a Tatt's lottery ticket. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,25</i>)

>  Porridge in New Zealand is horribly legal and Tatts delightfully illegal. <i>(Jun 14, 1923,Aussie (NZ Section),XVI</i>)

>  [Hdg] Tickets in "Tatts"... This clerk was acting in Government time as agent for "Tatts." <i>(Jun 3, 1916,Press, The,8</i>)

>  My plans would require the winning of the Irish Sweepstake as well as "Tatts". <i>(Apr 2, 1945,NZ Dairy Exporter,99</i>)

>  He whispers low to me at Tatt's, / All among the noisy Babel, / Jack won't speel, but Envious will, / Is the straight tip from the stable. <i>(Dec 22, 1883,Observer (Auckland),6</i>)

>  It is quite true that Tatt's. prize of GBP1800 in the Sydney Cup came to Dunedin. <i>(May 6, 1897,Otago Witness,33</i>)

>  Tatt's was known throughout the length and breadth of the land, and many are the stories told of persons unexpectedly lifted from penury to affluence by a lucky ticket in one of the big consultations. <i>(Jan 22, 1908,Tuapeka Times,3</i>)

>  If you can call the risking of 5s once or twice a year in a sweep gambling, then such moderate gambling gives us a distinct pleasure in life, and makes us more cheerfully bear the burdens which fate has fitted to our backs, for a ticket in 'Tatt's' gives us a scent of that one Flower of Eden over which the trail of the serpent is not - Hope! <i>(Jul 24, 1901,Otago Witness,51</i>)

>  At Ashburton they arrested a man for drunkenness and finding three tickets in "Tatts" in his pocket, charged him with purchasing the tickets, and he was fined 20/-. <i>(Dec 24, 1924,Christchurch Star,6</i>)

>  Sandy's expression could not have been more pleasing if he had won a Tatt's prize. <i>(Jul 25, 1925,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,60</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

### Tatts ticket
 
 <i>n.</i> [aka Tatt's ticket]

>  <b>SMITH</b> Between [Australia and NZ] there is one of the roughest seas in the world but that has never stopped us importing Cabinet Ministers and Tatt's tickets. <i>(1947,From N to Z,17</i>)

>  A great deal of fuss is made over a Tatts ticket and the sale of such gambling chances, but when it comes to joint stock company swindles, nothing is done to protect the public. <i>(Oct 22, 1921,NZ Truth,4</i>)

>  Should Poneke win this competition, the members ought to buy a "Tatt's" ticket apiece. <i>(Aug 5, 1911,NZ Truth,3</i>)

>  Weekly investment in a Tatt's ticket is a recognised practice among thousands of New Zealanders, denied the right to speculate in a similar lottery with such substantial prizes in this country. <i>(Jan 21, 1953,NZ Truth,5</i>)

>  Better than a "Tatts" ticket. <i>(Jan 18, 1918,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

### tatua
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Last year all the senior girls made ketes and tatuas. <i>(Sep 21, 1927,NZ Tablet,39</i>)

>  These will make the tatua or belt for the loin cloth worn by the men. <i>(Feb 1998,Mana,No. 20: 21</i>)

>  It's a look he seldom varies, except when he looses his hair, puts on tipare (headband), piupiu (flax skirt) and tatua (ceremonial sash) to lead Manutake in haka. <i>(Dec 2004,North & South,64</i>)

### tau inu
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [tauhinu](t-ten.md#tauhinu))

### tau tau
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>JOSEPH</b> `The jade, the pounamu ... It's a beautiful stone ... Look,' he said, holding up a long hooked ear-drop of pearly translucence, `that's tau tau and the stone is called inanga. And this, the pekapeka - they say the shape comes from the bat. This is for the ear, too - the mako, like a shark's tooth ... And these,' - he ran his fingers along a row of ... nephrite adzes - `toki. See this one' - he held the heavy red-mottled adze in his palm - `that's the red kind, totoweka, woodhen's blood. The deep green, that's kawakawa.' <i>(1962,Pound of Saffron, A,113</i>)

### taua
 
 <i>n.</i> war party [(Frequent in early North Island missionary journals and letters, and among those living among or dealing with Maoris. It is perhaps part of early missionary English (?jargon).) [check discards]]

>  <b>ELDER</b> The tear (<i>taua</i>) departed from hence on the 25th. The whole consisted of about thirty canoes and eight hundred men. <i>(Feb 20, 1817,Marsden's Lieutenants,135,1934 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1817 Feb 20 Kendall in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 135]

>  <b>ELDER</b> The tear (<i>taua</i>) departed from hence on the 25th. The whole consisted of about thirty canoes and eight hundred men. <i>(Feb 20, 1817,Marsden's Lieutenants,135,1934 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1817 Feb 20 Kendall in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 135]

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> Then he called in as ally the wily Waikato chief ... with a taua of two hundred warriors, promising them all the kuki (slaves) taken and all the booty they could carry away. <i>(1955,By Reef and Range,15</i>)

>  <b>H WILLIAMS</b> ... and war parties immediately assembled for revenge. Henry Williams ... went with the <i>taua</i>, and by his intervention peace was made. <i>(1961,Journal,20</i>) [NOTES: 1961 Rogers (editor's introduction) Journ. H. Williams 20]

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>taua</i>: war party <i>(1963,O Te Raki,194</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK3/52</b> In September 1819 a Ngapuki taua (war party) who had recently acquired muskets ... passed through Kauhia ... . <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK3/52</b> In September 1819 a Ngapuki taua (war party) who had recently acquired muskets ... passed through Kauhia ... . <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An</i>)

>  <b>BRAITHWAITE</b> TAUA War party. <i>(1967,Evil Day,381</i>)

>  <b>BELICH</b> <i>taua</i> war party <i>(1986,NZ Wars, The,337</i>)

>  I heard afterwards that they had just returned from a taua, or a rubbing excursion, into the country of the Waikato; with whom the tribe of Roto-rua is in constant hostility. <i>(Mar 30, 1844,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  Just before dawn one morning, the sleeping kainga was aroused by the yells of a band of armed men, a small roving taua of the Ngati-Tuwharetoa tribe from the distant Taupo country. <i>(Jul 2, 1910,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  With a taua (war party) bristling on the edge of the town, it was a moment of bloody racial tension that spilled briefly into war. <i>(Dec 14, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),41</i>)

>  Yet she refers to some of these taua (war expeditions) as "Hika's army". <i>(Oct 18, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),60</i>)

>  The greater portion of his <i>taua</i> was retained here as a reserve, and he madly set out to attack the thickly populated district of Waikato, with about three hundred men. <i>(Jul 27, 1855,Daily Southern Cross,1</i>)

>  At this juncture, the AFC took up the pursuit, as did a Tuhoe taua, both parties separately skirmishing with the Ringatu in October. <i>(Oct 4, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

>  The Omuturangi taua rode up shortly after, had a friendly meeting, camped for the night, and rode off next morning, having received a present of two horses and a calf, which quite satisfied the Omuturangi relatives of the parties concerned. <i>(Sep 5, 1872,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Taua, <i>s</i>. A meeting, an army, a battle, ... <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,209</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> At 9 o'clock Tohiapu with the <i>Taua</i> came round the point. <i>(Apr 21, 1829,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,153,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> ... some Chiefs were going to get payment for the Coco again ... or they would bring a Tower [<i>taua</i>] `for[c]ible seizure' ... [fn] Tower a foray for an Insult or Injury. E.M. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),53,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> [An envoy arrived] ... to advise the old chief to hasten with the building of his fortification, as the tribe of whom both parties were apprehensive had given Terarau ... intimation they would make a tawa [a's-acute], or war, against Kaka [a1-acute], while his pa [a-acute] was defenceless. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/129</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> <i>(1839,Te Ika a Maui,2/129</i>)

>  <b>BIDWIL;L</b> They so strongly dissuaded me from going that I waited for several days, but as there was no further sign of the `Tower' I began to get impatient, and at last made up my mind to set off and run the risk of meeting them. <i>(1841,Rambles in NZ,98,1952 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> This morning George King came running to me saying that the Taua had come over this side and was digging up Mawai's potatoes. <i>(Jan 13, 1845,Journal,3/59</i>)

>  <b>SELWYN</b> At Whanganui I found a `taua' or fighting party of 170 natives headed by Te Heu Heu ... <i>(1845,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1845 Selwyn Letter in 1847 NZ Part IV (The Church in the Colonies XII) 7]

>  <b>McKILLOP</b> ... a sufficient excuse for making what the natives call a <i>tower</i> on their property (which means, coming in such numbers as to defy resistance), and seizing everything on the premises, ... [65] He once made a<i> tower</i> or rush at me with his tomahawk, ... <i>(1849,Reminiscences,41</i>)

>  <b>POWER</b> The Governor ... commenced the operations ... which ended in the capture of Te Rauperaha, in baffling the <i>taua</i> from Wanganui, ... and thus laying the foundation of the present prosperity. <i>(1849,Sketches in NZ,xvlvii</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> Speaking of their `taua' or military force, which went out to fight in olden times, they will tell you that it was ... a great many. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,52</i>)

>  Thompson, unable to carry his point with the <i>taua</i>, it is believed will return to Waikato <i>(Apr 13, 1861,Otago Witness,6</i>)

>  <b>MANING</b> Altogether this <i>taua</i> is better armed and equipped than ordinary; ... <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,47</i>) [NOTES: see also p. 42]

>  <b>2/117</b> Got a little consolation from Matiu te Huia, who declares that the supposed taua (or war party) is really a heke (or migration) of women & children ... <i>(1864,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1960</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  <b>HUNT</b> Then put them under the mat or the <i>taua</i> will take them: ... I really felt for the poor fellow, and promised to <i>taua</i> with the best in his behalf. <i>(1866,Chatham Islands,48</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> The <i>tauas</i>, or war parties, rapidly decreased [with the advent of Christianity] in number, and those chiefs who could before muster a force of five or six hundred men, were afterwards satisfied with two hundred, and thus from the want of followers their contests gradually ceased, and with them their horrid feasts. <i>Ibid.</i> 272 A few weeks only elapsed before a taua arrived to revenge their deaths. <i>(1868,Past and Present of NZ,11</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> Give up our wars - war-dances - <i>tauas</i> - taboo,/ Whence all our wealth, and power, and fame accrue,/ <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/140</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> Only a few months, however, before our visit to the Waimate, a `taua,' i.e., a fighting-party, had come to the Bishop's house to demand `utu' (payment) for a trespass. <i>(1884,Our Maoris,37</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> The host of your <i>taua</i>, address'd as to fight! [fn3] <i>Taua (tah[a acute]-wah)</i>: A warrior band. <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,54</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> To rehabilitate the <i>mana</i> of Popoia two <i>tauas </i>or war-parties were sent forth from Mohaka. <i>(1926,Tutira,69</i>)

>  <b>LAMBERT</b> ... a strong <i>taua</i> (war-party) was being organized ... <i>(1936,Pioneering Reminiscences of Old Wairoa,149</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> In December, 1844, a <i>taua</i> or war-party of the Taupo tribe ... descended the Wanganui River ... <i>(1940,Sir Donald Maclean,12</i>)

>  <i>taua</i>, war-party: ta[a]ua and taua[a] have quite different meanings. The three words look much the same, but th different. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/158</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  WAR PARTY. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,176</i>)

### taua muru
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BELICH</b> <i>taua muru</i> customary plundering expedition, exacting compensation for an offence, in which blood is seldom shed <i>(1986,NZ Wars, The,337</i>)

### tauhau
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [tauhou](t-ten.md#tauhou))

### tauhino
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [tauhinu](t-ten.md#tauhinu))

>  The Maori name for a shrub, cassinia, which spreads rapidly as a weed in parts of New Zealand. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),406</i>)

### tauhinu
 
 <i>n.</i> [tawini, tawhina]



 
 <i>n.</i> a small shrub, the New Zealand cottonwood. [Maori] [Tawini, tawhine, tawhina, et al; the main application in NZ English is to the <i>Cassinia</i> sp., a troublesome weed of central New Zealand. Its eradication generates word-combinations: <i>tauhinu-contract, tauhinu-pulling, tauhinu-rash, to pull tauhinu</i>. =yellow scrub (?Golden tauhinu)]

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Tau |inu; A shrub so called. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,210</i>)

>  <b>DILLON</b> There are no trees except for the banks of the river which has little woods which the settlers call bushes but which I think you will understand better by the name of Tawhitti as they have no trees but large and high shrub. <i>(1844,Papers,61</i>) [NOTES: [?Marlborough]]

>  ... tauwhinau ... <i>(1874,Ena, or the Ancient Maori,WILSON,286 ed.</i>)

>  Tauhinu (Cassinia retorta) seeds very freely and is apt to spread. <i>(1876,TrNZI,205</i>)

>  <b>CRAWFORD</b> [In the Marlborough Sounds] after ... trees have been felled, fire will go through the forest and a scanty pasture be produced ... until the shrub called <i>tauwhinu</i> take possession and smother the pasture. <i>(1880,Travels in NZ,263</i>)

>  <b>CRAWFORD</b> [In the Marlborough Sounds] after ... trees have been felled, fire will go through the forest and a scanty pasture be produced ... until the shrub called <i>tauwhinu</i> take possession and smother the pasture. <i>(1880,Travels in NZ,263</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> This bird [Pukeko] often leaves its home ... to travel ... amongst the tauhinu bushes in quest of grasshoppers. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,2/82</i>)

>  ... resinous Tawhina <i>(1900,NZ Illustrated Magazine,354</i>)

>  In the portion of Marlborough north of the Wairau River care is necessary to prevent the land ... being overrun with brambles, briars, St. John's wort, manuka, tawhinau ... <i>(1901,TrNZI,33/327</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> Clear'd I have, and I've clear'd an' clear'd. / Yet everywhere, slap in your face, / Briar, tauhinu and ruin .... <i>(1903,Reuben & Other Poems,88</i>)

>  <b>ALEXANDER & CURRIE</b> <i>Tauhinu:</i> an aromatic shrub infesting poor soil. <i>(1906,Treasury of NZ Verse,289</i>)

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> [?Nelson, ?West Coast] ... tarwinie <i>(1926,Old Savage,47</i>)

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> <i>Tauhinu (Cassinia leptophylla, C. vauvilliersii</i>, etc.), commonly pronounced Tawhini, and also called yellow scrub; grows as a woody shrub, 3 to 4 ft. high with yellow leaves and white flowers. It is a native, and commonest on poor clay soils ... <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,212</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> ... dense thickets of manuka and other scrub, including [247] tawhini, ... <i>(1940,Te Waimate,246</i>) [NOTES: [spg tawhini]]

>  <i>(1944,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),406</i>)

>  [The Nelson farmer] called it tawhine, not tauhinu, and I've never heard a farmer call it anything else. <i>(Dec 26, 1947,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12</i>) [NOTES: (Sundowner)]

>  <b>LEVY</b> Gorse, blackberry, bracken fern, hard fern, manuka, cottonwood, mauhinu, and other scrub growths are neglected on the hills, ... <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,293,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MACFARLANE</b> There is bidi bidi, a native messing up the wool, ... ; tarweni, a shrub covering light land that is inclined to be sour. This plant is supposed to have arrived from South America eighty or ninety years ago; ... <i>(1954,Te Raka,41</i>) [NOTES: [spg tarweni]]

>  <b>DUFF</b> <i>(1955,New Zealand Now,92</i>)

>  tauhinau The river forced us to do much sidling through the tauhinau and matagouri and across a limestone face ... <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,76</i>)

>  <b>HILGENDORF & CAL;DER</b> TAUHINU (<i>Cassinia leptophylla, C. vauvilliersii</i> and also <i>Senecio cassinioides</i>) The cassinias have yellowish leaves and are therefore often called yellow scrub. ... All grow as rounded woody shrubs 3 or 4 feet high. <i>(1967,Weeds of New Zealand,225</i>)

>  <i>tauhinu [=] golden tauhinu mountain tauhinu: whatitiri</i> [s] ... <i>tauhinu [s] Cassinia leptophylla</i> (Forst.f.) R.Br. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,77</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> ...the sweet-scented Cassinia or tauhinau, generally known as `Tawini'; <i>(1970,Glorious Morning,108</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> ...the sweet-scented Cassinia or tauhinau, generally known as `Tawini'; <i>(1970,Glorious Morning,108</i>)

>  <b>McCARTHY</b> There were only four of us ... to cut 100 acres of scrub, tauhine and ti-tree (manuka). <i>(1973,Listen...!,35</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> . . . once the early fertility went the [D'urville Island] country was invaded with tauhine _ <i>Ibid </i>44 This was of course before the tawhine (or tauhinu) became such a problem. <i>(1873,Big Country,36,1977 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>CASSINIA LEPTOPHYLLA. Cassinia</i> is a genus of Australasian and South African shrubs. The five New Zealand species, known as `cottonwoods'or `tauhinu'... The grey-leaved <i>C. leptophylla </i>so invaded neglected hill pastures (as about Wellington) that it, along with the golden tauhinu <i>C. fulvida,</i> was designated a`scheduled noxious weed'. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,138</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> TAUHINU (<i>Cassinia</i> spp.) ... A native plant locally common east of the main ranges from Wairoa to Central Otago, and perhaps most abundant about Wellington and Marlborough Sounds. <i>(1981,Weeds of Roadsides,145</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> Gather round you chaps and I'll tell you a yarn/ When we pulled tawhina, down on Harris's farm. <i>(1985,I'm Ninety-five,36</i>) [NOTES: (Bill Henderson)]

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> [spg tawhine] The farm was poor and only partially improved, the majority being in its original state of scrubby tea tree and tawhine. <i>Ibid.</i>145 [spg tawhini] We dug fox-holes along the ridges ... at the edge of the windswept manuka and tawhini ... <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,64</i>)

>  <i>tauhinu n.</i> densely-growing NZ shrub with white daisy-like flowers. Pop. called <i>tawhine. </i> <i>Ibid.</i> 788<i>tawhine</i> see TAUHINU. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,787</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  She ... helped him stalk ...a stray wild pig through the manuka and tawhina scrub. <i>(1987,Happy Endings,110</i>) [NOTES: 1902 Lancaster Our Lady of the Plains in 1987 Happy Endings 110]

>  <b>WILSON</b> Thistles and rushes, docks and tawini scrub, grew in the empty paddocks. <i>(1984,South Pacific Street,8</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Tauhinu, <i>Col[enso]. Pomaderris ericifolia. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> POMADERRIS PHYLICIFOLIA <i>(TODD.)</i> ... The `Tauhinu'.- This small heath-like shrub is peculiar to the Northern Island, and grows abundantly in certain localities on dry fern hills. <i>Ibid.</i> 178 Tauhinu [Maori name] .. Tauhinu [Settlers' name] .. Pomaderris phylicifolia .. Shrub <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,91</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> [<i>Pomaderris phylicaefolia, Lodd.</i>] North Cape to Otaki and Cape Palliser. <i>Tauhinu.</i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,101</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> [<i>Pomaderris phylicaefolia, Lodd.</i>] North Cape to Otaki and Cape Palliser. <i>Tauhinu.</i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,101</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Pomaderris phylicaefolia (The Phylica-leaved Pomaderris).</i> A strongly scented, health-like shrub, which grows profusely amongst the small tea-tree, upon gum-lands. ... Maori name <i>Tauhinu.</i> [fn] This name is also applied to <i>Cassinia leptophylla. </i> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,238,1940, p. 251 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Pomaderris phylicaefolia (The Phylica-leaved Pomaderris).</i> A strongly scented, health-like shrub, which grows profusely amongst the small tea-tree, upon gum-lands. ... >  <i>tauhinu,</i> a troublesome scrub (Pomaderris Phylicaefolia), densely growing and persistently regenerating. C. leptophylla is tauhinu-korokia or tauhinu-koromiko, but these names are not in general use as is tauhinu, or tawhini as some miscall it. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/158</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Tauhinu, <i>Geolog. Surv. Podocarpus nivalis. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>) [NOTES: Podocarpus nivalis]

The grey-leaved <i>C. leptophylla</i> so invaded neglected hill pastures (as about Wellington) that it, along with the golden tauhinu<i> C. fulvida</i>, was designated a`scheduled noxious weed'. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,138</i>)

>  <i>mountain tauhinu</i> [s] <i>Cassinia vauvilliersii</i> (Homb. & Jacq.) Hook.f. <i>Ibid.</i> 77 <i>tauhinu, mountain </i>[s] <i>Cassinia vauvilliersii</i> (Homb. & Jacq.) Hook.f. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,50</i>)

### tauhou
 
 <i>n.</i> [Maori, stranger: the bird arrived from Australia in the mid-19th century] <i>Zosterops lateralis</i>, silvereye, white-eye, wax-eye, blight bird (See [silvereye](sho-spa.md#silvereye))

>  ZOSTEROPS LATERALIS, Lath. Tauhou. Blight-bird. We first noticed this bird ... in the Rockwood Valley, Malvern Hills, July 28th, 1856. ... It very soon obtained the name of Blight-bird ... from its habit of feeding on the American blight, with which apple trees in this colony are so generally infested ... <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/61</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 14. ZOSTEROPS LATERALIS. Lath. White-eye. Wax-eye. Blight-bird. Tauhou. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,6</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> ZOSTEROPS LATERALIS \{CAERULESCENS\}. (THE SILVER-EYE.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Tau-hou, \{Whiorangi, Hiraka, Motingitingi,\} Kanohi-mowhiti, Karu-patene, \{Karu-ringi,\} Karu-hiriwha, Poporohe, and Iringatau. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,{1888 2nd edn 1/77} ed.</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> ZOSTEROPS LATERALIS \{CAERULESCENS\}. (THE SILVER-EYE.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Tau-hou, \{Whiorangi, Hiraka, Motingitingi,\} Kanohi-mowhiti, Karu-patene, \{Karu-ringi,\} Karu-hiriwha, Poporohe, and Iringatau. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,{1888 2nd edn 1/77} ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Tauhau (<i>Zosterops lateralis</i>) is a beautiful little green bird, much like a wren. ... It is much seen about gardens and clearings, and settlers know it as the `blight-bird'. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/226</i>)

>  The natives distinguish the bird as Tau-hou (which means stranger), or Kanohi-mowhiti (which may be interpreted as spectacle-eye or ring-eye). It is also called Poporoke and Iringatu, names suggested by its accidental or periodical occurrence. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/81</i>)

>  LIST OF NAPIER BIRDS. ... <i>Zosterops lateralis. </i>(Blight-bird; tauhou.) ... <i>Himantopus leucocephalus</i>. (Red Stilt.) <i>(1901,TrNZI,33/215</i>)

>  Coming to our little white-eye, or tauhou, `the stranger', who came from Australia in 1856 ... is now as rare as he was common. <i>(1908,TrNZI,40/489</i>)

>  <i>tauhou</i>, a wandering bird (Zosterops halmaturina), blight-bird; twinkie; button-eye; wax-eye; ring-eye. Tauhou means stranger and this name was given by the Maori when the bird appeared, in 1856, in the central part of Newzealand, gradually spreading north and south till the islands were occupied. It is an Australian bird, and was blown across the Tasman sea in the year mentioned. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/159</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  SILVEREYE, FOREST BIRD. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,176</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> SILVEREYE <i>Zosterops lateralis Other names</i>: Tauhou, Waxeye, White-eye, Blightbird, Blightie. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,223</i>)

>  <i>263. Zosterops lateralis lateralis</i> (Latham, 1801) <i>Silvereye (Tauhou)</i> Originally Tasmania and Bass Strait islands; the species colonised the South Island ... (before 1856), North Island (1856) ... <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,69,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Silvereye Zosterops lateralis lateralis </i>(LATHAM, 1801) ... OTHER NAMES: <White-eye, waxeye, blightbird,</i> tauhou. <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,288</i>)

>  Grub's up: A waxeye, or tautou, feeds on a loquat tree in Greenhithe. <i>(Oct 13, 2005,NZ Herald,A16</i>)

>  We have a flock of lively tauhou, the white-eye or silver-eye..... pegging away at the scraps in tins hung on a tree for them. <i>(Jul 1937,NZ Railways Magazine,25</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [Maori: stranger. This the person, cf bird]

>  Accordingly, one of my boys being a tauhou (stranger), climbed up and tied an old soldier's coat on it. <i>(Apr 25, 1852,Wellington Independent,3</i>)

### tauihi
 
 <i>n.</i> prow of a waka

>  The tauihu, or prow, of a waka was discovered on a farm 15 km northeast of Tauranga 10 days ago. <i>(Feb 27, 1999,Evening Post,2</i>)

### tauira
 
 <i>n.</i> a student

>  Students, or tauira, interviewed "were consistently dissatisfied with the lack of learning resources information and reponses by Te Wananaga a Aotearoa to tauira complaints," said the report. <i>(Aug 17, 2003,Sunday Star Times,C5</i>)

### tauiwi
 
 <i>adj.</i> not Maori, foreign [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  She asked if she could move to the "tauiwi" group, where she would have a better chance of improving her Maori language skills. <i>(Aug 29, 1998,Dominion,8</i>)

>  Maori still had to ask Government for financial support for initiatives - but were forced to make them fit tauiwi [foreign] criteria. <i>(Oct 22, 1999,Evening Post,13</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> outsider, foreigner, non-Maori

>  Here, after all, was the woman who habitually refers to non-Maori New Zealanders as tau iwi, which, depending on the inflection of translation, means foreigner. <i>(Sep 16, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

>  `Pakeha' is a word that seems to be bugging many New Zealanders. Perhaps this is the reason why the Royal Commission on Social Policy in its booklet dealing with the Treaty of Waitangi uses the word `Tauiwi' instead of `Pakeha'. For example a section of the booklet is headed `Partnership between Maori and Tauiwi'. Tauiwi, according to the booklet's glossary, means `later settlers from another country'. A Dictionary of the Maori Language ... gives `strange tribe, foreign race' as the meaning of Tauiwi. As a New Zealander born and bred in this country I was disturbed at the unceremonious use of a word which seems to make me some sort of foreigner in my own land. ... On phoning the commission's office I was informed that despite considerable unease over the use of Tauiwi in the treaty booklet staff were told that it had to be used. I was also informed that the use of Tauiwi instead of Pakeha had been discontinued. <i>(Jun 6, 1988,Dominion Sunday Times,11</i>)

>  Here he was overwhelmed by a distinctly unappreciative din from his fellow indigenes and tau iwi alike. <i>(Aug 4, 2004,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

>  Just a couple of years back, one of the Prime Minister's colleagues.. insultingly classified non-Maori New Zealanders are [sic] foreigners in their own land by referring to them as "tauiwi". <i>(Jun 4, 2000,Sunday Star Times,A8</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a small tower

### taumaka
 
 <i>n.</i> rockfish

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Acanthoclinus quadridactylus</i> TAUMAKA. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,49,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> ROCKFISH (<i>Acanthoclinus quadridactylus</i> ), Taumaka of the Maoris, is tha small brownish-olive fish, about 7 inches in length, which is found lurking under boulders at low tide. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,72</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> ROCKFISH (TAUMAKA) <i>Acanthoclinus quadridactylus</i> (Bloch and Schneider) ... [ibid. 322] Those who do know this fish call it Rockfish, but I prefer the Maori name, Taumaka. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,320</i>)

### taupare
 
 <i>n.</i> a figure-head

### taupata
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  She [sc. Alison Gray] proved the point in a 1981 study, co-authored with Rosemary Barrington, called The Smith Women. This identified our tidy suburban homes, clipped taupata hedges, flower beds in well-cut lawns. <i>(Sep 7, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

>  <b>JACKSON</b> An arthritic taupata hedge sheltered it from the sea, though from the sunporch - which became my room - you could see the ocean ... <i>(1988,Rainshadow,98</i>)

>  This summer, as last year, rain was so plentiful in parts of Wellington that taupata berries have swollen up like juicy blueberries. <i>(Mar 8, 2003,Dominion Post,F15</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a shrub of the coprosma family often used as a hedge plant; <i>Coprosma repens</i> [ORIGIN: Maori, also called plumber's curse. [Ma. <b>taupata</b> coastal shrub (<i>coprosma repens</i>) cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 251.]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Taupata, <i>Col[enso]. Coprosma retusa. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> The Putiritiri, of the same order [as Karamu, coprosma sp.], is a beautiful tree with a small leaf, and also the Taupata, which having a fine bright leaf is a very ornamental tree ... <i>(1868,Past and Present of NZ,225</i>)

>  .. the curious red fruit of the Titoki, or Titongi, (<i>Alectryon excelsum</i>); the fruit .. of several species of <i>Coprosma</i>, - particlularly the Karamu (<i>C. lucida</i>), and (<i>C. robusta</i>), of the Papaauma (<i>C. grandifolia</i>), and of the two littoral species, Taupata (<i>C. retusa</i>), and Tataraheke (<i>C. acerosa</i>) ... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/31</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>C[oprosma retusa]</i> is much used for hedges in Wellington and Melbourne. In the former place it is generally known as <i>taupata</i> (sometimes <i>naupata</i>); in the latter it is called the <i>looking-glass</i> plant. <i>Ibid</i>. 395 <i>Coprosma [retusa] (The Taupata)</i> .. Maori names, <i>Taupata</i> and <i>Naupata. (Coprosma Baueri)</i>. [The true <i>C. Baueri</i> is a native of Norfolk Island]. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,392,1940 p. 420 (p. 423) ed.</i>)

>  The plant which is dominant and chiefly gives character to these island shrubberies is <i>Coprosma Baueri</i>, commonly known as the taupata. <i>(1924,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,7/185</i>) [NOTES: (Sladden)]

>  <i>taupata</i>, a tree (Coprosma retusa), with dark green leaves shining as if varnished; much used for hedges, cutting well till in the course of years it grows too woody. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/159</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> [Maori glossary] <i>taupata</i> Tree (<i>Coprosma</i> retusa) <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,302</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> And there's the lights on the leaves of the <i>taupata</i>, and on the<i> tutu</i> trees over the creek where the cows can't get at them. <i>(1960,Maori Girl,215</i>)

>  <b>DURRELL</b> <i>(1966,Two in the Bush,68</i>)

>  <b>MANTELL</b> There were trees growing thick there - ngaio, taupata, native fuchsia. <i>(1980,Murder and Chips,34</i>)

>  Between it and the front hedge (taupata) there were eight large trees. <i>(1985,Women's Work,114</i>) [NOTES: (Roche Structurally Sound)]

>  <i>taupata n</i>. NZ shrub of the coprosma family, widely used for shelter-fences. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,787</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>DAWSON</b> In the North Island particularly, prostrate shrubs of the shiny-leaved taupata (<i>Coprosma repens</i>), known as the `looking glass plant' in California where it is cultivated) are often prominent. <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,155</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> T|ao r|eka r|eka, s. A slave. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,207</i>)

>  Taupata has thick oval-shaped leaves with an almost plastic feel to them .. In the garden, where conditions are less harsh than the beach, the leaves grow broader and flatter, and taupata can be easily grown and pruned to a hedge eight metres tall. <i>(Feb 11, 2006,Dominion Post,20,Indulgence ed.</i>)

>  We spread our rugs in a spot where a wind-sculpted taupata and clumps of spinifex sheltered our backs from the gusty northerly. <i>(Jan 31, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),42</i>)

>  The vicar had observed that the taupata needed trimming to clear the harbour view. <i>(Apr 19, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),27</i>)

>  Taupata (Coprosma repens) is one of the commonest native shrubs in this part of the country, and it seems to evoke a love or hate reaction. <i>(Mar 8, 2003,Dominion Post,F15</i>)

>  The planting programme has wound down now that the pioneer plants such as ngaio, taupata and korimiko are established. <i>(Nov 2006,Footnotes,26 / 1</i>)

>  BOXTHORN - Extra fine plants in any quantity, and other hedge plants suitable for Hawera and district, such as Taupata, Olearia of sorts, Escallonia, Euonymus, etc. <i>(May 21, 1903,Hawera & Normanby Star,4</i>)

>  The City Council generously donated 300 taupata trees for the purpose of planting the school ground. <i>(May 1, 1914,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  <b>CROWE</b> Like other coprosmas, taupata belongs to the coffee family, a fact that inspired experiments in the late nineteenth century by a Mr J.C. Crawford, who presented a sample of ground coffee made from these seeds to the Wellington Philosophical Society in 1877. 'The beans .. 'he wrote,' when roasted and ground have a splendid coffee aroma and when made into coffee the results seem to be thoroughly satisfactory.' <i>(1995,Which Coastal Plant?,9</i>)

### Taupo Pumice
 
 <i>n.</i> . [geol. classification]

>  Similar large Taupo pumice is also found about Rotorua, beyond the range of the Taupo Ignimbrite. <i>(Jul 1998,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,116/42</i>)

>  Radiometric Ages of Taupo Pumice and Waimihia Lapilli at Gisborne. WA Pullar. NZ Soil Bureau, Whakatane - title of paper <i>(Feb 1964,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,15/16</i>)

>  The upper part of the section comprises Taupo Pumice, Kaharoa Ash and Rotomahana Mud intercalated with beds of peat. <i>(May 1975,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,39/36</i>)

>  Paul Froggatt, on the final morning, led us into the Taupo Forest to examine the 50 m thick Oruanui Breccia and the Taupo Pumice Formation. <i>(Feb 1982,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,55/32</i>)

>  The first day commenced near Matawai where a 6 m thick sequence (from Rotoehu Ash 50 kyr to Taupo Pumice 1800 yrs B.P.) is preserved in a road cutting on SH2 a few kilometers east of Matawai. ... At the top of Panikau Hill a thin but relative [sic] complete distal section is preserved and contains Rotoehu Ash through to Taupo Pumice. <i>(Jul 1984,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,65/17</i>)

>  They could easily sold as together as a set, possibly as part of a resource, with a photo set (currently available from the Taupo Observatory) and perhaps even a piece of Taupo pumice! <i>(Nov 1993,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,102/66</i>)

 <i>n.</i> traditional councils [plural? Etymology: Tokelau]

>  Tokelau is governed by taupulega, traditional councils of old men made up of the heads of each atoll family, but remains a New Zealand territory. <i>(Dec 18, 2005,Conservation Action,A4</i>)

### taura here
 
 <i>n.</i> urban grouping within an iwi [ORIGIN: Maori, (from tpk.govt.nz) Due to the large population and land area, TRAION has implemented Takiwa (natural geographical groupings) to assist with the management of issues relating to the population and the marae that fall into the Ngapuhi catchment area. There are also two Taurahere (urban groupings) since 80% of the tribe live outside the traditional Ngapuhi boundaries (40% in Auckland alone). The TRAION structure is grassroots focussed with the marae and all who whakapapa to these marae being the main drivers for the vision, and for the tribal immortality of Ngapuhi.]

>  The report said the onus to build a positive relationship with taurahere organisations was on council. ... He wanted the term tuarahere better defined. <i>(Nov 3, 2005,Horowhenua Mail,5</i>) [NOTES: -]

>  NGATI POROU IN AUCKLAND Your Taura Here seeks a Kaitiaki A Rohe .. *To foster relationships with Te haukaenga, tangata whenua, matawaka ... <i>(Jul 17, 2006,NZ Herald,D12</i>)

### Tauranga disease
 
 <i>n.</i> bush sickness

>  <i>tauranga n</i> -s [Maori]<i> NewZeal </i>: BUSH SICKNESS <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,2344</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a potentially fatal disease of sheep, caused by cobalt deficiency. [aka Tauranga sickness]

>  At the time of my visit the deaths had ceased for this year, and I was unable to obtain lambs for post-mortem examination that had died of the 'Tauranga disease.' <i>(Nov 1891,NZ Farmer,447</i>)

>  <b>DEPT OFLANDS & SURVEY</b> This wasting disease or condition in ruminant stock was known locally by several names: 'Tauranga disease' (because it was first reported from there in 1893 and again in 1897) ... <i>(1975,Prosperity from Pumice Land,7</i>)

>  During the 1890s, stock inspectors and veterinary reports described a condition known as 'bush disease' in cattle and 'Tauranga disease' in sheep. <i>(Mar 1979,NZ Journal of Agriculture,60</i>)

>  Take for instance the `Tauranga sheep-disease,' as it is called: professors of different colleges are sent for, to investigate it ... <i>(1892,TrNZI,24/626</i>)

>  The study of the deficiency disease for many years known as `bush sickness', `bush disease', `Tauranga disease', or `the skinnies', and now definitely and officially known as `iron starvation' was commenced by the writer in 1910. <i>Ibid.</i> 538 ) Gilruth (3) 1899 recorded his examination of a few cases of `Tauranga or Bush Sickness' [in 1899, Annual Report, Dept of Agriculture, 87]. <i>(1928,TrNZI,58/537</i>) [NOTES: Aston]

>  ".. Some experiments in contagious mammitis have yet to be done, and the 'Bush['] or 'Tauranga' disease, which affects a considerable area of country near Rotorua, still remains to be coped with." <i>(Jan 11, 1909,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  [Hdg] "Tauranga" or "Bush Disease". This peculiar wasting disease, which is confined to the tract of land lying in the vicinity of Tauranga, and extending from there to Rotorua and Lichfield, was made the subject of an investigation by Mr. Park three years ago... It appears that both sheep and cattle will do well and put on condition for the first few months after being placed on this diseased country, but as time goes on a gradual wasting sets in, which ends in death if they are not removed to another district. <i>(1899,Ann. Rep. Dept of Agriculture,VII/87</i>)

>  It would seem that to deal thoroughly with the matter much more time and research will have to be given to it, than the examination of a few sheep or lambs can accomplish, though done by as able an expert as Professor Thomas; whose instructions, as to parasitical disease, seem to have been carried out as well as opportunity allowed, and yet disclosed nothing specially distinct in the Tauranga disease. <i>(Apr 15, 1891,Bay of Plenty Times,2</i>)

### Tauranga/Tauranga class
 
 <i>n.</i> alternative name of P class q.v.

>  Mr Highet designed his prototype of the P-class yacht about 70 years ago. It had its first outing at Onerahi Bay,Whangarei, on New Year's Day, 1920... In 1924 the boat was officially recognised in the Tauranga seven-foot one-design class. <i>(Feb 6, 1989,NZ Herald,I/1</i>)

>  <b>CARTER</b> What is often referred to as the "Baby Class" of New Zealand, the 7-foot Tauranga boats, also owe their existence to the efforts of a Wellington yachtsman, Mr. M.H.A. Highet, who was the originator of the class. These tiny yachts, which are built on the chine principle, have a beam of 3.6 feet, and carry a single sail of the gunter type. <i>(1944,Little Ships: NZ's Yachting Fleet,132</i>)

>  <b>TITCHENER</b> The P-class is also known as the Tauranga class, because Mr Highet moved from Wellington soon after designing the yacht. The first yachts from this design sailed on the Tauranga Harbour <i>(1978,Little Ships of NZ,73</i>)

### Taurangian
 
 <i>n.</i> a person born or resident in Tauranga

>  <b>GIFKIN</b> All Pakeha Taurangians were superior to all Maori; even an educated 'Pakeha-fied' Maori would end by going 'back to the mat'. <i>(1988,Through the Looking Glass,128</i>) [NOTES: Meikle]

### taurekareka
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) a slave or war captive [ORIGIN: Maori, also taureka]

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>taurekareka:</i>slave, low person (a term of supreme contempt). <i>(1963,O Te Raki,194</i>)

>  <b>CRUISE</b> ... they expressed their astonishment that we could so far forget ourselves as to sit down and eat with a towra caracca, or prisoner of war; and this degrading appellation was universally applied to the American gentleman as long as he remained amongst us. <i>(1823,Journal,198,1957 ed.</i>)

>  [spg touwrikka] Price ... told the natives, Capt. K. was a towrikka, or cookie, and that he was of no note etc <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,793,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: also 1827 Boultbee Journal in 1979 Begg 196]

>  <b>POLACK</b> He has often been reproached as a slave, (<i>taurekareka</i>), a term that applied to a chief of spirit, would engender in his breast a determination to seek revenge ... <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/25</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> The rest of the men are either rangatira, free men, or tau-reka-reka, slaves. ... The slaves, tau-reka-reka, are the prisoners of war, male or female, and such of their children as are born in slavery. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/113</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> These [Maori] boys were very inquisitive, ... they wanted to know if the [Queen Victoria's] palace was any larger than their house at Te Awa iti, and what number of <i>taua reka rekas</i>, or slaves, the Queen had belonging to her. <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/281</i>)

>  <b>POWER</b> ... a man who has been found guilty of the crime [of theft] is considered a `tau reka-reka', `a slave'. <i>(1849,Sketches in NZ,145</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> <i>(1937,Vintage Murder,268</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,3/338</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> ... the <i>tohunga</i> made it ... <i>tapu</i>, so that the <i>kukis, taurekarekas, tutuas</i>, and other varieties of slaves ... would be afraid to meddle. <i>(1948,Taina,26</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> Aue, I know you recall. You lost your good temper and yelled at me all kinds of rude names and swearing, taureka and sloven, 'pokokohua and bitch. <i>(1986,Te Kaihau,115</i>) [NOTES: [spg taureka]]

>  Europeans who embraced Maori culture were sometimes called taurekareka Maori or slaves. <i>(Apr 2002,AA Directions,No. 51 (Autumn) : 61</i>)

>  <b>GRACE</b> You are a low-bred set of men, taurekareka, all of you. <i>(1901,Tales of a Dying Race,88</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> "I should have forgotten that an <i>ariki</i> does not lay hands on a t<i>aurekareka</i>." [Note. <i>Taurekareka</i>-slave, low-class person]. <i>(1937,Vintage Murder,232</i>)

>  'They are going to kill me, and make a taureka (slave) of you but that will never happen,' and picking her up, he knocked her brains out against the hatch-combing. <i>(Jul 18, 1896,Hawke's Bay Herald,5</i>)

>  If a tribe was.. nearly annihilated. and the small remnant was captured, they became the slaves (tuarekareka) and the land the absolute property of the conquerors. <i>(May 21, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,34</i>)

>  He answered, "I am a Ngapuhi chief, and a rangatira;" "but I found out," says Mrs. Moran bitterly, "on getting to the Bay that he was only a taurekareka." <i>(Jul 5, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)

>  'But,' the waitress protested, 'he's a big chief' Heni shook her head as an assertation of independence.'He may be big rangatira on his own marae, he just a taurekareka in my kitchen.' <i>(Mar 1963,Te Ao Hou,42: 16</i>)

>  They are naturally proud, and haughty; they have keen sense of honour; pride would not allow them to brook an insult; their honour trampled on, a degrading epithet applied, a chief called a taurekareka, or familiarities with his wives, were insults not to be passed over. <i>(May 17, 1851,New Zealander, The,2</i>)

>  The Provincial Government, which has been famliarly known, sometimes as the uneducated Government, and sometimes as the Taurekareka Government, has obtained for itself a new title. It is now known as - the I.O.U. Government. <i>(Jun 28, 1860,Daily Southern Cross,1</i>)

>  With a well-assumed air of surprise and innocence the scoundrel confronted the indignant chief, and said, "I paid this man here, your taurekareka, for the pigs. Ask him if I did not count out 70 sovereigns and give them to him in a bag sealed up." <i>(Sep 10, 1881,Observer (Auckland),614</i>)

>  Moreover, he resuscitates a forty-century old mummy, which he compels to go forth invisably on nefarious errands, to the unspeakable disgust of the spirit of the ancient Pharaoh, who has an excusable objection to being made the lackey of a mere taurekareka of these degenerate days, and in the sequel takes ample revenge. <i>(Feb 11, 1911,Evening Post,13</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a small shrub, Rhabdothamnus solandri (in DNZE as matata hom.2)(also called NZ gloxinia, waiu-atua)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Rhabdothamnus Solandri (Solander's Rhabdothamnus)</i> ... Maori name, <i>Taurepo. </i> <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,415</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [Possibly variant of "taureka"]

 <i>n.</i>
