### taw
 
 <i>n.</i> a large or choice marble; a big marble; a bonser (cf broncs, grandads) ca1918-20 Auckland (man 66 yrs) a big marble called `bonser' or `taw' -

>  Marbles, it seems, are 'in'.. and entered interest in the fortunes of the elusive "taw" surpasses all the blandishments of which I am master. <i>(Dec 17, 1935,Christchurch Star-Sun,12</i>)

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> `Reverend Mother talks of when she used to give you marbles for being a good little boy - pee-wees and agates, and stonies and glassies and a big tor. And once she tried to play with you, and you said she funked because her knuckle was inside the ring.' <i>(1933,Tides of Youth,153</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> <i>(1936,Passport to Hell,25</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> ... a mob of little boys had their tails up over a marble game. ... they fired their taws, green and blue and brilliant yellow, at the squares richly lined with the weatherbeaten old clay soldiers which sold at twelve a penny and were known as dakes. thought to be spelled <i>tor</i> [common G. Kennedy] <i>(1951,Witch's Thorn, The,176</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Marbles had a language of its own. There were names for the marbles, the games, and all the various aspects of the play. There were terms for marbles such as `taw' or `shooter', referring to the marbles used to fire with; `dubs', `dates', `stakes', `dukes', or `changers', referring to the marbles put down to be fired at.<i>Ibid.</i> 176 [Waitara, Taranaki, 1899] A `taw' was usually an `alley' or `blood alley' made of agate. We used to take them home and burn them in a fire to convert them into `blood alleys'. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,174</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> Some of them spat in the pathway of an opposing taw and cried 'Mississippi!' while others chanted, with deadly unselfconsciousness: 'Shut up, button yer lip, cork yer eye with a juniper pip! If you listen to this, you are bound to miss, miss, miss!' <i>(1951,Witch's Thorn, The,176</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> With red faces and eyes bulging with an afternoon of stooping, they fired their taws, green and blue and brilliant yellow, at the squares richly lined with the weatherbeaten old clay soldiers which sold at twelve a penny and were known as dakes. <i>(1951,Witch's Thorn, The,176</i>)

### tawa
 
 <i>n.</i> [2x timber, 1x waikurupopo]

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> The <i>Towa</i> spars are considered the best for this purpose. <i>(1842,Narrative of a Residence in  Various Parts of NZ,42</i>)

>  The properties of Tawa timber are as follows ... <i>(Jan 1884,NZ Journal of Science,110</i>)

>  21 Tawa slab. tall timber tree with edible blue-black berries. [Maori] <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,16</i>)

>  We camped again, having been fortunate to find a decayed tawa tree, from which the natives squeezed a kettle of bitter and not over clear water, known, I believe, as the waikurupopo. <i>(Jan 11, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,14</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a general name for various species [Maori] <i>Beilschmiedia tawa</i>, a forest tree, its timber, and its fruit (tawa-berry) [aka tower. [?d] Ed. Fairburn Maharatanga 47f. ... his bunch of towers.]

>  <b>McDONNELL</b> [spg tana] A great variety of hard-wood grows at New Zealand, admirably adapted for the timbering of any sized ships; among them is the boride[e-acute], rattar[as-acute], taraide[ase-acute], mai[a-acute], totara[a1-acute], koi katoa[a2-acute], toa[a-acute] toa[a-acute], tani raha[as-acute], to wai[a-acute], reiva[a-acute] reiva[a-acute], tana[as-acute][check ?sic or = <i>taua</i>] and many others. <i>towa</i>, a mis-spelling for <i>tawa</i> : cf <i>tarairi</i> below (laurus tarairi) : next entry <i>towai </i> <i>(1834,Extracts From a Journal,19,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> Tawa (<i>Laurus Tawa</i>) is a frequenter of damp and deeply - shaded woods, with leaf and branches similar to those of the Mairi-tree; ... <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),42</i>)

>  Tawa ... [check title and date, and expand citation] <i>(Apr 17, 1837,Sydney Herald</i>) [NOTES: 1837 Apr 17 Sydney Herald E.G. Wakefield and J. Ward British Colonies 324]

>  <b>POLACK</b> Towa [a-acute] (laurus Australis,) is a useful timber for boarding the interior of houses; ... <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/396</i>)

>  <b>BIDWILL</b> The trees were chiefly the Towa, [fn <i>Leiospermum racemosum</i>] a tree strongly resembling the beech in leaf and general appearance, and bearing a fruit about the size and colour of a damson, but with a very large seed. <i>(1841,Rambles in NZ,35 and fn,1952 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> The Kahikatea white pine, and the Mahi, Totara, Rata, and Tawa red pines, all grow to a great size; ... <i>(1842,New Zealand (various authors/titles),102</i>) [NOTES: (cf Towa earlier)]

>  There is also rata, tawa, honeysuckle &c., which, though of harder nature, may hereafter be applied to many useful purposes. <i>(Nov 27, 1842,Letters from New Plymouth,58,1968 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (1842 Nov 27) 1843 (1968) Letters from New Plymouth 58]

>  <b>JOLLY</b> [spg towa] ... about 8 miles from Nelson in a hut situated close to a small clump of White pine (Kahikatia) and Towa trees, ... <i>(1873,Reminiscences,4</i>) [NOTES: [c1844] 1873 Ed Jollie Reminiscences [MS] 4]

>  <b>CHAPMAN</b> Tower, Koi Koi, and Remo are the prevailing woods in this locality. [check date and title] <i>(1847,Journals and Correspondence</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> 6. Tawa, (<i>Laurus tawa</i>.) This tree abounds all over the district, and especially in the same situations as the <i>totara.</i> <i>(1848,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),141</i>)

>  <b>EARP</b> The fruit of the <i>tawa</i> resembles a prune damson ... <i>(1853,New Zealand (various authors/titles),129</i>) [NOTES: 1853 Allom letter in 1853 Earp NZ 129]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Neodaphne <i>tawa</i>, Hook. fil. ... Nat. name, `Tawa', <i>Cunn. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 217</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Tawa, <i>Col[enso]. Nesodaphne Tawa. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  <b>HAYNES</b> Tower trees (so called from their towering above all the other trees of the bush) ... <i>(1865,Ramble in the NZ Bush, A,18</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> <i>Beilschmiedia tarairi</i>, Hook. Tawa.--A lofty forest tree, 60-70 feet high, <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,129</i>)

>  [Native name] Tawa .. [Settlers' name] Tawa .. [Family] Lauriniae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,121</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 121]

>  Tawa .. Beilschmiedia tawa .. The wood is white, straight grained, and easily split; ... <i>(1891,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,14</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Tawa, n. </i>Maori name for a New Zealand tree, <i>Nesodaphne tawa</i>, Hook., N.O. <i>Laurinea</i>. The newer name is <i>Beilschmiedia tawa,</i> Benth. and Hook.f. Allied to <i>Taraire</i> (q.v.). A handsome forest tree with damson-like fruit. <i>(1898,Austral English,462</i>)

>  <i>Ta&#769;.wa</i> (ta.&#769;.w&adot.; <i>colloq</i>. tou&#769;.&adot.), <i>n</i>. [Maori.] <i>(Bot.) A lauraceous tree of New Zealand, (Beilschmiedia (Nesodaphne) tawa. [Local, N. Z.] </i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2032</i>)

>  <i>tawa</i>, a tree (Beilschmiedia tawa); from its straight-grained and light wood the Maori made his long bird-spears, from twenty to thirty feet in length: its large purple berries much sought by the pigeon. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/146</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>tawa</i> tree <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,176</i>)

>  A handsome New Zealand forest tree yielding a light, soft timber useful for making casks, buckets, etc. The plum-like fruit was used as food by the Maoris. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),406?</i>)

>  <i>tawa n </i>-s [Maori] : a New Zealand evergreen tree <i>(Beilschmiedia tawa) </i>of the family Lauraceae with slender branches and graceful foliage resembling that of the willow and white straight-grained wood used chiefly for rough work (as clothespins) <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,2344</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Tawa .. tawa .. <i>Beilschmiedia tawa </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <i>tawa n</i>. lofty New Zealand tree, with soft light wood used for box-making [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),626</i>)

>  <i>tawa ... n</i>. lofty N.Z. tree with soft, light wood used for box-making [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> Unlike the berries of the karaka, tawa (<i>Beilschmiedia tawa</i>) berries have a non-poisonous kernel. If eaten raw, however, the kernels have a distinctive, turpentine-like flavour which is unpleasant to most palates. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,6</i>)

>  <i>tawa n./i a New Zealand timber tree with edible berries. [< Maori] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,1199</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 788; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 493]]</i>

### tawa berry
 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> the fruit of the tawa [usually plural; aka tawa-berry; also towa berry]

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> The <i>Towa</i> and the Kraka berries also yield an useful oil. <i>(1842,Narrative of a Residence in  Various Parts of NZ,43</i>)

>  <b>SELWYN</b> Among these ... pools ... the natives are sitting up to their breasts in hot water, shelling Tawa berries, or peeling potatoes, or ... enjoying their never failing resource of smoking. <i>(Oct 30, 1843,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1843 Oct 30 Selwyn Journal in 1845 NZ Part II (Church in the Colonies VII) 31]

>  <b>SMITH</b> Scattered about [at Rotomahana] ... are ... large square slabs of white stone, laid in a horizontal position over hot springs, and are used ... for drying tawa berries; ... <i>(Jan 25, 1858,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Jan 25 Smith Notes of a Journey in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 374]

>  "Tawa berry halves," said Rangi, as he put one in his mouth. <i>(Sep 13, 1924,Evening Post,15</i>)

>  The pigeon in those days enjoyed a bounteous food supply, commencing in April with tawa berries, and following with those of the matai, kahikatea, miro, hinau, and maire, with the berries of the cabbage tree between times. <i>(Aug 2, 1930,Evening Post,17</i>)

>  The rebels are known to be living on the tawa berries, and the root of the tara [sic], which is cooked in various ways, by being boiled, roasted, or steamed in a hangi or kopura-maori. <i>(Apr 13, 1864,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  For seven days they saw no sun in the dense trackless forest, and subsisted only by eating such wretched fare as turpentine-impregnated tawa berries. <i>(Apr 14, 1871,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  As a token of the hospitable reception we had at the hands of this patriarch of the Children of the Mist, I may remark that within four hours of our arrival we were compelled to sit down to no less than three meals, at which we were regaled upon pork, potatoes, biscuit, coffee, preserved pigeons, honey, and tawa berries. <i>(Aug 12, 1897,Otago Witness,46</i>)

>  The tawa berries just now are falling, and the pigeons abound, while high overhead the paroquet chatters. <i>(Jun 15, 1903,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  Town children must now go further afield if they seek to commune with bird and flower in the woods, and take toll of the pigeon's tawa berries. <i>(Jan 21, 1911,Evening Post,13</i>)

### tawaapou
 
 <i>n.</i> variant of tawapou

>  Milk tree, or Tawaapou <i>(Epocarpurus microphyllus)</i>. The Milk tree of the Settlers, from the bark exuding a vegetable milk when wounded. <i>Ibid.</i> 48 Tawaapou. Epicarpus microphyllus, Raoul. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/37</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

### tawai
 
 <i>n.</i> occas. erron. variant of towai; variant of taewa



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Weinmannia</i> spp.

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Intermixed with these are the tawai [fn Leiospermum racemosum.] and hinau [fn Elaeocarpus hinau.]. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/55 This bird lives on the fleshy ... fruits of the hinau (Etacocarpus [sic =?Elaeo-] hinau), of the tawai (Leiospermum racemosum), [= <i>Weinmannia racemosa,</i> towhai, tawhero]of the miro (Podocarpus ferruginea), and of the mai (Dacrydium mai). <i>Ibid.</i> 1/399 The forest [near Rotorua] was mostly tawai (Leiospermum racemosum), miro, and hinau; the Dracophyllum, which often grows to the size of a small tree, and the reddish leaves of which render it a very remarkable and beautiful shrub, was ... to be met with. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/27</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> [Near Hikurangi] we passed through a noble forest of beech trees (Tawai) some were 8 feet in diameter and of great height <i>(Nov 10, 1845,Journal,3/166</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> 9. Tawai, (<i>Leiospermum racemosum</i>.) This tree has a rough, dark-brown bark, and a small round leaf, in shape like that of an elm. The wood is particularly well adapted for ship-building, and for the staves of barrels, and is commonly called `black birch' by the sailors and other Europeans acquainted with it. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,142</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Weinmannia <i>racemosa</i>, Forst. ... Nat. names, `Tawai', <i>Cunn</i>.; `Tawhere', Southern Island, <i>Lyall. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 80</i>)

>  <b>DRURY</b> TREES [MET WITH IN PELORUS]. Kawa-Kawa ... Flax - Korari of natives ... Tawai - a large tree, commonest of all trees in the Pelorus forests. <i>(1855,Sailing Directions,67</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Tawai, <i>Cunn. Weinmannia racemosa. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  ROBERT TOD HAS on hand a large stock of RATA and TAWAI (sound and dry), the best that Taranaki can produce. <i>(Aug 21, 1896,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)

>  We were obliged to stop a day here; and on the 6th we followed the left stream, on a flat covered with an open forest of tawai, for about ? of a mile, and as we observed that the flat did not extend any further, and that the hills descended in steep declivity towards the water, I ascended the hills with Mr. Deans and Eckford to have a look around us. <i>(Sep 12, 1840,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> beech, birch, black birch, red birch

>  The tawai, or black birch as it is called ... . <i>(1841,NZ Journal,2/29/51</i>) [NOTES: (Dieffenbach)]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Fagus <i>fusca</i>, Hook. fil. ... HAB. Mountains of the Northern Island ... Common in the Middle Island ... Nat. name, `Tawai', <i>Bidwill.</i> `Black Birch' of the colonists. ... [230] A lofty, handsome forest tree, 80-100 feet high ... Fagus <i>Menziesii</i>, Hook. fil. ... HAB. Mountains of the Northern and Middle Islands. ... Nat. name, `Tawai', [?tavai check] <i>Col</i>. `Red Birch' of the colonists. ... A very handsome tree, 80-100 feet high, ... Bark silvery, outer layers peeling off and exposing a red surface. <i>(1853,Flora NZ,II Flora Novae-Zelandiae I Flowering Plants 229</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> I had not then been accustomed to pass weeks in the dark tawai forest. <i>Ibid.</i> 11 I had noticed ... that the vegetation began to change; totara, kahi-katea, ... were intermixed with the tawai (black birch), which had till then been the reigning forest tree. <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,10</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Tawai, <i>Col[enso]. Fagus Menziesii. </i>... Tawai, <i>Bidwill. Fagus fusca. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> There is the cabbage-tree palm, with bare shank and top-knot; the nikau palm, with weird and wondrous frondage; the lancewood, ...; the fern-tree, .... There is the twisting, squirming rata; the gaunt ... kahikatea; the golden kowhai; the dark velvet-covered rimu; the feathery red tawai; the perfumy mangiao; ... <i>Ibid.</i> 2/190 The Tawai (<i>Fagus Menziesii</i>), called `red birch' by settlers, is a favourite for fencing when young. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/187</i>)

>  <i>tawai</i> (as tawhai), a tree (Nothofagus spp.), beech; wrongly called birch; Tawai-raunui and tawai rauriki recognized as species by Maori and Pakeha alike: there are also hybrids: Also called tawhai. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/146</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  The Maori name for one or two kinds of New Zealand beech. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),406</i>)

>  Description of land: All forest, composed of rimu, rata, tawai, &c.; generally flat; soil light, being a thin deposit upon volcanic ash and stone; well watered and more fit for pastoral than agricultural purposes. <i>(Jan 4, 1894,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

>  The forest is again formed of one sort of trees, the tawai, or black birch as it is called. <i>(Sep 12, 1840,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)

>  Tawai contains 5 per cent. of potash and about an equal quantity of lime. The ash of tawai must make a capital manure. <i>(May 5, 1908,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)

>  A.R., HAVING completed arrangements for a constant supply of FIRST-CLASS FIREWOOD, consisting of Tetoki, Tawai, and Manuka, is prepared to supply the same at the following prices ... <i>(Sep 1, 1886,Poverty Bay Herald,3</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i> a fish

 <i>n.</i> a bush hawk

>  HIERACIDEA \{HARPA\} NOVAE ZEALANDIAE. (QUAIL HAWK.) ..<i> Native names.</i> Karearea, Kaiaia, Kaeaea, Kakarapiti, Karewarewa, and Tawaka. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,1,1888 2nd edn 1/213 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> HIERACIDEA BRUNNEA \{HARPA FEROX\}. (BUSH-HAWK.) ...<i>Native names</i>. Karearea, Kaiaia, Kaeaea, Kakarapiti, Karewarewa, and Tawaka; but sometimes distinguished as Karewarewa-tara. `Sparrow-Hawk' of the colonists. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,6,1888 2nd edn 1/220 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a mushroom

>  <b>HUTTON</b> EUDYPTES PACHYRHYNCHUS. Gray. Crested Penguin. Tawaki.[?tawhaki <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,53</i>)

### tawari
 
 <i>n.</i> a tree with showy white flowers, found in the northern North Island. [Maori tawari[a1-macron]]

>  Of our shrubs and smaller timber trees, several are of strikingly beautiful growth ... (e.g.) the Houhere, <i>Hoheria populnea</i> ...; ..the Tawari, <i>Ixerba brexioides</i>; .. the Horoeka, M<i>Panax crassifolia</i>: and, on the sea-coast, ... the Karo, <i>Pittosporum crassifolium</i>; and the truly evergreen Ngaio,<i> Myoporum laetum ... </i> <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/5</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  353 Lithograph of tawari foliage. <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,22</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Ixerba brexioides [(The Tawari)]</i>. A beautiful tree, sometimes 70 ft. in height. ... [188] Maori name <i>Tawari. </i> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,186,1940, p. 188 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> The tawiri [check spg: should be tawari] (<i>Ixerbia brexioides</i>) of the Auckland upland forest is so showy that the Maoris had a special name, `whakou,' for its blooms. <i>(1910,NZ Plants,37</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> The tawiri [check spg: should be tawari] (<i>Ixerbia brexioides</i>) of the Auckland upland forest is so showy that the Maoris had a special name, `whakou,' for its blooms. <i>(1910,NZ Plants,37</i>)

>  <i>(1958,NZ Timber Journal,62/2</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> <i>(1961,Flora NZ,165</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>IXERBA BREXIOIDES.</i> Tawari is a northern tree found in some upland forests of the Auckland and Gisborne districts. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,78</i>)

>  <b>LOCKE</b> Botanical discoveries were spice to the dish. I shan't forget the first time I saw the tawari (<i>Ixerba brexioides</i>) in flowers all along the sunlit verges of the brick-red clay road along the ridge [near Auckland.] <i>(1981,Student At The Gates,73</i>)

>  Above 900 m hard beech is replaced by red beech which begins to dominate the canopy up to the summits of the Ikawhenua Range. Also at these altitudes are Hall's totara and tawari. <i>(1983,Land of the Mist,60</i>)

>  On the branches of the rata [a's-acute] and other trees, where the soil had been raised by heavy gusts of wind, ... flourished the wild indigenous para-[110]sitical plant called tawara [a2,a3-acute] (Astilia angustifolia). <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/109</i>)

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> The pulpy part of the middle of the stem of the <i>Tawara</i> or <i>Astilia</i> is an excellent substitute in pastry for the apple. <i>(1842,Narrative of a Residence in  Various Parts of NZ,45</i>)

>  Honey from the waxy white blossom of the native tawari tree, which grows in the warmer parts of the country, has a wonderful light, flowery flavour with a lingering butterscotch finish. <i>(Mar 18, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),52</i>)

>  The following woods, from New Zealand have been tested as to their capability of bearing pressure applied to the centre while supported at the two extremities .. 13. Tawiri. <i>(Jan 6, 1857,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  The following is a list of the Auckland contributions towards the Vienna Exhibition .. special collection of wood sections in the round; tawari, iserba bresioides ... <i>(Jan 23, 1873,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  The tree Ixerba brexioides is "tawari," [b]ut its flowers are "whakou." <i>(May 29, 1909,Evening Post,13</i>)

### tawatawa
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Taua taua; A fish so called. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,210</i>)

>  English Name Native Name ... ... Herring Tawa tawa ... ... <i>(1849,NZ Journal,9/125</i>) [NOTES: quoting Anglo Maori Warder Oct 5]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> <i>Tawatawa</i>, is about the same size as the kahawai, or mackerel, which it closely resembles in color and general form. <i>Ibid</i>623 The <i>Tawa tawa</i> is the same size as the <i>Kahawai</i>, or mackerel, which it resembles in color [sic] and general form. <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,413</i>)

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> MACKEREL ... This valuable fish (<i>Scomber australasicus</i>) is only known to me from ... Wellington Harbour about the 15th December, 1870, but it appears to be the fish that is called by the natives Tawa-tawa. In colour, size, and form it closely approaches the common Mackerel of England, and is very superior in delicacy to the Scad or Horse Mackerel, which is commonly called the Mackerel in New Zealand. <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,112</i>)

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> MACKEREL (<i>Scomber Australasicus</i>). ... This valuable fish is well known as an occasional visitant in Wellington Harbour, being called by the Natives tawatawa. In colour, size, and form it closely approaches the common mackerel of England, and is very superior in delicacy to the scad or horse-mackerel, which is commonly called the mackerel in New Zealand. <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,61</i>)

>  SCOMBER AUSTRALASICUS Cuvier and Valenciennes. <i>Southern Mackerel; Tawatawa</i>. ... Distribution: North Island and northern coasts of South Island. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/118</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Pneumatophorus australasicies</i> (Cuvier & Val.). SOUTHERN MACKEREL; TAWATAWA. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,45,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <i>tawatawa</i>, a sea-fish (Scomber australasicus), mackerel. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/159</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>BUCK2/212</b> <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>ADIANTUM ... affine </i>... Maiden-hair. TAWA TAWA. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,13</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> COMMON MACKEREL ... <i>Other names: Scomber japonicus</i>; southern mackerel, English mackerel, frigate mackerel; tawatawa (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,267</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> <i>Te Ika a Maui (Tny Eeka ah Mow-ee)</i>: The fish of Maui - the Maori name properly for the North Island only of New Zealand, but often for the whole country. <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,56</i>)

>  Another of our food fishes.. is the true mackerel (Scomber astralasicus) . . . The Maoris call the fish tawatawa. <i>(Jan 30, 1890,Otago Boys' High School,18</i>)

### tawhai
 
 <i>n.</i> = tawai

>  <i>Fagus cliffortioides</i> Hook. f. .. Mountain beech or birch. <i>[Fagus] fusca</i> Hook. f. .. Tawhai, black-birch.<i> [Fagus] Menziesii</i> Hook. f. .. Silver or red birch. <i>[Fagus] Solandri</i> Hook. f. .. White-birch. <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/144</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Tawhai, <i>Col[enso]. Fagus Menziesii</i> and <i>Solandri. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  Tawhai. Fagus Menziesii, Hook. f. ... Tawhai. Fagus fusca, Hook. f. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/48</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  ... the graceful fern-plumaged Kawaka, <i>([Libocedrus Doniana, Hook: f.])</i>, the handsome celery-leaved Tanekaha, <i>(Phyllocladus trichomanoides,)</i> ... the ornate Tawhai, <i>(Fagus Menziesii,)</i> and ... the ... Tawhai-rau-nui, <i>(Fagus fusca,). </i> <i>Ibid.</i> 1/III/44 (Colenso Botany North Isd) The Tawhai, and Tawhai-rau-nui, or Black and Red Birches, <i>(Fagus Solandri</i> and <i>F. Fusca,</i>) often form large ... trees. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/4</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  [Morris] Tawhai. Large and durable timber ... <i>(1873,Catalogue of Vienna Exhibition</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> <i>Nothofagus fusca,</i> Hook. Tawhai Tawhairaunui, Black-birch of Auckland and Otago (from colour of bark), Red-birch of Wellington and Nelson (from colour of timber). --This is a noble tree 60-90 feet high the trunk 5-8 feet in diameter. The timber is excessively tough and hard to cut. It is highly valued in Nelson and Wellington. ... <i>Nothofagus menziesii</i>, Hook. Tawhai, Red-birch (from the colour of the bark). --A handsome tree, 80-100 feet high; trunk 2-3 feet in diameter. The timber is chiefly used in the lake district of the South Island. <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,125</i>)

>  [Native name] Tawhai .. [Settlers' name] Entire-leaved birch .. [Family] Cupuliferae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,121</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 121]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Maori name for several species of New Zealand Beech-trees, N.O. Cupuliferae</i>. The settlers call them <i>Birches (q.v.). </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,462</i>)

>  The formation is chiefly Upper Palaeozoic and Upper Mesozoic rocks. 244,000 acres, mainly in the back country, are covered with bush, chiefly tawhai, tawhai rauriki, and tawhai raunui, the last being found along the shores of Lake Sumner and the banks of the Hurunui River, and, with the exception of the tawhai raunui, is of little value for timber purposes. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,262</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>F. Menziesii, Hook.f.</i> ... A tall forest-tree 60-80 ft. ... high; ... bark white or silvery, expecially in young trees; ... [641] <i>Tawhai; Tawai; Silver-birch; Red-birch</i>. ... The wood is dark-red ... but is not durable when exposed to the weather. <i>Ibid.</i> 643 <i>F. Solandri, Hook.f.</i> ... A lofty forest-tree 40-60 ft. high; ... bark black ... in old trees ... pale and smooth on young ones; ... Leaves ... clothed with an appressed greyish-white pubescence beneath ... usually in hilly or mountain districts. ... <i>Tawhai; Tawhai-rauriki; Black-birch; White-birch.</i> ... Wood pale-red, often streaked with black, not durable ... <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,640</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSEN</b> ... In my introductory essay ... T.W. Kirk was quoted on the singular exception in the case of the beeches, which the Maori seemed to class together as tawhai adding, perhaps, in our own manner, a descriptive modifying word or words, as tawhairaunui for the large-leaved beech. The recent studies of Dr. L. Cockayne (NZ State Forest Service, Bulletin No4, 1926), show that in this, too, the Maori was right ; the rigid specific distinction did not exist: As in Hebe (Veronica) there is much hybridization. ... and as the Veronicas were all Koromiko to the Maori, so the varieties of Nothofagus were all tawhai. <i>(1926,Popular Names,57/905</i>)

>  <i>tawai</i> (as tawhai), a tree (Nothofagus spp.), beech; wrongly called birch; Tawai-raunui and tawai rauriki recognized as species by Maori and Pakeha alike: there are also hybrids: Also called tawhai. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/146</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in]

>  <i>tawhai, tawai n.</i> any one of several species of New Zealand beech-trees [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),626</i>)

>  <i>tawhai, tawai n.</i> any one of several species of New Zealand beech-trees [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),626</i>)

>  </i>tawhai, tawai ... n.</i> any one of several species of N.Z. beech-tree [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)

>  <i>tawhai n. NZ</i> = NOTHOFAGUS. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,788</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 493, TAWAI]]

>  The crowns of the ranges are covered with tawhai, commonly known among bushmen as silver birch. <i>(Feb 5, 1924,Otago Witness,15</i>)

### tawhara
 
 <i>n.</i> . [aka tawera, tawara; also New Zealand screw pine. <i>Freycinetia banksii</i>. 1848 Brunner [see quot patiki]]

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> ... a kind of Lily a sort of Parasitical Plant ... [Blank space left for drawing with caption `Tawara Astilia Angustifolia'] <i>IbidD.</i> 91 [1963 fn 39 p 35] The drawing shows a large tree ... bearing, on the branches clumps of Astelia which Markham, following Yate, has erroneously labelled `Tawara Astilia Angustifolia'. `Tawara' is a rendering of <i>`tawhara',</i> the Maori name for the fruit of ... <i>Freycinetia banksii (kiekie) </i>.. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),61,1963 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [recheck p. no. and spg]]

>  <b>YATE</b> [The food of the New Zealanders] was formerly confined to the sweet-potato, the fern-root, and fish, with the sweet stalk of the Tawara (<i>Astilia angustifolia</i>), a parasitical plant, growing between the branches of the Kahikatoa and Puriri trees. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),107</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> On the branches of the rata [a's-acute] and other trees, where the soil had been raised by heavy gusts of wind, ... flourished the wild indigenous para-[110]sitical plant called tawara [a2,a3-acute] (Astilia angustifolia). <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/109</i>)

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> The pulpy part of the middle of the stem of the <i>Tawara</i> or <i>Astilia</i> is an excellent substitute in pastry for the apple. <i>(1842,Narrative of a Residence in  Various Parts of NZ,45</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> The food is served in baskets made of flax, or the long narrow leaves of the <i>tawara (Freycinetia Banksii)</i>, plaited so as to resemble coarse matting. <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/319</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Freycinetai <i>Banksii</i>, Cunn. ... Nat. name, `Tawhara', <i>Col. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 237</i>)

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> ... vine and mulberry show fruit; tawara ripe; ... <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/115</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Tawhara, <i>Col[enso]. Freycinetia Banksii. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  Tawhara. Freycinetia Banksii, A. Cunn. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/49</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  ... the Kahikatea ..., - the fruit of which was called Koroi ... and also the fruit (Ureure) and sugary bract-like spadices (Tawhara) of the climbing plant (Kiekie). <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/31</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  ... the tawera, or New Zealand screw pine, <i>Freycinetia banksii,</i> ... often climbing to the tops of the loftiest trees, and might be procured by thousands of tons. <i>(1874,TrNZI,6/56</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> We went out into the bush after <i>tawharas</i> this week. This is the fruit of the <i>kia kia</i>, a parasite which climbs and adheres to the dead trees. The Maoris make excellent hats resembling the celebrated `cabbage-tree' hats, from the fibre of the leaves. <i>(1935,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,135</i>) [NOTES: c1875 Meredith Adventuring in Maoriland (1935) 135]

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> The soft stamens of one of these are cream-coloured and covered with creamy-yellow pollen. This we called the `lady' or sometimes what the Maoris called it, <i>tawhera</i>. [check spg ?tawhara]The other, the `gentleman' giggy (<i>tiore</i>) had harder brownish-green stamens. <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,116,1981 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Tawhera or Kie-kie (<i>Greycinetia Banksii</i>) appears to be sometimes a parasite, sometimes a shrub, and sometimes a small tree. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/193</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Tawara, n.</i> Maori name for the flower of the <i>Kie-kie </i>(q.v.), <i>Freycinetia Banksii. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,462</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> Aye, and kie kie also, with its two kinds of sweetmeats, both on the same vine - the first at, and before Christmas, when the plant distills its nectar juices, and collects them in that bunch of tender succulence of fleshly leaflets the Maori called tawhara - the very name is maddening to think of! - and later, in winter, when its fruits, shaped like a corncob studded with the heads of translucent amber! Ah me! <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,16</i>)

>  <b>C HEESEMAN</b> [<i>Freycinetia Banksii, A.Cunn.</i>] ...<i>Kiekie; Tawhara</i> (the edible bracts); <i>Ureure</i> (the fruit). ... ripe fruit in May. ... The white fleshy bracts surrounding the spadices are often eaten; the fruit less commonly so. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,742</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>[Freycinetia Banksii] </i>Maori name <i>Kie-kie</i>. The flower is usually called <i>Tawhara</i>. ... [Freycinetia is called after the French navigator, Freycinet]. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,80,1940, p. 83 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FIRTH</b> The fleshy bracts of the root-climbing<i> tawhara, </i>the <i>perei</i> orchid,the young fronds of the <i>mauku</i> ... also furnished vegetable food. (<i>Colocasia esculenta</i>, Scholl.) ... <i>(1929,Primitive Economics of NZ Maori,51</i>)

>  <b>HYDE`</b> You can eat the tawhara in the woods around. This is the fleshy cream flower of a great green palm - sweet as honey and water-melon in one. <i>(1934,Journalese,171</i>)

>  <i>tawhara</i>, flower-bracts of Freycinitia eaten as a sweetmeat, <i>see </i>kiekie. [spg Freycinetia???] <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/159</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>LYON</b> The fruit [of the kiekie] was called tawha, and was a favourite Maori delicacy. <i>(1952,Faring South,104</i>)

>  <b>BOSWELL</b> ... each carrying ... a great bunch of tawharas or, in autumn, patungatungas (flower and fruit of the kiekie vine) ... <i>Ibid.</i> 150 There was in spring the delicious tawhara, the fleshy edible flower of the kiekie vine, or later the still more delectable patungatunga, the ripened seed-pod of the tawhara. <i>(1956,Dim Horizons,55</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>tawhara:</i> edible flower of kiekie <i>(1963,O Te Raki,195</i>)

>  Not only was there edible fruit, ureure, in summer; but, in spring the sweeet white inner leaves of the flower, tawhara, were also consumed. <i>(Nov 12, 2005,Dominion Post,Indulgence 23</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HAY</b> Where there is a great deal of supple-jack, or tawhera scrub, the work [of clearing] may get on as slowly as if the trees were comparatively large. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,1/187</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FEATON</b> Its [<i>Quintinnia serrata</i>, native lilac] durability is probably about equal to the wood of the Tawhero - <i>Weinmannia racemosa</i> - or Black Birch of Otago, to which it is indeed closely allied. <i>Ibid.</i> 136 WEINMANNIA RACEMOSA. (<i>Forst</i>.) ... The `TAWHERO' OR ``KAMAI'. This handsome and useful tree is found in both Islands; ... In Otago the tree is popularly known as the `Black Birch'. <i>Ibid.</i> 178 Tawhero [Maori name] .. Birch [Settlers' name] .. Weinmannia racemosa .. Tree <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,131</i>)

>  1. Tawhero .. All purposes to which mahogany is applied ... <i>(Mar 7, 1846,New Zealand Spectator & Cook's Strait Guardian,4</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  450 Lithograph of tawhero foliage. <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,25</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FEATON</b> in error for the TOWAI (W silvicola) WEINMANNIA SILVICOLA (<i>Banks & Sol</i>.) ... The `TOWAI'.- ... The wood is similar in quality to its ally <i>W. racemosa</i>, but is denser, and of finer grain; ... [136] This tree is sometimes called Tawhero, or Kamai, and is not unusually confounded with <i>W. racemosa</i>, the Tawhero or Kamai proper. In the same manner the tawhero is confused with this species by the uninitiated. <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,135</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>[Weinmannia sylvicola,</i> Sol.] <i>NORTH ISLAND: ... Tawhero. </i> <i>(1906,NZ Flora,139</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Weinmannia [sylvicola (The Tawhero)].</i> Maori names <i>Tawhero, Kamahi. </i> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,180,1940 p. 197 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> Confined to the north are ... the mangaeo (<i>Litsea calicaris</i>), the makamaka (<i>Ackama rosaefolia</i>), the tawhero (<i>Weinmannia sylvicola</i>), the toatoa (<i>Phyllocladus glaucus</i>), and some other trees and shrubs. <i>(1910,NZ Plants,46</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> Mangroves characterise the tidal mud flats, while certain poor clay soils, popularly known as gum-lands, were dominated by the kauri and its associate the tawhero. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,3,1970 ed.</i>)

### tawherowhero
 
 <i>n.</i> a tree

>  [Caption] Clouds often shroud Hautaru's 700 m-high peaks, which are covered with a stunted moisture-loving cloud forest rich in southern rata, tawa, tawhero, Westland quintinia and tawari. <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/15</i>)

>  ...by throwing a rope by aid of a stone over a tawhero tree we gained a wide ledge which let us travel down the stream bed. <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/28</i>) [NOTES: W M Hamilton's 1932 field journal in NZ Geographic]

>  The canopy tree tawherowhero, a prominent feature along the shores of the Bay of Islands and in montane forests, bears a mixture of red and green leaves in unpredictable proportions; both leaf types are apparently healthy, and the trees thrive. <i>(Nov 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 60/61</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [tauhinu](t-ten.md#tauhinu))

### tawhiri
 
 <i>n.</i> . [aka tawiri; these both variants of tawari according to Harry.]

>  <b>BENNETT</b> The Tafiri, the lemon-tree of the settlers [<i>tenuifolium</i> <i>(1860,Gatherings of a Naturalist,416</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> `Its floor ... with faint tawhiri-leaves besprent.' <i>(1872,Ranolf and Amohia,108</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> Tawhiri ..... O. Pittosporum; S. P. cornifolium. A large shrub; many varieties; leaves of some highly scented. <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/303</i>)

>  <b>BRACKEN</b> [Morris] The early breeze that ... stole/ The rich Tawhiri's sweet perfume. <i>(1884,Lays of the Maori,21</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Taawhiri,</i> or <i>Tawiri, n.</i> Maori name for the <i>Black Mapau.</i> A name applied to the tree <i>Pittosporum tenuifolium, N.O. Pittosporeae. </i>It is profusely covered with a fragrant white blossom. See <i>Mapau</i>. [1872 Domett]; 1884 Bracken <i>(1898,Austral English,462</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> The gum of the taramea [was mixed by the Maori with] ... the fragrant resin of the tawhiri (<i>Pittosporum tenuifolium</i>), ... <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,322,1940 p. 341 ed.</i>)

>  <i>tawhiri,</i> a tree (Pittosporum tenuifolium); so called because small branches were broken off to wave welcome while calling out haeremai to approaching visitors: tawhiri means to beckon. The tree has smallish chocolate-coloured flowers very fragrant in summer evenings and nights, when the bowers of the trees too are a-flutter with the visiting lady-moths: also called ko[o]hu[u]hu[u] and tawhiwhi. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/146</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>tawhiri, tawiri n. </i>New Zealand pittosporum, with fragrant white blossoms. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),626</i>)

>  <i>tawhiri, tawiri ... n. </i>N.Z. pittosporum, with fragrant white blossoms. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)

>  <i>tawhiri n</i>. NZ tree of pittosporum family with fragrant white blossoms. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,788</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>HECTOR</b> <i>Carpodetus serratus,</i> Forst. Tawiri, White Mapau, White-birch (of Auckland). --A small tree, 10-30 feet high, trunk unusually slender. <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,132</i>)

>  Tawiri-kohu-kohu, or white-mapau (<i>Carpodetus serratus</i>) <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,150</i>)

>  FIREWOOD. MIXED Rata, Birch, Tawiri, and Miro, sound and dry. <i>(Jun 10, 1927,Evening Post,14</i>)

>  The food of the whitehead, according to his observations, consists of the smaller insects and their larvae, varied occasionally with the seeds of certain trees, such as those of the tawari, tawhiwhi, and karo. <i>(Mar 6, 1937,Evening Post,17</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DOMETT</b> This cheerful cot .../ Its floor, for fragrant orange-scent/ With faint <i>tawhiri</i>-leaves [i1-acute] besprent;/ <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/157</i>)

 <i>abbr.</i> Telecommunication Carriers' Forum [ORIGIN: acronym]

The TCF works with a wide variety of industry participants and government agencies to promote competition and investment in the sector, accelerate the introduction of new generation services, and encourage excellence in customer service. <i>(2010,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: tcf.org.nz]

### tchaik

>  ... the most important [Chatham Island] edible sea-birds: AS WRITTEN AS PRONOUNCED ENGLISH hopo ho[macron]p' albatross hakoakoa ha[macron]ko[macron]k' mutton bird taiko tchai[macron]k' pied shear water tupoupou tchu[macron]poup' pied blue billie reoreo ro[macron]ro[macron] Mother Carys chicken turiwati tchu[macron]riwat' dottrel [sic] kuaka ku[macron]k' snipe Notes 1928 Baucke Manuscript in Skinner and Baucke The Morioris (Bishop Museum Memoirs 358 <i>(1928,Bishop's Guide to Wellington,Vol. IX No. 5 pp. 343-382) 358</i>)

### tchakat Mai-hor-r
 
 <i>phr.</i> the form used frequently by Baucke for <i>Moriori</i>

>  <b>SKINNER & BAUCKE</b> Unlike the Maori, the Moriori had no name for his race. When questioned, he answered: `tchakat Mai-hor-r.' This not having the assonance of Maori speech, this purist changed it to `tangata Moriori' (Moriori man); by which name the race became known to common use. <i>(1928,BP Bishop Memoirs,9(5):357</i>) [NOTES: 1928 Baucke Manuscript in Skinner and Baucke The Morioris (Bishop Museum Memoirs Vol. IX No. 5 pp. 343-382) 357]

### TCR
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Territorial Customary Right

>  But there was concern at how a finding in the High Court of a Territorial Customary Right (TCR) would be given effect, and Government MPs were looking at ways to give the courts some guidelines. A TCR is a collection of customary rights that would have amounted to full customary and fee simple title if the new law had not asserted Crown ownership. <i>(Nov 5, 2004,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

### Te Ika a Maui
 
 <i>n.</i> the traditional Maori name for the North Island (occasionally in former use for New Zealand as a whole), literally 'the fish of Maui. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  In present-day use <i>Aotearoa</i> is the accepted Maori name of the whole country and the North Island is <i>Te Ika a Maui</i> (Maui's fish). However, just as <i>Aotearoa</i> was used in the past of the North Island, so <i>Te Ika a Maui</i> has occasionally referred to the whole country. When Maori activist Titewhai Harawira went to the Netherlands in 1990.. she was reportedly advocating <i>Te Ika a Maui</i> and not <i>Aotearoa</i> as an indigenous replacement. <i>(Oct 2000,NZWords,4 : 2</i>)

>  There are some small difference [sic] in the Language spoke by the <i>Aehei-no mouweans</i> and those of <i>Tovy poe nammu </i>but this difference seem'd to me to be only in the pronunciation and is no more than what we find between one part of England and other: ... <i>(Mar 31, 1770,Cook's Journals,1/288,1955 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Te Ika a Maui-an);North Island(er) 1773 Cook Journal 2/68 -->[see kumara]]

>  <b>BAKER</b> <i>(1941,NZ Slang,59</i>)

>  <i>Te Ika a Maui n</i>. the Fish of Maui, the N. Is. of N.Z. [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)

>  Chants that emphasized the points of a weighty speech in the runanga-house, short ditties that maidens carolled forth about the pa, war songs that fired the hearts of warriors on the march-these were heard through the length and breadth of Te Ika-a-Maui, Maori poets vieing one with another to compose songs suitable for the occasion. <i>(1953,Te Ao Hou,6: 18</i>) [NOTES: Royal Tour number]

### Te Kakahu skink
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a very smooth chestnut-coloured skink known only from specimens found on the Te Kakahu, or Chalky, Island in Fiordland in 2002. [<i>Oligosoma "Te Kakahu"</i>]

>  Te Kakahu skink: Discovered in 2002 on an island in Fiordland. Found only in one small area of low, open vegetation at the top of a cliff. Has distinct colour pattern differences to other species and different scale counts. <i>(Aug 2, 2004,Dominion Post,A4</i>)

 <i>phr.</i> the Maori broadcasting funding body

>  Take the example of Te Mangai Paho, the agency that funds Maori broadcasting and shapes its development. <i>(Feb 22, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

### Te Maori
 
 <i>n.</i> international exhibition of Maori art

### te mura o te ahi
 
 <i>phr.</i> the flash of the fire (parliament) [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  We know that Parliament is perceived as a place of conflict and struggle as it is evident in how our people refer to the institution with terms such as * Te ana o te raiona - the den of the lion. * Te mura o te ahi - a reference to the flash of fire on a battlefield. * Te waha o te taniwha - the mouth of the taniwha. <i>(Dec 2, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,6</i>)

### Te Papa
 
 <i>n.</i> The Museum of NZ; previously the Dominion Museum, then National Museum of New Zealand

>  The heads will be brought direct to Te Papa, where they will be stored in a wahi tapu (sacred vault). <i>(Jul 2, 1998,Dominion,1</i>)

>  It was gimmicky, nostalgic, significant in a Te Papa kind of way. <i>(Jan 22, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),63</i>)

>  The Te Papa white elephant and the proposed new ministerial palace are prime examples of their [sc. the government's] largesse. <i>(May 30, 1997,Independent (Auckland),22</i>)

>  It was well-known trhat Clark, responding partly to a snotty review by Theodore Dalrymple in the.. <i>New Statesman</i> (he called Te Papa "the MTV of museums ... a temple to the short attention span"), helped to generate Te Papa's reinvention. <i>(Dec 1, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),51</i>)

>  When Defence Minister Max Bradford visited the [East Timor] base, he was greeted with a sign proclaiming "Kiwi Compound"' the words illustrated with a kiwi and a taiaha (long club). Below was scribbled "Te Papa" - apparently to drum into the Aussies that it wasn't <i>their</i> place. <i>(Oct 1, 1999,Dominion,8</i>)

>  The purpose of the Museum of New Zealand Te Papa Tongarewa is to provide a forum for the nation to present, explore and preserve the heritage of its cultures and knowledge of the natural environment in order better to understand and treasure the past, enrich the present and meet the challenges of the future. <i>(Jul 1990,Architecture NZ,31</i>) [NOTES: There is an earlier report in <i>NZ Museums Journal</i> 22(2) : 191-20]

>  "Strategic plan" covers everything from Te Papa to tap-dancing. <i>(Aug 5, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),37</i>)

### Te Papa-ised
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  Because even the Te Papa-ised type inspires dread in the hearts of the uninitiated in a way that theatres or opera houses don't seem to anymore. <i>(Apr 28, 1999,Capital Times,5</i>)

>  Thomson is best known for work like his now Te Papa-ised corrugated iron-covered stationwagon, and stampeding herds of elephants and broods of chickens. <i>(Dec 17, 2000,Sunday Star Times,D13</i>)

### Te Papa-ite
 
 <i>n.</i> an admirer of the Te Papa museum

>  If bright lights, computers, push buttons, taped narratives and startling juxtapositions.. are your scene, then you're probably a Te Papa-ite. <i>(Apr 2000,North & South,48</i>)

### Te Papaism
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  True to currently fashionable Te Papaism, he mistakes crowd attendance for a measure of intellectual and artistic quality. <i>(Aug 2000,NZ Books,2</i>)

### Te Puku o Te Whenua
 
 <i>phr.</i> The Maori electorate for the Central North Island

>  There's a toss-up in Te Puku o Te Whenua between Rino and Rana. And Peter may make it too. <i>(Jun 1996,Mana,No.13: 22</i>)

### Te Puni Kokiri
 
 <i>phr.</i> the Ministry of Maori Development

>  I came across this ad in a magazine while waiting for my order at the Chinese takeaway on the corner of the road that leads to our local Maori bacon factory, Te Kuni Porkery. <i>(Jan 2002,Cuisine,No. 90 : 266</i>) [NOTES: Tom Frewen]

>  Legislation is being drafted to allow Te Puni Kokiri, Ministry of Maori Development, to conduct audits of Government agencies on meeting their commitment to close the gaps between Maori and non-Maori... TPK wanted wider monitoring functions. <i>(Oct 5, 2000,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  Thse new bodies will function alongside Te Puni Kokiri (TPK), the bureaucracy already supposed to monitor whether government is responsive to Maori needs... "The responsibility for these commissions is going to be directly to me, not to TPK." <i>(Feb 8, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),27</i>)

### Te Rauparaha
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MILLER</b> In the Ngati Toa <i>pa</i> there was great indignation, and Te Rauparaha, their fighting chief, gathering together a war party, surprised and killed Te Uira and anothee Waikato chief, Te Aomarama. <i>Ibid.</i> 18 The acquisition of firearms from the whalers and flax-traders at Kapiti encouraged Te Rauparaha to embark on the conquest of the South Island. The story of these raids constitutes a perfect Iliad. <i>(1958,Early Victorian NZ,16</i>)

### Te Reinga
 
 <i>n.</i> the place (Cape Reinga at the far northern tip of the North Island) in Maori tradition from where the spirits of the dead are believed to enter the next world. [ORIGIN: Maori] (See [Reinga](red-roa.md#reinga))

>  <b>Ihimaera</b> This was the time when Maori believed that the spirits of the dead began their long voyage to Te Reinga. There, at the northernmost tip of Aotearoa, they waited for the sun to go down. <i>(2000,Uncle's Story, The,109</i>)

### te reo
 
 <i>phr.</i> the Maori language [ORIGIN: Maori, [Ma. <b>te</b> the (indicates singular)] [Ma. <b>reo</b> voice. language, speech, audio] cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 217.]

>  Text came to play a greater role during this period, too - Maori text that left Pakeha viewers like me groping for their te reo dictionaries. <i>(Sep 23, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),54</i>)

>  And not only does Hapeta deliver hits such as "E Tu" with increased power and confidence, but also his te reo rhymes roll far more readily off his tongue. <i>(Nov 23, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),62</i>)

>  We have to keep [Maori TV] bilingual or else the te reo nazis will capture it. <i>(Mar 27, 2004,Dominion Post,E3</i>)

### Te Ropu Maori
 
 <i>phr.</i> the Maori group or party [ORIGIN: Maori, see Ryan 224] (See [roopu](roa-ruv.md#roopu),[ropu](roa-ruv.md#ropu))

>  The students were taught a haka powhiri (or greeting) which is a traditional Maori performance by six of the members of Te Ropu Maori group. <i>(2005,Internet - see Notes,0</i>) [NOTES: http://www.bbc.co.uk/cambridgeshire/out_about/2003/07/whale_rider.shtml Accessed on 09/01/2006. true date of writing unknown.]

>  Translated as, say, "Te Ropu Maori", the party name would be a less effective marketing tool than the English version. <i>(Aug 31, 2005,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  Te Ropu Maori, the new Maori channel on the Alcohol Advisory Council's website, has been designed to support Maori communities working on health and substance abuse issues. <i>(2005,Internet - see Notes,ADA Newsletter Volume 2, Number 2</i>) [NOTES: http://www.adanz.org.nz/content/documents/14/news2.pdf Accessed 09/01/2006. Date of writing unknown, 2005 a substitute.]

### Te Tai Hauauru
 
 <i>phr.</i> The Maori electorate for Auckland and south to Taranaki

>  Another interesting tussle will be for Tai Hauauru which stretches from Mokau to the Manukau Harbour. <i>(Jun 1996,Mana,No.13: 22</i>)

### Te Tai Rawhiti
 
 <i>phr.</i> The East Coast Maori electoral district

### Te Tai Tokerau
 
 <i>phr.</i> a grouping of NI iwi; a Maori electorate covering Northland

>  I suppose Tai Tokerau want to be here - it's [sc. Waitangi] their marae after all. <i>(Feb 28, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),10</i>)

>  Tau Henare should win Tai Tokerau. <i>(Jun 1996,Mana,No.13: 22</i>)

### Te Tai Tonga
 
 <i>phr.</i> The Maori electorate for Wellington Province and the South Island

### Te Teko-ite
 
 <i>n.</i> a resident of Te Teko

>  What about Te Teko-ites lives? <i>(Nov 15, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,2</i>)

### te tuhi whakaniko
 
 <i>phr.</i> painting [ORIGIN: Maori, whakaniko: decorative curves (Ryan)]

>  A three-year degree programme covering five different aspects of Maori art including: Weaving (raranga), Carving (whakairo), Painting (te tuhi whakaniko), Architecture (whaihanga), and Body Art (ta moko). <i>(Dec 2, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,10</i>)

### te waha o te taniwha
 
 <i>phr.</i> the mouth of the taniwha (parliament) [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  We know that Parliament is perceived as a place of conflict and struggle as it is evident in how our people refer to the institution with terms such as * Te ana o te raiona - the den of the lion. * Te mura o te ahi - a reference to the flash of fire on a battlefield. * Te waha o te taniwha - the mouth of the taniwha. <i>(Dec 2, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,6</i>)

### Te Wahi Pounamu
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The name <i>`Te Wahi Pounamu'</i>, or `locality of greenstone', which they gave to the South Island, points in this direction. <i>(1898,TrNZI,30/554</i>)

### Te Wai Pounamu
 
 <i>n.</i> the South Island [also Te Waipounamu]

>  For the five weeks it took to feel their way around Te Wai Pounamu, Banks only had wistful glances of the landscape he so dearly wanted to explore and botanise. <i>(Jul 18, 2004,Sunday  Star Times: Escape,11</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> `Pounamu' (greenstone) was only to be found in the creeks of the inland ranges of [40] the Middle Island, and hence called `Te wai pounamu.' <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,39</i>) [NOTES: [kept in 1928 edn p.39]]

>  <b>COWAN</b> This volume ... is descriptive of the varied landscapes of the South Island, more particularly the alpine, lake and fiord scenery for which the Wai-Pounamu is famous. <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol II South Island) i(Preface)</i>)

>  <b>HINGSTON</b> So you land on the shores of the Te Wai Pounuma (the water of green-stone), the middle island of New Zealand ... <i>(1879,Australian Abroad, The,292</i>)

>  Inspired by his favourite pepeha - "Do not die a rotting death like mine, but instead pass from this world via the fragrant ovens of a battlefield" - Te Radar set out to find and tell stories about some of Te Wai Pounamu's unsung, often unhinged, heroes. <i>(Aug 20, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),43</i>)

### Te Waipounamu
 
 <i>n.</i> the South Island [aka Te Wai Pounamu; cf. Te Ika a Maui]

>  The old Maori-myth names of the islands Eaheinomawe and Tavai Poinammoo, as Cook calls them, or Ika-na-mawe and Tavai-Pounamu as D'Urville more correctly calls them, still disfigure many maps, but are utterly obsolete for all practical purposes. <i>(Oct 21, 1874,Timaru Herald,8</i>)

>  This man spoke of three lands the two above mentioned which he called <i>Tovy-poenammu</i> which signifies green/Talk or stone such as the[y] Make [sic] their tools on, oramints [sic] etc. ... <i>(Jan 31, 1770,Cook's Journals,1/243</i>)

>  There are some small difference [sic] in the Language spoke by the <i>Aehei-no mouweans</i> and those of <i>Tovy poe nammu</i> but this difference seem'd to me to be only in the pronunciation and is no more than what we find between one part of England and other: ... <i>(Mar 31, 1770,Cook's Journals,1/288,1955 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> The whole southern extremity of Tavai-poe-namoo, or the southern island of New Zeeland, ... [145] consists entirely of steep rocky mountains ... <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/144</i>)

>  On leaving Tavia [?typological error] Poenammoo, or the Middle Island, I was forcibly struck with the bleak and savage appearance of its chain of mountains ... <i>Ibid.</i> 112 DECLARATION of Sovereignty over <i>Tavai Poenammoo</i>. This island, called Tavai Poenammoo or Middle Island of New Zealand ... was accordingly taken possession of ... and Her Majesty's colours hoisted at the paa of Hoiakaka, Cloudy Bay ... <i>(1841,GBPP,(No.311) 109</i>) [NOTES: 1840 June 28 Thomas Bunbury's Report in GBPP House of Commons 1841]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> The natives procure ... quantities of a green jade, or serpentine, much prized by them for [314] the purpose of making war-clubs ... It is called by them `Ponamu', whence the name once assigned to the Middle Island - <i>`Te Wahi Ponamu'</i> or, `The Place of Green Stone'. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,313</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> The name Tovy Poenammoo, or, as it would [155] now be written, Te Wai-pounamu, meaning the Pounamu-water, by many still imagined to be that of the Middle Island, is, as Captain Cook suspected, `only the name of a particular place, where the natives got the green talc, or stone, of which they made their ornaments and tools, and not a general name of the whole southern district.' <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,154</i>)

>  Dissatisfied younger sons, ambitious women or slighted elders sought redress by force of arms or went into [72] the open spaces of Aotearoa or Tewaipounamu. Our Courts recognised the arikiship in the award of shares in land ... <i>(Mar 22, 1933,Na To Hoa Aroha,3/71</i>) [NOTES: Ngata]

>  `Te Waipounamu' as the name for the South Island [Maori context] <i>(Mar 1985,TV1</i>) [NOTES: Maori News]

>  ".. He killed all the tribes of Kapiti, and he also crossed over to Te Waipounamu (the Southern Island), destroying all the people " <i>(Apr 24, 1875,Colonist (Nelson),3</i>)

>  When Captain Cook asked what was the name of the Middle Island, he was told "Te Wahi Pounamu," or "The Place of the Greenstone," which was got most plentifully there, and on the old maps the Middle Island is named "Tarai Pounamu." <i>(Jun 13, 1866,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  "We have received .. a copy of Tepuki o Matariki (the Pleiades) 'the official gazette of the independent Royal Maori power of Aotearoa,' dated Maungakawa, Waikato, 12th April 1894, giving notice to the hapus and tribes of New Zealand from the tail to the head of Maui's fish, crossing the Sea of Raukawa (Cook Strait) to Te Waipounamu, that the following laws have been adopted by the Maori Kauhanganui (convention) and assented to by King Potatau Tawhiao under his royal seal and sign manual." <i>(Jun 7, 1894,Otago Witness,22</i>)

### Te Whiti
 
 <i>n.</i> [(attrib. uses)]

>  Mabel and I called on Wi Te Tahuhu ... and saw some 2000 tuna drying. ... I think there will be quite 20,000 eels in all for Te Whiti. Piripiri and Pene took a large contract from me[word missing???] fencing. They gave their word as Te Whiti men but would not sign an agreement. <i>(Feb 12, 1885,Chudleigh Diary,332,1950 ed.</i>)

>  In Wellington. It is settled that Major Gudgeon, a sargeant and six A.C. [Richards's note: Artillery Corps] go to the Chathams .. to run in the Te Whiti Maoris. <i>(Sep 3, 1889,Chudleigh Diary,367,1950 ed.</i>)

>  I do not know whether you heard the story about a Nelson woman beseeching Mr. Mackay to give up the pakehas and go and live at Parihaka, for Te Whiti had told his people that all the Europeans would be destroyed, and she begged her old friend to embrace Te Whiti-ism while there was yet time. <i>(May 10, 1879,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  Te Ua is now never mentioned, Te Whiti and Te Tou, of Taranaki, formerly slaves of the Waikatos, being the fancied prophets of God. <i>(Jun 20, 1867,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  It is as well to mention here that Te Whiti (who was one of the few who embraced Hauhauism) has recently been studying the scriptures, consequently he scarcely uttered a sentence at the meeting without quoting from the bible. <i>(Mar 26, 1870,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  At the district Court to-day, John Stitt of Opunake, filed a petition asking that Te Whiti, the Parihaka Chief, should be declared a bankrupt. <i>(Jul 14, 1891,Marlborough Express,2</i>)

>  Te Whiti, the Parihaka chief, died this morning at 5 o'clock, aged 78. <i>(Nov 18, 1907,Hawera & Normanby Star,4</i>)

### Te Whiti-ism
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>COWAN</b> In Taranaki the adherents of Te Whiti-ism - quite a different brand of old-time religion from the Ringatu - have their monthly meetings for prayer and exhortation at Manu-korihi, on the Waitara. <i>(1930,Maori, The,66</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> Rev. Croasdaile Bowen came in '76 but was unable to do much as Te Whiti-ism had such a grip of the Islanders. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,121</i>)

>  Buck, going to Te Aute from a boyhood in Taranaki, where the missions had given way to Pai Marire and Te Whitism, was pleasantly surprised at the strength of Anglicanism among the East Coast Maoris. <i>(1986,Na To Hoa Aroha,1/14</i>)

>  I do not know whether you heard the story about a Nelson woman beseeching Mr. Mackay to give up the pakehas and go and live at Parihaka, for Te Whiti had told his people that all the Europeans would be destroyed, and she begged her old friend to embrace Te Whiti-ism while there was yet time. <i>(May 10, 1879,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  There is a likeness of the late Wi Parata, M.H.R., of Waikanae, a man who did much for Te Whitism, one of Waharepouri in the act of handing over to the New Zealand Company the land about Port Nicholson. <i>(Nov 22, 1907,Star, The,1</i>)

>  If instead of year after year fostering those Parihaka meetings the Government had boldly pushed on the coast road by an armed force if necessary, Te Whitiism would have been a thing of the past; but so long as the Government ask for permission to make a road through confiscated land, what can be expected from irrational and hostile natives but a distinct refusal and bouncable threats. <i>(Jun 13, 1879,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  I cannot forget when Te Whitiism was rampant, and those who understood the native character best were in doubt from day to day what the result might be, you came forward like one man - not only you here but all throughout the district which I have the honor to command - for the purpose of preparing for the worst. <i>(Feb 28, 1881,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

### Te Whiti-ite
 
 <i>n.</i> [aka Te Whitiite]

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> The Rev. P. Anderson also baptised several white children and one Maori child. All the other Maori families were Te Whiti-ites. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,124</i>)

>  They seem to be anxiously expecting the return of Rangi Paunanga, the head man of the Te Whitiites, but he refused to return as long as one man remains in gaol. <i>(Jun 8, 1881,Nelson Evening Mail,2</i>)

>  She was a kindly old lady and very hospitable, but a strong Te Whitiite. <i>(Jul 9, 1881,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  "It reads as though during the past twenty-four years only 150 Te Whiti-ites have returned to the Church, whereas the number would be nearer 1500, and as though only one church had been built during that time ..." <i>(May 9, 1905,Wanganui Herald,5</i>)

>  He was a chief of considerable importance and a staunch Te Whiti-ite, having consistently refused to accept any rent. <i>(Dec 16, 1893,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

### Te Whitism
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [Te Whiti-ism](t-ten.md#te-whiti-ism))

>  Defendant used to preach to the people, and he believed that she had endeavored to get the people to abstain from drink and the children and women from smoking; did not know if she used to try and get people away from Te Whitism. <i>(Sep 23, 1910,Hawera & Normanby Star,6</i>)

>  The Christian religion recently found a strong defender in a North Island Maori, who had been converted from Te Whitism. <i>(Apr 19, 1899,Poverty Bay Herald,2</i>)
