 <i>n.</i> (See [whare](../W#whare))

### te-matau kauri
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [tumatakuru](tot-tyr.md#tumatakuru))

### tea
 
 <i>n.</i> a full meal, dinner

>  My girlfriend almost <i>did</i> get presented with `a plate' (empty), when she asked me to meet some folks at her place for dinner - whoops, sorry tea. <i>(1989,Chosen Place,22</i>)

>  Papa has brought home a gentleman, Colonel Lorrimer, and we are to wait tea for them... You say you have not had tea yet. Ask Grace to wait ten minutes. <i>(Jan 16, 1890,Otago Witness,42</i>)

>  "Such a splendid walk!" said the two young men. "And such a nice, early tea, "said Grace ironically, "for I am sure Mabel has lit the fire and put the kettle on. " <i>(Jan 16, 1890,Otago Witness,42</i>)

### TEA
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Tasman Empire Airways [ORIGIN: acronym] (See [TEAL](t-ten.md#teal))

>  A likely alternative for T.E.A. is to take over nearby yacht buildings as temporary passenger handling accommodation. <i>(Jul 29, 1950,Dominion,8</i>)

### tea billy
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BELL</b> <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,7</i>) [NOTES: ... his swag generally contained some [eatables] that, with the help of the ever-present tea-billy which he carried, could supply a meal if necessary.]

>  <b>COWAN</b> [In the year 1873] they observed that the tea-billy was upturned on the fire, things were in general disorder, and Sullivan and Jones were missing. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/515,1983 ed.</i>)

### tea break
 
 <i>n.</i> (political allusion) (See [cup of tea](con-cyp.md#cup-of-tea),[cuppa](con-cyp.md#cuppa))

>  1988 Dominion 26 Aug. 8 We are invited by Mr Lange, the chief architect of our adversity, to pause and take tea... A tea break is not going to solve anything. <i>(Aug 26, 1988,Dominion,8</i>)

>  Apparently the job has been done and it is simply a matter of lying back, waiting for the good times to roll again and taking the tea break of a lifetime. <i>(Sep 22, 1993,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  [Hdg] Time for 'tea break' in claims process - historian. It is time for the Government to stop for a cup of tea in the Maori claims settlement process, according to eminent historian Alan Ward. <i>(May 12, 1999,Dominion,7</i>)

>  Just as in 1988, when Prime Minister Lange announced a "tea break", the mood is "put the kettle on, mum". <i>(Mar 24, 1996,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>)

>  The Government's failure to press ahead with economic reforms had turned a "tea break" into an extravagant long lunch that was threatening the economy while favouring the rich. <i>(Jul 27, 1997,Sunday Star Times,D4</i>)

>  When David Lange made his remark in 1988 about the country needing to stop for a "teabreak" from the task of economic reform, he probably had no idea that his metaphor would enter the national vocabulary. <i>(Aug 1, 1997,Independent (Auckland),11</i>)

>  In calling a tea-break in order to defend Labour's social goals, Mr Lange effectively delivered government into the hands of the "natural enemy". <i>(Feb 26, 1995,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>)

### tea fight
 
 <i>n.</i> [cf. bunfight]

>  I have passed without serious injury through a terrific tea fight at Mr Woodward's ... <i>(Aug 12, 1866,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/121</i>) [NOTES: (H.R. Richmond)]

>  <b>SEDDON</b> "Tea fights" were church events of great interest to growing boys. The womenfolk of the congregation vied with each other in producing appetising arrays of home-made cakes. These were displayed with the best family china and linens on tables in a hall and were consumed with copious cups of tea by a paying public. <i>(1968,Seddons, The,32</i>)

### tea plant
 
 <i>n.</i> = tea-tree (cf. New Zealand tea = manuka Leptospermum scoparium )

>  <b>HAWKESWORTH</b> <i>(1769,Voyages</i>) [NOTES: 1769 Hawkesworth Voyages, Cook etc (And)]

>  <b>COOK</b> (And) <i>(1773,Voyage Towards the South Pole,1779 ed.</i>)

>  of Cooper: `... began to make Beer from a Tree of a Rosinish quality intermixt with the Tea Plant [fn] ... Tea plant Manuka <i>L. scoparium </i> <i>Ibid.</i> 2/138 The Tea Plant is a shruby tree which grows on dry soil near the shores, it bears a small leafe of which this [...] is the natural size, small white flowers and small [cones] which contain the seeds ... . [It is also] a substitute for tea, this we found out last voyage ... . <i>(1773,Cook's Journals,2/114 [fn],1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> The Tea plant we found in plenty and mix'd it with the Spruce in Brewing. <i>(Nov 1791,Journal of Voyage in HMS 'Chatham',50</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> On the hills we saw some of the myrtle species, but the tea-plant, so common in Otaheite, and said to grow here, [233] and from which the natives of that island extract a spirituous liquor, we could not meet with it in the progress of our excursion. <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/232</i>)

>  There are many shrubs with myrtle-leaves. The tea-plant, <i>kaikatoa</i> [a2-acute], covers the plains, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 2/394 The <i>Kaikatoa</i>, [a2,a3-acute] (<i>Philadelphus Australis</i>,) a polyanthus, called the tea plant from its leaves possessing the same myrtiform character. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/293</i>)

>  <b>BARNICOAT</b> ... tea plant. <i>(1842,Journal,46</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> Captain Cook, who had dubbed it [manuka] `teaplant', wrote `The leaves were used by many of us as tea, which has a very agreeable bitter taste and flavour when they are recent ... ` <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,18</i>)

>  We were glad to land and breathe in the first little bay which presented itself in the beautiful beach which surrounds the lower end of the lake; to squat upon a patch of green sward - not grass, but the Maori tea-plant; and to solace ourselves with scraps from the "shrunk shanks" of the larder. <i>(Dec 13, 1867,Otago Daily Times,4</i>)

### tea shrub
 
 <i>n.</i> manuka, tea-tree

>  ... finding that the decoction of Spruce alone made the Beer to [sic] astringent we mixed with it an equal quantity of the Tea plant. ... we first made a strong decoction of the leaves of small branches of the Spruce tree & [sic] Tea shrub. <i>(May 11, 1773,Cook's Journals,2/37</i>)

>  ... finding that the decoction of Spruce alone made the Beer to [sic] astringent we mixed with it an equal quantity of the Tea plant. ... we first made a strong decoction of the leaves of small branches of the Spruce tree & [sic] Tea shrub. <i>(May 11, 1773,Cook's Journals,2/37</i>)

>  <b>COLENSO</b> ... close growing Tee shrubs and briars. <i>(1841,Journal,1/119</i>)

### tea-boy
 
 <i>n.</i> [1989 Nov 7 W.P. Jeffries MP 9.45pm ... tea-boy ...]

### tea-broom
 
 <i>n.</i> manuka, (tea-tree) [see also tea plant, tea shrub]

>  <b>DOMETT</b> Manuka [a1 acute] ... The settlers often call it `tea-broom'. <i>(1872,Ranolf and Amohia,505 [notes]</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> Ma'nuka .... <i>O</i>. Myrtaceae; <i>G</i>. Leptospermum; <i>S</i>. or L. ericoides. `A large shrub or small tree; leaves used as tea in Tasmania and Australia, where the plant is equally abundant.' In the poem it is called indiscriminately manuka, broom, broom-like myrtle, or leptosperm. The settlers often call it `tea-broom.' <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/303</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Tea-broom, n</i>. a New Zealand name for the <i>Tea-tree</i> (q.v.). [Domett 1872] <i>(1898,Austral English,463</i>)

>  We were glad to land and breathe in the first little bay which presented itself in the beautiful beach which surrounds the lower end of the lake; to squat upon a patch of green sward - not grass, but the Maori tea-plant; and to solace ourselves with scraps from the "shrunk shanks" of the larder. <i>(Dec 13, 1867,Otago Daily Times,4</i>)

>  In the poem it is called indiscriminately manuka, broom, broom-like myrtle, or leptosperm. The settlers often call it "tea-broom." <i>(Jul 18, 2011,Evening Post,19</i>)

### tea-can
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HALL-JONES</b> Boiling our flesh or fowl in our tea-can (called a billy) ... <i>(1971,Mr Surveyor Thomson,60</i>) [NOTES: 1850s in 1971 Hall-Jones Mr Surveyor Thomson 60]

### tea-leaf
 
 <i>n.</i> thief; to thieve, general English slang recorded in NZ from at least the 1970s (G. McEwen, Auckland; and HNZD

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> Ralphie used to tealeaf anything that wasn't tied down. <i>Ibid.</i> 180 A tealeaf is just a cunt in my eyes . . . You're a peter thief and you're a dog. <i>Ibid.</i> 255 THIEF (n) and (v) Thief, steal. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,148</i>)

>  a tea-leaf confesses <i>(Mar 22, 1982,Salient,15</i>)

>  According to the Old Bill, three tea leafs went into the Willis Street shop on Tuesday about lunchtime, and while one kept the manager busy, the other two lifted four big ones from the till. ... Three people robbed Arthur Daley's Lockup of $400 on Tuesday. <i>(Nov 15, 1986,Dominion</i>)

### tea-oh
 
 <i>exclam.</i> tea's ready

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> The back country runs have quite a vocabulary of their own ... terms like<i> cow paddock</i> and <i>horse paddock</i> and <i>tea-oh</i> and <i>township</i> which, like most of these terms, took on their special use in Australia. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/679</i>) [NOTES: Wall NZ Speech]

>  I had not quite finished when the cook called out 'Tea-oh!' <i>(Dec 20, 1928,Evening Post,8</i>)

### tea-pot
 
 <i>n.</i> billy [[Swainson does not use billy]]

>  <b>CHAPMAN</b> Of course in these [bush] whares there are degrees of comfort and accommodation. In one tea is boiled in the kettle and drunk out of a pannikin: in another the tea is made in a tin teapot and drunk out of crockery. <i>(Nov 24, 1874,Letters to His Father, 1843-1851,140</i>) [NOTES: 1847 Nov 24 H.S. Chapman Letters to His Father [TS, ATB] in 1958 Miller Early Victorian NZ 140]

>  <b>SWAINSON</b> A knife, fork, spoon, pannikin, tin plate, and tea-pot, form a sufficient yet not extravagant equipment [for the bush traveller]. <i>(1853,Auckland,125</i>)

### tea-shower
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FRAME</b> ... parcels from the handwork sale, tablerunners and tea-showers in lazy-daisy and chain and shadow stitch. <i>(1958,Owls Do Cry,24,1958 ed.</i>)

### tea-tree
 
 <i>n.</i> [nb tea-tree scrub, tea-tree bush, tea-tree brush are listed separately]



 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  I saw the man in question take a tea-tree broom and place it against the full bags of sawdust. <i>(May 4, 1867,Daily Southern Cross,5</i>)

>  <b>BARLOW</b> Procuring a tea-tree stake about five feet long, he ... laid down a plan of attack. <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,91</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> The earthen floor [of the nikau whare] would be kept clean with a tea tree broom. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,44</i>)

>  A little old hut / With a hard clay floor, / A tea-tree broom just by the door. <i>(Apr 11, 1944,Daily Southern Cross,10</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> Before there was a ready supply of electricity the demand was for tea-tree firewood, and so we built up a constant trade. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,132</i>)

>  New Zealand palings, 5-feet, 11s. to 12s.; 6-feet, 14s.; tea-tree rails, 42s. per 100 ... <i>(Jun 26, 1869,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a scrubby bush, manuka (Leptospernum spp), often used to add flavour to billy tea. [nb see also ti-tree, incl fig: in the tea-tree] (See [ti-tree](ten-tot.md#ti-tree))

>  <b>KIRK</b> An infusion of the leaves is often used by bushmen as a substitute for tea: in all probability it owes its common name 'tea-tree' to its having been used for this purpose by early voyagers. <i>(1889,Forest Flora,136</i>)

>  The forests contain a mixture of trees of all kinds, from the giant akuri to scrubby tea-tree or manuka <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,2</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSTONE-SMITH</b> Ti-tree was plentiful but tawa had to be snigged home from quite a distance. <i>(1975,Battles, Buggies and Babies,42</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> tea-tree: manuka (Leptospernum spp). Some people will slip a sprig of the leaves in whilst boiling up the tea billy as it gives a pleasantly piquant flavour. [ti-tree] <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,84</i>)

>  Puriri Posts, house blocks and firewood (puriri, tea-tree, matai and tarariri). <i>(Sep 1907,Wairoa Bell & Northern Advertiser,[13]</i>)

>  Its Sputnik-like extremities become caught in a pink teatree. <i>(Jul 2000,NZ Geographic,47/35</i>)

>  The plant would be sure to take possession in a short time of land where fern and tea-tree grows [<i>sic</i>] <i>(Mar 30, 1897,Bruce Herald,6</i>)

>  The tea-tree country around Silverdale has been whiter than usual this season, and all the meadow flowers are earlier in bloom, writes a correspondent to the "New Zealand Herald." <i>(Dec 4, 1922,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  Despite her efforts to restrain him, he went into the tea-tree, from which she heard a shot. <i>(Jun 6, 1944,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  It comes out on a country covered with scrubby tea-tree, with a poor soil of sand and pumicestone. <i>(May 10, 1873,Auckland Weekly News,20</i>)

>  No hot water unless you go out an' cut a heap o' tea-tree. <i>(Jun 18, 2000,NZ Catholic,7</i>)

>  He saw a man's head in the tea-tree. <i>(Jan 8, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,19</i>)

>  You've got to get those stones really hot, so we burn tea-tree or other hard woods. <i>(Jul 2004,AA Directions,Spring/28</i>)

### tea-tree brush
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> The appearance [of the Kicaitore,<i>kahikatoa</i>]] is like what in Van diemans land is called Tea Tree Brush and it has a pretty Flower. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),33,1963 ed.</i>)

>  Some of our party have been engaged in a swamp nearly to their middles, cutting tea-tree brush and rushes for roofing, and all have been busily occupied. <i>(Sep 21, 1867,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

### tea-tree bush
 
 <i>n.</i> manuka, tea-tree

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> She slipped behind some tea-tree bushes and changed into the clean frock.. to be ready to go with Ripi to Rotorua in the service-car. <i>(1938,Brown Man's Burden,58</i>)

>  tea tree bush - man|nook [manuka] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,110,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: also 1826-28 Boultbee Vocabulary in 1979 Begg 283]

>  Silk drove his second low through the fence into a small tea-tree bush on the near side of the road. <i>(Apr 5, 1934,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  A leather belt had been knotted round a tea-tree bush and one end was round the neck of the dead man. <i>(Jan 18, 1945,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  TENDERS WANTED, TO Clear from 20 to 40 acres of fern and teatree bush on Thos. Craig's farm, Wairoa river. <i>(Sep 27, 1859,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  The land is as good as any in the block, having natural grass, flax, fern, teatree bush, and a few kauri trees. <i>(Oct 6, 1864,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  In the middle of the terrace were the remains of a ti-tree bush, which was standing where a small patch of soil was still uncovered. <i>(Jun 19, 1886,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  The contractor for repairs to the baths is now about to commence work at the enclosure, the delay having been caused by the great difficulty in obtaining stakes of sufficient length and diameter for the job, the titree bush at Katikati being now about worked out of stuff of the sort required. <i>(Dec 1, 1893,Bay of Plenty Times,4</i>)

### tea-tree country
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) land that grows scrub and other forms of secondary growth.

>  The land I would apply it to is fairly new fern and tea-tree country, but has been in grass for a few years ... <i>(Apr 15, 1912,NZ Journal of Agriculture,323</i>)

### tea-tree jack
 
 <i>n.</i> stick insect

>  <b>SATCHELL</b> [note] `Animated straws'. Sometimes the Straw or Teatree Straw Insect, usually the New Zealand Stick Insect or (formerly) Walking Stick Insect also known as the Teatree Jack, <i>Acanthodemus horridus </i>of Hudson (1892). Thin, segmented, attenuated fine legs; capable of camouflage by colour change. Satchell refers to this at the end of the paragraph. <i>(1902,Land of the Lost,215</i>) [NOTES: 1971 Smithyman in 1902 Satchell Land of the Lost 215]

>  <b>GEE</b> I launched ...pink and blue [paper] ships manned by spiders, caterpillars, beetles and small ti-tree jacks. [Auckland] <i>(1972,In My Father's Den,16</i>)

### tea-tree scrub
 
 <i>n.</i> [incl ti-tree scrub]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> We'd dragged our way through the short hakea and tea-tree scrub covering the long flat. <i>(1965,North & South,155</i>)

>  <b>BAINES</b> <i>Bush fashion.</i> [page heading] ... He [the Northland sawmiller] wore what is called a `blue shirt', that is, a sailor's shirt of serge, fitting very closely, and a pair of moleskin trousers, very much torn ... from walking through harsh fern and `tea-tree' scrub, ... and a wideawake hat ... <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,39</i>)

>  Towards the latter river, which had no water in its bed, but chains of lagoons parallel to its banks, the creeks were lined by a dense tea-tree scrub, half a mild or more broad. <i>(Jul 18, 1846,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,79</i>)

>  On Friday, however, she was happily discovered in a ti-tree scrub near Mr. Bruchall's Remuera, safe, though doubtless in a state of exhaustion. <i>(Jan 27, 1857,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  There are at the present time some sixty prisoners in residence there, and the work of clearing the high tea-tree scrub, preparing the foundations for the central buildings, cultivating the cleared area, and making concrete blocks for the buildings yet to be erected, is proceeding apace. <i>(Jun 8, 1914,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  These deep bays are filled with fern and dwarfed ti-tree scrub. <i>(Oct 5, 1925,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  HOW many Aucklanders can remember the day that the first horse-drawn tram careered proudly down Hobson Street or the time when the now populous suburb of Grey Lynn was a desolate waste of ti-tree scrub? <i>(Sep 18, 1930,NZ Truth,4</i>)

>  Tea-tree scrub had in many places replaced the glorious bush of days gone by. <i>(Jan 13, 1944,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  For instance, there are six or seven acres near the Botanical Gardens without any means of access, except through titree scrub and gorse, although a road is surveyed, which would give a capital frontage to each of the sections. <i>(Apr 22, 1890,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  Each in turn led her about for a distance through the teatree scrub, and then admitted that "it was'nt [sic] the place at all." <i>(Dec 29, 1904,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  The town of Invercargill is well laid out ... When I was in it before - 16 years ago - the roads had on them tea tree scrub, and in some places wide planks, instead of stones, to prevent the traffic being bogged in the mud, and its footpaths were made of battens nailed across, and evidence of the old state of things still remain [sic] in some of the streets. <i>(Aug 26, 1880,Timaru Herald,2</i>)

 <i>n.</i> stick insect, tea-tree jack

 <i>n.</i> formerly (Teachers) Training College, and still colloquially referred to as Training College: a training institution for school-teachers; now COLLEGE OF EDUCATION [HNZD (1979) 1133: 'formerly called Training College - a training institution for teachers.']

### teak
 
 <i>n.</i> in early New Zealand use, trees with teak-like wood, especially puriri and rata [usu. native, New Zealand teak; 1867 HNP, 1868 PPO (Native teak), 1919 W. Best (Otaki) Vernac. (Andersen, 7 entries)]

>  There are many reasons, says the author, influencing the choice of a name .. It may refer to its similarity to some other familiar plant: New Zealand oak, New Zealand ash, New Zealand teak. <i>(Jul 27, 1926,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Puriri (<i>Vitex littoralis</i>) is sometimes called `teak', or `ironwood'. ... The timber is hard, heavy, very durable, very hard to work, and of a greenish colour. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/190</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Teak, n.</i> The original Teak is an East Indian timber-tree, <i>Tectina grandis</i>, but the name has been trasferred to other trees in different parts of the world, from a similarity in the hardness of their wood. ... In New Zealand, it is <i>Vitex littoralis</i>; Maori name, <i>Puriri</i> (q.v.). <i>(1898,Austral English,463</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> Rata (native teak or `ironwood' of the colonists). <i>(1868,Province of Otago in NZ, The,34</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> <i>Metrosideros lucida</i> native teak <i>(1868,Province of Otago in NZ, The</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>POLACK</b> <i>Metrosideros lucida</i> native teak <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers</i>) [NOTES: And)]



 
 <i>n.</i> [black teal]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> an old name for puriri [[1919 July 29] Ver.(WB). = Vernacular names used by settlers supplied by W.Best, Otaki (And) <i>Vitex lucens</i> New Zealand teak]

>  <b>ANGAS33</b> The New Zealand teak (<i>Vitex littoralis</i>) grows near the sea, and produces a fine durable wood, which takes a fine polish, and is of great use in ship-building. <i>(1866,Polynesia</i>)

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> <i>Vitex lucens</i> New Zealand teak <i>(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter)</i>) [NOTES: (And) (list of trees from Taylor 1848)]

>  <i>Vitex lucens</i> New Zealand teak <i>(1871,TrNZI,3</i>)

>  The Puriri, or New Zealand Oak, or Teak, (<i>Vitex littoralis</i>,) is a large tree of irregular growth. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/43</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  <i>Vitex lucens</i> New Zealand teak ..[15] [Puriri] has been appropriately styled the New Zealand teak:it is ... closely related to the Asiatic teak, and affords a timber of great density and durability, closely resembling lignum vitae in general appearance. <i>(1875,Durability NZ Timbers</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  [Native name] Puriri .. [Settlers' name] New Zealand teak .. [Family] Verbenaceae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,121</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 121]

>  <b>KIRK</b> <i>Vitex lucens</i> New Zealand teak <i>(1889,Forest Flora</i>) [NOTES: (And)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Vitex lucens</i> puriri <i>(1869,TrNZI,1</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  The Black Teal are usually associated in small flocks ... <i>(1872,TrNZI,4/208</i>) [NOTES: (Travers NZ Birds)]

### teakettle broth
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BURTON</b> A `dish' known [during the Slump] as teakettle broth was sometimes a last resort, consisting simply of stale bread cut into cubes, sprinkled with salt and pepper and covered with boiling water. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,29</i>)

### teal
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A shipment of black ducks by the Cuzco are bringing 1s 9d each; teal, 10d; rabbits, 1s to 1s 2d, although a few of the latter are only realising 9d. <i>(Apr 21, 1894,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

>  A point in natural history was raised at the Dunedin City Police, when Mr A. C. Hanlon, a well-known solicitor, demanded proof positive as to whether a teal was a wild duck, which he did not admit. <i>(Sep 19, 1908,Thames Star,1</i>)

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> The Paradise and common duck, teal, widgeon, water-hen, and diver [check cit punct] are all found in great numbers on the rivers ... <i>(1842,Narrative of a Residence in  Various Parts of NZ,47</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> <i>Teal</i>, very like our English ones, are also common on the rivers and lakes. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,164</i>)

>  <b>POWER</b> ... flocks find abundant pasture where, within the settler's memory, there was food only for bittern and teal. <i>(1849,Sketches in NZ,203</i>)

>  <b>SWAINSON</b> Pigeons, ducks, teal, and other water-fowl, abound; ... <i>(1853,Auckland,120</i>)

>  <b>DRURY</b> BIRDS MET WITH IN PELORUS ... Ducks of several kinds - Parera of the natives. Teal. Cormorants - several kinds .. <i>(1855,Sailing Directions,66</i>)

>  `My friend' .../ You like hapuku drying;/ Kapai the smelt, the duck, the eel,/ And shark when putrifying./ <i>(Jul 1866,Canterbury Rhymes,85</i>) [NOTES: 1860 July in 1866 Canterbury Rhymes 85 (The Maori's Choice)]

>  <b>BUTLER</b> Our teal is, if not the same as the English teal, so like it, that the difference is not noticeable. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,ix/137</i>)

>  Grey ducks, Paradise ducks, (which are rather a species of geese) teal, and widgeon frequent the streams. ... Blue mountain-duck is found in the more elevated regions. <i>(1868,Province of Otago in NZ, The,31</i>)

>  <b>BARLOW</b> [At Kaipara] ... birds of various kinds, including duck, teal, shags eel-hawks [77], and flocks of godwit and red-shanked plover, added further life to the scene. <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,76</i>)

>  ... the light-eyed Teal slunk silently from view; or ... the little Grebe with rosy breast, dived and sported with restless activity; close by a group of sober Grey Ducks; whilst the watchful Paradise Drake basked on the sunny bank above ... <i>Ibid.</i> 2/72 ANAS CHLOROTIS, Gray. Puteke. Teal. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/47</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)[should be under BROWN TEAL???]]

>  <b>WHITWORTH</b> At the mouth of the river you might fill your boat with ... the blue, grey and paradise ducks, teal, ... and other birds ... <i>(1879,Spangles and Sawdust,25</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The species we have identified in the Kaipara and Hokianga are - the Putangi, or `paradise-duck' (<i>Casarca variegata</i>); the grey duck, or Parera (<i>Anas superciliosa</i>); the brown duck, an allied species or variety of the last; the Papanga, or `teal,' or `widgeon' (<i>Fuligula N.Z.</i>), and some other varieties that may be imported birds, or crosses, or other native species. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/225</i>)

>  A few paradise ducks, usually in pairs, grey ducks and teal, and black swans, were seen on the tarns at Tarndale, and Lakes Guyon and Tennyson. <i>(1884,TrNZI,17/355</i>) [NOTES: (Park)]

>  I noticed the following:- Pigeon, tui, korimako, kaka, ruru, piwakawaka, toutouwai, kingfisher, duck and teal in the lakes, and pukeko in the swamp, and various sea birds. <i>(1884,TrNZI,17/419</i>) [NOTES: (Gold-Smith)]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> On and around the [Central Otago] streams there were teal, grey duck, and the blue mountain duck, but the last-named was scarce. `I saw stilts, the black-fellows ... wading about ...' <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,120</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> A moulting bird is called a mounu [sic: ?maunu], and at this season great numbers of paradise and grey ducks and teal were taken. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,14</i>)

>  <i>Brown Teal Anas aucklandica</i> (GRAY, 1844) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Pateke, brown duck, teal duck. ...</i>DISTRIBUTION <i>Brown teal: Great Barrier Island and Northland. Rare and isolated elsewhere. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,146</i>)

>  The lake was covered with swarms of duck and teal, so tame that they allowed me to approach within a yard of them, without being in the least disturbed. <i>(Sep 16, 1842,New Zealand Colonist & Port Nicholson Advertiser,4</i>)

### TEAL
 
 <i>abbr.</i> (historical) Tasman Empire Airways Limited [ORIGIN: acronym] (See [see also TEA](t-ten.md#tea))

>  As the TEAL Lockheed Electra bringing the group from Australia taxied to a halt, the screaming nearly drowned out the noise from the plane's turbo-prop engines. <i>(Jun 12, 2004,Dominion Post,E1</i>)

>  During the year 1950-1951, Tasman Empire Airways Ltd., the main operator out of New Zealand carried only 220 tons of freight ... However, TEAL carried almost the same tonnage of mail! <i>(Oct 1952,NZ Geographer,157</i>) [NOTES: (Bishop)]

>  <b>CAMERON</b> New Zealand's ties with Fiji have been strengthened by air services (such as TEAL's Coral Route) ... <i>(1965,New Zealand (various authors/titles),34</i>)

>  We represent that airlines of the world... TEAL Tasman Empire Airlines Limited at the D.I.C. on the Quay. <i>(Jul 10, 1950,Dominion,3</i>)

>  Finally, a deal was struck to form Tasman Empire Airways Ltd (soon known as Teal), with ownership divided between the New Zealand Government, Qantas, Britain's Imperial Airways and local company Union Airways. The first Teal flight took off five days later. <i>(Apr 29, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12</i>)

>  A quarter of a century later, TEAL became Air New Zealand and brought overseas visitors to this country in wide-bodied jet aircraft. <i>(Nov 2003,AA Directions,No. 58/59</i>)

>  [Heading] Evans Bay base delays declared fault of TEAL <i>(May 25, 1950,Southern Cross (Wgton),7</i>)

>  The only major airline operating flying-boats today is TEAL. <i>(Apr 28, 1952,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  Captain Tredrea joined TEAL in 1946. <i>(Apr 3, 1952,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  <b>KEITH</b> Things were much more elegant aboard TEAL, New Zealand's first international airline which it owned jointly with BOAC and QANTAS. <i>(1991,Lovely Day Tomorrow, A,52</i>)

 <i>n.</i> stealing [ODE has tea leaf = thief (rhyming slang)]

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> At Paremoremo, some tealeafing used to occur . . . . <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,178</i>)

### team
 
 <i>n.</i> sc of sheep-dogs

>  <i>Team:</i> Each shepherd's dogs. He may have anything from two to eight. Usually in large teams only three are much use. The rest are for sale or being broken in. The distinction between<i> team</i> and <i>pack</i> (rabbit pack q.v.) was general in Canterbury until a few years ago [from 1933]; but several North Canterbury correspondents have told me that many of the younger shepherds now speak of their sheepdogs as a <i>pack. </i> <i>(1933,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: Acland]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> [gloss.] A shepherd refers to his dogs as his team. An ideal high-country team would be one heading dog, two handy dogs, and two huntaways. Or alternatively, two heading dogs, one handy dog and two huntaways. <i>Ibid.</i> 23 I loosened my `team' of two dogs. <i>(1947,Wayleggo</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> <i>Team:</i> A shepherd refers to his dogs as his team. An ideal high country team would be one heading dog, two handy dogs and two huntaways. Or alternatively, two heading dogs, one handy dog and two huntaways. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,190</i>)

>  WANTED, Situation, by experienced shepherd, aged 25, with good team of dogs, just arrived from Blenheim. <i>(Nov 8, 1902,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  The same competitor also won the president's prize of GBP2 2s for the best team of dogs, Mr Miles' prize rug for most points for two dogs, Mr Bain's prize stockwhip for the noisiest huntaway, and Mr Young's prize silver teapot for most points in the second contest. <i>(Apr 4, 1896,Timaru Herald,3</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [farming in general] a group of dogs owned by a single owner, or working in a group e.g. a rabbiting team of dogs, a mustering team etc.

>  <b>LILICO</b> The home shepherd invariably uses only one dog on his rounds, but his colonial brother-in-trade always has a 'team' or 'pack.' <i>(1919,Breeding and Training of Sheep Dogs,22</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> team: in a mustering gang, each man has a team of dogs - never, never a 'pack' of them. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,84</i>)

>  Shepherd General: Must be motivated, a good stockperson, reliable and have a good team of dogs. <i>(Feb 9, 2002,Dominion,31</i>)

### Team New Zealand
 
 <i>n.</i> NZ America's Cup yachting team

>  [Hdg] Sock it to them Team NZ... The "socks" were all sent up to Team New Zealand's headquarters on Friday, as the yachties counted down for the first race against Italy's Prada challengers. <i>(Feb 22, 2000,Independent Herald (Wellington),2</i>)

>  [Hdg] Team NZ 'not left in the lurch'. Russell Coutts, the hero of New Zealand's two successful America's Cup yachting campaigns, sees considerable irony in the possibility of him competing in the 2003 regatta against Team New Zealand and his protege, Dean Barker. <i>(May 23, 2000,Dominion,30</i>)

### team policing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  This was the era of "team policing", when the appearance of a task force in a pub was the signal to drink up, or get stuck in. <i>(Mar 25, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)

### teamster
 
 <i>n.</i> [1933 Acland]

>  <b>TURNER</b> New Zealand coaching days contributed the <i>teamster</i> named from his team of horses. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,157</i>)

### teapoteer
 
 <i>n.</i> one of the party faithful who man the teapots, cake stalls; a backroom party worker

### tear
 
 <i>n.</i> sc of kauri gum

>  The greater part of the <i>kauri</i>-gum ... is found in a fossil condition, a very small portion being the produce of living trees, although ocasionally it occurs in recent masses or `tears' of several pounds weight, at the junction of a large branch with the stem. <i>(1881,TrNZI,13/138</i>)

### tear hell out of
 
 <i>phr.</i> to tear to pieces (fig.)

>  <b>GASKELL</b> It just tore hell out of me. <i>(1947,Big Game,39</i>)

### tear into
 
 <i>v.</i> to attack vigorously (cf. RIP)

>  <b>SMYTH</b> `Three notes a man if we win out'. `Aw, make it five an' we'll tear into the job'. <i>(1929,Girl from Mason Creek, The,131</i>) [NOTES: EDD 8. work hard or strenuously; do anything with great speed or energy. Sc Ir and Eng. dialects; nf OED]



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>SARGESON</b> You could still hear them tearing into each other. <i>(1946,That Summer,93</i>) [NOTES: P: B1942: Austr: 20C]

### tear off
 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i>

>  <b>BAXTER</b> Do you know the story about the bloke that was tearing one off with his cobber's wife? ... [37] This bloke ... went round to his cobber's place to tear one off. <i>(1985,Horse,36</i>) [NOTES: c1962 Baxter Horse (1985) (Name and the Game) 36]

>  Tear one off at a picnic [heading of advt] its the drink that hits the spot on the spot ... because Fresh-Up Apple juice & Apple and Orange juice now come in a 12-ounce can with a tear-tab top! <i>(Oct 27, 1971,Otago Daily Times,14</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>tear off a tab - **tear off a scab</i>: is to OPEN a tear top CAN OF BEER. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,102</i>)

### tear tussocks
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [mustering] to be in a rage with one's dog.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> tear tussocks: to be in, or fly into, a rage over a dog's performance. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,84</i>)

### tear up for arsepaper
 
 <i>phr.</i> (informal) severely scold, reprimand; demolish in argument [in the 1947 cit. used humorously to express surprise; a var. of well, I'll be blowed, etc.]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Tear up for arse-paper</i>. To reprimand severely: New Zealand soldiers': in the G.W. (B. and P.) <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,869</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> `Livingstone. I believe', said Frank. `Well, I'll be torn up for arsepaper', said Pat Murphy. <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,300</i>)

### tearoomography
 
 <i>n.</i> reports on tearooms [word invented by Steve Braunias]

>  I need more tips, more tearoomographies. <i>(Jul 17, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

### teaser
 
 <i>n.</i> a steer kept on a bull-farm to stimulate the bulls into ejaculation to collect semen for artificial insemination; an animal which cannot get a female pregnant, used to test which females are on heat: either an entire male animal prevented by various means, such as vasectomy, tieing on an apron, etc., or a female treated with male sex hormone to make her act like a male

>  Male that cannot get a female pregnant. An entire male is made into a teaser by: Vasectomising ...Penis deviation ... Treating a female with male hormone to sexually activate her and make her act like a male ...Tie an apron on an entire male which prevents his penis entering the female when he mounts ... . <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,13</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a vasectomised ram or bull used to stimulate and/or detect oestrus in ewes or cows.

>  <b>DU FAUR</b> It may thus be advisable, where teasers are used, to put out two-thirds of the rams immediately, and to add the remainder when needed. <i>(1966,Sheep Farming for Profit,40</i>)

>  The teaser used for the first five days would be fitted with a harness containing oranger marker ink. <i>(Aug 1970,NZ Dairy Exporter,17</i>)

>  .. it has been found that, providing all teasers were efficient, submission rates in the pen system would at least equal single-sire paddock-mating rates. <i>(Jan 1977,NZ Journal of Agriculture,25</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  The presence of a "teaser" stallion in a nearby paddock didn't help... <i>(Sep 5, 2003,Dominion Post RaceForm,20</i>)

>  Once the ewes are on heat a marked teaser ram is put out and then the ewes are artificially inseminated. <i>(Sep 27, 2002,Dominion Post,C11</i>)

### teatime
 
 <i>n.</i> evening meal [aka tea-time]

>  He was outvoted, and compelled to quit Cambridge about an hour before tea-time. <i>(Feb 12, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  I saw him no more till tea-time, and he again told me his ribs were broke, and he could not get up, and that Frank Macdonald had done it. <i>(Jan 24, 1857,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,2</i>)

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> It was nearly teatime when I remembered his letter. He sat down to read it while I fried bacon and eggs for our meal. <i>(1959,Stone, The,65</i>) [NOTES: (The Stone)]

>  We're supposed to believe he has emerged from a dark teatime of the soul since his wife's death... <i>(Feb 26, 2004,Dominion Post,B11</i>)

>  'Hondai Lanka' Tea is playing an important part in many households - it is giving tea-time satisfaction! <i>(Oct 28, 1909,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,1691</i>)

>  In one case the work was finished by tea-time, whereas in the other no appreciable progress had been made. <i>(Jun 1, 1921,Ohinemuri Gazette,4</i>)

>  To English ears the notion of serving coffee at tea-time sounds a little incongruous. <i>(Jul 23, 1936,Evening Post,19</i>)

>  Another pilotless plane fell at teatime among people returning from their Sunday afternoon walk. <i>(Jun 19, 1944,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  At teatime I had bread and butter and tea, if I chose to take it. <i>(Mar 24, 1865,Daily Southern Cross,6</i>)

>  Teatime finds him fagged out, poor dear, but he eats with much gusto, then retires to his well-worn chimney corner and dozes his evening away. <i>(Aug 13, 1872,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

### teatspray
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to spray the teats of dairy cows prior to, and/or following, milking.

>  Teatspray all cows after every milking over the complete lactation. <i>(Oct 1999,NZ Dairy Exporter,51</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> <i>(1970,Not Here, Not Now,156</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a person from a Tech

### Technical School Institute

### technicolour yawn
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Just a small update on 'helicopter heave'; my brother recently spoke to a member of the Massey University Students' Assn, and he confirmed that the term is still in use (at Massey anyway). It is particularly associated with the "chunder-mile," a rather ,messy capping event... "Technicolour yawn" is used for an instance of vomiting, as is "liquid laugh', I believe. <i>(Aug 24, 1998,Letter,00</i>)

>  <b>GIFFORD</b> About an hour from home he leaps to the slidin' window [of the bus] and goes [does???] a technicolour yawn that MGM would have been proud to produce. <i>(1976,Loosehead Len's Bumper Thump Book,31</i>)

>  <b>TUCKER</b> ... Jerry suddenly lurched to the side of the vessel and produced what is now commonly referred to as `the technicolour yawn'. <i>(1978,Thoroughbreds Are My Life,21</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>technicolour yawn</i>: To empty the contents of one's stomach through the oral orifice; often done as a response to an excess of alcohol in the system. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,102</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>technicolour yawn</i> vomit. ANZ. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,112</i>)

>  New Zealanders have more words for chundering, hurling, producing technicolour yawns and making a long-distance phone call on the great white telephone than the Inuit have for snow. <i>(Feb 10, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

### technofencing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Contract fencers have put in 100ha of technofencing over the last six months and about 120ha of the farm have been drilled with new annual ryegrasses. <i>(Jul 12, 2005,Country-Wide,28</i>)

### technosystem
 
 <i>n.</i> [Wier or Weir in first citation?]

>  Pasture covers in the technosystem typically climbed to 3500kg DM/ha last year, but Livingston says as the system intensifies this year "the longest feed they will go into will be just over 3000kg DM/ha". ... About 150 units of N/ha were applied to the technosystem this season in five applications, however the input of the last 30kg N/ha in January was "under question" because pasture response was limited. <i>(Jul 2005,Country-Wide,29</i>)

>  . . . he split the 20-30ha-sized paddocks into 6-8ha, installed the Technosystem of intensive bull finishing devised by Bulls farmer Harry Wier, planted 13ha of pines on his rough country and began introducing the Rangitikei farmer Roger Marshall's Kelso composite sheep breed to the Romney flock that came with the farm. <i>(May 13, 2004,Dominion Post,C7</i>)

>  He started running a technosystem eight years ago, gradually increasing the size of the system to the stage where it covered about 80ha of easier contour last year. <i>(Jul 2005,Country-Wide,28</i>)

### Ted
 
 <i>n.</i> a German

>  It is the arrogance of the American labelling other peoples `gooks' and `wops': the New Zealand soldier had his `Wogs' and `Ities' and `Teds', at home there are the `Chinks' and `Ikes' and `Dallies' and even the `Horis'. <i>(Sep 1952,Landfall,23/220</i>) [NOTES: (Pearson `Fretful Sleepers')]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> These Welshmen are a stubborn lot, just like the Teds in the Gothic Line, ... <i>(1970,On the Ball,136</i>)

>  <b>Tucker</b> now we had realised that the Italian name for Germans was Tedeschi, so of course we had nicknamed them `The Teds'. Our nickname had absolutely no reference to Tedeschi at all, but merely was a shortening of `shitheads'. We derived great pleasure from looking at one another and saying, `Well, the Teds have cleared off,' or `The Teds have done this' or whatever had happened. <i>(1978,Thoroughbreds Are My Life,26</i>)

>  In connection with the visit of H.R.H. the Prince of Wales to New York last November, the New York World issued as a supplement on November 30 an autographed photo-gravure of "Teddy Woodbine," as the prince was familiarly styled in France, and a copy has been forwarded to THE EXPRESS Office. <i>(Feb 11, 1920,Marlborough Express,4</i>)

### tee
 
 <i>n.</i> a fence-post [ca1951 R. Gilberd (Okaihau) [?definition] 2. <i>`Swinging T' </i>- a foot with a length of wire attached which is either stapled to the post above the ground or twisted onto the bottom wire. 3. <i>`Anchored T' -</i> similar to a `swinging T', but no post is used. The anchor wire is twisted around each wire in turn, holding them at correct elevation. It is used where posts have to be packed to the fence-line, e.g. in the Gisborne district.]

### tee scum

### teegoomme
 
 <i>n.</i> tikumu

>  Several large bundles of the tomentum stripped from the back of the large alpine<i> Celmisia (tikumu).</i> [fn] 1773 edn Parkinson Journal of a Voyage to the South Seas 115 `At one particular place ... we met with a substance that appeared like a kid's skin, but it had so weak a texture that we ... were afterwards informed by the natives that it was gathered from some plant called <i>teegomme. </i> <i>(1897,TrNZI,29/175</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i> a breed of sheep: Texel, East Friesian, Romney [also tefrom]

The breeders Messrs R Welch, R Campbell & D Daniell have more than 1,000 ewes and are SIL recorded. <i>(Jan 13, 2004,Internet - see Notes,0</i>) [NOTES: http://www.nzsheep.co.nz/news.htm (accessed 04/01/2006)]

 <i>abbr.</i> Terrorist Emergency Group

 <i>n.</i> (See [taiore](t-ten.md#taiore))

### teina
 
 <i>n.</i> a younger brother or sister; a junior (compare TUAKANA). [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>Buck</b> To sum up briefly, a male is tuakana to all his younger brothers and teina to all his older brothers. ... If there are any daughters in the family, they follow the same principle, being tuakana to all their younger sisters and teina to their older sisters. <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,340</i>)

### teka
 
 <i>n.</i> a fib, a lie; tito [ca1950 V. O'Sullivan (Ahipara, Northland) to tell tekas, etc.]

>  You teka! she [a Maori grandmother] giggled. Who'd want to have you! <i>(1984,Some Other Country,162</i>) [NOTES: 1972 Ihimaera `Game of Cards' in 1984 Some Other Country 162]

### tekapo
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [Ma.] a New Zealand-bred cultivar of cocksfoot (orchard grass, Dactylis glomerata).

>  Tekapo - Densely tillered, prostrate cultivar suitable for hard grazing and dry environments. <i>(1995,Grasslands Range of Forage and Conservation Plants,20</i>)

### tekateka
 
 <i>n.</i> [empty headword] (See [tekoteko](t-ten.md#tekoteko))

### tekoteko
 
 <i>n.</i> carved figure on the gable of a house; figurehead of a canoe [ORIGIN: Maori, Ryan 256]

>  <b>IHIMAERA & LONG</b> Behind glass panes / the tekoteko stares helplessly <i>(1982,Into the World of Light,197</i>)

>  Where the barge-boards meet is a carved face, surrounded with feathers, and surmounted with a small figure called a tekoteko. <i>(1876,TrNZI,8/175</i>) [NOTES: (Stack Maori House)]

>  The <i>tekoteko</i> on the roof above is Takenga, one of the descendants of Tama te Kapua <i>(1895,TrNZI,27/674</i>)

>  They were often ... inlaid with pawa-shell (<i>Haliotis</i>), and finished off with a <i>tekoteko</i> (grotesque wooden figure) set up at the apex of the roof. <i>(1903,TrNZI,35/22</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> A carved face (<i>koruru</i>) was placed at the junction of the barge-boards, and above this was an image (<i>tekoteko</i>) which, especially on state occasions, was decked with feathers and ornaments. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,273</i>)

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> From the hut itself I took the long middle post made of totara, on which was a very beautiful tekateka, or carved figure, representing the face of Chief Tirorau fully tattooed. <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,65,1933 ed.</i>)

>  ... - her eyes like paua-eyes of the gable-<i>tekoteko. </i> <i>(1930,NZ Short Stories,32</i>) [NOTES: J.C.Andersen]

>  <b>BUCK</b> <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,2/121</i>)

>  <i>tekoteko,</i> grotesque carved human figure standing on the gable of a Maori house, above the koruru covering the join of the barge-boards at the gable. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/159</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>BUCK</b> A carved head(<i>koruru</i>) was placed over the vertical join of the two barge-boards forming the gable apex. ... Sometimes a complete human figure (<i>tekoteko</i>) was used instead of a <i>koruru</i> type ... <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,129</i>)

>  Beneath the carven counterparts of their ancestors and the fierce <i>teko teko</i> on guard high above ... the Maoris lament on their mats. <i>(Aug 1953,Here & Now,21</i>)

>  <b>ASHTON-WARNER</b> `I've designed a banner for the school. Look! Here's the Maori <i>tekoteko</i>, here a book to indicate learning. [fn.]Maori figurehead on meeting house. <i>(1958,Spinster,127</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> In the only Maori I knew/ I called/ Tihei Mauriora./ Above me the tekoteko raged./ He ripped his tongue from his mouth/ and threw it at my feet./ <i>(1979,Eyes of the Ruru,15</i>)

>  At Papawai Marae, near Greytown, the tall, red tekoteko - the carved figures that represent ancestors - mostly face inward. ... The tekoteko represent Papawai's position as a place of peace and harmony, and a new era. <i>(Oct 2005,Air New Zealand inflight magazine,88</i>) [NOTES: -]

>  He goes all round the posts, and other parts of the house searching for it, and at last walks outside to consult the small wooden image (tekoteko) placed at the end of the ridge-pole in the front of the house. <i>(Jun 22, 1921,Ohinemuri Gazette,2</i>)

>  The tekoteko, the figure on top of the meeting-house, represents Tuterangiwhiuiti, who was a descendant of Porourangi and a great warrior. <i>(Dec 1965,Te Ao Hou,53: 49</i>)

>  The prices for the more modern, or more recently acquired, articles included .. tekoteko, GBP5. <i>(May 24, 1912,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  The mahi figure on the doorway represents Pikiao, the ancestor of the Ngatipikias tribe, and the small figure, or tekoteko, that surmounts the top is called "Te Takinga," the son of Pikiao. <i>(Dec 19, 1889,Otago Witness,17</i>)

>  Maori material culture, the patterns and forms of tekoteko, taniko and tukutuku, is a tempting source of raw material for artists. <i>(Dec 14, 2005,NZ Herald,B4</i>)

>  To be honest the carvings I saw would not set Rotorua alight, but they are not for sale and the teko teko made by brothers Clinton and Jay Rei should soon grace the Patea High School. <i>(Mar 14, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a telephone cold-call [Renee]

### tele-conner
 
 <i>n.</i> a cold-call telephone operator [Renee]
