### telegraph pole
 
 <i>n.</i> a pole supporting telegraph or electric power lines or wires [Jim writes: This is defined in Chambers and Macquarie in the literal sense of a pole supporting telegraph wires. There is not entry in Collins. NSOD refers to "telegraph or telephone wires". In NZ the term was widely used informally also in relation to electric power lines (=<i>power pole</i>) (I cannot remember ever having seen telegraph lines in the strict sense). Does this loose use persist?]

>  <b>CONLY</b> Multi-storey commercial buildings at Napier and Hastings view with telegraph poles in swaying to the extremes. <i>(1980,Shock of '31, The,3</i>)

### telegraph time
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WATT</b> In May, 1877, a clock was placed in the eastern window of the telegraph office ... Hence, `telegraph time', another name by which standard time was known. <i>(1971,Centenary of Invercargill,156</i>)

### telescope fish
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Mendosoma lineatum</i>

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>TELESCOPE FISH Mendosoma lineatum</i> ... This species gets its name from the extensible mouth which can be protruded forward and downward to form a long tube. ... They are found from Cook Strait southwards ... <i>(1982,Collins Guide,248,1984 ed.</i>)

>  [species name] telescope fish .. [scientific name] <i>Mendosoma lineatum .</i>. [other name(s)] <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> [species name] telescope fish .. [scientific name] <i>Mendosoma lineatum .</i>. [other name(s)] <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,106</i>)

### telescoping
 
 <i>n.</i> tucking the legs of frozen lambs inside their carcass

>  <b>BELICH</b> ... 'telescoping' - tucking the legs of frozen lambs inside their carcass and so saving 15 per cent of shipping space. <i>(2001,Paradise Reforged,228</i>)

### teletangi
 
 <i>n.</i> a video-filmed tangi/funeral distributed by webcam and cellphone. [ORIGIN: blend]

>  Because our family is scattered worldwide and some of our young people are computer literate, we used the internet to share it [a funeral in 2007] online via webcams and cellphones. This <i>teletangi</i> was the first to be done by the Maori but I think it will become crucial to keeping families connected in death and life. <i>(Oct 13, 2007,New Scientist,52</i>) [NOTES: from article 'A Maori Farewell' by Paratene Ngata]

### Telethon
 
 <i>n.</i> a 24-hour TV charity fund-raising event; a television programme to raise money for charity, where viewers telephone the comperes to pledge donations over many hours or days [The first Telethon in NZ was held in 1979 but this date needs checking.]

>  It was winter, Telthon was happening in a cold showgrounds shed with equipment gleaned from obsolescent and surplus stock. There was a public who had heard of Telethon but had never seen one. <i>(Jul 10, 1982,Listener (New Zealand Listener),23</i>)

>  <i>telethon n. U.S</i>. a lengthy television programme to raise charity funds, etc. [C20: < TELE- + MARATHON] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,1203</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 791; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 495]]

### tell-pie-tit
 
 <i>n.</i> used occasionally for the more usual but general English, <i>tell-tale-tit</i> (your tongue shall be split,/And every little puppydog shall have a little bit)

>  <b>HYDE</b> Tell-pie-tit, his tongue will be split. <i>(1938,Godwits Fly, The,117</i>)

### telltale
 
 <i>n.</i> an instrument for measuring roof movement in a mine

>  At intervals, devices called telltales, resembling a pair of small fluorescent clock faces, had been installed. A telltale measures movement in the roof - telling miners what's happening in the seam above their heads. <i>(Jul 2004,NZ Geographic,No. 68/43</i>)

### temperance ship
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LANG</b> [The two American whalers] are both <i>temperance ships,</i> but I know well from past experience that they are by this time full of native women for the worst of purposes. <i>(1839,New Zealand in 1839,8</i>)

### temporary constable
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [caption] <B>THE UNREAL McCOY: Nick Udy (left) in the new polo shirt for temporary constables, and Constable Sione Tangi in the standard police uniform. <i>(May 31, 2006,NZ Herald,A4</i>)</b>

### tempured
 
 <i>adj.</i> tempura-battered

>  Despite a chronically over-cooked sludge of bok choi, the pork-stuffed chicken breast was succulent and good, its accompanying "tempured" (i.e. tempura-battered) coconut rice rolls standing uprioght on the plate.... <i>(Jul 22, 2004,Dominion Post,D4</i>)

### Temuka stone
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FRAME</b> Where were the cladders with their load of Temuka stone that was not genuine stone but layers of imitation wood painted the colour of stone? <i>(1988,Carpathians, The,147</i>)

### Ten Guitars
 
 <i>n.</i> the "unofficial National anthem", originally written and performed by Engelbert Humperdinck.

>  The notion that Kiwi soldiers abroad can readily win the hearts and minds of the locals - a few smiles for the kids and a rendition of "Ten Guitars" down at the market is usually believed to do the trick - is about to be put to a serious test. <i>(Jul 5, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  The evening began and ended with a singalong - "10 Guitars", what else? <i>(May 24, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),64</i>)

>  What happened to the good old "enjoy yourself and bugger the culture and the deeper meaning" theory? The black singlet, the meat pie and the knees-up to Ten Guitars at the local pub seem to have lost their place to a troop of line-dancing Peruvian whistlers. <i>(Mar 12, 2002,Evening Post,25</i>)

### ten skin bowling
 
 <i>phr.</i> beating up skinheads

### ten-acre block
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a hobby farm, or a semi-rural lifestyle property where emphasis may be on lifestyle rather than land production. [In some local body areas, a ten acre block represented the minimum area for optimal small-property production.]

>  Get yourself a 10-acre block and it can kill you. <i>(Dec 9, 1976,NZ Farmer,53</i>)

>  <b>FIRTH</b> There was an initial rush to buy 10 acre blocks 20 years ago. <i>(1988,Farmers are Bananas,151</i>)

>  <b>YEREX</b> At one time we talked about '10-acre blocks,' mainkly because that was the minimum size for farm subdivision. <i>(1992,New Zealand Off the Beaten Track,64</i>)

>  Small farms, or hobby farms or life-style blocks, used to be referred to as 10-acre blocks because county councils decided that farm land should not be divided into anything less than that size. <i>(1992,New Zealand Off the Beaten Track,106</i>)

>  'Ten acre blocks' can easily become productive units in a way that they never could be with cattle or sheep! <i>(Sep 10, 1996,NZ Emu Farming Association Journal</i>) [NOTES: Promo]

### ten-acre blocker
 
 <i>n.</i> a lifestyler on a ten-acre block

>  I was giving some classes to ten-acre blockers. <i>(Jul 29, 2004,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: Lunchtime News]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a hobby farmer.

>  <b>MEADOWS</b> This book would not be complete without reference to the hobby farmer or owner of a small block of land. Known in New Zealand as 'ten-acre blockers' because many small blocks are of that size, they range from retired farmers who can't quite let go through to rural-dwelling company executives.. <i>(1996,NZ Guide to Cattle Breeds,47</i>)

### ten-acre blockie
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a hobby farmer, or one running a farmlet on approx. ten acres. (See [lifestyle farmer](lay-liv.md#lifestyle-farmer))

>  These are not just the 10-acre blockies or lifestyle farmers who don't understand or don't care about the economics of calf-rearing... <i>(Apr 6, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,19</i>)

### ten-eighty
 
 <i>n.</i> sodium fluoroacetate, a poison used to kill noxious animals (See [1080](../1#1080))

### ten-gallon hat
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LYON</b> The diggers brought into general use [in the 19th century] the soft felt of various shapes, the larger of which were termed the `ten-gallon' or `eight horse' hats. <i>(1952,Faring South,98</i>)

### ten-minute gate
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a Taranaki gate, composed of fencing wire and battens.

>  <b>FISK</b> Taranaki Gates: Otherwise known as ten-minute gates because they take only ten minutes to make but supposedly just as long to open and close. <i>(1996,Practical Smallfarming,19</i>)

### ten-pound Pom
 
 <i>n.</i> an assisted-immigrant who paid [pounds]10 only towards cost of passage, and was bonded to Government directed work for a set period [also 10-pound Pom]

>  "I was enchanted by it all," he says, and took up the offer of becoming a "GBP10 pom". <i>(Oct 4, 2001,Dominion,11</i>)

>  Her parents sailed to New Zealand in 1962 as newly wed "GBP10 Poms", determined on a better life for themselves and children-to-be. <i>(Jul 7, 2007,Listener (New Zealand Listener),26</i>)

>  For years whenever we talked about Captain Cook with anyone I could never understand how we were called GBP10 Poms. One man said it's a misnomer to call us GBP10 Poms because at the time if you applied to Australia, it cost GBP10 and to Canada it was GBP10 - he said that's why he was in New Zealand, as he didn't have GBP10. <i>(Jan 22, 2003,Dominion Post,A6</i>)

>  His parents were among the wave of 10-pound Poms who came to New Zealand in search of a better life, when Jones was two. <i>(Jun 14, 2008,Dominion Post Indulgence,18</i>)

>  A job in a shoe factory, National Service, and then Bland comes to New Zealand, as a 20-year-old, another GBP10 Pom. <i>(Aug 14, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>)

### tena koe
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori [nonce?]

>  <b>NEWNHAM</b> I couldn't quite fathom out though why our teacher always called us "You Maori tena koes." <i>(1989,1840-1990. A Long White Cloud?,68</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> [aka tenakoe]

>  She "tenakoed" the hero of Khartoum with a vivacity that must have reminded him of some of his interviews with those most interesting folk, the Soudan Dervishes, and he smilingly "tenakoed" in return. <i>(Mar 24, 1910,Wanganui Chronicle,2</i>)



 
 <i>exclam.</i>



 
 <i>exclam.</i> a common form of Maori greeting (to one person) various early spg the [a[a]] has given rise to the usual anglicisation [te|naakwe; to greet with <i>tena koe </i>, sometimes used as a greeting by Pakeha, becoming (with KIA ORA) more frequent from c1975-1980 after about 50-60 years of infrequent use. [Compare tena koutou katoa. [Maori tena[e,a-macron] koe]]

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> Jim gave the salutation "Tenaqua". "Tenaqua Ehoa". <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,176</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Two natives put their heads in at the tent-door, and tena koe'd the Bishop. .sk 2;B. in English contexts, espec (?) North Island <i>(1959,Early Travellers,110</i>) [NOTES: 1855 Aug 15 Abraham Walk from Auckland to Taranaki in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 110]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> A Maori salutation used in the North Island of New Zealand. <i>(1898,Austral English</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Tena Koe?</.i> How do you do?: coll., North Island of New Zealand: late C. 19-20. Ex Maori (lit., `that is you'). Morris. Cf. <i>taihoa</i>, q.v. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,871</i>)

>  <b>McNAB</b> Now that our political leaders are less lavish with Maori greetings, spoken and cabled, `tenakoe' and `kia ora' are less assured of a long life. <i>(Apr 2, 1938,Press, The,18</i>) [NOTES: NZ Slang]

>  <b>BAKER</b> ...tena koe, a salutation... <i>(1941,NZ Slang,56</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,169</i>)

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> ... canoes ... which meet you ... with the everlasting greeting of `Naumai', or `tena-koit-ou', `welcome' or `hail to you', and exchange, as they pass, news up and down the river. <i>(1842,New Zealand (Heaphy),94</i>)

>  "Tena koe. Tena koe. Te Prince," exclaimed an elderly Maori dame, as she bore down upon the distinguished visitor, with hand outstretched and beaming countenance. <i>(May 11, 1920,Poverty Bay Herald,4</i>)

>  "Tenakoe," said Princess Elizabeth to Lieutenant A. T. Bennett, Rotorua (son of Bishop Bennet), and Lieutenant H. Wiremu, of Kaitaia. <i>(Jun 21, 1945,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  Saying <i>Taraqua.</i> [?tanaqua] <i>(1842,Letters from Settlers,48,1843 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (W. Ferguson)]

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> T|ena ra ko koe; Be thou healthy! or Good morning (day, or night) to thee! <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,211</i>)

>  how do you do? - ta'nna ra'kkoo ko'ee [tena ra ko koe?] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,112,1986 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> `Tenera coqui emera' the native salute. <i>(1838,Journal of a Voyage to NZ,212</i>)

>  <b>MATHEW</b> They flocked out ... to welcome us, with their usual salutation of `Tanerakakoi' or `How do you do', ... <i>(Feb 16, 1840,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Feb 16 F.Mathew Journal in 1940 Founding of NZ 48]

>  Saying <i>Taraqua.</i> [?tanaqua] <i>(1842,Letters from Settlers,48,1843 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (W. Ferguson)]

>  <b>BARNICOAT</b> We were eveywhere saluted with the usual Tenago or Howdyedo. <i>(1842,Journal,66</i>)

>  <b>WEEKES</b> [A maori] came towards me with extended hand and a friendly `Tenaqui'. <i>(1842,Journal,IV/4</i>)

>  <b>POWER</b> In the interior of the Pa the Wahines[e-acute], or matrons, are busy weaving flax-mats, cleaning potatoes or fish, or engaged in the superintendence of a Maori oven, or a huge gipsy-looking cauldron, called a `go-ashore', and can only afford to greet the visitor with a whining `tena koe pakeha', sighing as though they were very much to be pitied. <i>(1849,Sketches in NZ,160</i>)

>  <b>TANCRED</b> The salutation `Tina-koe', which being pronounced rapidly has the sound of a single word `tinaque'. <i>(1856,Natural History of Canterbury,32</i>)

>  <b>ASKEW</b> On passing their [sc. Maoris'] tents, I was saluted with `taner, ake-kouie,' (good [343] morning) by the men. <i>(1857,Voyage to Australia & NZ,342</i>)

>  <b>THATCHER</b> Ropata's whare soon they spied,/ `Tenakoe!' the deputation cried,/ `Haeremai, Pakeha!' Ropata replied,/ When they got to the Ohinemuri. <i>(1869,Wit and Humour: Local Songs...,33</i>) [NOTES: The Ohinemuri Deputation]

>  <b>ASKEW</b> I was saluted with: `taner, ake-ouie'. <i>Ibid.</i> [?...] 342 ... taner, ake-ouie. <i>(1857,Voyage to Australia & NZ,342</i>)

>  <b>THATCHER</b> We often used to visit them, and instead of looking black / At these Rangiriri rebels, we'd pat them on the back, / Shook hands, and said tena koe[e-dieresis], and pointed to the shore, <i>(1869,Maori Captives in Local Songs,16</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> But inasmuch as we only knew Maori enough to the extent of being able to say, <i>`Tena koe?</i>' (`How do you do?'), and as that terminated our conversational powers in their vernacular, nothing then was left us but to stare at each other. <i>(1881,Poenamo,3/4/162</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>a Maori salutation used in North Island of New Zealand. Lit. `That is you,' and meaning `How do you do?' Tena</i> and <i>Tera</i> both mean <i>`that'</i>; but <i>tena</i> implies the idea of nearness, `that near you,' <i>tera</i> the idea of distance, `that (or there) away younder.' Hence, while Tena koe is a welcome, Tera koe would be an insult. <i>(1898,Austral English,466</i>)

>  <b>ALEXANDER & CURRIE</b> <i>Tenakoe</i>: a word of greeting. <i>(1906,Treasury of NZ Verse,289</i>)

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> they meet a Maori again - barefooted and strong -she shouted Te nakohi? - <i>(1907,Urewera Notebook,70,1978 Gordon ed.</i>)

>  <i>tena koe</i> interj. good-morning! howdo you do! [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,12</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> From a shack doorway Old Kahu ... gave him `Tena koe'. She wore a man's plug hat, and had had children by so many whites that they all looked alike to her, big kids. <i>(1938,Godwits Fly, The,142</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> Her voice said `Tena koe', and he answered `Tena koe', dropping down to squat beside the mattress. <i>Ibid.</i> 192 Some [Maoris] called `Tena koe', but the beasts were more curious about strangers. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,133</i>)

>  <i>(1944,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),408</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,1/79</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> New Zealand colloquialisms which are of Maori origin include; ... <i>tena koe:</i> good day to you <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),117</i>)

>  <i>te[e]na[a]koe, tenakorua, tenakoutou</i>, greeting to one, two, or more. Really two words in all three - Tena koe! (That you! or There you are! Fancy!) I have heard it Tenaaqui)! <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/159</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>tenakoe</i> Maori greeting. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,176</i>)

>  <i>Tena koe</i> A Maori greeting which means `how do you do?', literally `there you are'. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),408</i>)

>  <b>HI;LIARD</b> <i>(1960,Maori Girl,92</i>)

>  <i>tena koe (phr.)</i> Maori greeting, meaning `How do you do?'. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),626</i>)

>  <b>BRAITHWAITE</b> TENA KOE Good morning! (Singular.) <i>(1967,Evil Day,382</i>)

>  <i>tena koe</i> ... (Phr.) <i>Maori </i>greeting meaning <i>There you are</i> and implying <i>How do you do? <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> .. at the church I murmured,`Tena koe',/ To the oldest [Maori] woman/ and she replied, `Tena koe'- <i>(1979,Collected Poems,460,1969 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>tena koe</i>: pronounced tena[acute]qway means ALOHA or hello and goodbye. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,102</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> tena koe <i>greeting </i> <i>(1982,Selected Poems,189</i>)

>  <i>tena koe int. </i>Maori greeting (to one person). (Other inflected forms for two or more people.) [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,792</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 495]]

>  <b>OTAGONIAN</b> The Squire replied by the word "<i>Tenakui</i>". <i>(1867,Rambles with a Philosopher,63</i>)

>  "Tena koe" and other polite compliments were exchanged, and inquiries instituted as to the whereabouts of a gentleman who - for the sake of the narrative - will be called Honi. <i>(Oct 7, 1872,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> The old folks sitting at the door hailed us with their cordial "Tenakoe." <i>(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter),250</i>)

>  'Allo, kia ora, bon jour, tena koe. Would you like puha on your baguette? <i>(Jul 19, 2002,Capital Times,13</i>)

>  "Tena-koe, Miss Elaine," said Rewa politely. <i>(Dec 14, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,17</i>)

>  "Tenakoe," said Princess Elizabeth to Lieutenant A.T. Bennett of Rotorua. <i>(Jul 2, 1945,NZEF Times,IV/210 : 1</i>)

>  Tenakoe, Sister Province, 'Neath Otira's snowy crest A hundred thousand welcomes I extend you to the West. <i>(Aug 29, 1918,Hawke's Bay Herald,6</i>)

### tena korua
 
 <i>n.</i> greeting to two persons [ORIGIN: Maori] (See [cf. tena koe](t-ten.md#tena-koe),[tena koutou](../T#tena-koutou))

>  <i>te[e]na[a]koe, tenakorua, tenakoutou,</i> greeting to one, two, or more. Really two words in all three - Tena koe! (That you! or There you are! Fancy!) I have heard it Tenaaqui)! <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/159</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

### tena koutou (katoa)
 
 <i>exclam.</i>

>  <b>BAKER</b> 'Tena koutou,' he said. The barely comprehensible formal greeting sounded more like 'tinna goat.' <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,118</i>)

>  ".. We saluted each other with friendly "tena koutou," exchanged tobacco for some of the finest wood-pigeons - a capital dish for our dinner, and passed on." <i>(Jun 17, 1870,Hawke's Bay Herald,3</i>)

>  "You have just surprised me - this is the first real haka I have seen for many years," and said again, "Kapai, kapai. Tena koutou." <i>(Jun 22, 1887,Bay of Plenty Times,4</i>)

>  The Maoris salute us from their pahs, "Tena Koutou!" to which we reply, "Karangamai!" <i>(Jan 8, 1898,Poverty Bay Herald,4</i>)

>  Every here and there a small kainga would be passed and "Tena koutou!" would cause hills and woods to resound again and again with friendly greeting. <i>(Dec 28, 1901,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  "Tena Koe" (salutation to you); if two persons "Tena Korua" (salutations to you two); if there are more than two he says "Tena Koutou" (salutation to you all). <i>(Dec 24, 1917,Thames Star,2</i>)

>  <b>MANING</b> I stepped into the circle formed by my new friends, and had just commenced a <i>tena koutou</i>, when a breeze of wind came ... <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,(II)58</i>)

>  <i>te[e]na[a]koe, tenakorua, tenakoutou,</i> greeting to one, two, or more. Really two words in all three - Tena koe! (That you! or There you are! Fancy!) I have heard it Tenaaqui)! <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/159</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>BRATHWAITE</b> TENA KOUTOU Good morning! (To several people.) <i>(1967,Evil Day,382</i>)

>  She spoke of the loyalty of the people, and of their services to the Empire on the battlefields of Gallipoli and France, and finished with the greeting, "Tena koutou. Kia ora koutou katoa." <i>(Dec 23, 1926,Evening Post,18</i>)

>  <b>SCOTT</b> We had so far met.. one man who took no notice whatever of us except to say "Jenokoi" (good day to one person) and if we had been three or four he would have said "Jenokuru". <i>(1897,How I Stole 10,000 Sheep,38</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> These days you even say tena koutou or haere mai without saying Hairy My and looking nervous. <i>(1988,Carpathians, The,30</i>)

### tenakoe-ing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WEEKES</b> .. half a dozen lean dogs ... commenced a yelping chorus which they kept up lustily during the period of <i>teneako</i>-ing [a-grave], handshaking, etc. <i>(1842,Journal</i>) [NOTES: also 1841 Weekes Progress of the Colony in 1940 Establishment of New Plymouth 92 .]

### tench
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>POLACK</b> Many other fish are equally numerous, answering to our <i>hakes, tench</i>,.. and <i>dog-fish. </i> <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/322</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> Among the piscivorous tribes, are those well known in Europe under the appellations of snapper, tench, .. cat and dog-fish, sting-rays, eels. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/202 fn</i>)

### tender
 
 <i>adj.</i> wool with a tensile weakness

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> `How's this hogget wool going, Bill?' `First class wool, just first class but it's tender - fleece after fleece. It's heartbreaking.' ... He plucked a specimen of the fine wool from the tender bin ... and gave a sharp tug. The wool parted along a line of weakness in its growth. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,5</i>)

>  <b>McDONALD</b> <i>Tender Wool </i>- Wool that will not resist reasonable strain without breaking. <i>(1915,NZ Sheepfarming,88</i>)

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> `How's this hogget wool going, Bill?' `First class wool, just first class but it's tender - fleece after fleece. It's heartbreaking.' ... He plucked a specimen of the fine wool from the tender bin ... and gave a sharp tug. The wool parted along a line of weakness in its growth. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,5</i>)

>  <i>Tender</i>:Wool with a tensile weakness.A less severe form of break. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,60</i>)

>  This wool is long stapled tender wool, unfit for combing, but might be improved, and the kemp and scurf eradicated. <i>(Oct 12, 1861,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,2</i>)

>  One grave error our farmers make is pursuing the practice of crossing beyond the first remove from the merino, the result being a coarse, kempy, tender wool, by no means a favourite with the wool buyers. <i>(Nov 16, 1878,Otago Witness,6</i>)

>  In trueness exists eveness and freedom from waste, for if the fibre is irregular in bulk an even cloth cannot be produced, and, if very tender, the fabric would fall to pieces in the process of manufacture. <i>(Nov 15, 1884,Otago Witness,7</i>)

>  A good deal of the wool has been strong and bright, but a considerable percentage of tender fleeces was found in a number of flocks which had been well fed throughout the year, and some people are unable to explain the cause. <i>(Feb 3, 1909,Otago Witness,8</i>)

### tender box
 
 <i>n.</i> an animal casket

>  One of her holistic therapy students has designed animal caskets, called "tender boxes".. along with special body bags for the burial of larger animals. <i>(Jun 2004,North & South,25</i>)

### tenni
 
 <i>n.</i> tennis [1951 16+ M 26 Marlborough C. 20 TENNI (TENNIS)]

### tennikoit
 
 <i>n.</i> a game of catching and returning quoits across a tennis net or taut wire at waist height [Jim writes This word (and the game it signifies, although one can guess its nature, are new to me. It is not in any dictionary I have seen... Possibly proprietary, possibly of brief currency (cf<i>taipo</i> as the name of a game from about the same period, possibly distinctive NZE. DEH notes : I know this from St Pat's Silverstream where it was played in the quadrangle on areas marked out as in tennis with doubles and singles lines; it is probably still played there today.]

>  <b>EDMONDS</b> [Caption] We played tennikoit on our small front lawn. <i>(1989,Hot October,52</i>)

### tens
 
 <i>n.</i> 10-acre allotments in Dunedin

>  Heeding Captain William Cargill's advice to "settle on the tens", they took up the suburban 10-acre allotments along the harbour or in areas such as North East Valley, Wakari and Andersons Bay. <i>(Apr 1998,North & South,78</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a ball-bouncing game

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Ball-bouncing games have come into their own on the asphalt playgrounds of modern schools and are played in many schools throughout the country. There is no evidence of such games before 1900, but a number of instances between 1900 and 1920 are recorded. The games were known as Fives, Tens, Sevens, and Walls and have been recorded more often since under the names O'Leary or Sevens. Before 1920 there is only one report of the game being played with a rhyme, and this was a rhyme more often used in skipping. <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,273</i>)

### tent
 
 <i>n.</i> part of the paraphenalia of backyard abortion

>  A `tent', if used, would cause a miscarriage within twenty-four hours, but it was absurd to say that one used in January would take effect in May. <i>(Jul 13, 1907,NZ Truth,108/5/5</i>)

>  She did not know what a `seaweed pencil' was, nor what a `tent' was. She had informed no girl that she had used a crochet needle for a certain purpose, and she had, as a matter of fact, never used the needle. <i>(Sep 28, 1907,NZ Truth,119/4/7</i>)

### tent camp
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a temporary musterering camp composed of tents and tent flies.

>  <b>MCCASKILL</b> With no accommodation on those distant blocks, a tent camp is needed for two or three nights, a useful experience for a young stockman. <i>(1969,Molesworth,194</i>)

>  A party of four (Pyne, Ward, Fitzgerald and a DSIR assistant) worked on the 2 m thick sea ice from tent camps, using specially designed ice auger, winch and corer. <i>(Feb 1981,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,51,11 ed.</i>)

### tent fly
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [fly](fin-fly.md#fly))

### tentage
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CRUMP</b> He'd read it in silence, then go on [check] to sort out some tentage which Trevor and I would take for the new camp at the head of the river. <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A,15</i>)

### tenth
 
 <i>n.</i> [usually tenths]

>  As it was a test suit by which their right to all the other sections was to be determined, their claim to the 'tenths' has passed away. <i>(1877,NZ Parliamentary Debates,517</i>)

>  The matter is still further complicated by the stipulation attached to the various trusts, such as the Kemp Trust, and the Native tenths, from which some of the medical officers are paid. <i>(May 16, 1913,Poverty Bay Herald,8</i>)

>  Attempts to place a contemporary value on the nearly four million acres Ngai Tahu should have owned had the Crown set aside the promised "tenth" of all land sold is extraordinarily difficult. <i>(Nov 1993,North & South,60</i>)

>  The Whakarewa Grant was part of what was called the `tenth', a term coined by the New Zealand Company for land it reserved for Maori people. The principle of the tenths was conveyed in the instructions from the New Zealand Company in 1839 to its principal agent, Colonel William Wakefield, before he set out for New Zealand. In `every purchase of land from the natives ... a proportion of the territory ceded will be reserved by the Company and held in trust by them for the future benefit of the chief families of the tribe ... `You will explain that after English immigration and settlement a tenth of the land will be far more valuable than the whole was before'. ... To Alexander MacKay, commissioner of Native Lands, writing in 1872, the tenths `guarded the natives that common failing of all Aboriginal races, want of foresight, and it secured them from the dangers to which colonisation exposed them'. In short, land was a better asset for the Maoris than cash. In Nelson, the `tenth' comprised a total of 15,000 acres of land, involving a mix of urban and rural land in Nelson, Motueka, and Golden Bay. Most of that land remains in Maori ownership and today is managed by an incorporation. The Whakarewa land - the most valuable area of the tenth - has however been subverted from the control of the ... <i>(Jul 1986,New Outlook,28</i>)

>  [Colonel William Wakefield] set about negotiating the purchase of some 160,000 acres and in Septmeber 1839 a deed of purpose, written in English, was signed by 16 local chiefs. It provided that one tenth of the land purchased would be reserved for the Maori owners of the Land... No proper explanation was given to Maori of the 'tenths' reserve scheme, nor were they made aware that the deed of purchase made no provision for the retention by Maori of their pa, cultivations, and burial grounds. <i>(Jun 2003,Wel-com (Wellington),13</i>)

>  They claim that they are descended from one of the chiefs who sold the land around Port Nicholson to Col. Wakefield in the early days of settlement, and that they are entitled to their share of what are known as the Wellington tenths. <i>(Sep 6, 1899,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  Fortunately for some of the Maoris of the Porirua and Hutt districts they are in receipt of small yearly sums from the proceeds of their interests in the Nelson and Wellington "tenths" and the rents of small pieces of land. <i>(Nov 2, 1906,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  Of the 110,000 acres so measured one tenth part is to be reserved for the use or benefit of the aborigines, according to the original plan of sale proposed by the New Zealand company; and the lands for the aborigines shall be selected by lot, as provided for by the same plan of sale. <i>(Dec 10, 1840,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,1</i>)

>  [The Maori reserves] comprise, for example, the Wellington, Nelson, and Motueka "tenths", being a tenth part of the land reserved for the Maoris by the New Zealand Company. <i>(1955,NZ Parliamentary Debates,307/2947</i>)

### tenting
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Tenting Amongst the Lyell Mountains. [title] <i>(Dec 1904,NZ Illustrated Magazine,163</i>)
